- Starts in the parent of the active project (of the group's first project for **add project**). `Enter` goes into the selected folder (or imports the selected `.code-workspace`), or opens the current one when nothing is selected. Typing a path walks it; a paste goes through the same path (e2e opens projects this way). Folders are read on the main thread.

## Worktrees (`git/worktree.rs`, `app/worktrees.rs`)

- Only when the project folder is a repo root. The name is the branch; the slugged name is the folder, under `<worktrees folder>/<repo>/<slug>` (`~/.cornercase/worktrees` by default). `git worktree add` runs on a thread and answers with an `AppEvent`.
- Every linked worktree of the repo shows up, also ones created outside cornercase (`App::refresh`, throttled to once a second).
- Removing asks first, runs `git worktree remove` (`--force` after a "remove anyway") and never deletes the branch. The worktrees a flow run gave its roles (#313, see Flows) go with the run's workspace: the check, the teardown and the removal cover each of them, and none is removed alone while its run lives.
- **Removal runs in the background** (#98): deleting a checkout with `node_modules` or a `target/` folder takes seconds, and the dialog used to stay open (`removing…`, unclosable) until git finished. Opening the dialog runs `git status --porcelain` on a thread (`git::worktree::changed`, `AppEvent::Worktree(WorktreeEvent::Checked { … })`), which is what makes `git worktree remove` refuse, so a worktree with changes says so at once and offers **remove anyway** before anything stops. Only an explicit clean answer counts as clean: a git status that fails (or a job that panics) counts as changes, so the shells keep running unless the user confirms **remove anyway**. Confirming closes the dialog (`Overlay::RemoveWorkspace`'s `Check`: `Running`, `Confirmed` when confirmed before git status answers, which shows `checking…` and goes on by itself when the answer is clean, then `Clean` or `Changed`, the latter being what offers **remove anyway**; `Esc` always closes it). The workspace becomes `Phase::Removing(label)`: its shells are stopped first, since an agent or a dev server would keep writing into a folder being deleted, and that is why the check comes before; the view steps off it to the nearest open workspace (`Project::step_off`), as closing does. `App::start_removal` is the one place that starts it, and only on an `Open` workspace, so a dialog and a command racing on one worktree start one removal. The phase keeps the label because a checkout git is deleting has no branch left to read: the row would turn into `default` halfway. Its row stays, dimmed, with `removing…` where `↓n` goes, without `×` or `+ Tab`; `App::workspace_hit` and `App::tree_hit` drop hits on it, so left clicks and right-click menus ignore it in every layout; search and the issue places list only `Workspace::open` ones. `sync_worktrees` keeps it even once its folder is gone, so the row does not vanish and come back while git cleans up. Git's answer drops the row with a `removed <name>` toast, or puts it back (`App::settle_removal`), without its tabs, with git's message in a toast; opening the dialog again checks again and offers **remove anyway**. Several removals can run at once. Checking at confirm time instead of on open was rejected: the dialog would need a busy state again. A server that stops meanwhile leaves `git` to finish on its own; `sync_worktrees` brings the worktree back if its folder survived.
- **Locked worktrees**: The removal check reads whether the worktree is locked (`git::worktree::lock`: `git rev-parse --absolute-git-dir`, then its `locked` file, whose text is the reason, as gitrepository-layout documents), since `git worktree remove` refuses a locked worktree even with `--force`, and that refusal used to come after the shells had stopped. Claude Code locks the worktrees it creates (`claude session <name> (pid N start …)`) and unlocks them only when it exits cleanly, so a ` restart now ` that kills it leaves the lock behind. A locked worktree's dialog names the reason and, when the reason names a pid (`git::worktree::Holder`), whether that process still runs (shown only: a pid can be reused, so it never decides anything), and its button is **unlock and remove** (`git worktree unlock`, then `git worktree remove`; the unlock runs only if the lock is still there, since a Claude Code in the workspace may have released it when its shell was stopped and `git worktree unlock` fails on an unlocked one; `--force` is added when it also has changes). A lock found after confirming early (`checking…`) stops there, like changes do. Unlocking silently when the pid is gone (it depends on Claude Code's undocumented format) and `--force --force` on **remove anyway** (which would skip locks under a button that only speaks of changes) were rejected. `cornercase close --remove-worktree` never unlocks: with or without `--force` it answers an error naming the reason and `git worktree unlock <path>`, before anything stops; `--force` now waits for the same check.
- `cornercase close --remove-worktree` still answers once the worktree is gone and takes the same path: it checks first and answers an error, stopping nothing, when the worktree is locked or, without `--force`, has changes; then it stops the shells and removes, and a refusal puts the row back. Every command that names a workspace (`close`, `new-tab`, `start`, `focus`, `rename`) refuses one being removed or closed (`usable_workspace`): a tab opened there would run in a folder being deleted.
- `.worktreeinclude`: files matching it **and** ignored by git are copied into a new checkout, before the project's setup runs (see Worktree setup). Matching is delegated to git (`ls-files`, `check-ignore`).
- **Commits to pull** (`↓n` at the end of a workspace row, before the `×`): every 3 s a thread per project counts `HEAD..@{upstream}` in each workspace and answers with `AppEvent::Worktree(WorktreeEvent::Behind { … })`; it runs `git fetch --all` first once `fetch_minutes` (default 5, 0 turns all of it off) have passed. One thread per project at a time. Git never prompts (`GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0`, empty `GIT_ASKPASS`, `SSH_ASKPASS_REQUIRE=never`); failures count as 0.

## Worktree setup (`git/worktree/setup.rs`, `project/settings.rs`, `app/worktree_setup.rs`, `app/project_settings_dialog.rs`, `ui/dialog/project_settings.rs`, #250)

- **Why**: a fresh worktree has no `node_modules`, no built tools, no `.env`; every issue started in one began with the agent (or the user) running the same commands by hand. A project can now say what to run when a worktree is made, and what to run before it goes.
- **Stored in `config.json`, per project folder** (`projects`: `{ "~/code/web-shop": { "setup": "…", "teardown": "…" } }`, `config::ProjectSettings`; keys match by expanding `~`, saving replaces every key naming the folder and removes it when both texts are empty). Not `session.json` (closing the project would lose it) and not a file in the repository: a cloned repository would run its own commands on the first worktree, which is why a project's flows show their `env` and `args` in red. A team shares setup by checking in a script and writing one line, `./scripts/setup.sh`.
- **Edited in a modal**, from ⋯ or right-click on a project row (**project settings**, only for a repository root, since only those have worktrees): two multi-line fields (`Editor`), `Tab` between them, `Enter` a new line, **save** the button only (as the flow import page). No text box when a project is added: a question asked at the moment one only wanted a project (as with group colours). The New workspace dialog says what will run instead (`then runs the project's setup: npm ci …`, `Note::Info`). **No list of files to copy**: `.worktreeinclude` already does it and is copied before the setup runs.
- **Setup runs visibly in the new worktree's first pane, which then becomes the user's shell.** `Term::spawn` runs `/bin/sh -c WRAPPER cornercase-setup SCRIPT STATUSFILE $SHELL ROOT BRANCH`: the wrapper runs the script with `sh -ec` (the first failing command stops it; `CORNERCASE_ROOT`, `CORNERCASE_WORKTREE` and `CORNERCASE_BRANCH` set for that command only), prints `✓ setup finished` or `✗ setup failed with exit status N`, writes the status to `setup-<pane>.status` next to the socket (a private 0700 folder; `set_setup_dir` makes it absolute, a relative `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` would resolve inside the worktree) and `exec`s the user's shell. Progress bars, colours and prompts work, the output stays in the scrollback, `Ctrl+C` stops only the script, nothing is typed into the user's shell and no "keep the pane after its program exits" machinery is needed. Rejected: typing the script into the shell (depends on its syntax and prompt timing), a separate setup tab, an OSC sequence or the title as the signal, and a background job (no output, no prompts). It runs under `/bin/sh` rather than the user's shell so it behaves the same everywhere; call your own shell for its functions (`zsh -lic 'nvm use && npm ci'`). No timeout: it is visible and cancellable.
- **The pane's state** is `Term::setup` (`setup::Pane`): `Running { status, since }` while the wrapper runs (the pane stays a command pane: `▸`, not at its prompt, listed by restart as running), then `Failed { code, unseen }`. `App::watch_setups` (every refresh) reads the status file, **the only signal that a setup ended**: guessing from the wrapper's process arguments was tried and dropped, since a forked child shows its parent's arguments until it `exec`s and the write races the `exec`. A wrapper killed without writing one ends with its pane, which the next watch sees as a vanished pane (`App::setups` remembers each running setup's workspace and file): its status file is removed, the workspace's launches are dropped like on a failure and a flow's run ends. Status files are also swept at server start. A `Failed` mark is cleared when the workspace starts another setup or one succeeds, so a later unrelated failure is never blamed on it. When the setup ends the pane becomes a shell (`became_shell`).
- **Agents wait.** Every launch in a workspace with a running setup is held (`held_launch_panes`, skipped by `drive_launches` and not counted by `tick_at`, so a long `npm ci` does not redraw every client ten times a second). On success the launches restart their quiet clock (`Launch::wait_from`), so the command is typed once the shell's prompt settles; on failure they are dropped, requests waiting on them answer `the setup of workspace W failed with exit status N, so its agent did not start; cornercase read --pane P shows its output`, a flow's run ends, and a toast and (for a hidden tab) a desktop notification say `setup failed in project › workspace`. The failed tab shows a red `!` (`Tab::status` rolls it up like an agent's) until it is shown. This covers the issues dialog, `start --worktree`, the queue, Telegram and Discord `/new` and flows, since they all go through launches. `restart --when-idle` counts held launches as typing, so a restart waits for the setup. Closing the setup tab drops every launch of the workspace, a flow's members included. `App::setups` is filled when the pane is spawned, not at the first watch, so a setup closed before the first tick is still noticed; a closed setup says so in a toast (`setup was closed in project › workspace: its agent did not start`) and the run ends with that reason in the log. A failed setup is blamed in an error answer (`setup_failure`) only until another tab or split starts in the workspace (`Failed.blame`), so a later unrelated failure is never reported as the setup's; the `!` and `status` stay.
- **Only when cornercase just made the worktree** (`worktree_created`, `open_workspace`'s `setup`, the control and flow paths after `git worktree add`), not for worktrees found outside cornercase, workspaces reused on an existing branch or restored sessions. **run setup** in a worktree workspace's menu opens a new tab running it with no agent, to retry after a fix. `new-workspace --worktree` answers once the setup is done (`Stage::Setup`; an error naming the exit status when it failed), and `status --json` gives each workspace a `setup` (`running` or `failed`), `(setting up)` / `(setup failed)` in the text.
- **Teardown runs in the removal job**, after the shells stopped and before `git worktree remove` (`/bin/sh -ec` in the worktree, empty stdin, the same variables, five minutes via `process::output_within`, which kills the process group). A failing teardown does not stop the removal: the toast says `removed X, but its teardown failed: exit status 1: <last line>` and `close --remove-worktree` prints `warning: …` on stderr and exits 0 (`Done::warning`). The removal dialog says `Its teardown runs first.`
- **Logs** hold sizes, exit statuses and timings, never the script or its output; a failed teardown's one-line reason (its last output line, at most 160 characters) is logged at debug, since it is also in the toast. Output of a running teardown is not shown anywhere while the row says `removing…`: a known limit, as the removal runs in the background.
- **Rows**: a workspace running its setup shows a muted `setting up…` tag (`WorkspaceEntry::setting_up`).
- **Simulation**: the project menu lists **project settings** and says it needs a real cornercase.

## Pull requests (`pull_request.rs`, `gh.rs`, `app/pull_requests.rs`, `ui/workspaces.rs`, #251)

- **Why**: after reviewing the diff, the loop went on outside cornercase: push, open the pull request, watch CI in a browser, come back to tell the agent what broke. With several agents in their own worktrees nothing said which line of work was green, still running or broken. The issue asks for creating the pull request too (`gh pr create` from a button, `cornercase pr`); this first step only shows the pull request an agent or a person already opened, and its checks, since that is what tells you where to look next.
- **Where it comes from**: `gh pr view --json number,title,url,state,isDraft,statusCheckRollup`, run in the workspace's folder with no argument, so `gh` picks the pull request of the branch checked out there with its own rules (the branch's upstream and push remote, a fork's owner, open ones first, only open ones for the default branch). Querying GitHub's GraphQL for several head branches in one call was rejected: it would redo those rules, and `gh` already holds the user's login, so cornercase never reads a token. `gh` saying `no pull requests found` is no pull request, not a failure.
- **`gh.rs`**: the runner moved from `issues/github.rs` into a shared leaf, used by both: the environment that keeps `gh` from prompting, paging or colouring, the first line it prints as the error, and now a minute's limit (`process::output_within`, shared with the Tailscale worker, which had the same loop), since a hung `gh` would otherwise hold a project's job for good. The limit covers reading the output too: a program that exits and leaves a child holding its stdout (a wrapper running something in the background) would otherwise keep the readers waiting, so the command runs in a process group of its own, and the whole group is killed when the time is up.
- **Checks** (`pull_request::Check`): `statusCheckRollup` mixes GitHub Actions jobs (`CheckRun`: `status`, `conclusion`, `detailsUrl`, `workflowName`) and commit statuses set by apps (`StatusContext`: `state`, `targetUrl`). Like `gh`'s own summary: a job not `COMPLETED` is pending; `SUCCESS` passed; `NEUTRAL` and `SKIPPED` skipped; `FAILURE`, `CANCELLED`, `TIMED_OUT`, `ACTION_REQUIRED` and `STARTUP_FAILURE` failed (gh counts the last as pending; a job that could not start needs looking at); a status's `SUCCESS` passed, `FAILURE` and `ERROR` failed, the rest pending. A check listed twice (same workflow and name) keeps the run started last, as `gh pr checks` does, except that a run not started yet (gh writes a zero `startedAt`) counts as the latest, so a queued rerun shows as pending rather than as the old result. `gh` fetches every page of the rollup when `statusCheckRollup` is asked for (`preloadPrChecks` in its finder), so a pull request with more than 100 checks is complete. The rollup (`Ci`) is failing if any failed, else pending if any runs, else passing if any passed, else none (no checks, or only skipped ones). They are listed failed first, then pending, passed and skipped, each by workflow and name, with commit statuses (no workflow) after the jobs. Unknown fields and kinds pass, missing ones are empty; `tests/fixtures/gh/2.101.0/pr-view.json` keeps the shape gh 2.101.0 printed.
- **When it is asked** (`App::ask_pull_requests`, on the 1 s sync step of `refresh`): every open workspace on a branch (`git::checked_out`: a detached `HEAD` has no pull request) of a repository with a remote (`git::has_remote`, from the config file, so a local repository, and every test repository, never runs `gh`), except the default branch (`git::origin_head`, `origin`'s `HEAD`, read from the file; gh would only find an open pull request from it, rarely what anyone wants, and the main checkout would cost a call a minute). One job per project at a time, which asks its due workspaces one after the other and stops at the first failure: the next ones would fail the same way (no `gh`, logged out, offline, not a GitHub repository), and calling them all in parallel would hit GitHub at once. Workspaces sharing a folder (plain workspaces) are asked once. A workspace is due every 20 s while its checks run (`RUNNING_EVERY`), every minute otherwise, a pull request newly opened included (`OPEN_EVERY`), and every 5 minutes once merged or closed (`SETTLED_EVERY`); a project whose job failed waits 5 minutes (`FAILED_EVERY`). Opening the dialog asks again at once and lifts the wait. With `gh` taking about a second, the job's line in `server.log` is promoted to `INFO` only past 30 s (`Job::slow_after`), not past the usual second. The cost: a call a minute per feature branch, a GraphQL point each, against GitHub's 5,000 an hour per user; the server keeps asking while no window is attached, like the `↓n` fetch, since `status` reads it too.
- **Branch changes**: what is found is kept per workspace id with the branch it was asked for (`app::pull_requests::Found`). A workspace that is now on another branch, a detached `HEAD` or the default branch drops it on the next sync, so a checkout never shows another branch's pull request for more than a second, and an answer for a branch the workspace has left is dropped. Closing a workspace drops it too (only open workspaces are listed).
- **The row** (`ui::theme::pull_tag`): `#485` plus the rollup's glyph, all in its colour: `✗` red, `•` yellow, `✓` green; muted without checks; merged in the brand purple (GitHub's merged colour is purple too) without a glyph, since its checks no longer matter; closed muted and crossed out. One colour for the whole tag rather than a muted number and a coloured glyph: `Tags::fit` treats each span as one tag, and one colour reads at a glance; the glyph keeps it readable without colour. It sits after the agents' attention icon and before `↓n` (`Tags::fit` drops the last tags first in a narrow column), on every layout that draws workspace rows (lists, tree, compact). A draft looks like an open one there; the dialog says draft. It is clickable (see The dialog).
- **The dialog** (`Overlay::PullRequest`): ` ⋯ ` or right-click on a workspace row offers **pull request #485** when there is one (`target_menu`), and `g p` opens it for the workspace on screen. It reuses the update dialog's view (`ui::Update`, as Restart does) with a second button: the title and number as its message, then the summary (`open · 1 failed, 3 passed`) and one line per check with its glyph and workflow. **copy link** (the submit, so `Enter` copies) copies the pull request's address through OSC 52, since the server may be on another machine and cannot open a browser on yours; **ask agent**, only while a check failed, pastes `pull request #485 failed its checks: <name> <link>, … ` into the workspace's agent like a hunk's reference (`App::ask_agent`), not sent, so the user adds a note; without an agent it copies the text. Pasting the failed job's log was rejected: logs run to megabytes, and an agent with the link reads the part it needs with `gh run view`. The dialog closes when the pull request goes away. Clickable check lines (to copy one job's link) were left out: the pull request's page lists them.
  - **The badge opens it** (#279): a left press on the row's `#485✗` opens the same dialog for that workspace, like ` ⋯ ` and `×` on press, without making it active, as the menu entry does (`open_pull_request` closes the compact menu). Pressing anywhere else on the row still grabs it, so selecting, dragging and folding work as before; a right-click on the badge still opens the row menu. The geometry is `ui::pull_badge` (`workspace_pull` for the lists, `tree_pull` for the tree), shared by drawing and hit testing: it runs `Tags::fit` over the same marks (`marks`, shared with `draw_workspace_band`) and asks `Tags::place` where the pull tag landed, so a badge that was dropped in a narrow row, a row being removed and a row scrolled out of view have no target. The area is the badge's cells, one row high, or the band's height in compact mode like its buttons. Hover underlines it in its own colour (`Tags::highlight`), so the colour keeps its meaning, only while `sidebar_hovered`. The name is truncated and measured in cells (`truncate_cells`, `text::cells`) rather than characters, since a wide name would otherwise shift the tags away from the click area. The simulation has no badges, so it has nothing to click.
- **Without gh**: nothing shows on the rows; **settings → Worktrees → pull requests** (`pull_requests` in `config.json`, on by default) says why in its note when every project's last answer failed (`install gh, GitHub's CLI, to see them`, `gh is not logged in: run gh auth login`, or gh's own line), `g p` toasts it, and `server.log` has one `WARN` per new reason (`pr: cannot read pull requests`). Off asks nothing and drops what was found. On by default because a user without `gh`, or without remotes, pays nothing (the spawn fails at once, or nothing is asked), and the feature is invisible until a pull request exists.
- **Scripts**: `status --json` gives each workspace a `pull_request` (`number`, `title`, `url`, `state`, `checks`, `failed`), the text form `pull request #485 open, checks failing`. A command to create one (`cornercase pr`) and events when checks finish were left for later.
- **Simulation**: its workspaces have no pull requests (it has no GitHub); its Worktrees tab lists the row and its keys menu `g p`, and both say they need a real cornercase, as the icon bar does.
- **Tests**: a fake `gh` (`test_util::fake_gh`) answers by the branch it runs on; app tests use a clone with a worktree, so the default branch and remotes count. `new_app` points a default `gh` at `/nonexistent/gh`, so no app test runs the real one even in a repository with a remote.

## Merged pull requests (`pull_request/merged.rs`, `pull_request/merged/`, `git/remote.rs`, `app/merged_pull_requests.rs`, #293)

- **Why**: Stats counted agent work but not what came of it. The pull requests you merged are the plainest number of work done, and they are already at GitHub and GitLab, so Stats asks for them: one row, `merged PRs`, in the Stats tab, `cornercase stats`, its JSON and the share card.
- **Whose tools**: only `gh` and `glab`, which hold the user's logins; cornercase's own GitLab token (the issues browser's) is not a source. Accounts come from `gh auth status --json hosts` (every host and login whose state is `success`; gh lists one twice when `GH_TOKEN` and the keyring hold it, so sources are deduplicated by provider, host and login, lower case) and `glab auth status --all --output json` (one account per host; it exits 1 when any host fails but still prints the JSON, so stdout is parsed whatever the status). A missing tool, no logged-in account, or a tool too old for those flags makes that provider **unavailable**: dropped silently, and not a partial count. Any other discovery failure keeps the known sources and marks them failed.
- **The token exception**: "cornercase never reads a token" (Pull requests) holds for the active account, which runs with the inherited environment. An inactive `gh` account cannot be queried without switching, and switching would change the user's login behind their back and race other shells, so its token is read with `gh auth token --hostname H --user L` and given to that one search as `GH_TOKEN` and `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN`, with `GITHUB_TOKEN` and `GITHUB_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN` removed from the child's environment. It lives in the job's memory only, never logged or stored, and is scrubbed from error text. `auth switch`, `login` and `logout` are never run. glab has one account per host, so nothing is injected; if glab refreshes its own OAuth token, that is its normal behaviour. Both tools run in the home folder (or `/`), never in a project, always with `--hostname`.
- **The query**: GitHub, one GraphQL search per account, `is:pr is:merged author:<login> merged:<from>..<to>` with UTC days from the day counting began, paginated by `gh api graphql --paginate` (one JSON document per page, read with a stream deserializer). No `repo:` qualifiers: one repository an account cannot see makes GitHub reject the whole query, and a global search keeps the cost at one call per account whatever the projects. GitHub caps a search at 1000 results, so a range reporting more is split in half by dates, at most six levels; a range that still passes the cap is `truncated` and makes the count partial. GitLab, `merge_requests?scope=created_by_me&state=merged&updated_after=<since>` (the REST API has no `merged_after`; a merge updates `updated_at`, so this is a superset) read one page of 100 at a time (`page=N`, up to `MAX_PAGES` 20, since `glab api --paginate` has no bound and a 60 s timeout would discard everything read), ending on a short page; reaching the bound with a full page, or a later page failing, keeps what was read and marks the list truncated (`n+`); a failing first page is the account's error. Then a local filter on `merged_at`. Every result is filtered locally on its merge time against `since`.
- **Scope and identity**: a result counts when its repository is a remote of an open project (`git::remote::of`: every remote of the project's common git config, so a fork's `upstream` counts and a worktree adds nothing). A remote is placed under the account host that serves it (equal, a subdomain such as `ssh.github.com` or `altssh.gitlab.com`, or glab's `ssh_host`; a GitLab web remote drops glab's `subfolder`). A remote matching no host (an SSH alias such as `github.com-work`) is kept as a bare path and matches any result with that path: the alias is never resolved and `~/.ssh/config` never read, since a work repository cloned through an alias is the common case. The identity of a pull request is host, repository (lower case) and number, so the same one found by two accounts, two projects or two remotes counts once. Scope is applied when counting, not when asking, so a new project needs no new query. `git/remote.rs` holds the remote URL parsing that the GitLab issues browser used (moved there unchanged).
- **Windows**: all three columns count merges at or after `Stats::since()`, with the hour-aligned 24 h and 7 d windows of the other rows (`stats::windows`), so total is never below the others and `counting for n days` states the period. Provider history older than that is ignored: a total beyond counting would not be comparable with the rows around it.
- **Cache and pacing** (`Cache`, in memory only): one entry per source with its last list and last error. A query that fails keeps the previous list and sets the error, so a failure never turns into zero; a source no longer listed is dropped. The row is `None` (hidden) until some source has ever succeeded; a real zero is `Some`. It is complete only when every source has a list, no error and no truncation, and no discovery failed; otherwise numbers show a `+` (`12+`). Nothing persists: `stats.json` is unchanged, so old files and older binaries are unaffected and `cornercase stats` without a server leaves the row out; the first read after a start takes seconds. `App::ask_merged` runs in the 1 s sync step: one job at a time (`pr merged`, level debug, promoted past 30 s), every 15 minutes, 5 minutes after a failure, when the set of remote hosts changes, and when Stats opens with data over 5 minutes old; drawing never waits. **No project with a parseable remote asks nothing**: that gate keeps every test repository, which has none, from running `gh` or `glab` (as `has_remote` does for pull requests), and `new_app` points both at `/nonexistent`.
- **The view**: `stats::View::merged` (`Option<MergedRow>`, JSON key `merged_pull_requests`, always present, `null` when hidden; `#[serde(default)]` so a newer command reads an older server) carries `24h`, `7d`, `total`, `complete` and the accounts with their errors; `App::stats_view` fills it, so the dialog, the share pill and the `stats` request agree. The rows come from `View::rows`, which the dialog and the text share, with the label appended last so the others keep their positions. The share link is `#v3` (an eleventh number per window) only when the row is shown and complete, else exactly `#v2`: a lower bound never goes on a public card. Logs: one `WARN` per new reason per provider and host (`cannot read merged pull requests`), then `info` when read again; hosts and counts only, never logins or tokens.
- **Hidden and shown cases**: no open project with a remote hides the row (an empty scope would otherwise show a real-looking zero). A remote on a host no account serves (Bitbucket, Gitea) matches nothing; only an SSH-alias-looking host (no dot, or starting with an account's host, such as `github.com-work`) matches by path, so a same-named repository on another service is never counted, while an alias that looks like neither is missed. With no agent counted yet the dialog still draws the table when the merged row exists (zeros for agents), since it is a separate metric and `cornercase stats` shows it; the share pill stays hidden until something is counted. The simulation's Stats page has the row too: its table is built from the site's `ROWS`, whose example carries the eleventh number.
- **Setting**: `merged_pull_requests` (Settings → Worktrees, on by default, its note names the accounts or the reason) and `glab`, the command, beside `gh`. Off drops everything and asks nothing.
- **Tests**: fake `gh` and `glab` scripts over fixtures from gh 2.101.0 and glab 1.123.0 (`tests/fixtures/gh/2.101.0`, `tests/fixtures/glab/1.123.0`, synthetic logins) cover discovery, no switching, pagination, the cap and its split, unavailable and partial states, duplicates and remote matching; the share fixture `stats/share-v3.txt` is checked by Rust and the site. Rejected: reading `~/.ssh/config` to resolve aliases, a persisted cache, and cornercase's own GitLab token as a source.

## Changes panel (`changes/`, `ui/changes.rs`, `app/changes_panel.rs`)

- Git workspaces only: ` Changes n ` next to ` Issues ` (` ± ` in the compact bar) opens a fourth column right of the pane (full screen in compact mode). Its border drags; its width is `Widths::changes` (`None` = half the free space, at most `DEFAULT_WIDTH`).
- Tabs: uncommitted (`git diff HEAD` + untracked), commits (`git diff <merge-base> HEAD`), all (`git diff <merge-base>` + untracked). The base is `Workspace::base` (saved per workspace) or the default branch: `origin/HEAD`, else `main`/`master`. Comparing from the merge-base keeps commits made on the base later out of the diff.
- Git runs on a thread, one job at a time, every second while it or the files panel is open (`Panel::watched`, for its marks) and every 3 s while closed (for the button's count). Flags matter: `--no-optional-locks` and `-c diff.autoRefreshIndex=false`, because a plain `git diff` rewrites `.git/index` (takes `index.lock`) even with `--no-optional-locks` and would fight an agent's `git commit`. `--relative` keeps paths inside the workspace folder. Untracked files are read directly (never `git add -N`), capped at 1 MiB, NUL in the first 8000 bytes means binary; a symlink shows its target path, like git, never the file it points to. The branch list and "unchanged lines" also run on threads and answer with `AppEvent::Changes(ChangesEvent::Branches { … })` / `AppEvent::Changes(ChangesEvent::Gap { … })`.
- The job hashes git's output; an unchanged hash answers without a diff, and files whose patch section did not change are reused, so highlighting runs only for what changed. syntect costs ~150-200 µs per line, far more than computing the diff.
- Rendering: lines paired delta-style (word tokens, changed share ≤ 60 %) get word emphasis; each hunk side (context + removed, context + added) is highlighted separately. Tints blend the host background with palette red/green when the client has `COLORTERM=truecolor` and the background is known; otherwise palette colours, and on dark themes only the `▎` bar, coloured numbers and word emphasis (palette greens and reds are too loud as line backgrounds).
- **Filter** (`changes/filter.rs`, pure): ` ⌕ ` left of the panel's `×` opens a field under the tabs (the header grows a row, so the summary and the base selector stay) that filters files by path only; searching the lines was tried and dropped as not useful. While it is focused (`Filter::focused`, and only while the panel shows and no overlay is open) keys and pastes go to it, like the global search: `Enter` keeps the filter and unfocuses, `Esc` or its `×` closes and clears. Any press outside the panel gives the keys back and keeps the query; a click on the field or ` ⌕ ` takes them again; closing the panel unfocuses it. Each word is an include, or an exclude with `!`; a file stays if it matches any include (or there is none) and no exclude, by its path or its old path. A word without `*`/`?` is a substring of the path; with them a `.gitignore`-like glob (no `/`: any path component, a trailing `/` only folders; with `/` or a leading `/`: the whole path, also everything under it; `*` and `?` stop at `/`, `**` crosses it). Smart case per word. Folds are untouched; typing resets the scroll. Shared by clients, never saved; switching tab or workspace keeps it. `Esc` on an empty field closes the panel (see TODO panel).
- Hunk actions on hover: open (new tab running `/bin/sh -c 'exec ${VISUAL:-${EDITOR:-vi}} "+$1" "$2"'`), ask agent (pastes `path:lines ` into the workspace's agent pane, found with `agents::detect`; without one it copies the reference), copy (the hunk as a patch, OSC 52). Viewed marks use a separate review identity, so they clear when the file changes while patch reuse and unchanged-line gaps follow its patch digest.
- **Scope** (#159): a row above the modes uses the dialogs' `tab_style`. The first tab is the active project's name, with ` › workspace` only when it has several workspaces, as in the agents section; `truncate_right` reserves the entire ` Every project ` tab and the filter/close buttons even at the minimum width. Project is the default and keeps its selected mode and base when another scope is chosen. Every project shows only uncommitted changes, with a muted `uncommitted` in place of the modes and no base selector: one base cannot describe unrelated repositories. Scope is shared and saved as an optional, defaulted field beside mode; session version remains 5.
- **Targets and grouping live in `changes/every.rs`** (#195): `targets` and `group` take the projects, the `sidebar_order` and the panel, and use neither `app` nor `ui`; `Body` moved into `changes` and `Header` into `every.rs`, where the app and `ui/changes.rs` import them. `App::every_targets` and `every_body` pass `sidebar_order`; `panel_view` keeps the folds, viewed marks, gaps and filter.
- **Grouping**: Every project follows `project::sidebar_order` with groups treated as open, then workspace order, so rows do not move according to how recently an agent saved. Only open git workspaces with changed files are listed; clean projects, plain folders and workspaces being closed or removed disappear. Each view resolves its scan targets once and shares them between grouping, the loading state and the live indicator, avoiding repeated reads of every checkout's HEAD during drawing and mouse handling. A project header carries its total files and line counts. Workspace headers appear only when several of that project's workspaces have changes, and use the workspace's label/branch. Clicking either header hides or reveals its file rows without switching focus or changing each file's fold. Project arrows sit one cell in, workspace arrows three and file arrows four, so files always read as children of their headers; project scope keeps its existing indentation. Scope tab drawing and clicks reserve the label's full cell width, including its trailing space, in the app and simulation. Headers whose files all fail the path filter are hidden; their totals still describe the complete group, like the panel's line totals. Until the first scan finishes an empty list says `reading changes…`; a completed empty view says `no changes in any project`. An open Every project view stays visible when the user switches to a plain folder or closes the last project; its entry buttons remain git-only.
- **Lazy patches**: Every project has separate file folds, initially folded, so opening it never inherits an expensive unfolded project view. Viewed marks and unchanged-line gaps remain keyed by workspace and path. A viewed file that changes loses its mark and opens again, as in project scope, so its new work becomes reviewable. The worker asks for porcelain v2 status and NUL-separated numstat, preserving renamed paths, binary files and workspace-relative paths. Untracked text uses the same byte, line and binary caps, without constructing hunks for the folded list. Patch parsing, tracked summaries and untracked reading share one fold classifier: binary first, then more than 3000 added and removed lines, then lockfiles, then ordinary text. The summary marks oversized tracked files as Large from numstat, before any unfold request, so they keep their counts-only state and never alternate between loading a large patch and discarding its hunks. Only unfolded files that can show lines and are visible under their headers and path filter get a patch and highlighting; their patches refresh on each visit. Folded headers therefore stop patch reads too. `collapse all` / `expand all` act across the collection; expanding all also reveals its headers. File actions carry their own workspace id and folder. Open and ask agent reveal the owning project, workspace and destination tab through the same navigation used by search and the agents list, unfolding the tree and closing the compact menu while keeping Every project open, so the user can immediately work in the editor or type a question after the reference. Copy keeps focus; ask agent without an agent keeps its clipboard fallback. An unfolded rename's patch request includes both its old and new paths: selecting only the destination prevents git from pairing the rename and incorrectly turns it into an added file.
- **Pacing and caches**: one shared worker slot serves both scopes, with a separate summary model per workspace so overview results cannot replace the project-mode diff used by the Files panel's git marks. Completion starts the next workspace immediately; each workspace waits at least three seconds between scans, and an unfold invalidates its wait. A worker holds the shared slot for up to 30 seconds, including across scope switches. After that both scopes can request another job, retaining the existing give-up behavior so one hung git cannot freeze every workspace's changes indefinitely; late answers are still accepted without releasing a newer job's slot. Closing Every project stops the sweep and resumes the existing active-workspace job. The Changes button still counts only the active workspace (uncommitted while Every project is visible), never the collection. Summary jobs use the same index-lock-avoiding flags and `log::Job` timings, labelled `summary`, with a workspace id; failed workspaces are omitted from the grouped files and follow the existing debug job logging policy. If the collection would otherwise be empty, its first scan error is shown instead of claiming every project is clean. A workspace fold only stops patch reads while its sub-header exists, so the fold cannot conceal the sole changed workspace after the others become clean. Closing a workspace or project, starting worktree removal, and discovering an externally removed worktree forget their models, request pacing, file folds, viewed marks, gaps and header folds. Late results for forgotten workspaces are ignored, keeping a long-running server from retaining removed checkouts' parsed diffs. Workspace-relative status prefixes are derived from the nearest checkout's .git entry, including linked worktrees, avoiding an extra rev-parse process on every scan.
- **Digests**: the summary hashes status, numstat, untracked bytes/metadata, HEAD, open paths and their patch bytes before constructing its diff; an unchanged digest skips numstat parsing and highlighting. For uncommitted files, review identity combines HEAD, path, status, counts and the checkout file's size, modification time and change time, shared by both loaders. Metadata catches edits with the same line counts without reading folded tracked files; change time also catches replacements or restored modification times. Every materialized file retains its patch-section hash for reuse and gap caching, separate from its metadata-based viewed identity. A write between git reading a patch and the metadata stat can give old and new patches the same review identity; their distinct patch hashes still replace the old highlighted hunks and discard stale gaps. Folded summaries use the metadata fingerprint until a patch is materialized. The full project loader still hashes patch output and reuses unchanged highlighted files. A scope switch preserves review marks for the same uncommitted file. Commits and All retain their patch digests: a committed comparison can change without touching the checkout file, so metadata cannot identify its patch.
- **Demo regression coverage**: website checks run the simulation on its virtual clock in wide and compact layouts, exercising the full scope-tab label, header indentation, action focus and the hero tour. The existing esbuild version is a direct development dependency for bundling those tests with the same image imports as the demo, keeping the checks independent of a browser and wall-clock timers.

## TODO panel (`todo/`, `ui/todo.rs`, `app/todo_panel.rs`)

- ` TODO ` opens a panel in the changes panel's column (`Areas::changes`, `Widths::changes`, `Border::Changes`); `App::side` selects at most one of the four (changes, TODO, files, flow), and opening the compact menu closes it through `show_side(None)`.
- **A dot while items are pending** (#179): the footer's ` TODO ` button shows a muted `●` after its label (` TODO ● `) while at least one item is pending, and nothing there when every item is done or the list is empty; the compact bar's ` ☐ ` gets the dot one cell to its right, inside its own 7-cell slot (`ui::chrome::todo_icon`, like the ` ≡ `'s badge), so the icon and the buttons beside it never move. Without it, a closed panel looked the same with ten pending items as with none, and items were forgotten. A dot rather than the count that was rejected before ("a number there would always be lit and say little"): it carries no number, it goes away when nothing is left, and it does not light up, since it is the muted grey (`View::muted`), never cyan or a status colour, so it does not compete with ` Changes n ` or an agent that needs you. The count stays in the panel's header. The dot sits inside the button, after its label (` TODO ● `), like Remote's green one (` Remote ● `), and the button grows two cells to the left to hold it, keeping its right edge in line with ` Changes ` (`Areas::todo_with_dot`, which drawing and hit testing share, so a click on the dot opens the panel). Until then the dot sat outside the button, on the cell left of its padding (` ● TODO `), so the button never moved; the user asked for it to work like Remote's. A column too narrow for the wider button (it would touch ` Files `) keeps the plain one and leaves the dot out. `todo_dot` in `config.json` (Settings → UI, on by default, like `counts`) turns it off, for whoever finds the mark noise.
- **`Esc` closes the side panel** (#164), whichever of the three shows (`App::panel_shown`, so a changes panel left open in a workspace without git leaves it to the pane), in every layout, as ` × ` does (`App::close_panel`: the files panel keeps its places, the changes panel its filter). A drag's `Esc`, the compact menu's and an overlay's come first, and a file shown full screen goes back to its panel first, a panel field with text keeps its `Esc` (it clears the files search or the changes filter, or drops the TODO field's text); on an empty field `Esc` closes the panel at once, so `f f`, `g d` or `t` then `Esc` opens and closes. That `Esc` never reaches the pane, so interrupting an agent or leaving insert mode with a panel open takes one more: accepted because the panel is on screen, so the first `Esc` shows what it did, whereas in compact mode, where the panel covers the pane, the `Esc` that reached the hidden pane did something nobody saw. Taking `Esc` only where the pane cannot use it (compact mode, an empty field) was rejected: in wide mode it would close a panel only sometimes, which is harder to learn than one rule. Giving the panel the keys on any click in it, with `Esc` then closing it, was rejected too: every click in the tree or the diff would quietly move the keys away from the pane; worth another look if the panels ever get keyboard navigation. One global list, the same in every project: lists per project and per workspace, and starting an agent from an item (` ▶ `, a worktree or tab per item), were built and dropped as more than a TODO list needs.
- **Storage**: `todos.json` next to `session.json` (`paths::todos`), its own `VERSION`, only `text` and `done` per item, written by a second `persist::Saver<todo::Saved>` once it settles, and flushed on shutdown (a list has no logout burst to fear, and a just-typed item must not be lost to `kill-server`). Item ids are `Todos`' own counter, not the hierarchy's. A missing, corrupt or other-version file means an empty list. The list lives in `App`, so every client sees the same.
- **Order**: pending items, then done ones. A new item goes to the end of the pending ones; checking moves an item to the end of the list, unchecking to the end of the pending ones; a drag keeps an item among its kind (`Todos::move_before` clamps). Undo (`todo::Removed`) puts items back at their old index, clamped to that order.
- **Layout** (`ui::todo`): a header (` TODO n `, ` clear done ` when something is done, ` × `), a gap, then a `ui::Rows` (variable heights, scroll by item, ` ↑ n more ` / ` ↓ n more `, sticky `+ New TODO`), the same as the sidebar lists, so the wheel, `Rows::edge` auto-scroll and `reveal` come for free. Items soft-wrap at a fixed text width (`text_width`: the row minus the checkbox and the reserved ` × ` column), so hover never reflows. The checkbox is `○`, a green `✓` once done, in the middle of its three cells (the hit area `[ ]` had). `ui::todo::{rows, check, delete, clear_done, spot}` serve drawing and hit testing.
- **Editing**: `todo::editor::Editor` is the only text field with a cursor (chars and an index; wrap keeps every char so line/column map back to the text; a full last line gets an empty one for the cursor). The terminal cursor is placed with `set_cursor_position`. `+ New TODO` turns into the field at the bottom; `Enter` adds and keeps it open for the next item, `Enter` on an empty field or `Esc` closes it. A click on an item's text edits it in place with the cursor at the click. Any press outside the field (in the panel or elsewhere) commits it (blur saves, so nothing typed is lost) and gives the keys back; `Esc` is the only way to drop a change. Saving an empty text keeps the old one. Pastes go in as one line.
- **Rows**: a press on an item grabs it (`Grab::Todo` in `Drag::Row` on `App::drag`); release in place is the click (edit), leaving the row is a drag. The landing is computed from the layout without the line (`ui::todo::drop`), and drawing inserts a one-row line. `[ ]` and ` × ` act on press; both also on the item being edited (`ui::todo::hit` looks at them before the field), committing the text first like any press outside the field (#108), so undo brings back what was typed. That item shows its ` × ` without hover: the row is not lit while it is a field, and the button used to vanish exactly while the item was selected.
- **Undo instead of confirming**: ` × ` and ` clear done ` remove at once and set a toast with an ` undo ` button (`Toast::undo`, `UNDO_FOR` 6 s instead of 2 s). Only the last removal can be undone. The toast's button is checked before overlays, so it works with a menu open.

## Star prompt (`star.rs`, `app/star_prompt.rs`, `ui/star.rs`, #276)

- **Why**: stars are how most people find and judge cornercase, and people who use it every day rarely think to give one. A small card asks now and then, in a way that never costs the user anything: no keys taken, no agent interrupted, nothing sent.
- **A card, not an overlay**: it is drawn like the toast (bottom right, shared by every window, `View::star`) and is not an `Overlay`, since an open overlay stops every mouse event from reaching the columns and the pane. It takes only the left press on its three buttons; any other mouse event inside its rect is consumed so nothing reaches the pane beneath; keys are never touched (`app::tests::star_prompt` checks they reach the pane). Without a star glyph: `★` is ambiguous-width in CJK locales.
- **When** (`star::Saved`, constants `FIRST_AFTER_DAYS` 14 and `LATER_FOR` 90 days): a day counts once when a window was drawn in the last 2 s (`App::draw` stamps it, the server draws every attached window at least each 500 ms tick) and some pane runs an agent, so it measures real use, not calendar days since install; UTC days, and a clock set back never counts twice. People who already use cornercase start counting from this release: seeding the count from `stats.json` would make the first thing after an update a request for a star. The first ask is due at 14 counted days, later asks 90 days after the last one was shown, never after **star** or **don't show again**.
- **Never in the way**: it shows only while nothing else wants the user's attention (no overlay, compact menu, drag, toast, TODO or files field, changes filter, and no agent working, waiting or failed; a pending or done mark does not count, or anyone with old review marks would never see it), and closes by itself the moment one of those holds. An unanswered card counts as **later**, with the 90 days counted from when it was first drawn (a card that closes before any frame showed it uses none), and the release of a press that answered it is swallowed with it, so it never reaches the pane, which also gives at most one ask per window session. The compact layout (below 90 columns) skips it, judged from the last drawn client like every other shared layout decision: the server cannot tell a `cornercase remote` window from a local one (the proxy forwards a plain `Hello`), and phones are compact. Keeping the card until it is answered was rejected: it would sit over a working agent's output.
- **Star on GitHub** copies the repository's address through OSC 52 (`App::copy`) and toasts it (`copied https://github.com/usecornercase/cornercase-terminal`) so it can be read without clipboard support: cornercase never opens a browser, since the server may be on another machine and cannot open one on yours, as with the pull request's link.
- **State** is `star.json` next to `session.json` (`paths::star`, version 1, plain pretty JSON, nothing sensitive): the first counted time, the days, the last counted day, when it was shown and the answer. A missing, corrupt or other-version file starts over. It is written on the server loop when a day counts, when the card shows and when it is answered (a few times a day at most). `star_prompt` in `config.json` (Settings → UI, on by default) turns it off: no counting, no card, no writes, and an open card closes on the next refresh. Logged as `star` (`active day`, `shown`, `closed`, `answered`), never anything but the counts.
- **Simulation**: only the settings row; the card needs 14 days of use.

## Queue (`queue/`, `issues/browser.rs`, `ui/dialog/queue.rs`, `app/queue_panel.rs`, `app/queue_dialog.rs`, `app/control/queue/`)

- A global queue separate from TODO: each entry carries an absolute UTC second, the client's zone, a captured prompt, agent command and mode, and a destination. It is the last tab of the Issues modal, `Queue` (`issues::browser::Tab::Queue`, always appended after the configured trackers), rather than a side panel with a footer button of its own: the footer, the compact menu and the icon bar already held seven buttons, the `schedule` action already sat beside `start` in that modal, and the modal's tabs, filter field, list, hint and button row fit a list of entries. The tab lists every project's entries, not only the modal's project, since the queue is global; each row is the local time, the label, and `status · agent · place`, the status coloured (failed red, missed yellow, started green), built by `App::queued_rows` and handed to the browser (`Browser::queued`) on every refresh and before each key or click, since the browser is a pure model. Its buttons follow the selected entry: `new`, `start now` (pending, failed or missed), `remove` (not starting), `clear started` (when something started) and `cancel`; `Enter` or a click edits a pending, failed or missed entry, opens a started one's pane and closes the modal, and does nothing while it starts. Typing filters. The schedule form keeps the browser it was opened from (`QueueRuntime::back`) and goes back to it when it closes: as it was on cancel, `Esc` or a click outside, and on the `Queue` tab after a save, so a scheduled issue shows up in the list. Nothing on the ` Issues ` button says that entries are pending (a dot was offered and declined); toasts and notifications say when one starts or fails. Without an open project the modal, and so the queue, cannot be opened, but nothing can be scheduled then either. Rows are chronological with insertion order for ties. Editing prompt or time preserves the captured agent and mode, including a removed custom choice; changing those choices captures the current command. Pending, failed and missed entries can be edited or started now; removing pending work cancels it. Started entries remain until cleared and link to their pane in the same server, with its token guarding against id reuse after a restart. Removing history does not stop an agent. `remove` and `clear started` remove at once and offer ` undo ` in the toast for 6 s, as the TODO panel does, instead of confirming. No recurring entries or concurrency limit.
- **Time**: Jiff parses client-local times (`14:30`, tomorrow and an explicit date), delays, and daylight saving transitions. The terminal's `Hello` carries its IANA zone or fixed offset, preserved by the remote proxy; the browser sends its own zone. A form captures the opening client's zone rather than changing under another client. Commands send their client's zone with each add/edit request. Ambiguous and nonexistent local times fail rather than guessing. A bare clock time is compared with the local clock before it is resolved, so `02:30` asked at 10:00 on the day the clocks go back means tomorrow's 02:30 instead of failing on today's repeated hour. A system zone without an IANA name (a copied `/etc/localtime`) falls back to `TZ` (an IANA name, a zoneinfo path or a POSIX rule such as `EST5EDT,M3.2.0,M11.1.0`), then to `/etc/timezone`, and only then to today's fixed offset, which would put a time chosen across a daylight saving change an hour off. Jiff bundles the time zone database (`tzdb-bundle-always`, about 200 KB), used when the server has no `/usr/share/zoneinfo`, so a zone name a client sends is never rejected for want of the system's files. `claude reset` and `codex reset` run the existing usage probe once while preparing an entry and capture the future `session (5h)` reset, with no account credentials read by cornercase and no polling. Missing, expired and unsupported resets fail visibly.
- **Destinations** use the project's path and, for a workspace, its path and optional custom name. Its id and server token identify it exactly during that server run; after restart a unique path/name match restores the destination. Closed, renamed or ambiguous restored destinations fail instead of falling back to whatever is active. Root starts use a workspace in the root, making one when absent. A new worktree uses its recorded branch. Issue URLs and keys are resolved by the existing tracker clients while adding; neither tokens nor tracker connections are saved in the queue. A URL names its tracker by its host, and the issue is then fetched through the connection of the project's tracker: GitHub and GitLab by its number in the project's own repository, the others by key. The URL found must name the same repository, site or workspace up to the issue's key (`queue::issue::place`; GitLab's `/-/work_items/N` counts as `/-/issues/N`), so a URL of another repository, Jira site, Plane workspace or Asana task fails instead of fetching the connected tracker's issue with the same key and starting it in the wrong worktree. A bare key goes to the only tracker that can read it: `#7` to the project's repository tracker (GitLab when it is connected and a remote in `.git/config` is on its server, else GitHub in a git repository), a number to that tracker or Shortcut, `sc-` to Shortcut, other keys to Linear, Jira, Plane or Asana (an ID-field key such as `ENG-42` looks the same in all four); when two can, `--source` is required rather than one being guessed. A bare number is never taken for Asana, whose task ids are 16-digit numbers: `--source asana` reads one by gid. An `app.asana.com` URL names Asana, and its task gid is the segment after `task` (`/1/<workspace>/project/<p>/task/<t>`, `/1/<workspace>/task/<t>`) or the third of the old `/0/<project>/<task>[/f]`; a URL with no task gid is an error. Asana compares the task gid only (`queue::issue::same_issue`), since Asana has moved its links between formats and a pasted one may differ from the `permalink_url` the API returns, so one of another task is refused as for the other trackers. `Issue` has no gid field (its key is the ID-field value when the project has one); the gid is read from the permalink, and `Issue::number`, which is 0 when a gid does not fit a `u64`, is not relied on. Editing an entry's issue moves its worktree branch to the new issue's, unless `--worktree` names one. UI scheduling uses the issue already selected and the configured prompt template.
- **Execution** reuses control's `Start`, worktree jobs and `Launch`, with internal clients in a separate range from terminal and chat app clients. Replies update the queue rather than going to a socket. Queued launches always accept folder trust questions, explicitly shown in the form; immediate starts still use `accept_trust_prompts`. They submit the captured prompt automatically rather than following the immediate issue start's `submit` setting, since the schedule form already lets the user edit it and waiting for Enter would stall unattended work; an explicit control request can still retain a draft. They keep the current focus. A launch failure or missing executable records the reason, with a toast and notification. An in-flight start is marked `starting` and written before creating terminals or worktrees; restore converts it to an interrupted failure so it cannot silently start twice. Starting entries cannot be edited or removed. The queue becomes ready once the server has a size and a restored session: from a window, or from `cornercase start-server` (`App::set_queue_area`, since `follow`, which sets it otherwise, only runs when a window is drawn); a stopped server or sleeping machine is not woken by an entry. A server is started by a window (`cornercase`, or the proxy of `cornercase remote`) or by `start-server`, and it keeps the last size after every window detaches, so the queue goes on running unattended. Restoring the session headless on every start when `queue.json` has entries, as a review suggested, stays rejected: the session would be restored before the first window's `Hello` on every start, with a size and colours nobody chose. `start-server` restores headless only when asked, with a size it was given or its 160×48 (see Client / server). A restart with no window attached leaves no server at all, which the queue does not change.
- **Clock and persistence**: `App::refresh_at` and `tick_at` take wall time separately from `Instant`, so scheduler tests advance it without sleeps. A due entry more than 600 seconds late becomes missed; exactly 600 seconds is within grace. The next due entry participates in `tick` after a window size exists, avoiding zero-duration wakeups before the session opens. `queue.json` beside `session.json` has its own version and durable ids; another session filename uses its stem plus `.queue.json` to isolate servers sharing a socket directory. Its `persist::Saver` is observed with the other independent stores and flushed on shutdown. Missing, corrupt or other-version files yield an empty queue. The saver starts from a valid file's original state, so restored missed/interrupted transitions are saved; an unreadable file uses the empty in-memory baseline and is not overwritten until an actual edit. Historical started links carry the original server token and never resolve against another server.
- **Forms and commands**: the schedule form asks the kind of time first (`at`, `tomorrow at`, `on`, `in`, `Claude reset`, `Codex reset`), then shows only that kind's values on the row below (a clock, a date and a clock, an amount and a unit, or nothing for a plan reset) and turns them into the same text the commands take (`14:30`, `tomorrow 9:00`, `2026-10-20 09:00`, `in 2h`, `claude reset`), so the server and the CLI did not change. A first version had one free text field for every format, with the formats listed in its label and reset buttons under it, and the place, agent and mode were rows that cycled on each click, with a `○`/`✓` toggle for a new worktree: what a click would pick next was invisible. Place, agent, mode and the delay's unit are comboboxes (see Dialogs), and `Open in` (`new tab` or `new worktree`) replaces the toggle; it is a choice only in a repository root, and the branch field shows only for a new worktree. A blank row separates the fields (the kind of time and its values stay together, as one field), since packed rows read as one block, and `Open in` keeps its row, saying `new tab` in grey where nothing else is possible, so the form (23 rows) keeps its height when the place changes. Field keys remain confined to that modal; `Tab` and `Shift+Tab` move through the shown controls, `Enter` saves unless a list is open. A line break in a prompt from a template or `--prompt` shows as `↵`, so each wrapped line stays one row and the cursor stays where it is drawn; typing and pasting still turn line breaks into spaces. A click in a scrolled prompt lands on the line it shows (`ui::queue_form::first_line`, shared by drawing and the click). In a window shorter than the form's 23 rows its error note takes the rows right above the buttons, over the trust notice. The issues browser drops ` schedule ` before anything else when its button row is too narrow, so ` start ` is never cut, and `Enter` on an issue always starts it, whatever button was pressed before. The issues browser's schedule action preserves its agent and place pickers. `queue add`, `list`, `edit`, `remove` and `run` use the existing JSON request channel, including asynchronous preparation for issue reads and plan probes. Adds print the queue id; list exposes pending, started, failed and missed entries as text or JSON. `queue add` without `--at`, or without `--prompt` or `--issue`, is wrong usage (exit 2) before the server is asked. The text list reads each entry's fields leniently, so a state or field added by a newer server prints instead of failing. Editing keeps unspecified properties.

## Files panel (`files/`, `highlight/syntax.rs`, `ui/files.rs`, `app/files_panel.rs`, `app/links.rs`)

- ` Files ` (on the todo row, under ` Issues `; ` ▤ ` in the compact bar) opens a panel in the changes panel's column with the active workspace's folder, so each worktree shows its own files; git or not. Read only: editing goes through ` open `.
- **Tree**: folders first, then files, by name ignoring case. Each open folder is listed on its own, on a thread, with `ignore` (one level; `.gitignore`, `.ignore`, the global and `.git/info/exclude` rules, also from parent folders; hidden files shown, `.git` never), at most `disk::MAX_ENTRIES` (10,000) entries. Listing per folder rather than walking the whole workspace keeps a huge folder (a home folder opened as a project) free until it is opened. The root and the open folders are listed again every `LIST_EVERY` (2 s) while the panel shows, one job at a time, so files an agent creates appear. Open folders, scroll, the open file and the last one opened live per workspace in `files::Panel`, in memory; the session keeps only whether the panel is open.
- **Viewer**: a click on a file reads it on a thread (`disk::read`): at most `disk::MAX_BYTES` (8 MiB) for text, or `disk::MAX_IMAGE_BYTES` (32 MiB) for a file whose first bytes are a recognised image (see Images), NUL in the first 8000 bytes means binary, invalid UTF-8 is replaced. Only regular files are read: the tree lists a link to a device or a FIFO as a file (a cloned repository can hold one to `/dev/zero`), and reading it would never end, growing the server's memory until it dies with every shell, or block its thread for good, so it shows as binary. The read itself stops after `MAX_BYTES + 1` bytes, so a file that grows after the size check cannot get past the cap. The first read answers plain text at once and the highlighted text after; then the file is checked every `CHECK_EVERY` (1 s) by size and modification time and read again when they change, answering only highlighted text, so colours never flash while an agent writes. Lines are cut at the panel's edge with `…`, tabs are four spaces. ` ‹ ` goes back to the tree or the kept search results, with the file's row marked when it belongs to the workspace. An outside file is remembered as the last file too, per workspace and only in memory, but has no tree row or git margin marks. An image file (by its first bytes, not its name) is not text: see Images.
- **Highlighting** (`highlight/syntax.rs`): tree-sitter through arborium, about 60 grammars compiled in: the languages agents touch most (TypeScript, TSX, JavaScript, Python, Rust, Bash, Markdown, Java, C) and every grammar under 0.3 MB (Go, JSON, YAML, TOML, HTML, CSS, GraphQL, Lua, HCL, Vue, Svelte, Dockerfile, Make…). Grammars weigh 0.01-17 MB each (SQL 9 MB, C++ and Kotlin 5 MB); all 112 of arborium's made a 169 MB binary. A language without a grammar borrows a relative's by its comment syntax (`highlight::syntax::FAMILIES`): `//` JavaScript, `#` Python, `--` Lua, `;` Scheme, so comments and strings come out right and only some keywords stay plain; a hand-written fallback lexer was built and dropped in favour of this table. Every JavaScript file goes to the TSX grammar, whose query colours JSX tags and attributes (JavaScript's does not). The grammar comes from the file name, then the extension, then the shebang. Captures map to palette colours (`highlight::syntax::style`) so the terminal's theme applies: keyword magenta, function blue, type cyan, string green, number and constant yellow, property and tag red, comment the muted grey in italics. tree-sitter was picked for speed and precision: a 3,500-line TypeScript file takes 48 ms against 0.47 s for syntect with fancy-regex and the JavaScript syntax (2.4 s with a real TypeScript one), and it colours types and fields the same everywhere. Sources over `highlight::syntax::MAX_BYTES` (2 MiB) stay plain. syntect still colours the changes panel and Markdown.
- **Git marks** follow the changes panel's project scope: its model for the active workspace, with its mode and base. A changed file gets its status letter and colour in the tree; a folder holding changes gets a `●`, yellow, or green when everything under it is new. Deleted files are not on disk, so only the changes panel lists them. In the viewer every change block of the file's hunks marks the margin (`files::gutter`): `▎` green on added lines, `▎` blue on changed ones, `▁` red on the line after which lines went (`▔` on line 1 when they were above it); an untracked or added file is all green. A click on a mark shows the old lines above the block in red, tinted like the changes panel's removed lines, and another click hides them (`Viewer::unfolded`, keyed by the line the block sits before; `files::Lines` maps rows to lines and old lines).
- **Search**: one bar under the title (`files::Query`, per workspace), with no query the tree shows everything. It searches the text in the files; the ` ▤ ` at its right switches it to file names and back (`files::Mode`, lit while on). Tabs (tree, names, text) were built first and dropped: one bar with a toggle is less to read, and an empty bar giving the whole tree makes the tree the search's starting point. The bar takes the keys like the changes panel's filter (`Query::focused`; a click on it takes them, any press outside the panel gives them back): typing searches at once, `↑` `↓` pick a result, `Enter` opens it, ` × ` clears it and `Esc` clears it and gives the keys back, or closes the panel when the bar is empty (see TODO panel). Until the answer to what was typed comes, the list keeps the previous results under `searching…` instead of going blank on every key (a text search can take tens of milliseconds on a large repository); an `Enter` pressed meanwhile waits for that answer and opens its first result (`Query::enter`), so typing and pressing `Enter` at once never opens a result of the previous query. Any other key, or a press outside the panel, drops the waiting `Enter`. Both modes search an index of the workspace's file paths (`search::index`: one parallel `ignore` walk with the tree's rules, at most `search::MAX_FILES` (200,000)), built on a thread once the bar is used and again every `INDEX_EVERY` (10 s) while it is; an answer remembers the index it came from, so a new index searches again. Measured on a 32,000-file repo: the walk takes 25 ms, a name search 1-20 ms, a text search 52 ms for everything and 1-3 ms for the first 50 matches.
- **Names** (`search::names`): nucleo's matcher (Helix's, fzf's scoring) with its path bonuses and smart case, best `MAX_NAMES` (200) first; on a tie the shorter file name, then the shorter path, so the file a query names comes before longer names in shallower folders (`workspace-session` gave `src/relay/workspace-session-handler.ts` before `src/renderer/src/lib/workspace-session.ts`); the matched letters are lit, and a path too long for the row loses its start so the file's name stays. One job at a time; a newer query waits for it and goes next.
- **Text** (`search::text`): ripgrep's `grep-searcher` and `grep-regex` over the indexed paths on up to 8 threads, the query a literal with smart case (regular expressions were left out: one more thing to learn for a quick look), binary files skipped (a NUL ends the file), at most `MAX_MATCHES` (1,000) matches: each match takes a slot before it is kept, so the threads together never keep more, and the search goes on until one more match is seen, so `Text::capped` (the note's `the first 1000 matches`) means a match was really left out, not just that the count reached the cap. A new query cancels the running search (`AtomicBool`) and starts at once. Results come grouped by file in path order; each line keeps its number, its text without the indent (at most 400 characters) and where it matched, and a long line is shifted so the first match shows. Opening a match opens the file at that line, selected so ` ask agent ` takes it at once, with every occurrence of the query lit in the viewer (`Viewer::find`); ` ‹ ` returns to the results.
- **Paths in panes** (`files/link.rs`): a path printed in the active pane, by an agent, a compiler, `git` or `rg`, opens in the files panel on a click, at its line or lines (`:12`, `:12:5`, `:12-30`, `#L12-L30`) and with them selected, so ` ask agent ` takes them at once; the panel opens if it was closed. The path is the run of path characters under the pointer (letters, digits, `_-./~@+,%:#`; brackets, quotes and backticks end it, a trailing `.,:;#` is dropped, a URL is none). A leading `~/` expands with the server's home folder (`config::expand_home`), and relative paths resolve from the foreground program's folder, then the workspace's root. Any regular file is taken, including one outside the workspace; folders, devices and FIFOs are not links. The canonical path stays relative inside the workspace and absolute outside it, so reading, ` open `, ` ask agent ` and copying the path reach the same file even when the agent runs in the workspace; a symlink is classified by its target. The one under the pointer is underlined in cyan (`ui::TabView::link`), checked on each draw: a snapshot row is already there and the check is a `canonicalize`. Claude Code's fullscreen renderer reads the mouse, so its clicks would never reach cornercase, but a plain click on a path does nothing there (it opens links with Ctrl+click, through a desktop file manager a server lacks). So a press on a path in a program that reads the mouse is held back (`Drag::Link` in `App::drag`): released on the same cell it opens the file and the program never sees it; moved to another cell, the held press is sent first and the drag goes on, so the program's own selection still works. The cost: a click right on a path in a Claude tool's header no longer expands it (a click elsewhere on the row does). A program that does not read the mouse keeps cornercase's selection (`Drag::Selection`, carrying the pending `LinkPress`); a release without moving opens the path instead of copying nothing. A pending path press whose release never reaches the pane is replaced by the next drag, so it cannot linger alongside another drag. Modifier clicks were rejected: the protocol cannot carry Cmd, and Ctrl and Alt clicks are taken by the outer terminal on some systems.
- **Lines for the agent**: a press on a line selects it and dragging selects a range (`Drag::Lines` in `App::drag`, holding the workspace, path and anchor, the window's area, whether it moved and when it last scrolled, followed by `continue_drag` even outside the panel; past the body's edges the nearest line counts). The next mouse event ends it if the files panel no longer shows that file in that workspace, replacing the resets in the panel's close and file-open methods. Any event but a left drag or the wheel ends it, like the other drags: a release outside the window may never be reported, and the next click would be swallowed. ` ask agent ` pastes `path:a-b ` (`path ` with nothing selected) into the workspace's agent like the hunk action, ` copy ` copies the selected lines, or the whole file with nothing selected (an image, a binary file, or one too large, gone or not read yet copies its path), ` open ` opens the editor at the first selected line in a new tab.
  - **Scrolling while selecting**: a file longer than the panel could only be selected one screen at a time, so it could not be copied whole. Once the drag has moved, a pointer on the body's first row or above it, or on its last row or below it (`ui::files::edge`; the body usually ends at the window's bottom, below which a terminal reports no row, so the last row itself is the edge), scrolls the viewer one line every `LINES_SCROLL_EVERY` (50 ms) and moves the selection's end to the line at that edge, as `↑ n more` does in the lists: on each drag event and, while the pointer stays still, on the loop's turns (`App::auto_scroll_lines`; `App::tick` wakes for it only while `App::lines_edge` says the pointer is at an edge that can still scroll, so a selection in the middle costs no frames). One line every 50 ms, like tmux's copy mode, rather than the lists' one item every 150 ms: a line is smaller than an item. A press that has not moved does not scroll, so a click on the last line shown only selects it. The wheel during the drag scrolls the viewer and moves the selection's end to the line under the pointer instead of ending the drag.
  - **Copy takes the file's own text** (`Body::Text`'s `source`, an `Arc<str>` kept beside the shown lines, read through `Content::text`): the shown lines have their tabs turned into four spaces and lose a `\r`, so a Makefile copied from them broke. The whole file is copied byte for byte (after the lossy UTF-8 decoding every read has), selected lines are joined with `\n`. It costs one more copy of the file per open viewer (8 MiB at most), and `disk::read` now returns the `Content` itself, since the source its `Read` carried for highlighting is in the body. Copying the path with nothing selected was dropped for the whole file: the header shows the path, and ` ask agent ` without an agent still copies it.
- **Full screen** (#278): an open file reads better with the whole window than with a column that has to be dragged out and back, which also stops at `MIN_PANE_WIDTH` and the sidebar. A button just left of the title row's ` × ` (`ui::files::full_screen_button`, only on a file: text, image, binary or gone, never the tree or the results) turns `▢` into `❐` while it is on, muted, cyan on hover and cyan while on, like the changes filter's ` ⌕ `; with Nerd glyphs the codicons `screen-full` and `screen-normal` (`icons::Glyphs`). The glyphs follow the window-title convention (one window to maximise, two stacked to restore), are one cell wide and not emoji; `⤢`/`⤡` were rejected for font coverage (not in DejaVu Sans Mono) and for saying nothing about in and out, `⛶`, `□` and `▣` for ambiguous width, `⊞`/`⊟` for reading as tree folding.
