One flag, `files::Panel::full_screen` (not `expanded`, which means open folders), cleared whenever the file or the panel closes (`close`, `open_file`, `close_file`) so it is never per workspace or saved: the session keeps only whether the panel is open. `App::file_full_screen` also requires the panel shown and a viewer open, and `Areas::with_full_screen` (after `with_tab_bar`, in `App::layout` and `ui::draw`) only moves `changes` to the whole window and empties its border, so `pane`, and with it the PTY sizes, and `Widths::changes` are never touched: restoring gives back the exact width, automatic or dragged. `ui::draw` draws only the panel and what goes over it (overlays, star card, toast): otherwise the active pane's cursor and the icon bar's tooltip would show through, and nothing under the panel is hit, since a press inside `Areas::changes` already goes to the panel first. Ways out: the button, `Esc` (before the `Esc` that closes the panel), ` ‹ `, ` × `, opening another file or a files search, every `App::goto` (` open `, ` ask agent ` that found an agent, results, notification clicks, ` go `, the keys menu's jumps: a jump shows its destination), any change of the focused tab, workspace or project seen by `App::follow` (keys, a new tab, the active tab closing, `cornercase focus`) and `reset_interaction`, so a viewer that panics while drawing cannot keep the window stuck. ` copy ` and ` ask agent ` without an agent stay full screen. Keys still go to the active pane even though it is covered, as in compact mode where the panel also covers it, so typing, pasting or an `Enter` act on a program the user cannot see until `Esc` or the button brings the panes back; that is the known cost of the choice; swallowing them or scrolling the viewer with the arrows would be new keyboard shortcuts, which AGENTS.md asks about first. The compact layout shows the button too and full screen also covers its top bar: one rule everywhere. While full, the covered tab counts as not visible (`App::visible_tab`, `App::shows`, like under the compact menu), so an agent finishing there gets its `✓`, toast and notification and its output stops forcing frames; `link_hover` skips its path lookups. The trace logs `ui: file shown full screen` / `file back in its panel`. The words are *full screen* and *back in the panel*, never *expand*, which is for rows. Rejected: remembering it per workspace or across restarts, a keys-menu entry, and full screen for the tree, the results or the other side panels.

## Images (`graphics/`, files panel)

- **Overview**: a click on a PNG, JPEG, GIF (its first frame), WebP, BMP or ICO in the files panel shows the picture itself, drawn by each window's own terminal, because agents write screenshots, diagrams and icons into the workspace and opening another app to look at one breaks the flow. Three protocols cover the terminals people use: kitty graphics with Unicode placeholders (Ghostty, kitty), iTerm2's inline images (iTerm2, WezTerm, Warp, VS Code with its image setting) and sixel (foot, Konsole, xterm as a VT340); a terminal with none gets, where the picture would be, a message naming what it lacks and how to get it, rather than an empty panel that looks broken. Each window decides for itself at startup (Detection) and the server follows: two windows on one server, say Ghostty and an xterm, each get what their terminal can show, in their own size. The pipeline: the reading thread decodes the file once into a `graphics::Picture` (Decoding and encoding); per window, `App::draw` fits it to that window's room and cell size and draws the cells of its area; the bytes for that window's protocol are made on a job thread and cached; the server writes them into that window's frame and keeps them right while the screen changes (Rendering). SVG stays text: it is source code an agent edits, and drawing it needs a renderer of its own. Animations show their first frame: playing them would mean a stream of frames to every window for an image someone glances at.

- **Detection** (`graphics/detect.rs`, `host/theme.rs`, `client/probe.rs`): each client decides once, at startup, what its own terminal can show and sends it in `Hello` as a `graphics::Support` (the protocol, or the `Missing` reason when there is none; the cell size; whether to wrap for tmux; `id_hi`); the server only follows it, since two clients of one server can sit in different terminals. `detect::decide` is a pure function of `Facts` (the replies, whether the kitty query went out, the window's cell size, what tmux said) and the environment, tested as a table on the replies the bench recorded (Ghostty 1.3.1, kitty 0.45, WezTerm stable and nightly, Konsole 25.12, foot 1.25, xterm 407 with and without `-ti vt340`, each directly, through tmux 3.6 locally and as seen over ssh), and checked live in each of them.
  - **The probe** adds `CSI 16 t` (cell size), XTSMGRAPHICS `CSI ?1;1;0S` and `CSI ?2;1;0S` (sixel colour registers and geometry) and, where it is wanted (below), the kitty query `ESC _G i=31,s=1,v=1,a=q,t=d,f=24;AAAA` ahead of OSC 10/11, OSC 4 for colours 0-15, XTVERSION and DA1. One batch, ending `OSC 4;15;? CSI > q CSI c`, keeps DA1 as the end marker and adds no round trip over ssh. Asking all 256 colours produced several kilobytes of replies against macOS' 1024-byte tty input queue: replies, including DA1, could be lost even while the client was reading; missing DA1 was also observed over ssh to Linux. The batch size test includes 16-bit RGB, shorter RGB and hash colour formats, the graphics reports, a kitty error, a 256-byte terminal version and long DA1, and pins the largest reply at 862 bytes, below 1022. Colours 16-255 keep the emulator's cube and grey defaults (Terminals). The parser reads APC replies (`G i=31;OK`), CSI with private markers, intermediates and empty parameters (kitty's `?62;52;c`, iTerm2's `?1;3;S`), and skips any other reply whole (DA2, DECRQM's `$y`). Strings never nest: an ESC inside one that is not its `ST` drops it and parsing starts again there, so Alt+`_`, Alt+`P` or Alt+`]` typed during the probe cannot swallow the replies after it. It runs right after `ratatui::init()` and before mouse, paste and kitty-keyboard modes are on. Nothing is asked in band once the input thread runs. A probe that ends on its deadline rather than on DA1 still drains until 200 ms pass without a byte (at most 1 s); starts ending on DA1 pay nothing. The input decoder also drops complete OSC 4/10/11 (and turns an OSC 99 answer into a click, see Agent status), XTVERSION, kitty and private CSI replies outside bracketed paste, even if they arrive after the drain or much later. Filtering raw bytes avoids both typing late strings into a pane and crossterm 0.29 buffering a private CSI ending in `S` together with every following click and key until `c` or `u`. A lone Esc or an Alt string prefix at the end of a short read is delivered immediately during ordinary input, as in crossterm. After either probe ends without DA1, the input thread expects late replies for 1 s; dropping a reply renews that window for 1 s. Only in that window, or when a full read may have more bytes waiting, is a trailing Esc held for up to 100 ms; ambiguous string prefixes are held only while more bytes or late replies are expected. Poll waits shrink to the held sequence's remaining time. Two Escs in separate reads remain two key events, including while replies are expected; two Escs in one read retain crossterm's single-event interpretation. Interrupted strings restart at the new ESC. Incomplete replies expire after 1 s idle and non-paste sequences are capped at 4096 bytes. A reply whose prefix was lost, or whose pieces arrive after its expiry or outside the reply window, can still look like ordinary typing: there is no safe way to distinguish its remaining printable text from the user's keys. The smaller batch prevents the known queue overflow, late replies received together are harmless, and preserving ordinary input takes precedence over dropping arbitrary text indefinitely.
  - **The kitty query** goes out only outside a multiplexer the environment shows (`TMUX`, `STY`, `ZELLIJ`, `TERM` `tmux*`/`screen*`): tmux turns an unwrapped APC into the pane's title (`Gi=31,s=1,…` in the status line), screen into its hardstatus. And only when `TERM`, `TERM_PROGRAM`, `KITTY_WINDOW_ID` or `GHOSTTY_RESOURCES_DIR` point at Ghostty, kitty or Rio, the only terminals whose answer changes anything: Konsole and WezTerm answer `OK` and still draw `U=1` at the cursor (bench), so the name decides, never the reply, and the reply only tells a Ghostty with `image-storage-limit = 0` (which then answers nothing) from a working one. Only `OK` counts as an answer; an error is taken as silence (its text stays in the log). Without the query (Ghostty over ssh with `TERM=xterm-256color`), XTVERSION alone decides, so a Ghostty with `image-storage-limit = 0` reached that way shows blank cells and no message: kept, since sending the APC to every terminal without a hint is what the gating avoids. If it went out and XTVERSION then says tmux (`default-terminal` set to `xterm-ghostty`, over ssh), tmux has renamed the pane, so the client sets the title to `cornercase` rather than leave the query's text there (checked live).
  - **Picking**, in this order: `CORNERCASE_IMAGES`, the multiplexer, the terminal's name, DA1. Kitty placeholders only for Ghostty, kitty ≥ 0.28 and Rio ≥ 0.5.27; iTerm2's inline images for iTerm2, WezTerm, Warp, mintty, older Rio, and xterm.js when DA1 has `4`, which its image addon adds (`TERM_PROGRAM` tells Tabby and Hyper from VS Code, so each gets its own setting in the message; Cursor reports `vscode` too and gets VS Code's; over plain ssh the addon's own DA1 `?62;4;9;22c` names xterm.js, which needs no cell size); sixel for Konsole ≥ 22.04, foot, xterm and mlterm when DA1 has `4`, contour, VTE before 0.83.90 with `4`, and any unknown terminal with `4`; a `Missing` otherwise, naming the terminal by brand and version (`xterm 407`, not `XTerm(407)`). The name comes from XTVERSION by prefix, else from the environment, where a hint naming a terminal that answers XTVERSION is ignored when DA1 came without one: `TERM_PROGRAM=ghostty` inherited by an Alacritty started from Ghostty would otherwise get placeholders, drawn as tofu. Sixel needs a cell size (`Missing::NoCellSize`); kitty and iTerm2 images fall back to 8×16. Zellij's DA1 `4` is trusted only from 0.45 (`Zellij(4500)`), which advertises sixel only when the terminal has it; older ones always do. `TERM=screen*` with neither `STY` nor XTVERSION is a multiplexer cornercase cannot name (screen, or a tmux older than 3.2, which has no XTVERSION and nothing this design uses), and gets a message saying so. An unknown `CORNERCASE_IMAGES` value is ignored and written into the log's `probe` line.
  - **tmux** (XTVERSION `tmux …`, or `TMUX` with no reply). Locally, `tmux display-message -p -t $TMUX_PANE '#{pane_tty}|#{allow-passthrough}|#{client_termtype}|#{client_termfeatures}'`, started before the batch and waited for at most 500 ms, names the outer terminal and says whether passthrough is on, without reading stdin. `allow-passthrough` prints `off`/`on`/`all` from 3.4, `0`/`1` in 3.3 (a flag there), and nothing before 3.3, when passthrough was always allowed. A `pane_tty` that is not the client's own tty means `TMUX` was inherited by a terminal started from a tmux pane: it is ignored, else Alacritty (no XTVERSION) would get placeholders decided from the tmux client's Ghostty. Over ssh, where `TMUX` does not arrive, or when that command fails, a wrapped probe (kitty query, XTVERSION, DA1, each in `ESC P tmux;` with ESC doubled) asks the outer terminal, at least 150 ms after entering the alternate screen, since with `on` tmux drops passthrough while the pane's redraw is pending (it got through 16 of 16 times at 0-50 ms on the bench; a dropped probe would show the passthrough message for the client's whole life), and waits twice the batch's round trip plus 300 ms, at most 1 s: silence means passthrough is off, which costs that wait at every start until it is turned on or `CORNERCASE_IMAGES` skips the check. tmux sends the outer terminal's replies to the client's active pane, so a cornercase started in a pane that is not active (`new-window -d`, a layout script) or in a hidden window under `on` hears nothing and its message says so; the late-reply drain protects only cornercase's own pane, and nothing tells that case apart remotely. Kitty placeholders go through (wrapped transmissions, plain cells) for an outer Ghostty, kitty or Rio when passthrough is on, else the passthrough message; otherwise tmux's own sixel, unwrapped, when tmux's DA1 has `4` and the outer terminal has sixel (`client_termfeatures`, or the outer DA1 over ssh), since tmux decodes and redraws it (foot, WezTerm, Konsole and xterm as a VT340 through tmux on the bench); a sixel terminal behind a tmux without sixel (older than 3.4, or built without it) gets `TmuxNoSixel` with tmux's version, since the fix is tmux, not the terminal; iTerm2 images through passthrough are never offered, as tmux's redraws wipe them. tmux inside tmux, GNU screen, and Zellij without sixel get messages.
  - **tmux's clients**: `#{client_termtype}` is the most recently active client of the session, and tmux shows one pane to every client, so placeholders and wrapped images reach all of them, including one that draws tofu; nothing in cornercase can fix that. When the local check was used, every resize (attaching from a terminal of another size sends SIGWINCH to the pane) runs it again on a short-lived thread, one at a time, whose answer comes back through the window's event channel, so input never waits; a changed decision is sent as `ClientMessage::Graphics`. A remote client (the wrapped probe) cannot be asked again, since nothing may be asked in band once the input thread runs. tmux 3.0 and 3.1 have no `client_termtype`, so locally the outer terminal is unknown and the message says so. When tmux does not know the outer terminal's pixel size it reports a 16×32 cell; it is kept, since tmux places its own sixel images with it, and kitty placeholders are scaled by the outer terminal anyway, so the only effect is an image smaller than the panel allows.
  - **Cell size**: `TIOCGWINSZ` pixels over cells (crossterm's `window_size`; OpenSSH forwards them), else the `16 t` reply, the reply winning when both exist and differ (yazi does the same), each only when plausible (2-256 by 4-512 px, at most twice as wide as tall). On a resize the client reads `TIOCGWINSZ` again; a new window cell size means the font changed, so the old `16 t` answer is dropped (it cannot be asked again), and when the decision changes it sends `ClientMessage::Graphics(Support)` before the resize event, so the server lays out with it (Ghostty's font zoom, checked live).
  - **Messages** (`Missing::lines`): lower case, the first line what, the next ones what to do, naming the setting to change (`allow-passthrough`, with `~/.config/tmux/tmux.conf` as the other place tmux reads and `all` marked as tmux 3.4 or later; `enableImages`; `-ti vt340`; `sixel=no`; `image-storage-limit`, which Ghostty applies on a config reload) and the terminal and its version where known. `CORNERCASE_IMAGES=kitty|iterm|sixel|off` in the client's environment overrides the decision and skips the kitty query and the tmux checks, for terminals nothing can recognise (Blink and a-Shell send no XTVERSION) and to turn images off; per client like `COLORTERM`, not a setting, since each client has its own terminal.
  - **Kitty ids and leaving**: `id_hi`, random in 1-255 per client run (`RandomState`, no new crate), is the high byte of every kitty id of that client (`KITTY_LO` the two low bytes), so clients drawing in one outer terminal (tmux panes) do not collide, and the client can delete its own images without the server, whose link may be gone. kitty and Ghostty keep virtual placements and their data across ED 2 and the alternate screen, so on every way out (quit, detach, restart, kill-server, a signal, a remote window giving up, a panic on the main thread) the client writes `a=d,d=I` for both ids, wrapped under tmux, before leaving the alternate screen; a panic on another thread does not end the client, so the hook leaves its images alone. Checked live: after quit, SIGTERM and kill-server, directly and through tmux, Ghostty answers `ENOENT` for the client's ids and still has a control image under another `id_hi`. A killed client leaves its images until the tab closes, and so does one leaving while its tmux window is hidden under `on`, which drops the wrapped deletes like any passthrough (`all` avoids it).
  - **Protocol and log**: `Hello` carries `graphics` and `probe`, one line of what the terminal answered and how long it took, which `server.log` keeps with the decision on the `client attached` line (with `pointer=`, see Layout) (`images=kitty cell=10x19 tmux=none missing=- id_hi=175 probe="da1=62;22;52 kitty=OK 16t=10x19 window=10x19 registers=- geometry=- tmux=- ms=13"`), so a wrong decision in an untested terminal can be fixed from a log; `protocol::VERSION` went to 2 for it (3 since the pointer, see Layout).

- **Decoding and encoding** (`graphics/decode.rs`, `encode.rs`, `kitty.rs`, `iterm.rs`, `sixel.rs`, `tmux.rs`): pure functions with no I/O, run on the reading thread (decoding) and on encode jobs (encoding), never on the server loop.
  - **Sniffing** (`decode::sniff`, which needs the first `decode::HEAD` bytes): the format comes from the content, never the name: the PNG signature, `FF D8 FF`, `GIF87a`/`GIF89a`, `RIFF`…`WEBP`; BMP's `BM` alone would take a text file starting with `BMW` for a bitmap (and show `cannot read this image` instead of the text), so it also needs a known DIB header size at byte 14, and ICO's `00 00 01 00` needs at least one entry with a zero reserved byte and 0 or 1 planes. TIFF, AVIF, SVG and the rest stay text or binary. The name (`PNG`, `JPEG`, `GIF`, `WebP`, `BMP`, `ICO`) is `Picture::format`, which the viewer's header shows.
  - **Decoding** (`decode::decode`): `image` 0.25 with only `png`, `jpeg`, `gif`, `webp`, `bmp` and `ico` (+1.2 MB). The decoder is opened first with only a 256 MiB allocation cap, its size read from the header, and a picture over `LIMIT_SIDE` (16384) per side or whose decoded pixels pass `LIMIT_BYTES` (256 MiB) is refused before any pixel buffer exists, as `too large to preview (W×H)`: a 12 KB PNG can declare 50000×50000, and an OOM kill of the server takes every shell with it. The decoder then gets the full limits for its own buffers. Each format's bomb is a test with a crafted file (PNG `IHDR`, JPEG `SOF0`, GIF screen, WebP `VP8L`, BMP header, a PNG inside an ICO); image-webp wraps a 16384-wide lossless header to width 0, so that one is checked at 16383. EXIF orientation is applied (`into_decoder` → `orientation` → `apply_orientation`, which `decode()` alone skips), on the shrunk pixels so a full-size copy is never made, and `Picture::width`/`height` are the size after it, for the header. Animations give their first frame (`from_decoder` on GIF, animated WebP, APNG), as most terminal image viewers do; playing them would need per-window frame pacing. Pictures larger than `MAX_SIDE` (2048) are shrunk once with an area average (`thumbnail`, the fastest on big sources and the right filter for shrinking: 104 ms for 6000×4000 against 314 ms for Triangle), premultiplied when the source is 8- or 16-bit RGBA so transparent edges do not darken (transparent pixels are usually black); grey with alpha and float images are averaged as they are, so a huge one is never converted to RGBA at full size first. Errors are short and lower case, for `cannot read this image: <reason>`: `too large to preview (W×H)`, `this kind of <format> is not supported`, `the file ends too early`, `the <format> data is damaged`.
  - **Fit** (`encode::fit`): `s = min(1, room·cell / size)`, never enlarging (a 16×16 icon stays 16×16 pixels), `cols = ceil(width / cell width)`, at least one cell, with 8×16 assumed when the terminal gave no cell size. It takes the protocol, unlike the first sketch, because each has a ceiling: kitty's diacritics table numbers 297 rows and columns, iTerm2 (and Warp) index image rows in a byte (255), and a sixel's pixels are the size it is drawn at, so its ceilings must shrink the box rather than the bitmap: the terminal's XTSMGRAPHICS geometry (xterm drops what passes its 1000×1000), `SIXEL_PIXELS` (1.2 MP, about 2 MB for a photo) and whole bands (below). Kitty and iTerm2 scale the bitmap into the cells themselves, so their ceilings never change the cells. `Fit` carries the cell size it used, since the iTerm2 payload is padded to exactly `cols·cell width × rows·cell height`, and `encode::Key` (the payload cache's key) holds the whole `Fit`.
  - **Kitty** (`kitty.rs`): the picture is sent as PNG (`f=100`) rather than the zlib-compressed raw pixels (`f=32`/`f=24`, `o=z`) the research started from: the `png` crate's fast mode filters rows before compressing, so it is both smaller and faster (the research's figures, before base64: an 800×589 photo is 806 KB in 6 ms against 1.34 MB in 34 ms, a screenshot 216 KB against 232 KB), no zlib crate is needed, and every terminal that gets placeholders decodes it (Ghostty, kitty and Rio read `f=100`; the bench showed `a=T,U=1,f=100` in Ghostty 1.3.1 and kitty 0.45). RGB when the picture is opaque, RGBA otherwise; it is never flattened, since the terminal blends it on the cell's background itself. It is sent at the fit's pixel size, or the picture's own pixels when those are fewer (the terminal scales into `c×r`, so enlarging would only cost bytes). `kitty::transmit` cuts the base64 into chunks of `CHUNK` (4096) bytes, all but the last a multiple of 4: the first `a=t,q=2,i=<id>,f=100,m=1`, the middle ones only `m=1,q=2` (the spec allows only `m` and `q` on continuations), the last `m=0,q=2`, one chunk with no `m` at all. Every command carries `q=2`: after startup a reply would reach the input thread and be typed into the pane. `encode` ends with the virtual placement, `a=p,U=1,q=2,i=<id>,p=1,c=<cols>,r=<rows>` (always `p=1`, so placing again replaces it instead of adding one, and the terminal never has to choose); `kitty::place` and `kitty::delete` (`a=d,d=I`, which frees the data too) are there for re-placing and cleaning up, unwrapped (callers wrap them for tmux). `kitty::cell` gives `U+10EEEE` with the row, column and high-byte diacritics in every cell, so a cell drawn alone (ratatui rewrites single cells, overlays cut rows) is still complete, and the id's low byte as a 256-colour foreground, which tmux passes as it is (it reduces 24-bit colours when it does not know the outer terminal has them). The table is kitty's `rowcolumn-diacritics.txt`, checked identical to Ghostty 1.3.1's `graphics_unicode.zig`, sorted (Ghostty searches it by bisection); a test checks that every cell, all 297×297 with three high bytes, is one column wide for ratatui.
  - **iTerm2 inline images** (`iterm.rs`): one `ESC ] 1337 ; File=inline=1;size=<n>;width=<cols>;height=<rows>;doNotMoveCursor=1:<base64> BEL`. The payload is exactly the cell box in the terminal's pixels, the picture centred in it like kitty letterboxes, on the window's background, or transparent when it is unknown: terminals disagree on how `width`/`height` scale and on `preserveAspectRatio` (WezTerm and xterm.js let the width win, Konsole stretches), and a payload with the box's exact shape looks the same in all of them. No `name` (some extensions make iTerm2 render the file as a document), no `preserveAspectRatio`, no `inlineMedia` (Konsole plays it as audio); `size` because xterm.js's addon refuses a file without it; `doNotMoveCursor` is WezTerm's and ignored by the rest. Format: PNG while anything is see-through (padding included); otherwise JPEG at quality 85 for a JPEG source, and for the rest PNG unless it comes out more than twice the JPEG, which catches photos saved as PNG or lossy WebP while flat graphics and screenshots stay sharp. The terminal gets PNG or JPEG whatever the file was: WezTerm draws a WebP as a black box and Konsole draws nothing.
  - **Sixel** (`sixel.rs`): `icy_sixel` (pure Rust, +0.1 MB) with Wu's quantiser and Floyd-Steinberg at 0.875 (as good as libsixel to the eye, 25-35 ms for 800 px), at most 256 colours or the terminal's registers (0, unknown, means 256; at least 2). It writes `ESC P 9;1;0 q "1;1;W;H`: square pixels, raster attributes with the exact size (WezTerm and xterm.js crop to them), and P2=1. The picture is laid on the window's background when it is known, so the payload is opaque (Konsole ignores P2: on the bench it painted the transparent pixels of an uncomposited sixel with colour register 0), else pixels under half alpha stay unpainted. Its height is a whole number of 6-pixel bands (`sixel::bands`, in `fit` and in every smaller retry: at most 5 rows less, the width scaled to keep the shape), because Konsole also paints the unused rows of a partial last band, a 4-pixel strip of register 0 under the picture on the bench (img2sixel's and chafa's sixels leave a strip there too). The final `-` before `ESC \` is cut: it moves the sixel cursor a band down, and terminals that leave the text cursor at the sixel cursor would then scroll on the last rows. No DECSDM (`?80`, whose meaning flipped in xterm 369), `?8452` or `?1070`: the renderer positions with CUP inside DECSC/DECRC.
  - **Size budget** (`encode::BUDGET`, 1 MiB): tmux drops a whole DCS longer than its `input-buffer-size` (1 MiB by default), including the raw sixel its native sixel support decodes, which `encode` cannot tell from a terminal of its own, and every payload has to cross the server's 1 s write timeout to remote windows. A sixel over it is encoded again with 64 colours (`sixel::FEWEST`), then, if still over, at `sqrt(budget / size) · 0.9` the scale, at most three times; an iTerm2 image wrapped for tmux only shrinks. Colours go first because a smaller sixel no longer fills its cells (it sits in their top-left corner) while 64 dithered colours look close to 256: a 1271×936 photo (Retina-sized cells) is 1.7 MB with 256 colours and 1.0 MB with 64, which then fits at full size, where shrinking alone left it at 898×661. Kitty needs no budget: each chunk is wrapped on its own and stays far below.
  - **tmux** (`tmux::wrap`): `ESC P tmux;` + the sequence with every ESC doubled + `ESC \`, one wrap per sequence (each kitty chunk, the placement, a delete), never around the placeholder cells, which tmux carries as text.
  - **Payload sizes** (release build, bytes on the wire with base64 and escapes; `~/.cctmp/images/live/encode/measure.txt` has every image): in an 80×40 room at 10×19-pixel cells (an 800-pixel box), a 1738×1280 photo is 1.0 MB as kitty PNG, 161 KB as iTerm2 JPEG and 804 KB as sixel; a 1998×1250 screenshot 278 KB, 94 KB and 265 KB; a 512×512 16-bit icon 159 KB, 64 KB and 140 KB. At Retina-sized 16×34 cells (a 1280-pixel box) the photo is 2.3 MB, 342 KB and 1.0 MB (64 colours), the screenshot 652 KB, 195 KB and 585 KB. Encoding takes at most ~75 ms at 10×19 and up to ~300 ms at 16×34 (a photo's sixel, encoded twice); decoding a 6000×4000 JPEG (450-550 ms) is the slowest step, and it runs on the reading thread. For the same 24×20-cell box at 10×20-pixel cells, chafa 1.18 sends 519 KB for kitty (raw RGBA) and 512 KB for iTerm2 (an uncompressed TIFF) against 159 KB and 30 KB here; sixel costs about the same per pixel (1.5 bytes against 1.7). Kitty's PNG is the heaviest for photos (kitty takes no JPEG), so it is the one to watch over slow links.
  - **Checked live** on the bench (`~/.cctmp/images/live/encode/`, a small crate that draws `encode`'s output the way the renderer does): every format (PNG, JPEG with EXIF orientation 6, GIF's first frame, lossless and lossy WebP, a 16-bit RGBA icon, an 8×8 icon left at 8×8, alpha) in Ghostty 1.3.1 and kitty 0.45 (placeholders), WezTerm 20240203 (iTerm2 and sixel), Konsole 25.12 (iTerm2 and sixel), foot 1.25 and xterm 407 `-ti vt340` (sixel); tmux 3.6 with passthrough on (placeholders in place in Ghostty and kitty), off (blank cells, no tofu), and tmux's own sixel in foot, WezTerm and xterm; side by side with chafa 1.18 for the same boxes, which looks the same.
  - **Dev builds**: `image`, `png`, `fdeflate`, `zune-jpeg`, `image-webp`, `gif`, `icy_sixel`, `quantette` and `palette` build with `opt-level = 3` in dev, like syntect: unoptimised, quantising a 1 MP photo took seconds, which made the tests slow (one sixel test 6.3 s, 1.5 s now) and the dev binary unusable on real photos.

- **Rendering** (`files/disk.rs`, `ui/files.rs`, `app/images.rs`, `server/images.rs`, `server.rs`):
  - **Reading**: `disk::read` sniffs the first bytes (`decode::sniff`) before the binary check (every image holds a NUL) and decodes on the reading thread it already had, so the server loop never decodes: `Body::Image(Arc<Picture>)`, or `Body::Unreadable { format, bytes, reason }` shown as `cannot read this image: <reason>` (logged at info with the path, never pixels). Images may be up to `MAX_IMAGE_BYTES` (32 MiB), text files still 8 MiB: a phone photo is often 10 MB and decoding is bounded by the decoder's limits anyway; the cap is chosen from the head, read first only for files over 8 MiB. Each read takes a new `Picture::id` from a process-wide counter, so the 1 s change check (size and time, as for text) gives an image an agent rewrites a new id, and every cache and every terminal sees a new picture. The `Arc` keeps one decoded copy (at most 2048 px a side) whatever the windows.
  - **The viewer**: the header reads `PNG · 1280×720 · 245 KB` (the original size, before any downscaling); ` ask agent ` pastes `path ` (agents read images from a path) and ` copy ` copies the path; ` open ` is left out, since an editor cannot show it; an image has no lines, so clicks select nothing and the wheel scrolls nothing. The picture is centred horizontally under the header and top aligned, in the body less a column each side, cut to what that window sees (`ui::files::image_room`: a smaller window is cropped, so its picture is fitted to what it shows), and never on the window's last row: iTerm2 makes an image's rows with line feeds and several terminals add one after a sixel, so an image ending there scrolls the whole screen. It is never enlarged (`encode::fit`): a 16 px icon stays small, as it is.
  - **Per window** (`app::Sight` in, `app::Placed` out): `App::draw` takes what the window can do (its `Support`, its visible rect, its background, and for kitty which of its two ids to use and whether that id already holds this picture) and returns what it placed (`encode::Key`, rect, path, payload if ready, whether its cells are on screen), so the frame differs per window while everything else stays shared. A window without a protocol gets `Missing::lines` in place of the picture (muted, wrapped, a blank line after the first, which says what; the next ones say what to do).
  - **Payloads** are made off the loop: `App::payload` looks the key up in a small cache (`images::Payloads`: 8 payloads and 64 MiB at most, the least recently used going first, the last one always kept) and starts a job under `panics::job` when it is missing, answered by `AppEvent::Encoded`; at most two jobs run, and a key that is not started is asked again by the next draw, so dragging the panel's border runs the latest size rather than a queue of every step. A job that fails or panics marks its key failed (the window says so) instead of retrying every frame. Until its payload comes a window shows blank cells: a placeholder text that flashes for 50 ms was worse than nothing.
  - **Dialogs hide the picture** (`Overlay::is_modal`): the image is drawn as plain blank cells while one is open, since the dialog dims the screen with SGR 2, which no terminal applies to an image, and a bright picture behind a dimmed screen looks like a bug. Menus, the search, the keys menu and toasts are drawn over it like over text.
  - **Kitty** (placeholders): the picture's cells are `U+10EEEE` with its row, column and high-byte diacritics (`kitty::cell`), the low byte of the id as a 256-colour foreground, `ForcedWidth(1)`, so they are plain text to ratatui, tmux and every overlay: a menu covers its cells and the image comes back when they are drawn again, at no cost. Each window has two ids, `id_hi << 24 | 0xF0` and `| 0xF1` (`id_hi` random per window, from Detection), and a new picture takes the one not on screen (`server::images::Graphics::sight`), so the new cells always differ from the old and ratatui rewrites every one of them; the id is chosen before the payload is asked for and kept while the picture shows, since it is part of the payload's key. After the frame, the server writes the payload (transmit and the virtual placement) when this id does not hold the picture yet, or holds it smaller than the new fit; when it holds it as large or larger (the panel got narrower, the window smaller), only a new placement (`kitty::place`, ~60 bytes) and the terminal scales it down; while a larger payload is being made the old one is placed larger meanwhile, so a resize never blanks it. Then it deletes the other id. A picture hidden by a dialog or by the compact menu stays in the terminal; it is deleted once the viewer shows no image (another file, the panel closed, another workspace), and the window deletes both ids when it leaves (Detection), since kitty keeps virtual placements across clears and screen switches. A screen reset (resize, attach, a panic while drawing) costs nothing: the cells are drawn again. Inside tmux a transmit never goes out in the same turn as a reset, but 100 ms later (`Graphics::wake` shortens the server's wait): tmux drops a passthrough sent right after the pane is redrawn.
  - **iTerm2 inline images and sixel** are drawn by the terminal over cells it no longer tracks, so ratatui's diff must leave those cells alone and the server must know when something else drew over them. The picture's cells hold a marker (`U+10FFFD`, which nothing else draws) with `CellDiffOption::Skip`, so the diff never writes them. After every widget, overlay and toast, a pass in the same draw (`app::images::owned`) keeps `Skip` on marker cells and resets the others in the rect to `None`, since `set_symbol` keeps `Skip` and a menu drawn there would otherwise never reach the terminal; the picture is owned when every cell is still a marker. `CropBackend::draw` sees every cell ratatui writes, and one inside the shown rect marks it damaged (a menu or toast drew over it, or Konsole and tmux dropped all of it); a reset of the screen does too. After the frame the server sends the payload when the picture is owned, its payload ready, and its key or rect changed or it was damaged: `CSI ?2026h ESC 7 CSI 0m`, printed spaces over every row (ECH and EL do not erase sixel in xterm, and WezTerm keeps the old text under an iTerm2 image, where it shows through transparency), the cursor to the corner, the payload, `ESC 8 CSI ?2026l`, so the blank-then-image never shows where synchronized output is known. While something covers it nothing is sent; the uncovered part stays where the terminal erases per cell, and the whole picture comes back once it is owned again, so a toast costs one more send when it goes. When the picture leaves, its marker cells become ordinary cells that differ from the last frame's, so ratatui writes them and erases it like text; nothing else is needed (both protocols live on the alternate screen and go with it).
  - **Two windows**: everything above lives per window in the server's `Client` (`Graphics`: the `Support`, the background for compositing, the kitty ids and what they hold, what was sent and whether it is intact); the payload cache is shared, and two windows with the same terminal, size and background share a sixel or iTerm2 payload (kitty's carry the window's own id). A window that attaches later gets everything sent to it on its first frame.
  - **Big frames and `cornercase remote`**: payloads are sized for the display, not the file (a full-panel photo is ~100-800 KB), but the server writes each window with a 1 s timeout and drops one that makes no progress. Locally that never happens; through `cornercase remote` the proxy used to copy the socket to ssh's stdout in one blocking loop, so a slow link stopped it reading the socket and the server dropped the window. The proxy now reads the socket on a thread into a channel and writes stdout from another (`remote::relay`), so only the proxy buffers and the server always finishes its write. Payload sizes are logged per window at debug (`images` target), each picture shown at info with its path.

## Issues (`issues/`, `issues/browser/`, `http.rs`, `app/issues_dialog.rs`)

- `Browser` takes `browser::Room` (list rows, detail rows and width) and `browser::IssuesHit`, and returns `Action`s. `app/issues_dialog.rs` builds the issues view and hit-tests that same view with `ui::issues_hit` or, for a detail, `ui::issue_button_hit`; the model imports no `ui` types. The browser is `browser.rs` (its types and what `App` feeds it) with a child per concern: `buttons.rs`, `token_form.rs`, `people.rs`, `picker.rs`, `input.rs` and `view.rs` (the detail's Markdown lines; the view itself moved to the app in #193, so it is small, and kept as its own file so the lines stay out of the state). `Choice` and the other types stay in the parent because several children read their private fields. It keeps its buttons and labels, token-field text and masking, and Markdown lines: the scroll limit needs their count, and ratatui text is independent of the app's view.
- `Browser` is pure state that returns `Action`s; `App` does the I/O on threads. Answers carry their query and issue key so stale ones are dropped, and lists, people, reads and token checks also the source's epoch, bumped by `App::forget_issues` when its connection goes or changes (disconnect, token removed or replaced, GitLab, Jira, Plane or Asana settings changed), so an answer still in flight cannot refill the cache with the old connection or save a token checked against it. Lists are cached in memory and in `issues.json` (titles and metadata only, never tokens).
- GitHub goes through `gh` in the project folder, run by `gh.rs` (see Pull requests). GitLab (REST v4), Shortcut (REST v3), Linear (GraphQL), Jira Cloud (REST v3) and Plane (REST v1 and v2) go through `ureq`, capped at 100 issues. People filters go into each tracker's query.
- Tokens come from `GITLAB_TOKEN` / `SHORTCUT_API_TOKEN` / `LINEAR_API_KEY` / `JIRA_API_TOKEN` / `PLANE_API_KEY` or are typed in the app, checked, and saved to `secrets.json` (0600). `issues::Secret` hides them in `Debug`; they are never logged.
- **No redirects with a token in a custom header**: ureq keeps every header but `Authorization` when it follows a redirect, even to another origin, so GitLab (`PRIVATE-TOKEN`), Shortcut (`Shortcut-Token`), Plane (`X-API-Key`) and Asana (`Authorization: Bearer`; #305 asked for the same rule as the other token trackers) read through `http::get_without_redirects` (`max_redirects(0)`), which answers a 3xx with an error saying the redirect was not followed and to use the address it leads to. Jira and Linear send `Authorization`, which ureq drops on a redirect to another origin, so they keep `http::get` and `http::post`; so do the update checks, which carry no token.
- **Starting an issue**: in a repo root, a worktree on `issue-<n>-<slug>` / `<iid>-<slug>` (GitLab) / `sc-<n>-<slug>` / `ENG-123-<slug>` / `PROJ-123-<slug>` (Plane's keys look the same) / `ENG-42-<slug>` or `asana-<gid>-<slug>` (Asana, with and without an ID field); elsewhere a new tab. GitHub and GitLab issues belong to the repository the browser was opened from (`Source::in_repository`); Shortcut, Linear, Jira and Plane issues ask which open project or workspace to use. Then the agent starts with the prompt.
- **Jira** (Cloud only; Server/Data Center use other auth, API v2 and wiki markup): HTTP Basic with `email:token`. The site and email are not secret, so they live in `config.json` (`jira_site`, `jira_email`) and only the token in `secrets.json`; the browser's connect form asks site → email → token (one field, three steps; `SaveJira` saves the first two before the token is checked), settings has a row for each. `jira_jql` is ANDed with the generated JQL (its `ORDER BY` dropped). `/rest/api/3/search/jql` refuses unbounded queries, so a list with no condition at all sends `project IS NOT EMPTY`. Jira Cloud no longer takes names in JQL, so a picked person is `Who::User { id, name }` (the `accountId`); other trackers keep `Who::Person(handle)`. Descriptions and comments are ADF (JSON), turned into Markdown by `jira/adf.rs`; unknown nodes give their text. A `config.json` without `jira_site` was written before Jira, so `config::load` appends `jira` to its `issue_tabs` (once saved, the key exists and hiding the tab sticks). Changing the site, email or filter forgets the cached lists, people and account. Timestamps carry an offset (`+0200`), which `parse_time` applies.
- **Plane** (Cloud and self-hosted, `plane.rs`): `X-API-Key` with a personal access token. The workspace slug, the server's URL (`plane_url`, blank is Plane Cloud: API at `https://api.plane.so`, web app at `https://app.plane.so`; a self-hosted server serves both from one URL) and the filter are not secret and live in `config.json` (`plane_workspace`, `plane_url`, `plane_filter`); only the key is in `secrets.json` (`plane_api_key`, or `PLANE_API_KEY`). The connect form asks workspace → key (`SavePlane` saves the workspace before the key is checked), settings has a row for each. The list is the workspace-wide `GET /api/v2/workspaces/{slug}/work-items/`, one request per page of 100 with the cursor envelope (`paginate=cursor`; at most 10 pages, so a local creator filter cannot loop), ordered by `-updated_at`, with `expand=state,assignees,labels` (v2 has no `project` expand, but each row carries its readable key as `identifier`, so the key is the row's and nothing else is needed to build it). Open means `state_group__in=backlog,unstarted,started` (the state's group, which every workspace has whatever its states are called); the assignee is `assignee_id`, and `plane_filter` is raw query parameters (`key=value&key=value`, trimmed, not validated: the server's own error is shown) added to the request. A `state_group__in` in it is merged into the open one rather than sent twice (which of two the server reads is up to it, and the filter's would bypass **closed**): with closed off only its open groups are asked for, and none of them means an empty list without asking for work items; with closed on it is sent as it is. v2 has no creator filter, so the creator is filtered after fetching, on `created_by_id`. v2 rows have no `updated_at` among their documented fields, so `updated_at` falls back to `created_at` when the server leaves it out. Names come from the members list (`/api/v1/workspaces/{slug}/members/`, also the people picker, active non-bot members as `Who::User { id, name }`), so a list takes three requests (the account, the members, the work items) against Plane's 60 a minute per key. The key is checked against the account and the workspace together (`whoami` reads `/api/v1/users/me/` and the members), so a misspelled workspace fails at the connect step. The v2 work item has no description and no comments, so reading one goes through v1: `GET /api/v1/workspaces/{slug}/work-items/{KEY}/` (the key alone, no project id) and its comments under `projects/{project}/work-items/{id}/comments/`. Descriptions and comments are HTML (TipTap's), turned into Markdown by `html.rs` (a small tolerant parser: unknown tags give their text), falling back to the plain text the API also sends; the Markdown helpers it shares with `jira/adf.rs` live in `md.rs`. The parser is `issues/html.rs` (it was `plane/html.rs` until Asana needed it too, in a commit that only moved it). A work item is closed when its state's group is `completed` or `cancelled`. A `config.json` without `plane_workspace` was written before Plane, so `config::load` appends `plane` to its `issue_tabs`, as for Jira. Changing the workspace, URL or filter forgets the cached lists, people and account. Not covered: Plane's pages, intake and epics as separate things (an epic is a work item), and servers too old to serve `/api/v2`.
- **Asana** (`asana.rs`, #305): an issue is a top-level task of one workspace; subtasks are not listed, and neither are milestones and approvals (only `resource_subtype` `default_task`, and rows with `is_rendered_as_separator` are dropped: a task shows under a project when it was added to it directly, whatever its kind). The token is a personal access token sent as `Authorization: Bearer`, from `secrets.json` (`asana_token`) or `ASANA_ACCESS_TOKEN`; `CORNERCASE_ASANA_API` moves the API (`https://app.asana.com/api/1.0`) for tests. Requests go through `http::get_without_redirects`. `config.json` has `asana_workspace` (a name or gid; empty is the account's only workspace) and `asana_projects` (gids or project links, kept as typed; settings save them as gids joined by `, `, and `asana::project_gids` reads both forms at use time, so old values keep working). **Key and branch**: the value of the task's ID custom field when its project has one (`representation_type` `custom_id`, `display_value` such as `ENG-42`), else the gid; the branch is `ENG-42-<slug>` or `asana-<gid>-<slug>`, the full gid since Asana has no shorter handle. `Issue::number` is the gid when it fits a `u64`, else 0. The URL is the task's `permalink_url`, never built by hand: Asana moved its links from `/0/<project>/<task>` to `/1/<workspace>/project/<p>/task/<t>`. The state is the name of the task's section in its first project (`memberships.section.name`), `completed` once it is, else `open`; closed is completed; labels are the tags. **Settings → Issues → Asana** has the workspace, the projects and the access token, in that order; the token is checked against `/users/me` alone, so it needs neither first. The workspace row shows the workspace's name once the account is known (the token row's account, with no extra request) and puts the saved ID in its note (`ID 1200…`); before that it shows the saved value, and `your only one` when none is set.
  - **The list is plan-independent**: with no projects set, `GET /tasks?assignee=me&workspace=<gid>` (your tasks); with projects, `GET /projects/<gid>/tasks` once per project (at most 10), merged without repeats. Asana's search (`/workspaces/<gid>/tasks/search`) has every filter and sorting, but is premium only (402) and capped at 60 requests a minute, so a free workspace could not use the tab. Each call asks `limit=100` and explicit `opt_fields`, follows `next_page.offset` (at most 10 pages per source, stopping at 100 kept tasks), and the merge is sorted by `modified_at` and cut at 100. Open sends `completed_since` as the current UTC time in RFC 3339 on both calls: the keyword `now` is documented only on the project and section lists, and on `GET /tasks` the parameter is typed as a date-time. Closed sends none, so completed tasks come too. People filters are local (`GET /tasks` cannot filter by assignee together with a project, nor by creator at all), on `assignee.gid` and `created_by.gid`; **me** is the gid of `/users/me`. In the your-tasks list the assignee is already you, so an assignee other than anyone or me is not sent anywhere and the list answers that it is your tasks and to set **Asana projects** to filter by someone else; the creator filter still applies. The people picker is `GET /workspaces/<gid>/users` (at most 20 pages). `created_by` carries only a gid, so the author is resolved from that same list, which makes a list three or more requests against Asana's 150 a minute on free plans (1,500 on paid ones).
  - **Choosing the workspace**: `whoami` reads `/users/me?opt_fields=name,email,workspaces.name`. The setting is matched by gid or case-insensitively by name; with none set, the only workspace is used. With several and none matching, the list answers an error naming them and the setting. The connect form asks for the token first and, when the account has several workspaces and the setting names none of them, goes to a workspace step that reuses `Step::Workspace`: type a name or gid, checked against the token's workspaces (the help lists their names), saved to `asana_workspace` as its gid, with the token, which is written only then. The gid rather than the name, since two workspaces can share a name and a saved name would then resolve to the first; a name typed in settings that two workspaces share is refused with their ids, and the account's name still shows in the list's hint. That ambiguity comes back from the check as `Error::Workspaces` (boxed, carrying the account's name and the workspaces) rather than as a field of `Account`: the check has to succeed for the settings (their token row needs no workspace first) but the form needs the names, and a new field in `Account`, even boxed, pushed `server::Step` over clippy's `large_enum_variant` limit; the settings treat the variant as a good token. With a single workspace and a setting that names another, the check fails with the plain error.
  - **Reading**: a key of digits is a gid (`GET /tasks/<gid>`); any other is an ID-field key, looked up with `GET /workspaces/<gid>/tasks/custom_id/<key>`, which takes no query parameters, and then `GET /tasks/<gid>` with `opt_fields` (the list's plus `html_notes`, `notes`, `due_on` and `memberships.project.name`). Comments are the task's stories with `resource_subtype` `comment_added` (also checked locally; up to 5 pages), so the activity log (assignments, section moves) stays out. The info line is the section, `due`, `created by`, `assigned to` and the projects. Rich text is Asana's XHTML (`html_notes`, `html_text`) through `issues/html.rs`: a mention (`data-asana-type="user"`) and a link the reader cannot open (`data-asana-accessible="false"`) become their text, and an image becomes `[image]`, since its URL expires within minutes and has no alt; empty rich text falls back to `notes` or `text`.
  - **Errors**: 401 and 403 say the token was rejected, 429 that the rate limit was reached (the shared text of `http.rs`, as for the other trackers), 402 that this needs a paid Asana plan, and any other status the first of `errors[].message`. A `config.json` without `asana_workspace` was written before Asana, so `config::load` appends `asana` to its `issue_tabs`, as for Plane; it is last in `Source::ALL` and `Source::REMOTE`. Changing the workspace or the projects forgets the cached lists, people and account. Not covered: subtasks, milestones, approvals, premium search, portfolios, goals and writing back to Asana.
  - **Why built in**: herdr, whose design cornercase's was compared with, keeps trackers out of its core and leaves them to plugins (herdrdev/herdr#301 asked for a built-in board and was closed for that reason). cornercase builds them in because a plugin could not reach the Issues tab, `start`, the queue, flows or `/new`, which all read an `Issue`.
- **GitLab** (gitlab.com and self-hosted, `gitlab.rs`, #253): it works like GitHub, not like the trackers: the tab lists the issues of the GitLab project the open project's repository comes from, and **start** works in that repository without asking where. A workspace-wide list was rejected: GitLab's `/issues` with `scope=all` is every issue the token can see, which on gitlab.com is every public project, and the issue asked for a project's issues. `glab` was not used the way `gh` is: the issue asked for a base URL and a personal access token, and the trackers already go through `ureq` and `FakeHttp`. The token goes in a `PRIVATE-TOKEN` header and needs the `read_api` scope; it is in `secrets.json` (`gitlab_token`) or the `GITLAB_TOKEN` environment variable, which cornercase reads itself; the name is the one `glab` uses, so a token already exported for it works too. The server is `gitlab_url` in `config.json` (blank is `https://gitlab.com`; a self-hosted server serves the API under `/api/v4` of the same URL, also under a path such as `https://acme.dev/gitlab`), so the connect form asks server → token (`SaveGitlab` saves the server first; an empty one is gitlab.com) and settings has a row for each. **Which project**: every job runs `git remote -v` in the project folder (on its thread), takes the fetch URLs (`origin` first) and keeps the first whose host is the server's or one of its subdomains (`altssh.gitlab.com`), reading scp-like (`git@host:group/project.git`), `ssh://` and `http(s)://` URLs (a server under a path drops that path from web remotes only, since ssh paths never carry it); the path, with every subgroup, goes URL-encoded into `/projects/{path}/…`. No match says where the remotes are, and names the setting when one of them looks like a self-hosted GitLab. A host alias from `~/.ssh/config` does not match; not covered. The list is `GET /projects/{path}/issues` (`state=opened` unless **closed**, `order_by=updated_at`, `sort=desc`, 100 per page, one page), people are usernames (`assignee_username`, `author_username`, `Who::Person` like GitHub; **me** is the token's user, read from `/user` on every list, which also gives the account), and the people picker is the project's active members (`/members/all`, inherited ones included, at most 5 pages, bots left out). Reading an issue is `/projects/{path}/issues/{iid}` and its notes (`sort=asc`), leaving out system notes; descriptions and notes are Markdown already. The key is `#<iid>`, as GitLab writes it inside a project. The branch is GitLab's own `<iid>-<slug>`, not `issue-<iid>-…`: a merge request from a branch that starts with an issue's number followed by a dash gets `Closes #<iid>` in its description, so the issue closes when that merge request reaches the default branch, as long as the project keeps automatic issue closing on (the default; a self-managed server can also change the closing pattern). 401 and 403 say the token was rejected and may have expired or lack `read_api`, and a 404 on the project says the token cannot see it (GitLab answers 404 for a private project). A `config.json` without `gitlab_url` was written before GitLab, so `config::load` puts `gitlab` after `github` in its `issue_tabs` (at the end without one); `Source::REMOTE` puts it last, so the settings page adds its section after Plane's. Changing the server forgets the cached lists, people and account; the cache and the people are kept per project, as for GitHub. Requests go through `http::get_without_redirects` (see above): following a redirect, even from `http://` to `https://` on the same host, could send the token elsewhere, so a 3xx answer says to use the URL it leads to instead. The URL may still be `http://`, for a server on a private network: refusing it would leave those users out, so the settings notice and the connect form warn that the token travels unencrypted, except on `localhost`, `127.x` and `[::1]`.
- **Copy URL** uses OSC 52 through `App`'s `outbox.host_writes`, because the server may run on another machine.
- Markdown renders with `pulldown-cmark`; code blocks are highlighted with syntect (pure Rust `regex-fancy`), mapping scopes to palette colours so the terminal theme applies. Highlighting is cached and warmed on the reading thread; syntect and its regex crates build with `opt-level = 3` in dev.

## Agents (`agents.rs`, `agents/known.rs`, `agents/{claude,codex,gemini,opencode,grok,cursor}/`, `agents/launch.rs`, `app/launches.rs`)

- **One identity per supported agent** (#198, PR 2): `agents/known.rs`, re-exported as `agents::Known`, replaces the string constants, `stats::Kind` and `usage::Agent`. Its exhaustive dispatch arms call a rule or read a constant from the agent's folder: names, titles, modes, argument normalization, resume arguments, wrapper detection, input-box and survey checks, prompt queuing, local commands, missing-record explanations and optional usage specs. The folders also state their unsupported capabilities explicitly. Resume and input-box callbacks keep optional return types even when one agent always supports them, so every dispatch arm remains a direct folder call; local `unnecessary_wraps` expectations record that interface choice. `Known::of` searches `Known::ALL` rather than repeating the names in another match. Config and serialized reports still use strings, so custom launch agents and older server reports remain supported. `OTHERS` keeps the launch-only catalogue after `Known::ALL`, preserving the lists' order; the four known binaries still default to their names. Codex's npm wrapper suffix is defined once in its folder and shared by process detection and rollout discovery.
- **Adding an agent**: create `agents/<agent>/` with its rules, readers, counting, last-message reader, fake and optional plan probe; declare its module in `agents.rs` and remove it from `OTHERS` if present; add its `Known` variant last in `Known::ALL` and fill every dispatch arm. Add its `Reader` and `Record` variants and fill their exhaustive arms in `agents/context.rs`. State the constants every folder has: `COMMAND` (the binary typed when `agent_commands` names none: `Known::command`, since the launch-only catalogue is the only other source and an agent that leaves it must keep its command, as `cursor-agent` shows), `MODEL_FLAG` and `MODEL_ALIAS` (how a model is given and every spelling `with_model` replaces), `UNATTENDED` (the mode that never asks, which every flow role runs in), `MODELS` (model ids the Flows dialog offers, none rather than invented ones), `CATALOGUE` (a reader that lists more on a job, or `None`), `EFFORT` (its effort switch and levels, or `None`), `ADD_DIR` (the flag that gives it one more folder, or `None`, see Flows) `RECORD_BY_FOLDER` (its records are found by its folder rather than its pid, so another of its kind there hides them) and `STATUS_BY_FOLDER` (its status too, so a flow gives it one role; a test pins both sets, see Flows). Extend `app::tests::agent_status::Watched` and the applicable parameter lists, add fixtures under `tests/fixtures/<agent>/<version>/`, and update the texts the title tests guard (`SEND_HELP`, `READ_HELP`, `WAIT_HELP`, `cli::SKILL` and the control refusals). Update `RESUME_NOTE` if it resumes, these design decisions and the docs. `Known` is public because the e2e stats test names a variant; its methods and constants remain crate-visible. No agent-specific arm is needed in `app`, `term`, `stats`, `usage`, `settings` or `restart`; extensible stats persistence belongs to PR 4.
- **Cursor CLI** (#290, `agents/cursor/`, bundle 2026.10.14-941492e; **read from the bundle's code, not checked in a live session**: running one spends the user's Cursor account, so the store layout below is unverified and every reader fails closed): the kind keeps its name `cursor`, the title is `Cursor CLI` and the command `cursor-agent`; `cursor` alone is the IDE's shim, which fails without the IDE. Cursor left the launch-only catalogue (`OTHERS` went from 10 entries to 9; `kinds()` keeps its order, since it was the first of them). The launcher runs `exec -a "$0" node --use-system-ca …/versions/<ver>/index.js`, so `argv[1]` is a flag and Node 24's main thread is named `MainThread`: `detect` now looks at the first argument after `argv[0]` that is not a flag (what `discovery::named` already did), `cursor::runs_as` takes `index.js` inside `cursor-agent/versions/<ver>/` (which also finds the `agent` binary and the `cursor agent` shim, never the bare name `agent`, since `~/.grok/bin/agent` is Grok's), and `term::program` treats `MainThread` like `node-MainThread`, so a Cursor started as `agent` gets the full-argv detection instead of a tab named `MainThread`. Its `worker-server` daemon (parent 1, LSP and indexing only, never chats) is never in the foreground. Flags: `--model` only (no `-m`; effort lives inside the model id, `id[effort=high]`, so there is no effort switch), `--mode plan|ask` (`--plan` and `--mode=x` normalise to it), `-f`, `--force` and `--yolo` are one flag (`--force`, which also approves MCP servers; `--trust` skips the folder question and is part of the unattended arguments), `--auto-review` for Smart Auto. Resume is `--resume <chat id>` and only works in the folder the chat started in. A prompt typed while a turn runs is queued in memory and written when delivered, so `holds_prompts` is true; `/` and `!` lines are local for confirmation (`/plan x` and skills do send a message, but treating them as local only skips a confirmation, it cannot hang). The trust question (`Do you trust the contents of this directory?`, `▶ [a] Trust this workspace`) already matches the default pattern and Enter picks it. First-run dialogs (sandbox, MCP approval) were not seen live, so a prompt pasted into one is possible.
- Config: `agent` (`auto` = the one running in the tab, else ask), `agent_args`, `agent_modes`, `agent_commands`, `prompt` (placeholders like `{url}`, `{key}`, `{title}`), `submit`, `accept_trust_prompts`, `trust_prompt_pattern`.
- `agents::launch::Launch` types the command once the shell is quiet, waits until the agent is in the foreground, has drawn something and its screen is quiet, waits for the trust prompt to be answered (or answers it), then pastes the prompt. Readiness is a heuristic. "Drawn something" means the screen is no longer the shell's screen plus the command's echo (or part of it), compared without whitespace (`Launch::echo`): the quiet second used to count from the echo, so an agent that took over a second to draw (a loaded machine, a cold start) got the prompt before its trust question and read it as the answer (#34). A screen that is not the prompt plus the command (zsh's `RPROMPT` on the same line, a line scrolled off the top) counts as drawn, as before. `AGENT_LATEST` (30 s) still types the prompt into an agent that never draws.
- **The trust prompt is left to the user by default** (`accept_trust_prompts`, off). It is the agent's guard against running a repository's own agent config (project settings, hooks, MCP servers) before anyone looked at it, so answering it for the user would run a hostile repository's config as soon as an issue starts. It used to be on by default, as `auto_accept_trust_prompt`; the key was renamed so every saved `true` (settings save the whole file) falls back to off once, and turning it on again is one click. A per-project "trust this project for agents" was left out: off costs one Enter per issue started, and agents remember the folders already trusted. Whatever the setting, the prompt is detected: a launch that may not answer it parks (`Launch::waits_for_you`) until the agent prints again, which the answer does; its clock restarts from that output, so `AGENT_LATEST` never types into the question, and once the screen is quiet without the question it pastes the prompt. A parked launch is not polled: `App::tick` asks for the 100 ms launch wake-up only for launches that are not parked, since the user may take hours to answer and every wake-up draws every client. Before, a launch with answering off typed the prompt into the question, and Enter picked Claude's highlighted "No, exit". If the user says no, the shell comes back and the launch is abandoned as usual. A flow's launches answer it whatever the setting (`launch::Spec::trusted`, see Flows). `trust_prompt_pattern` is matched only against the last 15 non-blank lines of the screen (`agents::asks_trust`), where an agent draws the question, so older output above cannot match; the regex is compiled once per pattern (`agents::TrustPrompt`).
- Gemini CLI has a built-in `plan` mode (`--approval-mode plan`). Normalize its YOLO aliases (`-y`, `--yolo`, `--approval-mode yolo`, including `=`) when finding or replacing a mode, so changing modes cannot leave a dangerous alias behind. Apply this only to the Gemini kind: other agents can use the same flags with different meanings. The live 0.63.0 trust dialog highlights `Trust folder`, has no yes option and restarts its child after Enter. The existing launch waits for that new screen before pasting.
- When answering, the prompt may highlight "No" by default (Claude Code does), so `answer_keys` moves the selection to the "yes" option before pressing Enter.

## Agent status (`agents/activity.rs`, `agents/{claude,codex,gemini,opencode,grok,cursor}/mod.rs`, `app/agent_watch.rs`)

- **Program status comes first** (#249, `term/status.rs`, `term/status/stream.rs`): OSC 7501 reports override the agent's file and title heuristics while a record exists. The protocol uses `error`, which becomes `Activity::Failed`, `Status::Failed` and the script-facing `failed`, a bold red `!`. `blocked` becomes waiting; `working` stays working; reported `idle` is `Activity::Ready` so cancelling a turn does not create a guessed completion; explicit `done` marks unseen work even without a preceding working sample and stays acknowledged once the tab is opened. Reported idle preserves an existing unseen mark, including one restored with the conversation, but never creates one by inference. Failed stays visible until replaced or cleared. Waiting outranks failed, then pending, done, working and idle. Failure settles and notifies with the same one-second rule, including the chat apps while away. Failed ends a turn for stats, `wait --until idle` and idle restart. Model, context, resume and last-message readers stay agent-specific.
- **Any recognised agent can report status**: `activity::Pane` follows the kind's string, including the launch-only catalogue and `agent_commands`, rather than requiring a `Known` reader. Process arguments still establish identity; an OSC `app` cannot turn a shell or a build into an agent. Each record is associated with the foreground pid that emitted it, so retained results do not override a later agent. Changing foreground process expires active and idle records; done and error records survive back at the shell with the originating agent identity (`Term::status_agent`), and are never applied to a new foreground agent. A finished result at the shell is acknowledged when its tab is seen; failure remains until replaced or cleared. Shell output never inherits the identity of a departed agent; already recorded results retain their original pid. A shell prompt (OSC 133 A) expires active and idle records too. Alternate-screen switches and soft resets keep records; RIS clears all. `clear` removes an id and its descendants (or all records at the root), returning to heuristics when no applicable record remains. The most urgent record wins, including children whose parent has no record.
- **Keep the published Ghostty binding pinned**: `libghostty-vt` 0.2.2 predates the upstream OSC 7501 callback. A bounded streaming observer beside the emulator recognises OSC, shell prompts and RIS without reading strings embedded in DCS/APC/PM/SOS. Ghostty continues to receive every byte and owns rendering and its existing replies. For a support query, the emulator feeds the prefix through Ghostty before sending the fixed reply through the same PTY writer, so replies stay in request order even within one read. The observer accepts BEL and ST across arbitrary read boundaries, validates the revision 0.4 grammar and all size/base64/control-character limits before applying a report, keeps at most 256 records and evicts the oldest updated one. Optional labels and messages are validated but not displayed; `kind`, `progress` and `app` do not affect presentation. No heartbeat, settings, hooks or new dependencies. The specification is [Program Status Protocol](https://www.superlogical.com/rex/docs/build/program-status); the upstream implementation is [ghostty#14560](https://github.com/ghostty-org/ghostty/pull/14560).
- Every 500 ms (`WATCH_AGENTS_EVERY`, from `App::refresh`) each pane whose foreground process is Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI or opencode (`agents::detect` on its arguments) gets an `Activity`: working, waiting or idle. For Claude the source is the file Claude Code keeps for each running process, `<CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR or ~/.claude>/sessions/<pid>.json`, the registry behind `claude agents --json` (its documented interface for status bars): `status` is `busy`, `waiting` (permission prompt, question, dialog), `idle` or `shell` (turn over but a Bash it started with `run_in_background` still runs). `shell` counts as working, like Claude's own agents view: Claude wakes up when the command ends, so it was not done (a dev server left running keeps the tab `◐`). It is its own `Activity::Shell` all the same, which `Pane::status` shows as working (so the UI, the rollups and the notifications are unchanged) and only the commands for scripts tell apart (`Pane::background_shell`, `Pane::state`; see Commands for scripts and agents): an orchestrating agent whose agents leave watchers behind had no way to stop waiting for them (#127). A Monitor that runs a command is a background Bash too, so it gives `shell` (checked live on 2.1.289); one on a WebSocket runs no shell and may leave it `idle`. Its fields are not documented, so a missing file or an unknown value falls back to the title Claude sets (`◐`/`◑`, or braille in older versions, while busy; `✳` otherwise; it stays `✳` when Claude sees `TMUX`, `STY` or `ZELLIJ`), then to idle.
- No hooks and no screen scraping. Hooks mean editing the user's `~/.claude/settings.json` (or shipping a plugin), `Stop` never fires on an Esc interrupt and the permission notification comes ~6 s late; Claude's screen changes too often to match. The session file turns idle within a tick of an interrupt and waiting as soon as a prompt shows.
- `Status` adds done: a pane that went from working or waiting to idle while its tab was not the visible one (`App::visible_tab`: the active tab, unless the compact menu or a file shown full screen covers the pane) is done until that tab is shown. `watch_agents` clears it for the visible tab on every `refresh`, not only every 500 ms, so a click shows `○` at once. An unknown state counts as idle, so a fresh Claude never looks done.
- **Pending review** (`Status::Pending`, a bold `●` in the brand purple 99): a mark the user puts on an agent's pane by hand, because launching several agents left finished work to review and `✓` was not a record of it: it goes the moment the tab is shown, a quick look through the tabs wiped it, and a restart lost it. The mark lives on the pane (`activity::Pane::pending`), not the tab, since the agents section and a split tab hold one agent per pane; a tab's menu (` ⋯ ` or right-click on its row, in the lists, the tree and the tab bar) marks or reviews every agent pane in it, the pane's right-click menu, a right-click on its row in the agents section (`App::open_agent_menu`, the one menu those rows have, so a busy list can be gone through without leaving the pane being read) and the keys menu's `m` act on that pane, and `cornercase mark [--pane|--tab] [--reviewed]` lets a script or an agent do it (its own pane by default, so an agent can mark itself at the end of its turn). Only panes with an agent cornercase reads take it (`Pane::agent`); others are refused or offered nothing. Showing the tab does not clear it (`Pane::see` leaves it); it goes when marked as reviewed, which clears `done` too, or when the agent goes from idle or waiting to working, which means it got a new prompt or an answer and the user is iterating on it again. Going from a background shell to working does not clear it (the agent woke up by itself), and neither does a mark put on while it works: it shows once the turn is over. It sits between done and waiting: a question still comes first, and it beats `✓` since it is the user's own statement. It needs you (`Status::needs_you`), so it reaches the workspace, project and group rows, the compact `≡` and the keys menu's `a`, but it never notifies (`Status::notifies` is waiting, failed and done only: the user just put it there) and stats do not count it as time waiting for the user (it may stay for days). Waits end on it as on idle (`--until idle`, the default, `turn-over`, `restart --when-idle`); `status`, `--panes` and `events` give it as `pending`. Only by hand for now: turning every unseen finish into a sticky mark was left out, to see first how the manual mark is used.
- A tab shows its most urgent pane (`Tab::status`, the order of `Status`): waiting `!` > failed red `!` > pending `●` > done `✓` > working `◐` > idle `○`, before the name. **Working icon** (`working_icon` in `config.json`, Settings → UI, `config::WorkingIcon`): `moon`, the default, keeps the still `◐` (`activity::MOON`); `spinner` steps through `⠋⠙⠹⠸⠼⠴⠦⠧⠇⠏` (`activity::SPINNER`), one frame every 100 ms (`activity::SPIN_EVERY`), the same frame on every row, from the time since the process started (`activity::spin_frame`). `View::spin` carries the frame, `None` for the moon, so one value reaches every place the icon is drawn (tabs, the tab bar, the agents section, the compact `≡`, the toast). `App::tick` wakes for the next frame only with the spinner on and some tab working; otherwise the loop stays on its 500 ms tick. The spinner was the only icon in 0.12.84 to 0.12.86 (#240): it shows at a glance that a turn is still going, but a column of moving icons keeps pulling the eye away from the pane being read, and with an agent working it draws every window ten times a second, so the moon came back as the default and the spinner became a choice. A pick rather than a switch, like `tabs`, since the row names what it shows. A still with the spinner on, such as a test, shows the first frame. Chat messages (Telegram, Discord) keep `◐` whatever the setting, since a message does not move. `!`, `✓`, `○` and the shell marks never move. A tab without an agent shows its shell instead (`TabEntry::icon`), in the muted grey: `▸` while a program runs in the foreground of any of its panes (`Tab::running`), `›` at the prompt. Every tab has an icon so their names start in one column: a shell tab used to start two cells to the left of an agent's, and the list read as two indents. Two states rather than one fixed mark, so the icon answers what an agent's does (is it doing something?) and a dev server that died back to its prompt shows at a glance. It is `!Term::shell_in_foreground()`, read when the row is drawn, like the tab's label (an editor started with a command counts as running); caching it on the 500 ms agent reads was tried and dropped: the label already looks up the foreground process on every draw, and the cache only added a field and up to half a second of lag. Grey, never yellow, so a dev server that runs for hours does not compete with an agent working, and no done: every `ls` in a hidden tab would leave a mark. The agents' own glyphs were rejected for the same reason (a `◐` then a `○` means done for an agent and would not for a program), and `▶` because it has an emoji presentation that some fonts draw wider than a cell. Shell icons never reach the workspace, project or group rows. Workspace, project and collapsed group rows show only what needs you (`agents::activity::attention`: waiting, pending or done), right-aligned and before `↓n`; working stays on the tab so the sidebar is not always full of icons. A collapsed workspace is the exception (#294): its tabs are hidden, so its row shows the most urgent status of its agents (`agents::activity::highest`, the order of `Status`, so `◐` or the spinner and `○` too), in the tree and the lists (`ui::workspaces::Badge`); shells show nothing. The compact `≡` shows the same for every tab but the visible one (`View::attention`). The agents section (see Layout) lists each agent pane with its own status, not its tab's.
- **Codex** (`agents::codex::Reader::activity`, checked live on 0.160.0): waiting while the title starts with `[ ! ] Action Required` or `[ . ] Action Required` (it blinks between the two during an approval); working while it starts with a braille spinner and a space (`⠴ <thread> | <project>`) or while the rollout the context line follows (`agents/codex/rollout.rs`) is inside a turn (`task_started`, until `task_complete` or `turn_aborted`); otherwise idle (the title is then `<thread> | <project>`, or just the project). Approvals are never written to the rollout, so `!` needs the title; the title's items are configurable (`/title`, `tui.terminal_title`), and without its activity item the rollout still gives working and done. Both rather than one: the rollout alone has no waiting, the title alone breaks when its activity item is removed. Hooks were rejected as for Claude (they mean writing to `~/.codex`).
- **opencode** (`agents::opencode::Reader::activity`, checked live on 1.18.35): no status until the job has found its database, read it and told its session apart (a query that fails, or two opencode in one folder, give none, rather than an idle that would turn a working pane into a false done); idle while it has no session yet; working while the newest message of its session is the user's (sent, its reply not written yet) or an assistant message without `time.completed` (an Esc interrupt completes it, with a `MessageAbortedError`; `/compact` is a reply like any other, with `summary: true`), else idle. The session comes from the database the context line reads (`agents/opencode/database.rs`, see Context use), on the same thread. opencode never says it waits for you: a permission or a question lives only in its memory (the tool part already reads `running`), its title stays `OpenCode` or `OC | <session title>` whatever it does, and its log (`log/opencode.log`, shared by every process, no pid) writes `asking` but never the answer, so a pane waiting for you shows `◐`. Rejected: reading that log (a format of its own, cut by `--log-level`, and the reply would have to be guessed from the database), its HTTP API (`/session/status`, `/permission`, which only exists with `--port` and then listens on localhost without a password), and its own notifications (`attention` in its TUI config, off by default and only while its window is unfocused, so cornercase would have to inject a config and report focus). It asks no trust question, so a launch goes straight to the prompt once its screen is quiet.
- **Gemini CLI** (`agents::gemini::Reader::activity`, 0.63.0): trim the padded OSC 0 title; `✋` means waiting, `✦` and `⏲` working, `◇` idle. Fixed (`ui.dynamicWindowTitle` off), hidden (`ui.hideWindowTitle` on) and unknown titles give no status, because losing a signal does not mean the turn ended. There is no session fallback: the file has no turn markers, text can precede a pending tool, and tool results and message updates arrive separately. Hooks would require writing the user's settings, so they are rejected. Follow Gemini's quiet-shell `✋` heuristic even when it mistakes a slow command for input; guessing from descendants would be less reliable. Fixtures pin working, ready, thought, fixed, confirmation and silent-shell titles observed inside tmux. Hidden titles were checked against the source. The source has no tmux, screen or Zellij exception; screen and Zellij were not checked live.
- **Grok Build** (`agents::grok::Reader::activity`, checked live on 1.0.52): no status until `active_sessions.json` has a row for the foreground pid. On Linux that row's `pid_start` must equal field 22 of `/proc/<pid>/stat`; macOS has no start time, so a missing one does not reject the row. The previous status stays on screen while the next read runs, including while that read holds the session tails.
