A turn is open from `events.jsonl` `turn_started` until `turn_ended` (`completed` or `cancelled`). The reader keeps one tail per file and the fold, and applies only new lines; a replaced or truncated file clears the fold. The first read, and a reset, seeds the open turn from the newest `turn_started` or `turn_ended` behind the tail, so a turn whose start is more than a megabyte earlier still reads as working. If that backwards scan reaches its bound without either marker, the turn is unknown, not closed: newer phase or tool events keep it working until a `turn_ended` arrives, so a wait or an idle restart does not treat it as finished. Waiting is a `permission_prompt` phase, a `permission_requested` with no later `permission_resolved` for that same tool, or an open `ask_user_question` or `exit_plan_mode` until its `tool_completed`. Resolving one permission does not clear another tool's request. A question's permission returns `allow` with `wait_ms` 0 and the phase goes back to `tool_execution` while the question is still unanswered, so that phase does not close the ask; `tool_completed` does, and the same turn then continues (`loop_started`) as working. `enter_plan_mode` is how a turn enters plan mode and is not itself the approval. A live `/plan` called `exit_plan_mode`; its permission returned `allow` with `wait_ms` 0 while the approval was still open, and `tool_completed` arrived when it was approved, after which the same turn continued (`loop_started`, then another tool). The ask stays open until that `tool_completed`. Any other tool, including `run_terminal_command`, is working. `waiting_for_model` is how a healthy turn starts. An unknown model is rejected before any turn. Starts that have `turn_started` and no `turn_ended` in real logs are not explained yet, and there is no timeout. A background command with `block_until_ms: 0` ended the turn while the process kept running, so there is no `Activity::Shell`. Esc did not end a turn. Ctrl+C did (`cancelled`, `mid_turn_abort`, `cancellation_context.trigger` `ctrl_c`). One Enter while a turn is open queues the text and leaves the turn running. A second Enter is Grok's send now: the open turn ends `cancelled` with trigger `send_now`, and the next `turn_started` has `redirect_kind` `queued_after_cancel`. `send` presses Enter once, so it queues and is not refused. A title containing `Action Required` forces waiting only while the turn is open. A braille spinner, and the word Waiting in a compact title, do not, and neither overrides `turn_ended`. The trust screen says "Do you trust the contents of this directory?" and "Yes, proceed", which the default trust pattern already matches; cornercase does not write `trusted_folders.toml`.
- **Cursor CLI** (`agents::cursor::Reader::activity`): the title is OSC 0 and carries no state by default (the chat's name or `Cursor Agent`); with `display.showStatusIndicators` (`/status-indicators` in Cursor, off by default) it reads `<name> - <emoji> <text>`. The last ` - ` is taken, so a chat name holding one is no problem: `❓` and `🔐` mean waiting, `⏳` `🧭` `⌨️` `📋` `📝` working and `✅` idle; any other (`🔄` reconnecting, `📂` loading, `📤` moving to the cloud) says nothing and falls through, as does a title without one. Without a title signal the status comes from the transcript (`agent-transcripts/<chat id>/<chat id>.jsonl`, read with the shared incremental tail): a `user` line opens a turn, `{"type":"turn_ended"}` (`success`, `aborted` or `error`) closes it, and a rewrite (compaction) is read again from the start of its tail; the file has no timestamps. "Send now" writes no `turn_ended`, so the next user line keeps the turn open. Nothing on disk says waiting, so without `/status-indicators` a permission prompt shows as working, like opencode, and `!` never appears; the optional fallback of reading the screen's `Waiting for decision (y/n/p)...` placeholder was left out, since this design avoids reading the screen for status. A fresh Cursor before its first turn has no store and shows idle, so a first turn without indicators shows working a moment late. Cursor writes no OSC 7501.
- Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, opencode and Grok Build; supported agents register through `Known` as described under Agents. `activity::Pane` stores the kind string so launch-only and custom agents can report OSC 7501, while `context::Pane` stores `Option<Known>` for its readers; `app::agent_watch::agent_in` detects the identity, updates its reader, remembers its conversation, calibrates the input-box check through `Known`, and asks the pane for activity. It has no agent-specific arm. An agent running through `ssh` or in a container is not seen (no local process, no local file).
- **Notifications**: `Pane::update` returns `Waiting`, `Failed` or `Done` once that status has held for `NOTIFY_AFTER` (1 s) and its tab was not shown meanwhile, once per change, so a prompt answered at once stays quiet. `App::notify` sets a toast with the status icon (`<agent> needs you in <project> › <workspace>`, the agent's kind such as `claude` or `codex`, control characters stripped) and queues a `host::notify::Notification`, which the server encodes per client. Panes of the same agent that settle on the same status in the same workspace on one tick give one notice: the text would be the same, and Ghostty drops a repeat within 5 s anyway. The agent is part of that key, so Claude and Codex in one workspace each get theirs. A pane's state remembers its agent (`agents::activity::Pane::follow`) and starts over when another one takes the pane, so a Codex replacing a waiting Claude without a tick in between still notifies. They go through the outer terminal like OSC 52, so they work over SSH (no `notify-send`/`osascript` on the server's machine).
- The channel is the client's: inside tmux, screen or Zellij it is BEL (tmux drops notification OSCs, passthrough is off by default, BEL goes through); otherwise `host::notify::detect` trusts the XTVERSION reply when there is one (asked with the colours, so it survives SSH, where only `TERM` arrives; env vars may also be inherited from another terminal), else `TERM_PROGRAM`, `LC_TERMINAL` (iTerm2 sets it for SSH), `TERM`, `KITTY_WINDOW_ID`, `KONSOLE_VERSION`. OSC 777 for Ghostty, WezTerm, Warp, Rio, foot before 1.18 and Konsole before 24.12 (or with no version); OSC 9 for iTerm2 (its only one); OSC 99 with `o=unfocused` for kitty, Contour, VS Code (VS Code knows only 99), foot 1.18+ and Konsole 24.12+ (from the XTVERSION version, or `KONSOLE_VERSION` 241200, since those two report a click); BEL otherwise. Never several, since terminals that know several would show each.
- OSC 9 and 777 end with BEL, which every parser accepts; OSC 99 with ST, as kitty's spec says. `;` in the text becomes `,`: WezTerm, Rio and Konsole cut the body at it, WezTerm drops an OSC 9 holding one. Ghostty on macOS, foot, Konsole and Warp hide notifications while their window is focused; the others show them. `desktop_notifications` (`auto`, a channel, `off`) overrides the channel; the toast always shows. The visible tab never notifies, even while the outer window is unfocused: focus reporting (`CSI ? 1004 h`) is now on, but it is per window while what is visible is shared, so it only brings a toast back (below).
- **Clicking a notification (#316)**: a desktop notification carried only its text, so a click could not say which agent it was for.
  - **Tokens**: each agent notice (`App::notify`, through `App::tell` in `app/notifications.rs`) gets `cc-<nonce>-<n>` (`[a-zA-Z0-9_+.-]`, the nonce per server run like the chat buttons', so a notification left in the notification centre across a restart is ignored rather than taken for a pane that now has its id). `Notices` keeps 64 (`notify::KEPT`, oldest out) with the agent, place, status, project, workspace and the panes behind it: the dedup key of `notify` (agent, status, workspace) covers several panes, so `watch_agents` collects them. `cornercase notify`, queue, setup-failure and flow bypass-consent notices keep no token and the old sequence.
  - **Asking for the report**: `Channel::Osc99` with a token encodes `ESC ] 99 ; i=<token> : a=focus,report : o=unfocused ; text ESC \`. `a=focus` keeps what the terminal did; terminals that know neither key ignore them, as kitty's spec requires. kitty (checked live on 0.45), VS Code, Contour (`i=x:p=activated;`), foot 1.18 and Konsole 24.12 write back `ESC ] 99 ; i=<token> ; [button] ESC \`. Ghostty parses OSC 99 but does not act on it (ghostty-org/ghostty#5634), so it stays on OSC 777 and its click only focuses the window; the same goes for iTerm2, WezTerm, Warp and Rio. A `p=?` query in the startup probe was left out: kitty's answer alone is about 230 bytes and the probe's largest reply is kept under macOS' 1024-byte tty input queue.
  - **The client reads the answer**: `ESC ] 99 ;` joins `REPLY_PREFIXES`, so it is never decoded as `Alt+]` and typed text. `host::notify::activation` parses leniently (the metadata before the first `;`, `i` of 1 to 64 allowed characters, `p` absent, empty or `activated`, so `close`, `alive` and `?` answers give nothing) and the decoder emits `client::input::Input::Activated`, which the window sends as `ClientMessage::Activated` (last variant, `protocol::VERSION` 5; the browser bridge sends it too). A split inside the 5-byte prefix is held only while replies are expected or more bytes wait, like every reply, since Alt+`]` must not be delayed; a terminal writes its answer in one write. Bracketed paste stays text.
  - **The app jumps**: `App::notification_clicked` ignores an unknown token (not logged), drops the notice's own toast and missed mark, goes to the first of its panes in sidebar order whose agent still needs you, else the first that exists, through `jump_to_pane`; with all its panes gone, to the workspace if it is open, else a toast `<agent> in <place> is gone`. A project closing or a workspace being removed counts as gone. An open overlay stays, and focus is shared, so every window shows the jump as after any click. The log says the pane or `workspace` or `gone`, never the text.
  - **The toast's ` go `** (`Toast::go`, `ui::ToastButton::Go`) does the same for the same token in any terminal, also inside tmux, and lasts 6 s like an ` undo `. It is the way to the agent where a click reports nothing.
  - **The toast again on focus**: the client enables `?1004` and disables it on every way out (`restore_input_modes`, `release_screen`); the server passes `FocusGained` and `FocusLost` to `App::window_focus` and they are not a use. State is per window (`Notices::windows`, dropped in `forget`): a notification sent while a window is unfocused marks it missed, and on gaining focus its toast shows again with ` go ` if the agent still needs you and none of its panes is in the tab on screen. Ghostty, iTerm2 and WezTerm bring the window forward on a click, usually after the 6 s. Jumping straight to the agent on focus was left out: alt-tabbing back looks the same. In kitty the click's answer and the focus arrive in either order; the click drops the missed mark and a notice whose tab is on screen is skipped, so there is no duplicate.
  - **Leaving**: the server keeps what it sent each window (`notify::Sent`, the tokens of notices sent as OSC 99, 64 at most) and sends their closes (`p=close`) before `Detached`, `Shutdown` or `Restart`, so a click later writes no answer into the shell that outlives cornercase. A killed or lost window can still leave one; its answer then shows as typed text in the shell.

## Context use (`agents/context.rs`, `agents/claude/transcript.rs`, `agents/codex/rollout.rs`, `agents/gemini/session.rs`, `agents/opencode/database.rs`, `agents/grok/session.rs`, `agents/cursor/store.rs`)

- `agents/context.rs` owns `Record`, the last-message location chosen by a pane, and dispatches its reads to each agent's folder. `context/message.rs` keeps only the shared backwards JSONL and text helpers and `Said`. The `context::Record` path used by callers stays the same.
- **One reader per pane** (#198, PR 3): `context::Pane` owns only the shared identity and first-seen time, shown context, newest prompt time, counting ledger and `Option<Reader>`. The exhaustive `Reader` enum dispatches to `claude::Reader`, `codex::Reader`, `gemini::Reader` and `opencode::Reader` in their folders' `mod.rs`. Each reader owns its record state and discovery job: Claude's transcript, subagent tails and last session file; Codex's rollout; Gemini's session plus the record retained while a job owns the session; opencode's session and model cache. Readers update the shared shown value directly, so Claude still recomputes it only on a new reply, and return their newest prompt time for the pane's monotonic maximum. A changed pid or identity replaces all shared and reader state, and no detected agent clears the pane. Conversation switches keep the prompt time and counting ledger as before. `record_for` first checks the pane's current identity, then asks its reader, so a process that execs another agent cannot answer from the former record. Conversation and activity use the same reader; unknown Gemini titles and unread opencode databases still give no activity.
- `Look` carries the process id, owned arguments and title, the Claude config folder and a lazy screen callback. Arguments move from process detection into the look and back to the common watcher's resume step, without another copy. Gemini reads the screen every watch for its footer; Claude's update reads only its session file. `Known::input_box` accepts a lazy callback so the common watcher reads Claude's screen only until calibration succeeds, and never reads Codex's or opencode's screen. Each discovery job remains one in flight per pane at the same 500 ms pace; no new thread or scan is added. Claude's `Limits::read` takes the look's pid and arguments directly, replacing the transient `Claude` wrapper. Its pane tests write the same session data to an isolated registry now that the reader owns that read; their assertions, fixtures and test names stay the same. The record-selection test follows one reader and checks that every other identity gets no record.
- A tab running Claude gets a second line, `Opus 5.5 · 23%`: the model and the share of the window used, as Claude's statusline computes it (`round(used / window * 100)`, at most 100). `sessions/<pid>.json` gives `sessionId` (re-read every tick: `/clear` changes it) and `cwd`; the transcript is `projects/<cwd with every non-alphanumeric UTF-16 unit as '-'; past 200 characters cut, plus '-' and a base36 hash>/<sessionId>.jsonl`. Its last `assistant` line (not `isSidechain`, model not `<synthetic>`) gives `message.model` and used = `input_tokens + cache_creation_input_tokens + cache_read_input_tokens`; a later `system`/`compact_boundary` hides the line until the next reply.
- The transcript is read as it grows (the first time, its last MiB), lines scanned backwards to the first reply or boundary, so a quiet tick costs one `stat`. The window is worked out again only when the reply changes.
- No file holds the window, so it mirrors Claude Code 2.1.289's own rule: `DISABLE_COMPACT` with `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` gives that; otherwise 1M for models with a native 1M window (Opus 4.7 and later, anything from version 5) or a model setting ending in `[1m]` that names the transcript's model (`--model`, `ANTHROPIC_MODEL`, then `model` in local > project > user settings), unless `CLAUDE_CODE_DISABLE_1M_CONTEXT`; `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_CONTEXT_TOKENS` for non-Claude models; else 200k, or 1M once past 200k. Variables are the process's (`process::env`) overlaid by each settings file's `env`, as Claude applies them. Not seen: an unsaved `/model` switch, accounts without 1M, the SDK's 1M beta header. Claude's statusline input has the exact numbers but needs a `statusLine` command in the user's settings (rejected like hooks).
- Wide mode gives the tab two rows (`ui::Details::lines`, height `pitch.max(lines)`), with `×` on the first only; compact mode puts the line on the band's third row. The model is cut first and dropped below 4 columns; the percentage turns orange at 75 % and red at 90 % (`agents::usage::Severity::of`).
- What the line shows is three switches in `config.json` (Settings → UI, `settings::Detail`): `model` and `context` (the percentage), on by default, and `memory` (off, see Memory below). `App::tab_details` applies them once into a `ui::Details { model, percent, memory }`, used by the view and by `App::tab_lines`, and `ui::Details::lines` gives the second row when any part is there, so drawing and hit testing agree; with all three off every tab takes one row. 0.6 had one switch, `context_line`, for model and percentage together: `config::load` reads it as `context`, and a file without `model` gets `context`'s value, so a line turned off then stays off. This happens on the JSON before deserializing rather than with a serde `alias`, which would reject a file holding both keys and fall back to all defaults. Plan limits stay in the ` Usage ` modal; showing them under every tab was tried and dropped, since they belong to the account, would repeat the same number in every tab and push out the one thing each tab has of its own.
- Codex context (`agents/codex/rollout.rs`) was verified against CLI 0.160.0; versioned synthetic fixtures and tagged source links are in `tests/fixtures/codex/README.md`. Associate by the JSONL held open under the agent's `CODEX_HOME/sessions` (or its `HOME/.codex/sessions`), never the newest rollout. Follow only Codex descendants of the foreground process, including npm's `node …/codex/bin/codex.js` wrapper; accept CLI/exec/VSCode source labels, reject subagent metadata and ambiguous root rollouts. A child whose environment cannot be read (a `fork` about to `exec`, a process that just exited) is skipped, never a reason to drop the line. Discovery (`agents::codex::rollout::rollout_path`: `lsof` on macOS, every process in daemon mode) runs on a thread per pane, one job in flight, so it never blocks the server loop; the main thread only takes its answer and reads the rollout as it grows. Linux reads `/proc` (children across all threads); macOS uses `proc_listchildpids` / `proc_listallpids` (both return a count of pids, not bytes) and `/usr/sbin/lsof -n -P -a -p <pid> -Fn`. Missing inspection access omits the line.
- **Codex's app-server daemon**: 0.160.0 installs and starts `codex app-server --managed-daemon` (pid in `CODEX_HOME/app-server-daemon/daemon.pid`, parent 1); the TUI talks to it over a socket and the daemon writes every rollout (hence `source: "vscode"`), so no descendant holds one. Nothing links a TUI's pid to its thread (locks, snapshots and the socket carry no pid), so when no descendant holds a rollout the folder decides, only when unambiguous: the daemon's open rollouts whose `session_meta.cwd` is the agent's cwd, written since the pane first saw that pid (the daemon keeps a finished session open ~60 s, so a new Codex in the same folder would otherwise take the old one), exactly one of them, and exactly one native Codex TUI (`argv[0]` named `codex`, no `app-server`) in that folder with that home among all processes (`process::all`). Two Codex in one folder show nothing; after one exits the other comes back once the daemon closes its rollout. `codex -C <dir>` gives a cwd that does not match, so no line. The line appears with the first turn: the rollout is created lazily.
- Codex models come from `turn_context.model` and `thread_settings_applied.thread_settings.model` for the current thread. Usage is `last_token_usage.total_tokens / model_context_window`, rounded and capped at 100%; no lifetime totals, extra cached/reasoning tokens, inferred window or Codex baseline adjustment. `Context.percent` is optional: unavailable/malformed usage shows the model alone. Model switches and compaction clear the old percentage; switching rollouts, replacement, truncation, deletion or process exit resets the reader. Read only complete JSONL lines, starting with a tail of at most one MiB, then bounded incremental batches; validate session metadata at the head. An initial tail without a model omits the line until a model is reported.
- **opencode** (`agents/opencode/database.rs`, checked live on 1.18.35; fixtures in `tests/fixtures/opencode/1.18.35`, rows from a real session with paths replaced): every opencode process shares one SQLite database (WAL), `$XDG_DATA_HOME/opencode/opencode.db`, `opencode-<channel>.db` outside the stable channel, or what `OPENCODE_DB` names. The pane's database is the one its opencode holds open (the foreground process or an `opencode` descendant: `process::open_files`, `lsof` on macOS, like Codex's rollout), read with `rusqlite` (SQLite compiled in, `bundled`, so nothing is needed on the machine) read only, with a 100 ms busy timeout. No row holds a pid, so the folder decides, as for Codex's daemon: the newest session (no `parent_id`, which marks a subagent's; not archived) whose `directory` is the process's cwd and that was updated since the pane first saw that pid, and only while no other opencode holding the same database runs in that folder (an `opencode run` counts too). So the line appears with the first prompt, follows `/new` once it is used, and two opencode in one folder show nothing. The whole job (find, query, parse) runs on a thread per pane, one in flight, started again each refresh, so the server loop only takes answers. The line mirrors opencode's own footer: the newest assistant message with output, `input + output + reasoning + cache.read + cache.write` over the model's `limit.context`, rounded; after `/compact` that is the summary's reply, as opencode shows. The model is the newest message's (a user message names the one picked), so a switch shows the new model without a percentage until it replies. Names and windows come from models.dev's file opencode caches (`$XDG_CACHE_HOME/opencode/models.json`, or `OPENCODE_MODELS_PATH`), 5 MB, so it is parsed only when the model or the file changes; a model it lacks (a custom provider's, whose limits live in opencode's config) shows its id without a percentage. Tests use `agents::opencode::fake::FakeOpencode`: a database built from the fixture schema, with rows written by the test, held by a script named `opencode` that app tests type into a pane, or by `FakeOpencode::start`, a `/bin/sleep` named `opencode` with the database as its stdin, for the tests that call `look` on a process of their own. That one never forks: the script's shell forks for each `sleep` of its loop, and until that child `exec`s it is a copy of the fake (its arguments, its folder, the database on fd 3), which the check for another opencode in the folder rightly counts. macOS' `/bin/sh` is bash, which forks where Linux's dash uses `vfork`, so `two_opencode_in_one_folder_show_nothing` failed there now and then, finding a second opencode after the second fake was gone.
- **Gemini CLI** (`agents/gemini/session.rs`, 0.63.0): discovery and parsing share one job per pane, using `discovery::Job` with Codex and opencode and `jsonl::Tail` with Codex. Read the process's `GEMINI_CLI_HOME/.gemini` or `HOME/.gemini`. `projects.json` maps its canonical cwd to a slug; `.project_root`, when present, checks the owner to avoid symlink and slug collisions. Choose the newest top-level `kind: main` JSONL updated since the pane first saw the pid. Failed discovery, stale files, missing registries and subagents give no conversation data. Follow Gemini children past Node runtime flags: the npm parent may keep its original cwd while the child enters `--worktree`. One CLI family counts as one; an independent Gemini sharing that home and cwd hides conversation data. The process list is read first and the agent's descendants after it, so a child born between the two is in the family: taken the other way round, a child forked at that moment (a copy of its parent until it execs, which bash's `/bin/sh` on macOS makes for every command of the fake) counted as another Gemini and one look hid the line, the record and the last message until the next job (`a_process_the_agent_starts_during_the_scan_is_not_another_gemini`, through `look_among`'s scan seam). A look that finds nothing still clears the record: an ambiguous folder must not keep answering from an old one. opencode's check for another process holding its database takes the same order and leaves the holder's descendants out; Codex's daemon fallback has no family to leave out (it counts native TUIs only). Different homes are independent. In 0.63.0's source, `--worktree` changes the child's cwd and slug; `--include-directories` adds folders without changing the storage root. Those flags were not checked live.
- Do not inject `--session-id` into issue launches, `start` or resumes. `/clear` and `/resume` change sessions in the same pid, so an exact initial match would still need folder discovery and another lifecycle to abandon that id. The folder rule is smaller and also works for agents started by hand. Two Gemini hide context, resume and last-message data, while their titles still provide status.
- Read complete Gemini lines from an initial tail of at most one MiB, then bounded batches. Replacement, truncation and deletion reset the reader. Keep the newest value of each reply id in conversation order, at most 512 replies, and of each only its model, input tokens and time: tool results and long answers are not held in the server; `$set.messages` replaces the whole array. Follow Gemini's ignored-user-content rule: empty text and trimmed text starting with `/`, `?`, `<session_context>` or `<hook_context>` cannot confirm a prompt or make a session resumable. Also exclude function responses, `<state_snapshot>` and `<scratchpad>`. A Gemini response makes a session resumable, including compression's synthetic acknowledgement, because compaction removed its real prompts. That acknowledgement confirms no send and is not a last message. `Pane` keeps prompt times across message replacement and conversation switches; a new process starts fresh. A live resume restored `$set.messages` with the original times. Use those times, so new metadata cannot confirm a send. Model, usage and last message were checked in an isolated cornercase server.
- Show Gemini's recorded model ID, as for Codex, because there is no stable display-name source. Context use is the newest real reply's `tokens.input / tokenLimit(model)`, rounded and capped: 1,048,576 tokens, or 256,000 for Gemma 4. Do not add output, thoughts, cached or lifetime totals. An `info` record `Model set to <id>` changes the model and clears old usage; `/model set auto` wrote one live. The unsaved picker only changes the footer. Read its `/model` column in the last six screen lines, splitting header and values on two or more spaces and requiring equal cell counts. This handles hidden cwd and trailing context/quota columns without mistaking a branch for a model. Accept only complete Gemini/Gemma IDs or `Auto`; hidden, missing or truncated values fall back to the file. A changed selection clears old usage until a fresh reply. Show `Auto` as `auto` only after an explicit change, then use the reply's routed ID. This footer read only supplies context; it cannot refuse input or invent status. `/clear` follows the new file and clears old usage. Live `/compress` replaced history with synthetic context and a summary without fresh `tokens.input`. Its reported reduction from 10,570 to 505 tokens was not saved, so keep the model and hide the percentage until a real reply rather than estimate it.
- **Grok Build** (`agents/grok/session.rs`, 1.0.52; fixtures in `tests/fixtures/grok/1.0.52`): one discovery job per pane. The job takes the previous session so the tails and the fold survive, and a snapshot keeps the status on screen until the job returns. Home is `GROK_HOME` when set and non-empty, else `HOME/.grok`. The row's cwd is percent-encoded (uppercase hex, RFC 3986 unreserved bytes kept) to find `sessions/<name>/<id>/`. A short directory is accepted when its sibling `.cwd`, or `summary.json` `info.cwd`, is that folder; the hash in the short name is not rebuilt. A live subagent directory was not captured. What was checked is a `session_relationship` other than `primary` in the same events file: a prompt belongs to the turn whose start and end cover its time, so a worker prompt is not a confirmation. A `turn_completed` belongs to the newest turn that started at or before it, including one written a few milliseconds after `turn_ended`, so a worker completion keeps that turn's model and adds tokens only, never the parent's percentage. An immediate child directory that has `updates.jsonl` still adds that file's tokens only; that separate layout was not seen live. The model is `summary.json` `current_model_id`, else the latest `turn_started` `model_id`. The window is `signals.json` `contextWindowTokens`, else `summary.json` `context_window`. The percentage is the newest primary `turn_completed` when `modelCalls` is at most 1, `inputTokens` is within the window, the model matches (a usage record with no model is allowed) and the reply is newer than `auto_compact_completed` or `compaction_checkpoint` and newer than the recorded model or window change (a new `turn_started` of the new model; a reply read in the same poll as that change still counts). A model change that is only in `summary.json`, with no new `turn_started` for it, drops the retained reply and the current `signals.json` reading until a newer reply or a newer signals file, so switching back does not restore the old percentage. An older one-call reply is never used. A normal multi-call turn instead uses `signals.json` `contextTokensUsed` over `contextWindowTokens` when that file's mtime is at or after the reply, `primaryModelId` is the current model, and no compaction or model boundary is newer than the file. It stays hidden when the file is older than the reply or after a boundary. The window is taken from `signals.json` (a live session used 500000, not 256000). `contextWindowUsage`, `compactionCount` (it stayed 0 across a real compact) and `_meta.totalTokens` are not used. Stats split that usage into input minus `cachedReadTokens`, output (not plus `reasoningTokens`), cache read and cache write, once per prompt id. A prompt is a `user_message_chunk` with `promptIndex`, timed from `agentTimestampMs` (else the update's `timestamp`). `_meta.syntheticPrompt` decides when it is present: true is never a prompt and false always is, including when the text begins with `<system-reminder>` or `<user_info>`. That tag check applies only when the field is absent. One submission is `promptIndex` plus that millisecond, so a repeated index still counts again and chunks from the same instant count once. `chat_history.jsonl` is not paired for prompts. It still decides `resumable`, with the last message. On a first read or a reset, the newest human chunk behind the tail is recovered. Only a bounded set of recent prompt ids is kept; counting once is the ledger's job. `?` is a prompt. `/` and `!` are local. There is no survey and no input-box marker. The last message is the newest assistant string, and its time is the `agent_message_chunk` whose text is that string, not a later `turn_ended`. A session is resumable once it has a prompt, an assistant message or `compaction_meta`. Resume is `grok <normalized args> --resume <id>`. `grok --resume` is the flag; `--session-id` starts a new session. `--yolo`, `--always-approve` and `--permission-mode bypassPermissions` normalize to `--always-approve`. There is no built-in plan mode. `Known::usage` is `None`: `grok usage` is per session, not an account plan. Inherited `GROK_SESSION_ID` and `GROK_AGENT` did not join the parent session, so they are not stripped, and neither is `GROK_HOME`. `grok worktree create` did not lock the git worktree. OSC 7501 is not read. Old sessions are not imported into stats.
- **Cursor CLI** (`agents/cursor/store.rs`, `proto.rs`, bundle 2026.10.14-941492e, **from the code, no live store was read**; synthetic fixtures are built by `agents::cursor::fake::FakeCursor`, whose encoder is test-only): the chat store is `<CURSOR_CONFIG_DIR, else $XDG_CONFIG_HOME/cursor, else ~/.cursor>/chats/<md5 of the folder>/<chat id>/store.db`, SQLite in WAL mode with `blobs(id, data)` (content addressed: the id is the lowercase hex SHA-256) and `meta(key, value)`, whose one row `0` is the hex of a JSON with `latestRootBlobId`, `lastUsedModel` and `createdAt`. The TUI is the only writer and is expected to keep the file open, so the pane's chat is the one `store.db` under `chats/<32 hex>/<id>/` that `process::open_files` shows for the foreground pid, and only when there is exactly one; no folder rule is needed, so two Cursor in one folder each follow their own chat (`RECORD_BY_FOLDER` is false). If a live check shows the TUI does not hold the file, the fallback is the folder rule Codex's daemon uses (newest chat of the folder updated since the pid started, exactly one Cursor there), and `RECORD_BY_FOLDER` becomes true. The root blob is protobuf `agent.v1.ConversationStateStructure`: field 5 `token_details` (`1 used_tokens`, `2 max_tokens`, exactly what the TUI shows as context use) and field 8, the turns as blob ids (raw 32 bytes, or 64 hex characters, both taken). A turn blob is `1` an agent turn or `2` a shell (`!`) turn; an agent turn has `1` the user message blob, `2` the step blobs, `7 routed_model_display_name` and `10 user_message_id`; a step is `1` an assistant message (`1 text`, `2 started_at_ms`, `3 completed_at_ms`), `2` a tool call or `3` a thinking message. `proto.rs` is a wire reader of about 80 lines (varints, length-delimited fields, fixed widths skipped, groups and truncation refused) rather than prost and a build step; every malformed, truncated or missing blob gives nothing, never a wrong value, and the fields are named by number only where this document says so. The meta row is read on every poll and the root decoded only when `latestRootBlobId` changes; turn blobs are cached by id and dropped when they leave the list (the newest turn's blob changes id with each step, its message id does not). The model is the newest turn's routed name, else `lastUsedModel` (`default` shows as `Auto`); the percentage is `used_tokens / max_tokens` when the window is positive, and when `lastUsedModel` changes while the root stays the same the percentage is hidden until the next checkpoint, as for Codex. A prompt is a turn whose `user_message_id` (else the user message's own id) was not known: the turns found on a first read are the history and never count, unless the chat was created after the pane first saw the process (`createdAt`, so a `/clear` chat or a new one counts its first turn); the first read is the first one that opens the store, even before it has a root. Its time is the user message's `started_at_ms` when the server filled it, else the moment it was read, which DESIGN allows for prompts ("counts from when it was read"), so a first checkpoint that comes late shows working late and the 10 s confirmation of `send --enter` can fail before Cursor records the prompt. **Token usage per turn is never written to disk** (`TurnEndedUpdate` stays in memory), so Stats counts agents, prompts, turns and time for Cursor and no tokens. The last message is the newest non-empty assistant text of the newest turn that has one (thinking, tool and shell steps skipped), with its completion or start time; the record is the store's path. The transcript is found from `meta.json`'s `cwd`: `<CURSOR_DATA_DIR, else ~/.cursor>/projects/<cwd with every non-alphanumeric run as -, trimmed>/agent-transcripts/<chat id>/…`. The same chat open in two panes shares one store and is a known limitation.
- Codex fake-agent tests use `agents::codex::fake::FakeCodex`, with a shell imitating the npm wrapper's parent/child topology, and no real model calls. `FakeCodex::serve` starts a fake daemon (a `codex app-server` script holding the rollout, not a descendant of the agent, pid in `daemon.pid`) and `FakeCodex::agent` a TUI that is `/bin/sleep` with `argv[0]` `…/codex` (`Sleeper::named`; a symlink would break multi-call coreutils). App tests cover two sessions sharing cwd and Codex home, session/model changes, compaction and cleanup; UI snapshots cover wide and compact lines, including model-only text.

## Memory (`term/memory.rs`, `app/agent_watch.rs`)

- `memory` in `config.json` (Settings → UI, off by default) ends every tab's second line with the memory it uses: `Opus 5.5 · 23% · 1.2 GB`, or `1.2 GB` alone with the model and context off or in a shell tab. Shell tabs got no row at first (agents are what grows, and a row for a shell's few MB doubles every tab's height), but a dev server, a build or a test run in a shell tab grows as much as an agent, and with the switch on every tab gets the row, so the list keeps one rhythm. Off by default: most people never need it, and it costs a thread every 2 s per pane on screen.
- What: the memory each process holds on its own (`process::footprint`), for each pane's shell and its descendants (`process::descendants`, breadth first over `process::children`, at most 256), summed per tab over all its panes: the figure is what the tab costs, so a split shell beside the agent counts too. macOS reads `ri_phys_footprint` (`proc_pid_rusage`, `RUSAGE_INFO_V0`), the figure in Activity Monitor's Memory column and `top`'s MEM; Linux reads `RssAnon` + `VmSwap` from `/proc/<pid>/status`, its anonymous memory including what was swapped out. Neither counts clean file-backed pages (the program, its libraries, mapped files), which every process maps and the system can drop at any time, so the sum over a tree is close to what the agent costs. The two are not identical (footprint also counts compressed and IOKit memory), but both mean memory only that process keeps.
- RSS (`statm`, `pti_resident_size`) was used before and overstated about twice: it counted Claude Code's 224 MB binary once per process (a fresh Claude showed ~440 MB against ~200 MB of footprint; a session with five `npx` MCP servers 1.26 GB against 0.79 GB), and it fell when macOS compressed an idle agent although the memory was still taken. PSS (`smaps_rollup`) was rejected because the kernel walks every mapping; `status` only reads counters. `process::tests::footprint` measures the test process itself: reading a mapped 64 MiB file must not count and writing 64 MiB must (the only check that catches a constant), written to an anonymous mapping of its own rather than a `Vec`, since macOS' malloc hands back pages another test freed, which were counted already, so other tests could disturb it only by allocating or freeing half of that within the same milliseconds. The mapping needs `rustix`'s `mm` feature, enabled in `[dev-dependencies]` only.
- When: `App::watch_agents` (every refresh) calls `term::memory::Pane::update` for the panes whose tabs are on screen, while the switch is on: the active project's, or in the tree every unfolded workspace of an unfolded project in an open group (`App::drawn` tells which layout was last drawn). A collapsed workspace's tabs are off screen, so they are skipped in the lists too (tabs listed) as in the tree. The sum runs on a thread every `term::memory::MEASURE_EVERY` (2 s), one job in flight per pane, like Codex discovery, so a deep tree never blocks the server loop. Panes not on screen keep their last figure, so switching projects shows an old number for one refresh instead of making the row jump. The tree's gate is tested (two open projects are both measured); that hidden panes are skipped is not, since skipped work cannot be observed without timing.
- `ui::workspaces::memory_size`: binary units, whole MB below 1024 MB, then GB with one decimal. In a narrow column the model is cut first, then dropped (`MIN_MODEL_WIDTH`); the percentage and the memory stay. At the default width (26) with both on, a two-digit percentage leaves no room for the model.

## Plan usage (`agents/usage/`, `agents/{claude,codex}/usage.rs`, `app/usage_dialog.rs`)

- ` Usage ` sits under ` Settings ` (wide: its own row; the workspaces column puts ` Files ` on that row under ` Issues ` and ` TODO ` right-aligned under ` Changes `, and keeps a blank row level with ` Quit `, so its separator and ` Issues ` stay level; compact: the footer is settings | usage | quit in thirds). It opens `Overlay::Usage` on Stats (see Stats); the Limits tab has the plan probes, and ` done `, `Esc` or `Enter` close it.
- One section per agent (`Known::ALL` entries with a `usage::Spec`: Claude Code, Codex), in that order (Cursor CLI has none either: its plan limits are not a command-line interface cornercase uses; opencode has none: it uses whichever providers the user set up, its own Zen and Go subscriptions among them, each billed apart, and cornercase asks none of them for usage; Grok Build has none either: `grok usage` accounts for one session and does not answer while that session is running, and cornercase does not read its credentials), each with its title and plan, its status on the right (`loading…` or `updated 2m ago`), its error under the title and its bars: thin `━` lines in the severity's colour on a track of the same line in the line colour. Only agents whose command (`agent_commands`, else the name) is an executable file, by path or on the server's `PATH` (`agents::installed`), get one; when neither is, both show, so each says why it could not run. A modal taller than the screen is cut at the bottom, the ` done ` row stays.
- Gemini CLI has no usage section. API-key sessions have no plan limits; Google-login `/stats` (`/usage`) calls Code Assist's `retrieveUserQuota` with OAuth. There is no usable CLI probe: headless `/stats` omits the non-text quota report, and ACP exposes sessions rather than account usage. Reading or refreshing Gemini's credentials ourselves is rejected for the same reason as Claude Code's. Use Gemini's own `/stats` instead.
- **Claude** (`agents/claude/usage.rs`): Claude Code's SDK control protocol: `claude -p --setting-sources '' --no-session-persistence --input-format stream-json --output-format stream-json --verbose`, then an `initialize` and a `get_usage` control request on stdin; the `control_response` for `get_usage` holds `subscription_type`, `rate_limits_available` and `rate_limits` (`limits[]` with `kind`, `percent`, `severity`, `resets_at`, `scope.model.display_name`; `spend` for extra usage). No prompt, no tokens, ~3 s. Claude handles OAuth (credentials file, Keychain, refresh), so cornercase never reads or refreshes a token; `GET /api/oauth/usage` was rejected for that (and refreshing the token ourselves can log Claude out). `--setting-sources ''` keeps the user's hooks from firing, `--no-session-persistence` keeps the probe out of the session list, `--bare` does not work (API key only).
- The protocol is not documented as a CLI interface, so parsing is lenient (unknown fields and kinds pass, a missing `limits[]` falls back to `five_hour` / `seven_day`, an unknown `severity` comes from the percentage), but `rate_limits_available` must be there, and `rate_limits` too when it is true, so a changed answer shows as unavailable instead of "no plan limits". Every failure shows `usage unavailable: <reason>`.
- **Codex** (`agents/codex/usage.rs`, checked live on 0.160.0): `codex app-server` (stdio JSON-RPC, lines without the `jsonrpc` field): `initialize` with `clientInfo`, the `initialized` notification, then `account/rateLimits/read` (`excludeResetCreditDetails`, so it skips a second backend call). ~1 s, no Codex thread is created, no daemon starts (the stdio server is the probe's child and dies with it), no model call; Codex reads its own login from `CODEX_HOME`. The answer has `rateLimits` (`planType`, `primary` / `secondary` with `usedPercent`, `windowDurationMins`, `resetsAt` in Unix seconds, `credits`) and `rateLimitsByLimitId` (one per metered bucket, `codex` first, others suffixed with their `limitName` or id). Windows are named by their length, never by their slot: 300 minutes is `session (5h)`, 10080 is `week`. Unix-second resets need no date parsing. Not logged in answers the error `codex account authentication required to read rate limits`; it becomes `Report::notice` (a grey `not signed in · run codex login`, not a red error), so a Codex installed once and never used does not look broken. The rollouts' `token_count` events also carry `rate_limits`, but only as of the last turn (a window that reset since still shows its old percentage), so they were rejected.
- **The view is built in `app`** (#193): `usage` keeps the probes' state and reads it through `State::shown`, `Probe::last`/`loading`/`error` and `Known::title` and `usage::Spec::no_limits`; `app/usage_dialog.rs` turns them into `ui::UsageSection`s and `App::usage_view` builds the `ui::Usage` once, so `usage` imports no `ui`. The tests of the built sections live beside that code and reach the probe fixtures through `agents::claude::usage::tests` and `agents::codex::usage::tests`, `pub(crate)` under `cfg(test)`.
- `usage::Spec` holds the command builder, request producer, answer parser and no-limits text supplied by the agent folder. `usage::State` keeps one probe per `Known`, indexed by its position in `Known::ALL`; only entries with a spec are shown or run. `AppEvent::Usage` carries that same identity.
- On demand only: showing the Limits tab starts one probe per shown agent on a thread (never two for one agent at once) that answers with `AppEvent::Usage(agent, result)`; nothing polls. The last answer per agent stays in memory (not in the session file) and shows with its age while a new one loads. Probes run on the server's machine with `agent_commands`, are killed after `agents::usage::TIMEOUT` (10 s; `App::usage_timeout` in tests); Claude's strips `agents::claude::CLAUDE_SESSION_ENV` like `Term::spawn` and inherits `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, Codex's inherits `CODEX_HOME`.
- Reset times are relative (`resets in 2h 13m`, `3d 4h`): local time would need a time zone database, and the server may sit in another zone than the client anyway.

## Stats (`agents/stats.rs`, `agents/known.rs`, `app/agent_watch.rs`, `app/usage_dialog.rs`)

- Per-agent arrays use the position in `Known::ALL`, whose names and order are pinned by a test. New variants go last. PR 2 leaves the four-entry schema and serialization unchanged; accepting other lengths belongs to PR 4.

- Counts begin with this feature and only for Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, opencode, Grok Build and Cursor CLI in this server's panes (Cursor has no token counts, see Context use). No histories are imported. Grok Build and Cursor CLI are last in `Known::ALL`, so a stats file written before them still loads. `Stats` takes wall times and durations, with fixed times in its unit tests; `App::watch_agents` supplies the wall clock and the `Instant` delta. A start is a changed `(kind, foreground pid)` in a pane, suppressed while the pane is automatically resuming. Start identity is kept separately from the previous activity sample, as one last `(kind, pid)` per pane. An absence does not clear it: Claude's foreground editor must not count as another start when Claude returns. A different pair replaces it, so reused pids following another agent count. Closing panes has no dedicated counting lifecycle; retaining a small numeric entry per pane opened is simpler than adding one. The context pane remembers the first observation even before its record exists. Manual resumes start a process and count. Working excludes `Activity::Shell`, as keeping awake does. A turn counts working or waiting → idle, done or shell. Waiting and unseen done accumulate `Status::needs_you` time.
- Intervals belong to the previous sample's state, ending at the new read: finishing must not discard the final half second of work, and entering a waiting state must not charge that preceding work to waiting. No duration is charged on first observation or across a changed process. Each interval is capped at two seconds. Integer milliseconds preserve the half-second samples without float drift; command JSON reports fractional seconds, while share links floor seconds. Busy time is global time with any matching previous sample working; removed or replaced processes cannot extend it, keeping busy time within summed agent time; peak parallelism is a maximum of current working samples. The three windows are current epoch hour plus 23 or 167 preceding hours, and a separate lifetime total. Records use their own timestamp's hour; samples use the read's wall-clock hour. Per-kind counters hold starts, prompts, turns, working/waiting milliseconds and four token kinds; busy time and peak are global.
- `agents/context/counting.rs::Ledger` keeps each conversation's prompt high-water time and recent ids, reply high-water time and last maximum token values per id, bounded to 512 of each. It stays in the context pane across conversation switches, `/clear`, `/resume`, truncation and replacement, and is reset only when the agent process changes. Full id maps are retained for only the 16 conversations most recently used by nonempty record batches. Older conversations keep only prompt/reply cutoff times and a cumulative time/token baseline; returning seeds these cutoffs, so old records cannot count again. This bounds expensive id storage while preserving counting once across arbitrarily many conversation switches; compact numeric cutoffs remain until the process exits. Evicted records older than the high-water time cannot be counted again. An eviction also records a cutoff that excludes unknown ids at that same timestamp: with more than 512 ids sharing one instant, preferring an undercount to a replay double count keeps bounded memory and the counting-once guarantee. Records without a parseable time, and those before first observing the process, contribute nothing. Confirmation retains its existing permissive rule for missing times. Claude's shell input is excluded from stats while it can still confirm a send. Typed slash commands count, but enqueue records only confirm a send. Claude Code records a queued human prompt either as an absorbed `attachment` (`queued_command`, `commandMode: prompt`, `origin.kind: human`) or, after dequeue, a human `user` record. Stats counts those final records by their uuid and timestamp, never the enqueue, task notifications or peer origins. The synthetic queued-prompts fixture covers both paths, each once. Confirmation and counting use the same parsed JSON value, keeping their different rules explicit. A substring gate before parsing accepts usage, origin, queued-command attachments, queue operations, slash commands, shell input and compact boundaries. Ordinary tool results, inputs without usage and metadata skip JSON allocation, including on a resumed pane's initial MiB. The subagent job uses the same gate; the context marker also skips assistant lines without usage.
- Every reader produces only timestamp/id/numeric observations during its existing pass. Gemini and opencode still do their reading on their existing background jobs. Claude now shares Codex and Gemini's bounded `jsonl::Tail`, keeping deletion and truncation behavior; its subagent files use the same tail on one background job per pane, so enumerating or reading many Task transcripts cannot block the server loop. Claude main and subagent transcripts keep separate conversation ledgers. The subagent directory is re-listed only when its modification time or path changes. A transcript modified before the pane began starts at EOF, including skipping any old partial line, so a resumed conversation does not read a MiB of every old Task file; subsequent complete appended records still count. Subagents and old `isSidechain` records add tokens only, never prompts or sampled activity. These transient ids and paths never enter `stats.json`, a share link or the stats log.
- Token semantics were checked against installed Claude Code 2.1.296, Gemini CLI 0.63.0 and opencode 1.18.35 sources, and read-only numeric record shapes. Claude repeats the same usage per `message.id` across content blocks and writes Task transcripts under `projects/<dir>/<sessionId>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl`; each id contributes only positive changes. Its output already includes thinking. Codex counts cumulative `total_token_usage` differences, with `last_token_usage` only when no preceding baseline was read. Repeats add zero; a lower total starts a new baseline. Input excludes cached input; output includes reasoning. opencode's installed accounting subtracts `reasoningTokens` from stored `tokens.output` (and its newer usage object exposes `visibleOutputTokens` with the same subtraction): add stored output and reasoning. Count a completed id only once, at `time.completed`. Its numeric records also showed total = input + output + reasoning + cache kinds. Subagent sessions remain excluded.
- Gemini's installed `recordMessageTokens` maps `input` to `promptTokenCount`, `cached` to `cachedContentTokenCount`, `output` to `candidatesTokenCount`, `thoughts` to `thoughtsTokenCount`, and `tool` to `toolUsePromptTokenCount`. Cached input is part of input: inspected numeric records had total = input + output + thoughts, with substantial cached values, and [the API's usage definition](https://googleapis.github.io/js-genai/release_docs/interfaces/types.UsageMetadata.html) describes cached content as part of the prompt. `tool` is separate API tool-use prompt usage, not generated output or the CLI's local function-response text. It is omitted in v1 as specified: no extra invented input category. A rewritten reply adds only increases over its last values; a `$set.messages` restore retains deduplication state and cannot add old history. Synthetic versioned fixtures record these shapes without copying conversation text.
- `stats.json` lives next to `session.json`, including private instances selected by `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`. Version 1 is compact JSON: `since` in Unix seconds, a separate total, and sparse `hours` arrays `[epoch_hour, [claude, codex, gemini, opencode], busy_ms, most_at_once]`. Each agent's array is `[agents, prompts, turns, agent_ms, waited_ms, input, output, cache_read, cache_write]`. The total uses named counters for inspection; hours use arrays to keep months of data small. Keep 26 weeks and one day, pruning only when the epoch hour changes; snapshots also filter retention. Missing, corrupt or unknown versions begin empty. The per-agent arrays hold `Known::ALL.len()` entries (`Bucket::by_agent` and the hours' lists): reading pads a shorter list with zeros and ignores entries past the last known agent, while writing is unchanged and `version` stays 1, so adding an agent last in `Known::ALL` never makes serde reject the file and start over empty. Tests cover a file with every agent written back byte for byte, one with an agent fewer and one with an agent more. Every numeric mutation increments `Stats::revision`. `agents::stats::Saver` observes only that number until a minute has passed from the first dirty instant, then builds one `Saved` directly for writing; it keeps no cloned saved or pending buckets. Shutdown and restart flush the current changed revision. This removes full-map serialization, comparison and cloning from the half-second server save steps. Shared `persist::write` atomically renames a temporary file, writes compact private files at 0600 and syncs them; secrets reuse it too. The ordinary session and TODO savers retain their quiet-time policy.
- Usage opens on Stats and remembers its page only for the server run. Limits probes run only when that tab shows, including reopening there. The tab row reuses settings' geometry and drawing. Tab, BackTab and horizontal arrows switch pages; the simulation's client routes Tab and Shift+Tab to Usage instead of letting browser focus take them; Enter, Esc, done and a press outside close the dialog. Height is the larger of the limits body and the actual stats lines at the dialog width, plus tabs and buttons, clipped to the screen while keeping done. Stats ends with one blank line separating its content from share/done, including the empty state, as in Limits and other dialogs. Computing height from the same lines used for drawing removes the three unused wide rows while accounting for wrapped phone headlines and legends. Below 49 screen columns the dialog takes the full width, with the usual one-cell padding inside each border, leaving 42 text columns at 46 columns. Labels of 18 and three seven-column values then have explicit one-column gaps, so full durations cannot run together. Narrow legends wrap to a second line. Shared stats number formats drive the dialog and plain command output; counts below 10000 stay whole, larger thousands keep one decimal, tokens use k/M/B, and durations use minutes or hours/minutes.
- Heatmap blocks are the preceding 24-hour buckets from the current hour, newest at the bottom right, up to 26 seven-block columns. They are rolling blocks, not weekdays. The quartile boundaries are the nearest-rank 25th, 50th and 75th percentiles of positive displayed blocks; ties share their level. Every one of the 182 blocks is drawn, in the dialog, `cornercase stats` and the card: a block with no agent time takes the line colour, also before counting began. Those used to be blank, so a server counting for a day showed two loose squares in an empty frame instead of a grid with two days lit, which read as broken; a block before `since` holds no work, which is what grey says, and `counting for n days` says how long the numbers go back. The share map carries the blocks that overlap the counting period, the first incomplete day and the block containing `since` included, so `min(days + 1, 182)` levels, while `days` remains whole counting days; the page draws the rest as zeros. This deliberately changes the issue's `min(days, 182)` contract: hiding the first day and the oldest partial block omitted work already included in the counters. Rust, the strict site parser and the shared fixture enforce the revised contract. `days` reads `1 day` in the singular. Dark purples are 60, 97, 141, 99, ending in brand purple; light purples are 189, 141, 99, 55, ending darker so the maximum remains visible. Snapshots cover both. The site's term renderer draws U+25A0 as a snapped square with equal sides, independent of font fallback.
- `cornercase stats` requests live memory without starting a server; with none running, it loads the file. JSON includes every window counter and every kind, plus 182 blocks newest first. Text uses the same formatting and `·░▒▓█`, written through the existing closed-pipe-safe output function. Unknown-command refusals retain the normal old-server behavior. `--share` and the Stats share pill use the same pure link builder; the pill copies with OSC 52 and a toast only after something was counted.
- Sharing is opt-in copying, never uploading. The constant URL is `https://usecornercase.dev/stats/#v2`; fields are `24h`, `7d`, `all` (agents, prompts, whole agent seconds, whole busy seconds, peak, output tokens, turns, whole waited seconds, input tokens as the dialog's `tokens in` counts them (uncached input plus cache writes), cache reads), whole counting `days`, and `map`. The card shows the Stats tab's nine rows in its order (`ROWS` in `model.ts`, which the simulation's table also uses) for the window picked on the page; v1 carried only the first six numbers, and a card with five of the dialog's nine rows looked like something was missing. v2 appends the four rather than reordering, so v1 is its prefix: a v1 link, made by 0.12.36 to 0.12.85, still parses and draws the five rows it holds, while each version must carry exactly its count of numbers. v3 (#293) appends an eleventh number, the merged pull requests (see Merged pull requests), and is made only when that count is complete, so the card has 9 or 10 rows; the simulation's `EXAMPLE` carries the eleventh number. Pack three bits per level, newest first, most significant bit first within each byte; unused trailing bits are zero. Base64url has no padding. The shared fixture contains an exact link, its windows and nonzero levels, checked by Rust and the site. Agent breakdowns, models, project names and paths are omitted. Anyone with the link sees its numbers.
- `/stats/` is an English static page, with no analytics or third-party script. Its strict whole-fragment expression, 1024-character cap (thirty numbers of up to sixteen digits fit), safe integer checks, exact decoded map size, legal levels, canonical base64url and zero trailing bits reject crafted links before drawing. No text goes through HTML. CSP permits only self scripts/styles/fonts, self/blob/data images and no network connections. The fragment never reaches the host. No fragment shows an explicitly marked example. The default card window is seven days, with tabs for 24 h and total. The card is a ticket (next bullet). Font loading completes before drawing. Every stats-page mount gets a generation number: font and mask awaits, drawing and action callbacks discard stale generations so a slow old fragment cannot overwrite the current ticket, handlers or status. File-share support is probed with an empty PNG-typed File only when the share button exists; the PNG is rendered and encoded on click, keeping initial mounts free of needless image encoding. PNG download, supported clipboard and file sharing run entirely in the browser. Intent links carry the public share URL; the written post text is visible for copying, and the page tells users to attach the PNG because crawlers cannot read fragments. `stats-og.png` is a 1200×630 example: the same render with `?preview`, whose stage is 630 high.
- **The card is a ticket that moves** (`site/src/lib/stats/`: `ticket.ts` what it says, `face.ts` drawing it, `sheen.ts` the foil and the light, `pose.ts` the 3D matrices, `stage.ts` the PNG, `tilt.ts` the live page): the first card was a flat picture of the Stats tab's terminal box, and it was asked to be more visual, in 3D and moving, like the conference tickets people post. The face is drawn once on a canvas at 2×, 1000×520: the logo; the window in a pill, which also says `EXAMPLE` (in yellow) on the example and the link previews; the parallel factor as the hero, shrunk to fit beside `in parallel` and the agent and busy times, or `no agent work` below a minute of busy time; the Stats tab's rows in its order, five to a line, their columns sized by their widest label or value, values shrinking before columns meet; the counting days. Past a perforation with two notches, the stub holds the 182 heatmap blocks as 7 columns by 26 rows of weeks, oldest at the top, so the newest block stays at the bottom right as in the app and the blocks read like text, with a caption on its edge instead of a legend, and the address. The ramp is the ticket's own (dark violet to light lavender, the top level glowing), not the terminal's palette indices, since nothing here is a terminal.
  - **One matrix, two renderers**: a pose (rotations, lift, scale) and the light give one 4×4 matrix with CSS's conventions (`rotateX`, `rotateY`, `rotateZ`, `perspective`; `css` in `pose.ts` writes it as `matrix3d`). The page sets it on the ticket, its halo and its shadow inside a 1200×675 stage scaled to the column; the PNG projects the same face through the same matrix as a 40×20 mesh of affine triangles, each grown half a pixel against seams. So the ticket at rest on the page is the PNG, and a test checks that the matrix CSS gets is the one the canvas draws with. WebGL was rejected for the PNG, since a context can be missing or blocked by privacy settings while the mesh is exact enough at this distance; one affine transform (no foreshortening) made the ticket read as skewed rather than tilted.
  - **Foil**: two layers masked to what is printed in foil, the masks being canvases turned into blob URLs (CSP allows blob images): `iris` on the hero, multiplied over its white-to-lavender fill with the brand's colours, since a colour dodge on white text only gives white; and `edge` on the border and the pill, a colour dodge of the full rainbow. Their gradients repeat every 520 pixels, so each layer is the ticket plus one period, moved with `transform` only; the light sets the phase. The canvas rebuilds CSS's gradient line and repetition, so the page and the PNG agree. A soft-light spot and streak follow the light.
  - **Motion**: a mouse or a pen over the stage presses the side under it away and brings the light there, and so does a touch drag (`touch-action: pan-y` keeps vertical scrolling); otherwise the ticket drifts slowly around its rest pose. A spring follows the targets. Frames run only while the stage is on screen, and `prefers-reduced-motion` gets the rest pose, still. The PNG is always the rest pose with the light at rest, 2400×1350.
  - Left for later: a video of the ticket moving (MediaRecorder gives MP4 only in some browsers, and X refuses WebM), a name on the ticket (the link carries none, and adding one is a new link version), and tilting by the phone's gyroscope (iOS asks a permission for it).

## Keeping awake (`agents/awake.rs`, `app/agent_watch.rs`)

- **`keep_awake`** (`config.json`, Settings → Agents, off by default; #160): while at least one agent works, the server holds the system's lock against idle sleep, and lets it go once none has worked for `agents::awake::KEEP_FOR` (30 s), so agents that end and start turns back to back do not take and drop it every few seconds. An agent left working used to stop with the machine: its process froze, its API call or build timed out, and the way around it was `caffeinate` or `systemd-inhibit` by hand. Off by default: it costs battery, and an update should not change how a laptop sleeps. Claude Code already keeps a Mac awake while it works (it runs `caffeinate -i -t 300` itself while it works; seen on 2.1.296), so the setting matters for Codex, opencode and Gemini CLI, which do not, and on Linux; it is kept for every agent rather than per agent, since one rule is easier to explain and the two locks do not conflict.
- **What counts as working** (`App::keep_awake`, after `watch_agents` on every refresh): `Status::Working` without a background shell, the same rule as `--until turn-over` and `restart --when-idle`, so a dev server an agent left running does not keep the machine up all night. Waiting does not count (the agent has no request in flight); agents cornercase cannot read never hold it; opencode never reports waiting, so one at a permission prompt holds it until it is answered.
- **Only idle sleep is blocked**: the display still turns off, the screen still locks, and sleep chosen on purpose (the menu, the lid) still happens. Keeping a Mac awake with its lid closed needs root (`pmset disablesleep`) or clamshell mode and stays out. It is the server's machine that stays awake; with `cornercase remote` the laptop showing the window may still sleep and reconnects when it wakes.
- **How**: no crate and no `unsafe`: the server spawns the system's own tool (`agents::awake::tools`) and kills it to let go. macOS: `caffeinate -i -w <server pid>` (a `PreventUserIdleSystemSleep` assertion, also on battery; `-w` ends it with the server). Linux: `gnome-session-inhibit --inhibit suspend … cat` first when the session bus is reachable, since gnome-settings-daemon only reads gnome-session's own inhibitors, never logind's locks; then `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep:idle --mode=block … cat`, then `--what=idle`, which polkit allows from an SSH session where it refuses the `sleep` block lock, and which still holds back logind's `IdleAction`. The tool's stdin is a pipe the server holds, so when the server dies, even from `SIGKILL`, `cat` reads its end and exits and the lock goes with it.
- **Failures**: a tool that cannot start (`not installed`) or exits while it is wanted (a refusal: polkit, no gnome-session) gives way to the next one, its last stderr line kept as the reason; when none is left the lock is unavailable until the setting is turned off and on again (or the server restarts), with one `WARN` in the log, so a machine that cannot hold it does not retry and log every turn. After a normal release the next take starts from the tool that worked. The settings row's note says whether the lock is held and by which tool, or why it cannot be taken (`agents::awake::State::note`); `status --json` has `keep_awake` (`held`, `by`, `unavailable`) while the setting is on; `server.log` says when it is taken (with how many agents work) and released.
- **Rejected**: IOKit (`IOPMAssertionCreateWithName`) would name `cornercase` in `pmset -g assertions` instead of `caffeinate`, at the cost of CoreFoundation FFI; `caffeinate` is enough. Insomnia (a macOS menu bar app) was looked at as an alternative: it keeps a Mac awake with its lid closed through a `sudoers` rule for `pmset` and stops other apps with `SIGSTOP`, which is more than a terminal should ask of its users, and it is a Swift app, not a library. Holding the lock only on power (`caffeinate -s`, `/sys/class/power_supply`) and a mark in the window while it is held were left for later: the settings row, `status` and the log say it.
- Not checked live: GNOME, KDE Plasma (whether PowerDevil honours logind's lock; if not, `org.freedesktop.PowerManagement.Inhibit`), and a server started over SSH. Tests use fake tools: scripts that record their arguments and `exec cat`, or refuse like polkit (`agents::awake::tests`), and the fake Claude Code working and finishing (`app::tests::control_requests::agents::keeping_awake`).

## Flows (`flows/`, `app/flow_runs.rs`, `app/flow_panel.rs`, `app/flows_dialog.rs`, `app/start_flow_dialog.rs`, `app/control/flow.rs`, `ui/flow.rs`, `ui/flow/`, `ui/dialog/flows.rs`, `ui/dialog/start_flow.rs`, #263)

- **Why**: a team of agents (one coding, others reviewing, testing, auditing, one orchestrating) already worked through the commands for scripts (`start`, `send --enter`, `wait --any --until turn-over`, `read --last-message`), and much of cornercase was built that way, but every run was set up by hand: each agent with the right CLI, model and mode, a long brief for the orchestrator with the pane ids and the rules learned the hard way, and no way to keep a team that worked. Nothing showed how the work moved between them either. A **flow** is a saved team and the plan it follows; starting one opens the team in a new worktree with an orchestrator that runs the plan, and the **Flow** panel draws the plan and lights it up as the work moves.
- **The orchestrator is an agent**, not a workflow engine: it reads what the others answer and decides what comes next, and cornercase never parses a verdict out of an agent's text. A deterministic pipeline was rejected: it only fits straight flows, and a loop ("back to the coder while the reviewer finds problems") needs judgement.
- **The model** (`flows/mod.rs`): `Flow { name, description, notes, end, orchestrator, roles, stages }`; a `Stage` holds one role or several in parallel and may go back (`Back { to, when, at_most }`, `to` counted from 1, `when` the loop's label); `End` is a pull request or the commits. Structured so it can be drawn before anything runs: free text alone was the first idea, and nothing could be drawn from it. `Flow::problems` gives sentences, shared by the editor, the start dialog and the commands (names, known agents, roles in stages, loops only to earlier stages, `env` keys, and the rules below). **A role works in one stage**: progress and the diagram resolve a role to its stage, so `coder → reviewer → coder` could never reach its third stage; a loop is how a role works again, and occurrences of a role were rejected. Files are TOML (long prompts read well in a repository): yours in `flows/` next to `config.json`, the project's in `.cornercase/flows/`, the built-ins compiled in. A file is named after its flow's slug and renamed with it, one that cannot be read is listed with its reason rather than dropped, and a newer `version` is refused.
- **Several roles on one agent**: roles work in the run's one worktree unless one asks for a worktree of its own (below), and cornercase finds some agents' records by their folder rather than their pid: Codex when its app-server daemon writes the rollouts, Gemini CLI by its home and folder, opencode by its database (Context use). Two of one kind in one worktree hide each other's records. Each agent folder states both sides (`Known::record_by_folder`: Codex, Gemini CLI, opencode; `Known::status_by_folder`: opencode alone). Codex and Gemini CLI still take several roles: their status and waits come from their titles, so only `read --last-message` fails for them, and the brief tells the orchestrator to read such a member's screen instead (`read --lines 300`): an LLM reads a screen well enough, where a parser of cornercase's would not. Their `send --enter` cannot be confirmed from a hidden record either, so it returns once Enter is pressed when the agent finds its record by folder, has none, and another pane of this server runs the same agent in the same folder; a lone fresh Codex keeps the confirmation. opencode's status itself comes from its database, so its waits would fail: `Flow::problems` refuses it in several roles of one worktree, and the built-ins move a repeated opencode to another installed agent; roles with a worktree of their own do not count (opencode can take one per worktree). A per-role home (`CODEX_HOME`, `GEMINI_CLI_HOME`, `OPENCODE_DB`) would lift all of it and is left for later, since Codex and Gemini keep their login there. A worktree per role for every flow was rejected: members would stop seeing each other's uncommitted work, and each worktree builds from scratch. It is a choice per role instead (#313, below).
- **Cursor CLI in flows** (#290): it can take several roles, since its record is found by the store its own process holds open and its status by title and transcript, neither of which another Cursor in the folder hides (if a live check shows the store is not held, `RECORD_BY_FOLDER` becomes true and `known/tests.rs` and this paragraph change with it). Its unattended arguments are `--force --trust`, it has no effort switch (effort is part of the model id) and no model list (`--list-models` needs the network).
- **Effort per role** (added at the user's request, as pstack does per role): `Role::effort`, a pick of the agent's own levels in the editor, and `start --effort`. Each agent folder states its switch (`EFFORT`, `Known::effort`): Claude Code `--effort` (low to max), Codex the config key `-c model_reasoning_effort=` (minimal to xhigh), Grok Build `--reasoning-effort` (none to max, from its README; its per-model ids such as `deep` are not offered); Gemini CLI and opencode have none, and an effort set on them is a problem. Like the model, a role's effort replaces every spelling of one in `agent_args` (other `-c` settings stay), so no flag is given twice; without one, the role's own `args` beat the settings'. The diagram's boxes and the brief show it (`claude · opus · high`).
- **Built-in flows** (Full issue, Cross review, Quick, Bug test first, Arena) ship with cornercase, so improvements reach everyone with each update, and **can be edited**: one opens like any other flow, and saving it writes your version under the same name into your folder, where it takes the built-in's place (tagged `edited`) and stops getting updates. For it, ` restore default ` asks first, deletes your file and unhides the name, so the built-in comes back (`Action::Restore`, not a plain delete: a built-in that is also deleted must not vanish). Restore is a delete under another name on purpose: there is nothing else to undo, and the built-in improves with updates where your copy does not.
  - **Built-in flows can be deleted** (#322): they live in the binary, not in a file, so deleting one **hides** it by name (`hidden_flows` in `config.json`, `Vec<String>` compared with `store::same_name`, trimmed and ignoring case): your choice, kept across updates, which a file in `flows/` would not be (it would shadow the built-in as an edit). A built-in added by a later release still shows, and a renamed one comes back. ` delete ` on a built-in asks, then hides it; on an edited one it deletes your file and hides the built-in too (` restore default ` stays beside it, and the `Delete` key asks the delete question, since a keypress should not pick the gentler one). `store::list` takes the hidden names and moves those built-ins out of `Listing::flows` into `Listing::hidden`; `Listing::builtins` keeps **every** built-in name, so a deleted one stays reserved: `unique()` never gives its name to a duplicate or an import, `share::collision` refuses it, and a project's flow named like it gets the red problem `the deleted built-in … keeps its name` and does not start (`store::conflict` takes the built-in names). If yours has a deleted built-in's name it is listed (tagged `edited`) and the built-in is in neither list. `flow run` and `flow export NAME` refuse a deleted built-in's name, even when a project's flow has it, and say where to bring it back; `flow list` omits it, and no request or contract changed. A run already going keeps its flow: the session holds it. The list ends with a muted, foldable entry `▸ deleted built-ins (n)` (click or `Enter` to expand; each hidden flow muted with its diagram and a ` restore ` button, no question since it is not destructive). `flow delete` and `flow restore` commands were left out: they would be two new request names, and `hidden_flows` is already the scriptable way. They were read only at first (duplicate one to change it), until the user asked to change them in place after trying flows. They are built from the installed agents (`builtin::for_config`): the default agent orchestrates and codes, and reviewers take other installed agents, so a review comes from another provider when there is one; then the agents are given out under the rule above, roles that edit first and the orchestrator last. No model is named: each agent's own default.
- **Agents never ask** (the user's decision): every role starts in its agent's mode that skips permission prompts (each folder's `UNATTENDED`), since a team that stops to ask defeats the point and the orchestrator must not answer another agent's prompt. A run's launches answer the trust prompt whatever `accept_trust_prompts` says (`launch::Spec::trusted`). The dialogs said `N agents run without asking for permission` in red at first; the user had it removed, since it is the premise of flows and the docs keep the caution. What stays red is what a flow could not show otherwise: a project's `env` and `args`, the not-a-repository line, the flow's problems and Claude Code's consent below. Its limits:
  - **Claude Code's one-time Bypass Permissions consent is the user's**: the launch recognises the screen (`agents::claude::screen::bypass_consent`, whitespace ignored, since a narrow pane wraps it) and parks there like an unanswered trust prompt, with a toast and a desktop notification naming the role; the orchestrator, which starts after its members, waits. Typing the prompt into it could pick "No, exit".
  - **A project's flow comes with a cloned repository** and would run its own `env` and `args` unattended: both dialogs spell them out in red for each role (`flows::dialog::project_risks`), and a project's flow never stands in for yours or a built-in one of the same name (see Commands below).
