- **Starting a run** (`App::start_run`, `open_run`): refused before anything opens on the flow's problems, an agent not on the server's `PATH` (it would type a command that fails), an empty task or branch, a project that is not a repository root or is closing, or a branch that already has, or is starting, a run in that project. One worktree per run, made by the existing worktree job and shared by the command and the window; a role can ask for a worktree of its own (#313, below).
  - **The branch**: when nobody chose one (`flow run` without `--worktree`, or the start dialog's branch still the one it derived), cornercase takes the slug of the task's first eight words (`flows::branch`), or that slug with four hex digits when it is empty (a non-Latin task), reserved (`main`, `master`, origin's `HEAD`, the branch of the project's own folder) or taken (by a workspace, a local branch read from `refs/heads` and `packed-refs` without git, or a run starting on it), so two runs of one task never share one. A branch someone chose is refused only when it is the project folder's or a default branch; a workspace on it is reused when it is a linked worktree with no run.
  - **All tabs or none**: one tab per role, named after it, orchestrator first. Every shell is spawned before a tab is added, and a workspace the worktree hand-off added is removed again, so a failure leaves nothing half open. A run never opens in a project or workspace that is closing or being removed, checked again when git answers: the team's shells would keep a closing project open, or work in a folder git is deleting.
  - **The orchestrator starts last** (`Launch::after`: its launch waits while a member's is going) and its brief begins with a `wait --all … --until turn-over`. Both: starting last means every pane it addresses has its agent in front; the wait covers members still in their first turn. The instruction alone was rejected: a wait on a pane whose agent has not started fails, and a model may skip it.
  - Members are in `App::runs` as soon as the tabs exist, so the panel draws the run from the first frame. A run ends when its workspace goes or none of its panes is left.
- **A worktree of its own (#313)**: Arena-like flows have coders that compete, and in one worktree they overwrite each other, which is why a role can ask for its own: `Role::worktree` is `Worktree::Shared` (the default, left out of the file) or `Own`, a pick in the editor's role tab (not on the orchestrator) and `worktree = "own"` in TOML. Models and pure rules are in `flows/`; the job and the tabs are in `app/flow_runs.rs`.
  - **File version 2, only when it is needed**: `VERSION` is 2, but `to_text` writes `version = 2` only when a role is own (`Flow::file_version`), else 1, so every flow without one stays readable by older cornercase and the built-ins' site copy only gained Arena. `Flow.version` defaults to 1 when a file has none, and `store::parse` refuses a file with an own role under version 2 with the reason: an older cornercase would ignore the unknown key and run the coders in one worktree, the opposite of what the file says. After the check the flow is normalised to `VERSION` in memory, so a round trip is equal.
  - **Branches and folders**: `flows::role_branch(run, role)` is `<run>--<slug of the role>` (24 characters, `role` when the slug is empty), and its folder is the ordinary `checkout_path`, so it is a worktree like any other. Two own roles whose names make one branch are a problem. Role worktrees are made from the run's branch (`git::worktree::create_from`, where `create` is `create_from(.., "HEAD")`), not from the project's `HEAD`. A run on a **new** branch needs its role branches absent (auto-naming skips a run branch with a leftover one; a chosen branch is refused with the leftover's name), since a stale branch would start from old code; on a branch that **exists**, role branches and their worktrees (`git::checkout_of`, read from files) are reused, continuing earlier work.
  - **One job** (`spawn_run_worktrees`, `WorktreeEvent::Run`) makes the run's worktree when it is missing and then each role's, in order, and answers once (`Made`), replacing the `Stage::Run` arm of `worktree_ready`: a failure anywhere (a plain folder in the way) opens no tab, and the job first removes every worktree it made itself (registered before `git worktree add` runs, so one that fails after adding is cleaned too; a path that does not exist is skipped) (`git worktree remove --force`, branches kept), as does a hand-off that fails afterwards (`open_run`'s error), so a retry does not meet leftovers; reused worktrees are never touched. `.worktreeinclude` is copied by `create` as for any worktree.
  - **Tabs stay in the run's workspace** (the panel, the brief, launches and saved seats all assume one workspace), and a role's shell starts in its worktree folder; `Starting::trees` carries them to `open_run`. Records already go by the pane's folder, so Codex, Gemini CLI and opencode in different folders do not hide each other, and `Flow::problems`' folder rule counts only roles that share. Each role worktree is also an ordinary sidebar row (added by `open_run`, found by `sync_worktrees` anyway), so its Changes and Files panels compare candidates; nesting them under the run's row was left for later. Each row asks `gh pr view` once a minute like any worktree.
  - **The project's setup runs in every worktree cornercase made** (the run's, as before, and each new role's, with its own `CORNERCASE_BRANCH`), in that role's setup pane, which belongs to the run's workspace: `held_launch_panes` holds every launch until all setups end, and one failure drops the launches and ends the run as for a single worktree. A reused role worktree runs no setup, as any reused one.
  - **Two editors in one worktree are a problem** (and the flow does not start), also for flows written before: two roles of one stage that edit and share the worktree overwrite each other. The fix offered is a worktree of their own or stages of their own. The orchestrator cannot be own (it works where the team is). This changes user flows that had two parallel editors, which is in the CHANGELOG.
  - **Integration is a role's job** (cornercase never merges): the brief's `## Worktrees` section tells the orchestrator how to read a candidate (`git log run..B`, `git diff run...B`, `git show B:path`, build in its folder) and to ask a role that edits the shared worktree to merge or cherry-pick, never to check a role's branch out itself. Each own role's prompt tells it to commit on its branch, since nobody sees the rest. The built-in **Arena** has two or three own coders (agents spread over the installed ones, a second coder on the same agent when only one is installed), a read-only judge, an integrator that edits the shared worktree and a reviewer; it is listed last in `planned()` so `Start::new` keeps Full issue as its default. Merging by a `flow` command was left for later.
  - **Removal goes with the run's workspace**: the removal check also reads each role worktree's changes and lock (`worktree::status`; a role's lock is the one shown, carrying the role and folder (`Lock::role`) so the dialog and the command's `git worktree unlock` hint name that worktree rather than the run's, and **unlock and remove** unlocks them all, while `close --remove-worktree` refuses with the lock's reason), the confirmation names how many roles' worktrees go, and the job runs, for each role worktree still on disk, the teardown (with its branch) and `git worktree remove`, then the run's own, whose result stays the job's; a role's failure is a warning in the toast naming the role and the folder that stays (`could not remove the worktree of coder A, which stays in /…: …`) rather than a refusal, since the run's checkout is what the user asked to remove. One role worktree alone cannot be removed while its run lives (the toast, and `close --remove-worktree` as an error, name the role and the run's workspace): its checkout belongs to the run, and removing it behind the run's back would leave a pane in a deleted folder. Once the run ended the row is an ordinary worktree. Branches are always kept.
  - **The diagram** tags an own role's box on its top border, muted and right-aligned, with ` own worktree ` or, when the name leaves no room, ` own ` (`Node::own`; `natural()` reserves the short tag so a box is wide enough for it); the simulation draws the same, and the site's listing, its page's team table and `flow list` (` (edits, own worktree)`) and `flow run --json` (`worktree` and `branch` per member) say it too. Costs: a checkout and a build per worktree, and the setup per worktree, which the docs say.
- **The run's folder (#312)** (`flows/folder.rs`, `app/flow_runs.rs`): agents handed long reports over `read --last-message`, so each stage's output piled up in the orchestrator's context and nothing was kept once the run ended. Every run now gets a folder for its files, and roles write each answer to a file and reply with its path and a few lines.
  - **Where**: `<state>/cornercase/runs/<date>-<workspace folder slug>` (`paths::runs`; with `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`, `<stem>.runs/` beside the session file like `paths::queue`, so two servers sharing a socket folder never share one; `App::set_runs_dir` makes it absolute). Mode 0700 for the root and each folder (set again after creating it, so a tight umask does not matter); a name taken the same day gets `-2`, `-3`… (`folder::make`). A path that is not UTF-8 is refused: it goes into the environment and the brief as text. Rejected: inside the repository's `.git` (agents refuse to write there), and under the worktree in an ignored folder (cornercase would edit the repository's ignore rules, `git clean -fdx` would delete it, and the files panel's ignore rules would hide it).
  - **A run that cannot get its folder does not start** (`could not make the run's folder: …`); a spawn that fails afterwards removes the empty folder, so a failed start leaves nothing.
  - **The variable**: every pane of the run gets `CORNERCASE_RUN_DIR` at spawn (`App::pane_env`), also after a restore, since roles are spawned with it again (`App::spawn_with`). Only a run's panes have it.
  - **Always a file, no opt-out**: each role writes every answer to `<role slug>-<n>.md` and replies with the path; the orchestrator passes paths on and writes its own decisions (`orchestrator-<n>.md`). One rule is simpler than a threshold, and `read --last-message` stays short. A `files = false` flow option was left for later; a flow's own prompts can still say otherwise. A role that may not edit is told "the worktree's files", or it would be forbidden to write its answer.
  - **Agent flags** (`Known::add_dir`, `ADD_DIR` per folder; checked against installed Claude Code 2.1.302, Codex 0.162.1, Cursor CLI 2026.10.14 and Gemini CLI 0.64.0, no live runs): Claude Code, Codex and Cursor CLI take `--add-dir DIR` so their file tools reach the folder; the role's command appends it last (`flows::command`, `flows::resume`), so Claude's variadic flag only meets an option (`--resume`) or the end, and Codex's `resume … --add-dir DIR <id>` reads as one value. Gemini CLI has `--include-directories` but is left out: with folder trust on (the default) an included folder with no rule in `trustedFolders.json` opens a dialog at every start (`MultiFolderTrustDialog`), which a first launch would answer but a resume (`Launch::command`, which never answers) would not, and pre-trusting it means writing Gemini's config. Its shell already writes there under `--yolo`, as opencode's and Grok Build's do (they have no such flag); the prompt says to use the shell when file tools cannot reach it.
  - **Lifecycle**: the workspace owns its folders (`Workspace::run_dirs`), since a run ends when its tabs close while its files must stay, and a workspace can run a second flow later (a folder each). They go with the workspace, once it is really gone (`App::defer_run_dirs`: a closing workspace keeps them until its shells have exited and it left the hierarchy, checked every refresh, so an agent still writing cannot race the removal; `sync_worktrees` removes at once, since nothing runs there), in a background job each (`App::remove_run_dirs`, logged with files and bytes): `drop_workspace` (a plain close and a successful worktree removal), `close_project`, and `sync_worktrees` for a worktree that vanished. They stay on a failed removal, on `close --workspace` of a worktree (it only stops the shells), on a server stop and when a restore skips a workspace or project (a missing disk must not cost files). Removal walks regular files only, never follows a link, and visits at most 2,000 entries to count them.
  - **Saved as names**: `RunState::dir` and `WorkspaceState::run_dirs` hold folder names only, joined to the runs root on restore, and only when `folder::valid` (one plain component), so an edited `session.json` can never point a removal elsewhere. A deleted folder is made again (`folder::ensure`, which refuses a link or a file). Optional fields, so `VERSION` stays 5 and the v5 fixture is unchanged. A run saved before this has none, since its brief never named one.
  - **Shown**: the Flow panel lists the folder under the diagram (`Files · n`, newest first, name cut at the end and age right-aligned), from a listing job every 2 s while the panel shows (one in flight, given up after 30 s; at most 200 files, three folders deep, `n+` when cut); a click opens the file in the files panel with `open_link` (an absolute path, like any file outside the workspace). `flow run` prints the folder on stderr (`run_dir` with `--json`, so stdout is unchanged), and `status --json` gives each workspace `run_dir` (the run's, else the last one made), so a script finds the files of a run that ended. An ended run's files in the panel (it shows only while a run does) and an opt-out are left out.
  - **With a worktree of its own (#313)**: the folder is the run's, not a worktree's, so a role in its own worktree gets the same `CORNERCASE_RUN_DIR` and `--add-dir` (the folder is outside every worktree, and that role's shell and resume line pass it like any other), and a role whose worktree is gone and works in the run's now keeps it too. The folder belongs to the run's workspace, whose removal (with its role worktrees) removes it.
  - **Log**: the folder made (path), made again at a restore, each removal (path, files, bytes), listings at debug (a count); never file names or contents.
- **The brief** (`flows/brief.rs`, pure): the task, the team (role, pane, agent and model, whether it edits), the stages in words with their loops and limits, the final action, the flow's notes and the orchestrator role's prompt, then the driving rules from the skill and the fleet runs: one self-contained `send --enter` per step, `read --last-message` rather than the screen, never edit the worktree's files, commit or answer a permission prompt, stay in the worktree, and at the end the final action and `cornercase mark` on its own pane. With the run's folder (below) a `## Files` section sits before the rules, and they say the folder is the only place the orchestrator writes. **Waits run in the foreground**, with `--timeout 300` and a shell-tool limit longer than that, again on a timeout: a background wait ends the orchestrator's turn, which then showed as finished while its team worked (Claude Code 2.1.300 writes `idle`, not `shell`, for a background Bash). Short on purpose: a long brief wastes the orchestrator's context. Each member's prompt ends with whether it may edit the worktree's files, then (with a folder) where its answers go, and with "Wait for the orchestrator's first message", once, so a user's prompt that forgets them keeps both rules.
- **Members' notifications and marks**: a member's finished turn is the orchestrator's business, so it gets no `✓` (it is marked seen on each read, and the run's workspace row stays clear) and no toast, desktop or Telegram notice. A member waiting (a permission prompt the orchestrator must not answer) or failing still notifies. The orchestrator turning from working to `pending` (its own `cornercase mark`) notifies once, like a finished agent, since `pending` never notifies. Before, one small run of three agents sent seven notices and its end none.
- **The session**: `WorkspaceState::run`, optional so `VERSION` stays 5, holds the whole flow (a built-in changes with updates and a user's may be edited or deleted, but the team follows the plan it was briefed with), the task, each role's tab and pane position (pane ids last one server run) and where the plan stands (the active stage and the loop rounds, as numbers). Messages are never saved. A restore rebuilds the run with the new pane ids, and a member resumes with its role's command (`flows::resume`) only when the saved conversation is its role's agent. A resumed orchestrator not marked for review gets `brief::restored`, its team's new pane ids: the new server numbers panes from 1, so its old ids may name other panes, in another project. Telling it to look panes up again was rejected: it cannot notice that an id now names another pane. It is also told the run's folder, which a restore keeps.
- **Commands** (`cli/flow.rs`, `app/control/flow.rs`): `flow list` and `flow run`, request names of their own (`flow-list`, `flow-run`) so an older server refuses them. **The lookup order** is yours, then the built-in flows, then the project's (`Origin::rank`, names compared trimmed and ignoring case): a flow of yours with a built-in's name is how a built-in is edited, while a project's flow still never runs instead of one you know, since it comes with a cloned repository and would run its `env` and `args` unattended. Built-in flows came first at the start, which kept them read only in practice. `store::list` applies the rule once (it drops each built-in yours replaces, then sorts by name and rank), so `flow run`, `flow list` and both dialogs share it; a project's flow named like another gets the problem `yours is also named X and runs instead`, drawn in red and not startable, and `flow run`'s note says which runs. `flow run` answers once the tabs exist, with each role's tab and pane.
- **Sharing** (`flows/share.rs`, `flows/dialog/import.rs`, `control/flow.rs`, `app/control/flow.rs`): a flow is shared as its own TOML, the file `flows/*.toml` already holds, under one comment line saying how to import it (`share::HEADER`), by **export** in the Flows dialog (OSC 52 through `App::copy`, like every copy, since the server may be on another machine), `cornercase flow export NAME > file.toml`, or a file committed to `.cornercase/flows/`. Not an opaque code (base64, a link): whoever imports a flow runs its `env`, `args` and prompts unattended, so the text must be readable before it is accepted, and a file also diffs and reviews in a chat or a repository. A hosted share link was rejected like the stats card's upload: cornercase never sends anything anywhere by itself. **256 KiB at most** (`share::MAX_BYTES`), checked on export, on import and on a paste, since real flows are a few KiB and the text travels through the clipboard (OSC 52 adds a third, under tmux's 1 MiB `input-buffer-size`); the CLI reads at most one byte more and refuses. `store::parse` reports `line N:` for a bad file, now also for a flow read from disk. The server does the work, since it knows `config.json`'s place (which follows `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`), the built-ins for this config and the project's path: `flow-export` and `flow-import` are commands of their own, so an older server refuses them by name instead of ignoring the fields. Moving the requests of flows into `control/flow.rs` was its own commit, since `control.rs` was at 951 lines.
  - **Every import is checked for risks**: only a project's flow had its `env` and `args` named in red, but an imported one is someone else's just the same. `share::risks` is the sentence (`project_risks` is its project wording, and `share::env_warning` the export's: check no secret goes with it). The dialog never saves an import: the page keeps what was pasted (a multi-line field like the prompt's, `Esc` goes back) and its `import` button opens the flow in the editor, unsaved (`Editing::imported`: an empty original, so leaving asks), with the red line and a note for agents not installed here, and a name made unique (`Quick` becomes `Quick 2`, ignoring case, built-ins counted); saving is the normal save, which refuses while there are problems. A paste on the list opens the page with the text, so the usual way is copy, open Flows, paste. The CLI lists the roles on stderr, and when the flow sets `env` or `args` asks `[y/N]` (after a `check` request, so collisions and problems fail before the question); without a terminal, or reading `-`, it refuses unless `--yes`, as the restart does.
  - **Names** (`share::collision`, compared as `store::same_name`: trimmed, case-insensitive): into yours, a name of yours is refused, or overwritten with `--replace`; a built-in's is refused without `--replace` (yours would take its place and be tagged `edited`) and allowed with it; a project's is allowed, since yours runs first. Into the project (`--to project`), a name of yours or a built-in is refused even with `--replace`, since the project's flow would never run, and another project flow's is refused unless `--replace`. Every refusal suggests `--name`. `flow-import` refuses a flow with problems, listing them: the dialog shows them in the editor instead. The log has `flow imported` and `flow exported` with sizes and the place, never the text (`Command::fields` gives `text_bytes`).
- **Community flows** (`flows/registry.rs`, `site/src/lib/registry.ts`, `site/scripts/refresh-flows.mjs`, `site/src/data/flows.json`, `site/src/pages/flows/`): a directory of flows that anyone publishes, shown at `usecornercase.dev/flows/` and imported by `OWNER/REPO`. **Open discovery, like a plugin directory, no pull request here** (the user's decision, over a curated folder in this repository): any public repository with the topic `cornercase-flow` and a `flow.toml` (else `cornercase-flow.toml`) at its root is listed. It is not reviewed, and the page, the import and the docs say so: a flow's prompts drive agents that never ask for permission, and what an imported flow does is the importer's responsibility. A curated registry would have bought review at the price of a bottleneck, and its prompts could not be audited by a script anyway.
  - **A scheduled script builds the list** (`refresh-flows.mjs`, `npm run flows`): the GitHub search for `topic:cornercase-flow` (sorted by stars, at most 300 repositories, forks, archived and private ones left out; `GITHUB_TOKEN` when present, and sent only to the default API address, never to a `GITHUB_API_URL` override), then each repository's file from `raw.githubusercontent.com/<repo>/HEAD/` (at most 256 KiB, 15 s per request, eight at a time), written to `flows.json` with the stars, the repository's description and topics. The build reads only that snapshot, so it needs no network and the committed one is the fallback: `pages.yml` runs the refresh before the check on every build that is not a pull request (push, the hourly `schedule`, manual) with `continue-on-error`, so a rate limit or an outage keeps the last snapshot and the site still deploys, and a following step (`steps.flows.outcome == 'failure'`) writes a workflow warning that the list is stale. The snapshot is not committed back by CI (it would need a token that pushes to `main`); it is committed by hand now and then, and an empty one lists nothing. The schedule is hourly, at minute 23 (GitHub delays scheduled runs most at the top of the hour): a build takes a minute or two and makes one search, so the list is meant to be about an hour old at most instead of a day, and more often would mostly redeploy an unchanged site. It is a target, not a deadline: GitHub can delay scheduled runs under load and drop some. GitHub turns scheduled workflows off after 60 days without activity in the repository.
  - **The loader validates for listing** (`registry.ts`, `problems`): the 256 KiB cap, a TOML that parses, a name, 1 to 8 roles, known agents, unique role names, every role in exactly one stage, loops only to earlier stages, no control characters, **no `env` or `args` in any role**, `worktree` only `shared` or `own` (never on the orchestrator, and `own` only in a file that says `version = 2`; a version above 2 is a newer cornercase), no two roles that edit the shared worktree in one stage, a repository name of the shape `owner/repo` (never `.` or `..`, since it becomes a path and a link), and caps on what a page prints (name 80, description 300, a role's name, model, effort and a loop's label 60, a prompt 32 KiB), with types checked. The Rust `Flow::problems` is not run here (the site cannot build cornercase), so the two lists are kept in step by hand: the TypeScript one is deliberately a little stricter about what a listing may contain. An entry that fails is skipped with one log line (`flows: skipped owner/repo: …`), never fails the build.
  - **Built-in flows are listed too, first** (`site/src/data/builtin-flows.json`, `builtIns()`): the page would otherwise be empty until someone publishes, and it shows what a flow looks like. The site cannot run Rust, so the file is generated from `builtin::flows` with every agent installed and `claude` as the default, and `flows::tests::site_copy` fails when the committed file differs, with `CORNERCASE_UPDATE_BUILTIN_FLOWS=1 cargo test --locked site_copy` to rewrite it (so a change to a built-in flow's prompts must be committed with its copy). They sort before the community's rather than among them by stars (they have none), are tagged `built in`, name `cornercase` as author without a link, link the project's repository, live at `/flows/built-in/<slug>/` (a community repository whose owner is `built-in` is skipped, so the paths never collide), carry no import command or caution but a note that they already come with cornercase and are edited or deleted in Settings → Flows, and go through the same validation, which fails the build instead of skipping. The empty state is for the community's flows only.
  - **The pages**: `/flows/` lists cards most starred first, with a client-side search over name, description, author, repository and tags (an inline script, nothing external, and the cards are plain HTML without it); `/flows/<owner>/<repo>/` shows the stars and the link to the repository, a caution naming how many agents run without asking, the import command, the plan drawn with the simulation's own diagram, the roles, **every prompt in full** and the raw file as it was at the last refresh. There is no raw `.toml` endpoint: the repository serves it.
  - **The CLI downloads, the server does not**: `flow import SOURCE` resolves the source (`registry::source`: `-`, an `https://` address, an existing path, which beats `owner/repo`, then `owner/repo`; `http://` is refused) and sends the text in the same `flow-import` request, so there is no protocol change and it works with older servers. `owner/repo` reads `raw.githubusercontent.com/<repo>/HEAD/flow.toml`, then `cornercase-flow.toml` on a 404 (`HEAD` is the default branch, so no GitHub API call or token is needed). A source that is an address or a repository always asks `[y/N]` (without a terminal it refuses unless `--yes`), with a line saying where it came from, that nobody reviewed it, and where to read it, besides the risks of `env` and `args`. `CORNERCASE_FLOWS_URL` replaces the raw host for tests. `registry::fetch` reads through `http::download_strictly` (20 s, `share::MAX_BYTES` plus one byte; at most 3 redirects, and none from an `https://` address to plain `http://` (`https_only` on the agent, since a typed address must not end up somewhere the user did not type; the cap is tested with chains of three and four redirects, but the downgrade refusal is **not tested**, because it needs a TLS server); no token is sent). A body past the limit is the `too long for a flow` message, not a download failure. A redirect to a private address is not checked: the server fetches only what the user typed, and the answer is shown as text to be read before anything is saved.
  - **The Flows dialog** takes the same sources on the import page: one line that `registry::typed` turns into a repository or an address makes **import** fetch it on a job (`AppEvent::FlowFetched`, `fetching…` on the hint line) and open the flow in the editor unsaved, with a notice that nobody reviewed it, like any import. While it waits the hint names the exact address (`fetching https://raw.githubusercontent.com/…/flow.toml…`), so a line typed as a path but read as `owner/repo` is visible before the answer. An answer for a source that is no longer being fetched, or after the page closed, is dropped. A line like `dir/file` that is meant as a path cannot be told from a repository there, since the server has no say over the user's disk; on the page a pasted text is the way to import a file.
  - **Rejected or left for later**: a curated folder reviewed through pull requests (and a `[registry]` table, `min_version` and author checks in `cargo test`, which went with it); versions and pinning (an import is a copy); a GitHub API call per import; committing the snapshot from CI; an `index.json` and browsing the list inside the app (nothing consumes them yet).
- **Recording** (`app/flow_panel.rs`, hooked in `app/control.rs`): from the requests, not from what agents say: a request whose caller pane (with the server's token) and target pane belong to the same run becomes a message with role names: `send` with its text, `keys`, `read` and `wait` (one per target) when accepted, `read --last-message` with the message when its answer comes, kept for its own run (`Run::id`) so a late answer never reaches the next run in that workspace. So a new way to send work cannot be forgotten. Texts stay in memory, cut at 32 KiB, never logged (the log keeps sizes) nor saved. What roles hand over lives in the run's folder (below), where it outlives the run.
- **Where a run stands** (`flows/progress.rs`, pure): only the orchestrator's `send`s into a role of a stage move the work: a member writing to another is recorded but moves nothing. Into the next stage is its arrow; into the stage the active stage's loop returns to, a round of that loop; anywhere else, a step outside the plan (the last one is drawn dashed on the left). A send to another role of the active stage travels that stage's arrow again (each parallel branch lights), a follow-up to the same role moves nothing. Rounds past `at_most` still count and turn the label orange: the orchestrator decides, the diagram shows it. The last message is kept per arrow and target role, so each branch of an arrow into a parallel stage opens the message its own role got (before, the reviewer's branch opened the one sent to security).
- **The diagram** (`ui/flow.rs`, `ui/flow/`): one renderer for the editor's preview, the start dialog and the panel (`Diagram::plan`, overlaid for a run with `Diagram::live`). Vertical, to fit a side panel: the orchestrator on top, apart and without arrows (it moves the work, it is not a step; arrows to every stage were noise); stages top to bottom; parallel roles side by side, or stacked under a bracket when a box would get under 14 columns, since names are what the diagram is for; one lane per loop on the right with its label (`findings · at most 3`, live `findings · 1/3`); the end as `◆ open a pull request`. Every line cell carries its connections and its arrows, so glyphs come from the bits and a click anywhere on an arrow knows it (strings per case were the first idea). Roles take the group presets' colours without red (failed) and orange (waiting, and a loop past its limit). Live: a working role's box gets a heavy border in its colour, the arrow the work last took the colour of the role it left, a `●` travels along it for a second (the only time frames are asked for), and `◆` turns purple once the orchestrator marks its pane for review. No times anywhere. Widths are display widths with saturating sums, so a wide name is cut inside its box and a huge loop label falls back to its short form.
- **The Flow panel**: `Side::Flow`, the fourth panel of the changes column, shown only while the active workspace has a run, so another workspace gets its width back. Opened by **show flow** in a run workspace's menu, `w f`, the icon bar's Flow slot, and when a run starts from the window. A click on a box goes to its pane; on an arrow or a loop's label that carried a message (only those light up), **Message** (`orchestrator → security · sent`, the text, ` copy `), which closes when its run goes.
- **The Flows dialog** (`flows/dialog/`, `app/flows_dialog.rs`), Settings › Flows (#322, see Settings): one list sorted by name, with a muted tag only for `this project` and `edited` (the other origins are the common case, and section headings cost rows and made the list read like Settings; files that cannot be read stay at the end with their reason), and an edit page per flow drawn with the settings dialog's pieces, up to 140×40 so the diagram fits beside the form from 112 columns (else behind ` diagram `). **The list previews the selected flow too** (#292), from the same 112 columns, in the same box: a user's flow is picked from a name and a count, and seeing its plan took opening it, so a click now selects and draws, and a second click edits as before. `Dialog::preview` answers `Preview::Flow` (the edit page's flow, or the selected one on the list) or `Preview::Note` where there is no flow: a file that cannot be read says `<file> cannot be read as a flow, so it has no diagram: <reason>`, an empty list says how to get one; importing and a text field have none. Narrower, the list's buttons get ` diagram ` / ` list ` (the editor's ` diagram ` / ` form `) after the selection's buttons, so ` import `, ` new ` and ` done ` keep their places. Duplicate saves a copy at once (a file is what the start dialog lists), new saves on ` save `, delete and leaving with unsaved changes ask first, and ` save ` refuses while there are problems. Stages open in place; loops stay valid by rule when stages move or go, and a stage's `when` or `at most` being typed follows its stage. Renaming a role renames it in the stages; changing its agent clears its model. `+ add stage` and `+ add role` are worded as actions. Models are a pick: Claude Code's aliases (`MODELS`), opencode's from the models.dev cache it keeps (`CATALOGUE`, read on a job), `default` and `other…` for the rest; no model id is invented.
- **The preview scrolls** with the wheel within the diagram's height, with ` ↑ more ` / ` ↓ more ` at its edges (`ui::flow::draw_scrolled`, which the Flow panel draws through too), and the offset belongs to the dialog's model: the list's is `Dialog::preview`, apart from the editor's (`Editing::preview`), so opening a flow starts at its top and coming back finds the list as it was; it goes back to the top on every change of selection, through `Dialog::select` (click, `↑` `↓`, `Tab`, the wheel over the list, and a listing that changes the selected entry). The wheel over the diagram scrolls it and, over a note, does nothing; elsewhere it moves the selection. `room()` still counts the editor's preview alone: the list's buttons already drop by a whole button row, and the list's rows never wrap. In the editor **it follows the stage being changed** (the one whose pick or field is open, else the one under the cursor): its boxes get the heavy border in their own colour (`Diagram::focused`), a working box's look without its status icon, so it cannot be taken for a live one; when that stage changes, the preview scrolls once to show all its boxes (`ui::flow::scroll_to`), only when they are out of view, so the wheel stays free. A background behind the stage (it fights the diagram's colours) and a marker beside it (the boxes move with the layout) were rejected.
- **The start dialog** (`flows/start.rs`, `app/start_flow_dialog.rs`): one dialog for the three entries (an issue's ` start a flow `, in the Issues list beside ` start ` and on the issue's page; the list's button reads the issue and asks the place first when the tracker needs one, as ` start ` does, but needs no agent; a narrow row drops ` schedule ` then ` start a flow `, **start a flow…** in the workspaces' `+ New`, `w s`). From an issue, the prompt and the branch are what ` start ` would use, so a flow and a single agent land on the same branch name. The prompt has the keys first (`Enter` is a new line there; in the branch field it starts), and the branch follows the prompt until it is touched. The list is a `Flow templates` heading and two lines per flow: the name, its tag and ` N agents `, then the description, or the first problem in red, so flows with problems stay in the list, a click from ` edit `. It takes `1 + 2·items` rows and the prompt the rest; a click on either line selects the flow, and choosing another one brings the preview back to its top. Refusals stay in the dialog in red, and it stays busy until the run opens, showing a git failure with the task kept: it closed at once before, and a long issue prompt was lost to a toast. ` edit ` opens the selected flow's edit page, whatever its origin, in the Flows dialog for the dialog's own project; ` done ` goes back to that list and another ` done ` to the start dialog, with the edited flow selected.
- **Line breaks and display columns** (`todo::editor`): the TODO field stays single-line; prompts and notes get line breaks, paste with lines and vertical scrolling. Wrapping, the cursor and clicks count display columns (`unicode-width`), so wide characters wrap and place correctly, in the TODO panel too.
- **Live check** (Claude Code 2.1.300 and Codex on a scratch repository, a dev server on a private socket): Quick ran in about 70 s, and a strict reviewer took a loop to its limit, where the orchestrator stopped and listed what was left. It found the background waits, the seven notices and the silent end above. Two restarts brought both runs back with each member on its role's flags, and the orchestrators already marked for review were left alone. A dev server whose agents call `cornercase` needs an absolute `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`: panes inherit it, and a relative one starts a second server.
- **Tests**: the model, files, built-ins (every set of installed agents), brief, progress and editor are unit tests; the diagram is `insta` snapshots from 30 to 100 columns, plan and live, with hits and hover checked by cell; app tests drive whole runs with a fake `claude` that asks the trust question and logs its arguments and prompt (restore, refusals, cleanup, a project closing while git works); the dialogs' pages are snapshots; the site's tests play the simulated run.

## Settings (`config.rs`, `settings.rs`, `settings/`, `app/settings_dialog.rs`)

- `app/settings_dialog.rs` assembles `ui::Settings` from the model, and `App::overlay_view` wraps it in `ui::Overlay::Settings`. Sections, removal and movement controls, pick items and scroll, edit errors and the busy note belong to that assembly. The model imports no `ui` types.
- `Settings` owns the row labels, values, notes and danger flags as `RowText`, the edit field's label and masked value, the pick title and clamped selection, and the hint. These are rules about the settings, so their existing text tests read the model directly. `Row::movable` is shared by view assembly and mouse hit testing; both must offer source reordering on the same rows. Sidebar and tab choices are read from `config`, their owner.
- **Flows tab** (#322): flows were one row of the Agents tab that opened a dialog, which hid them: they are teams and plans, with their own list, diagram and editor. The tab is `Overlay::Flows` drawn in the settings frame (`Dialog::in_settings`), not a page of the `Settings` model: one model keeps the list, editor, import page, confirmations, model reads, fetch jobs and `refresh_flows`, so the settings model has no flows code and `Page::Flows` has no rows. Choosing the tab (a click, `Tab`, `←` `→`, or reopening on the remembered page) makes `open_flows_tab` replace `Overlay::Settings`; a click on another tab or `Tab`/`←`/`→` at the list level (`Action::Tab`, `Action::StepTab`, via `Page::step`) goes back through `open_settings`. `↑` `↓` move between flows there, and the buttons end with ` restart ` and ` done ` like every settings tab. The settings dialog takes the Flows dialog's size, 140×40, **on every tab** (`settings_area` is `flows_area`), so switching tabs never moves the tab row or the buttons, the diagram fits beside the list from 112 columns and the editor opens in the same box; the alternative of 110×30 would always put the diagram behind a button. Only the editor and import page change the title (`Flows › X`) and drop the tabs. The start dialog's ` edit ` keeps its own frame (title `Flows`, no settings tabs), since a click on a settings tab there would leave the start dialog behind, as Settings has no back. The icon bar's Settings is lit for `Overlay::Flows` too. Agents added with `+ another agent…` and not configured stop being listed after a visit to the tab (its transient state goes with the overlay).
- Tabs: Worktrees, Agents, Flows, Issues, UI, Remote (the browser, then a section per chat app: Telegram, Discord), Plugins (see Plugins). Every change is saved to `config.json` at once (turning a plugin on or off goes to `plugins.json`). The prefix key row is the one that captures a key instead of editing text (see Keyboard shortcuts). File: `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/cornercase/config.json`, or next to the socket when `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` is set (so tests never touch the real one). Missing keys take defaults; a corrupt file means all defaults.

## Terminals (`term.rs`, `term/emulator.rs`)

- The emulator is libghostty-vt: it answers terminal queries (DSR, DA, DECRQM, kitty keyboard…) that programs like fzf and nvim wait for, and reflows on resize.
- Its types are `!Send`, so the emulator lives on the server's main thread; the reader thread only forwards bytes. Query replies go out from `on_pty_write`, ordered with the output that asked.
- Every write to a PTY (keys, pastes, launches, query replies) goes through one writer thread per pane (`term::spawn_writer`), in order: once a terminal's input buffer is full, a write to a program that doesn't read blocks, and on the server loop that froze every client (a long paste or `cornercase send` into a pane running `sleep`). Each pane queues at most 16 MiB (`term::MAX_QUEUED`): past that `Term::write` refuses and returns `false`, `send` and `keys` answer that the program is not reading, and typing or pasting in the UI shows a toast instead of losing keys silently. A write is taken whenever the queue is below the limit, so one long paste still goes through. Only Linux blocks a write to a full terminal; macOS drops what the terminal cannot take, so its queue never fills, and the App test that needs a full pane runs on Linux only. Pastes drop `\x1b[201~` from the text (`term::bracketed`), so a paste cannot end itself and type the rest.
- Cells keep palette indices so the outer theme applies. The client asks the outer terminal for its foreground, background and 16 ANSI colours (OSC 10/11/4, then XTVERSION, then DA1 as an end marker) before starting the input thread, and every emulator uses them as defaults. Entries 16-255 keep the emulator's (libghostty-vt's) default colour cube and greys: these are normally fixed, unlike the themed ANSI colours, and indexed cells still draw through the outer terminal's own palette. The 256-slot `HostTheme` and its wire representation stay the same; unset entries leave the emulator defaults intact. Programs querying a customised entry above 15 therefore see the default value, while their indexed output still uses the host's value.
- **A snapshot also knows what a surface needs** (#286): `Cell::spacer` marks the right half of a wide character (Ghostty's `CellWide::Wide` on the cell before it, asked only for cells with text; its `symbol` stays `" "`, so `contents()`, path links and launches keep their columns), `Snapshot::shape` and `blinking` the cursor's DECSCUSR style, and `Snapshot::scroll` the scrollbar, computed once (`below` comes from it; Ghostty warns the call can be expensive). The window ignores the spacer, since ratatui's diff already skips the cell after a wide symbol. `Emulator` also answers `focus_events` (DEC 1004) and `kitty_keyboard` (the flag bits).
- cwd, name and arguments of the PTY's foreground process group leader come from `process.rs`: `/proc` on Linux, `proc_pidinfo` / `proc_name` / `sysctl(KERN_PROCARGS2)` on macOS (with `getpeereid`, the only `unsafe` and the only use of `libc`). In a pipeline the leader may be dead (`process::alive`), so the shell is used instead. Agent launch and detection depend on it.

## Keys and mouse (`term/keys.rs`, `term/mouse.rs`, `app/input.rs`, `app/panes.rs`)

- The client owns its byte decoder (`client/input.rs`, `client/input/parse.rs`), adapted from crossterm 0.29's Unix parser under its MIT licence (`LICENSE-CROSSTERM`), and still sends crossterm `Event`s, plus `Activated` for a notification clicked. Crossterm's public input reader provides neither a raw-reply filter nor a parser reset: catching its panic alone can leave the offending bytes buffered, and a private CSI can buffer clicks forever. Owning the decoder makes filtering and recovery possible without vendoring the entire dependency or changing key encodings. Zero mouse and cursor coordinates are rejected with checked arithmetic in both debug and release. A decode panic is contained per sequence; its bytes are cleared before the next byte is decoded, including the rest of the same read. Bracketed paste bypasses the reply filter so pasted escape sequences stay text.
- Special keys, and all keys once the program enabled kitty, go through Ghostty's encoder; a legacy encoder handles the rest. The client asks the outer terminal for kitty's disambiguate level, so `Ctrl+Enter` / `Shift+Enter` keep their modifiers.
- Pane mouse events are forwarded only when the program enabled a mouse mode, in its encoding, relative to the pane, clamped to its edges.
- **Paths**: a simple click on a path in the active pane opens it in the files panel, even in a program that reads the mouse (see Files panel); a drag that starts on one still reaches the program.
- **Selection:** when the program did not ask for the mouse, press-drag-release selects with Ghostty's selection and copies via OSC 52, with a toast. Programs' own OSC 52 writes are forwarded to the outer terminal too (reads and primary-clipboard writes are dropped).

## Scrollback (`term/emulator.rs`, `app/panes.rs`, `ui/pane.rs`)

- **The wheel reads a pane's history**: over a pane whose program does not read the mouse, on the main screen, the wheel moves that pane's view `WHEEL_ROWS` rows through the history the emulator keeps, which before only `read --lines` reached. It acts on the pane under the pointer, active or not, without moving the focus, as tmux and Ghostty's splits do: reading a neighbour's build output should not take the keys from the agent you are typing to. A program that reads the mouse keeps the wheel, as before.
- **How much history**: `term::SCROLLBACK_BYTES`, 10 MB per pane, Ghostty's own default: about 14,000 lines in an 80-column pane, 4,500 at 250 columns. libghostty-vt's `max_scrollback` is documented as lines but Ghostty reads it as bytes (`Screen.init`, `PageList`'s `max_size`), so the 5,000 it was given kept Ghostty's minimum of two pages, about 600 lines at 80 columns and 300 at 250, while the docs promised 5,000. Pages are allocated as output arrives, so a quiet pane costs nothing; one that printed a lot holds up to 10 MB in the server. `term::tests::scrollback` pins a floor at both widths.
- **libghostty-vt's own viewport** (`scroll_viewport`, `scrollbar`) holds the position, so the snapshot the UI draws, the selection (viewport points with a tracked anchor) and path links (`files::link::at` on snapshot rows) follow the scrolled view unchanged. The position belongs to the pane, in the server, so every client, windows and views alike, sees the same view, like the other interaction state (no per-view viewport yet). Rejected: a scroll offset per client (snapshot, selection and links rewritten, and it breaks shared interaction state), and a line buffer of cornercase's own (Ghostty already reflows, styles and measures wide characters).
- **New output does not move the view** (Ghostty pins a viewport that is not at the bottom); ` ↓ n more ` at the bottom right of the pane (`ui::scrollback_badge`, shared by drawing and hit testing; `Snapshot::below`, `total − offset − len` of the scrollbar) says how many rows are under it and grows while the program prints. It covers the pane's last row and reserves nothing, so the PTY size never changes. A click on it returns to the bottom, as does a key or a paste from a window (`forward_key`, `handle_paste`). `cornercase send` and `keys` do not: an orchestrating agent sending work must not pull a person away from what they are reading.
- **The alternate screen** has no history: the wheel sends `WHEEL_ROWS` Up or Down keys there (encoded like typed arrows, so application cursor mode applies) while the program leaves alternate scroll (DEC mode 1007) on, which is libghostty's default and Ghostty's, iTerm2's and kitty's behaviour, so less, man and git's pager scroll. libghostty scrolls only the active screen, so a program that switches to the alternate screen leaves the main screen where the reader left it, and it shows there again when the program ends. A program that turns mouse reporting on while its pane is scrolled gets its pane back at the bottom on the next snapshot (`Emulator::snapshot`), so the wheel's meaning never depends on a stale view.
- **Readers that decide what a program does read the active screen, never the view**: `Launch` reads `Emulator::active_contents` (the bottom snapshot, the viewport restored after), since a `send --enter` into a pane someone is reading up in would otherwise compare the history with its echo and trust prompt; dialog and survey detection, `read` and `wait --text` already read the active screen.
- Left out: searching the history, keyboard scrolling (keys menu entries or a copy mode), a scrollbar. The landing page's simulation has no history in its fake shells.

## Keyboard shortcuts (`shortcuts.rs`, `app/shortcuts.rs`, `ui/keys.rs`)

- **Opt-in, behind one prefix key** (#31): the mouse stays the default and nothing changes for whoever does not turn it on. It is for people whose hands stay on the keyboard, and for clients the mouse does not reach (some mobile SSH apps, a terminal with mouse reporting off).
- **The prefix** is `prefix_key` in `config.json`, set in Settings → UI → **prefix key** by pressing it (`Settings::capturing`: the next key is taken, `Backspace` turns it off, `Esc` keeps the old one). No default is shipped: `Ctrl+b` is tmux's (and `e2e::input::ctrl_b_reaches_the_shell`), `Ctrl+Space` switches the input source on macOS, `Ctrl+]` needs AltGr on Spanish and German layouts. It must be `Ctrl` or `Alt` with a character, or a function key with any modifiers; `Ctrl+c`, `Ctrl+d` and `Ctrl+z` are refused, since every one of them would open the menu instead of reaching the program. Known clashes (`Prefix::clash`: `Ctrl+b`, `Ctrl+a`, `Ctrl+Space`, …) are named in the row and the notice, not refused. `Prefix::normal` makes a key match however a terminal sends it: the legacy bytes for `Ctrl+4`…`Ctrl+7` are `Ctrl+\` `Ctrl+]` `Ctrl+^` `Ctrl+_`, an upper-case letter is the letter with `Shift`, and `Shift` on a symbol is dropped (it made the symbol). The string form (`ctrl+]`, `alt+k`, `f12`) is what the file and the row show.
- **Where it works**: while no overlay but a right-click menu is open, also while a panel's field has the keys (the files search, the changes filter, a TODO field: none of them takes `Ctrl` or `Alt` keys anyway). Dialogs keep it: they already take arrows, `Enter` and `Esc`, and the settings must get the key to capture it.
- **The menu** (`Overlay::Keys(Option<Group>)`) works like Neovim's which-key: the prefix opens it, the next key runs an action or opens a group (`f` find, `g` git, `w` workspace), whose menu replaces it. A key is either an action or a group (`shortcuts::Step`), never both, so nothing waits for a timeout. `Esc` or a key the menu does not list closes it and is dropped, since it was meant for the menu; the prefix again closes it and types the prefix in the pane, for programs that use that key; a paste closes it and goes to the pane. A click on an entry runs it, a click elsewhere closes it; entries naming several keys (`1-9`, the arrows) are not clickable. It is drawn at the bottom of the pane, in columns filled top to bottom, over the whole screen when the pane cannot hold it, and dims nothing (like the row menus, it belongs to what is behind it). Like every overlay it is shared by every client. It shows at once: which-key's delay (draw only after ~300 ms without a key) needs a timer wake-up and was left for later.
- **What the keys do** (`shortcuts::items`, `App::shortcut`): tabs (`n` `p`, `1`-`9`), workspaces (`]` `[`) and projects (`}` `{`) wrap around; workspaces skip one being removed, projects follow the sidebar's order (every group open) and skip one closing. Arrows focus the pane on that side among the shown ones (`project::split::beside`: the nearest that shares part of that edge, then the one sharing most). `a` jumps to the next agent pane, in the agents section's order after the focused one, whose status needs you (waiting or done), or toasts `no agent needs you`. `x` asks first: `Overlay::ClosePane` in a split tab, `CloseTab` for its last pane (a key is easier to press by mistake than `×`); `w x` asks like the compact `×`, and a worktree gets the removal dialog. A panel opened from the keyboard takes the keys where it has a field, or the mouse would be needed right after: `f s` opens the search in sessions (see Search) and `f f` and `f w` open the files panel with its search focused in that mode (closing an open file, `files::Panel::search`), `g d` focuses the changes filter, `t` opens a new TODO field; `f e` only opens or closes the files panel, `g b` opens the changes and their branch picker; `g p` opens the workspace's pull request (see Pull requests) or toasts why there is none; `o` turns away on or off on every chat app at once (see Telegram). `w s` opens the start dialog for the project on screen, `w f` the Flow panel of the workspace on screen or toasts `this workspace runs no flow` (see Flows); the Workspace group reads `n s r f x`. `g` outside git and a split without room toast why. Restart, kill-server and closing a project were left out (destructive and rare), and so was remapping the actions: the menu is on screen, so there is nothing to memorise.

## Client / server (`client.rs`, `client/`, `server.rs`, `server/startup.rs`, `protocol.rs`, `cli/start_server.rs`, #284)

- The server owns the PTYs so shells outlive the UI. The client starts it if needed (`cornercase server`, its output going to `server.log`, see Log).
- One server per socket, guarded by `flock` on `server.lock`. The server calls `setsid` and ignores SIGHUP.
- **Input failures**: the input thread polls the tty with a 100 ms bound and reads one ready batch. Poll and read run under `panics::contain`; an interrupted system call immediately polls again without clearing partial input, pausing or logging, so SIGWINCH cannot break a split key or mouse sequence. Other transient errors and reader panics clear partial decoder state, log to `server.log` and retry after 20 ms, so a bad fd cannot spin a core. Diagnostics repeat only at powers of two. EOF, hangup, an invalid fd or a gone terminal send `Incoming::InputEnded`, which detaches the window even during remote reconnection; a drop guard also sends it if the input thread unexpectedly unwinds; the server's shells keep running. SIGWINCH supplies resize events through the signal thread. The client's panic hook restores modes, images and ratatui only for its main thread: calling ratatui's previous hook on a contained input panic would disable raw mode, mouse capture and the alternate screen while frames kept arriving. Other threads' panics are logged without touching the terminal. A debug PTY reproduction confirmed this path for zero-coordinate SGR input; a timeout alone did not kill input on Linux, and the original macOS failure's exact bytes remain unknown.
- The server renders, the client only writes frames. Input travels as serialized crossterm events. Several clients mirror each other; the shared size is the last used client's (attach, key, paste or mouse other than a bare move), like tmux's `window-size latest` (windows only: a program that opened a view sizes the tabs it claims, see Views of your own), so a hung client (a phone whose SSH dropped) never shrinks a new one. Ping/pong would not catch that: the hung client is a healthy local process. Smaller clients get the frame cropped by `CropBackend`.
- **Starting without a window** (`cornercase start-server`, #284): a fresh server waited for the first window's `Hello` before it restored the session, so a script or an external UI could not start cornercase, look at its session and launch work without opening a terminal window; every command that creates a terminal answered `NO_SIZE`. `start-server [--cols N] [--rows N] [--json]` starts the server if none runs (`client::connect_or_start`, the same socket, lock and `setsid` as a window; no terminal needed), sends a `start-server` request, and answers once the session is restored and its terminals exist. It is a JSON request, so it skips `Hello`'s build check and needs no colour probe.
  - **One start, whoever comes first**: `Server::startup` is `Waiting`, `Headless` (started by `start-server`) or `Windowed`, replacing the `started` flag. Restoring (`open_first_terminals`, `server/startup.rs`) runs once, on the first `Hello` or the first `start-server`, with the same rules either way: `resume_agents`, the partial restore and its `session.json.bak`, and the folder the server was started from when there is no session (`open_here`, the same rule as a window; a `--no-project` was left for when an external UI needs an empty start). Run again, `start-server` restores nothing and answers `already: true`, so it is also how a caller learns that the start is over. The answer is `control::start_server::Started`: `already`, `restored` (a session was found), `complete`, `cols`, `rows`, `size` (`window` or `start-server`, where the size in use comes from), `boot` and `version`. `restored` and `complete` describe only the call that restored: an `already` answer says `false` and `true`, since keeping the first start's result in `Server` only for a later caller who missed it was not worth the state. When no first terminal opens (`open_here` fails, say the shell cannot start), the answer is an error with the cause rather than a success a script would act on: the server stays `Headless`, so a later call answers `already: true`. It comes when the terminals exist, not when resumed agents are back in their conversations: their resume lines are still being typed (`Launch::command`), and `status` or `wait` say when.
  - **The size without a window**: 160×48 by default, wide enough for the wide layout (`COMPACT_WIDTH` is 90), where 80×24 would give compact mode and panes of another width than a normal window's. `--cols` takes 90 to 1000 and `--rows` 10 to 500 (the browser bridge's caps); one given alone keeps the other's default. A size out of range is refused before connecting, so nothing starts, and the server checks again (`StartServer::size`): structured clients get `malformed`, since retrying the same size cannot work. It is set only while no window gave one (`area` is `None`), and a second `start-server` with another size changes nothing, so the first size wins and concurrent scripts do not fight over it; the answer says the size in use. A window that attaches later takes it over by the usual rule (`fit`, the last used client), and once every window leaves the last size stays. Other requests never claim it, only `start-server` sets it: their connections have no size, so `status`, `events`, `contract`, `stats`, `read` and `wait` neither set it nor start the restore. A program that wants a size of its own opens a view (see Views of your own): the windows keep mirroring each other, and the view sizes the tabs it claims.
  - **Colours come with the first window**: shells made before any window use the emulator's default colours (`HostTheme::default()`). The first window after a headless start gives its theme to every open terminal (`App::retheme`, `Emulator::set_theme` over the same `apply_theme` a new emulator gets) and the start becomes `Windowed`; later windows change nothing, as before. Limit: a program that asked for the colours (OSC 10, 11 or 4) before that window got the defaults and keeps them.
  - **Another build**: since the request skips the build check, a server left from another build (after `brew upgrade`) answers `already`; the command compares the answer's `version` with its own and warns, naming `cornercase restart`, without failing. Same version and another build gives no warning; the next window asks, as it does today.
  - `start` would have read as starting an agent, which `cornercase start` already does, and the docs never say daemon, so the name mirrors `kill-server`. Restoring headless on every start was rejected (see Queue): the request makes it explicit. `cornercase restart` with no window attached still leaves no server, since only windows `exec`; run `start-server` again.
- **When the server draws** (`Server::due`): a frame is drawn when an event other than PTY output arrived (input, a client, a job's answer, a request, a timeout wake-up), when a pane on screen printed (`App::shows`) and `FRAME` (16 ms) has passed since the last frame, and at least every `TICK` (500 ms). Output in a hidden tab draws nothing (a `surface` that streams it renders in its own `Step::Surfaces` after the draw, only for the surfaces that show that pane; see Streaming a tab's terminals): the sidebar's icons and `↓n` come from `refresh` and show within a tick. A visible pane printing without pause gets at most ~60 frames a second, counted from the last frame that showed output (`printed_at`), not from any frame: a key draws at once and its echo, a moment later, draws at once too instead of waiting a frame. Host writes (OSC 52, notifications) go out in their own `Step::Flush` every turn, framed or not. A draw that panics sets `changed`, so the screen is rebuilt on the next draw (forcing an immediate turn instead would spin while a draw keeps panicking). The session is observed at most every `SAVE_EVERY` (500 ms), since building `App::state()` looks up every pane's cwd and the `Saver` waits 2 s anyway. Before, every batch of events drew every client and built the state (#25).
- Every shell gets `CORNERCASE=1`; a client seeing it refuses to start (no nesting).
- **`kill-server` asks before stopping the server it runs in** (#297): it is how a dev build is stopped after every rebuild, and typed in a pane of the real server (an agent working on cornercase does) it killed that server, every shell and the agent's own session, whose resume then shows nothing. The command asks the server for a `status` report first (`client::report_now`, the one-second read `running_now` already used) and takes `Report::caller` as the signal: the server sets it only when the request carries its own `CORNERCASE_SERVER` token, so a pane of another server (reached through `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`) is not inside. In that case it lists what stops (`restart::stopping`) and asks `[y/N]`; a no keeps the server and exits 0; without a terminal it refuses with status 1 unless `--yes`, naming `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` as the way to say which server. A hung server, a server that predates request support or an absent one gives no report and the command goes ahead as before. No protocol change, so it works against any server that answers `status`. Rejected: a refusal in the server of a `KillServer` from another build (it cannot tell a script from a person at a prompt, and `client::kill_server` is also the restart fallback); the `CORNERCASE` variable as the signal (every pane has it, also when `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` points at another server).
- Every shell loses Claude Code's per-session variables (`agents::claude::CLAUDE_SESSION_ENV`): a server started from inside Claude Code would otherwise make every pane's `claude` a child of that session and expose its messaging token. An explicit list, not a `CLAUDE_CODE_*` prefix, so user settings like `CLAUDE_CODE_USE_BEDROCK` and `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` pass through.
- `Hello` carries a protocol version and build id, and the notification channel of the client's terminal; a server from another build rejects the client. Keep `ClientMessage::KillServer` and `ServerMessage::Rejected` as the first variants (`protocol::tests::compatibility`).
- Socket: `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/cornercase/server.sock` or `$TMPDIR/cornercase-<uid>/server.sock`; `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` overrides it. Paths must fit in 108 bytes.
- **The socket's folder must be the user's alone** (`protocol::check_socket_dir`): a real folder, not a symbolic link, owned by the user and not writable by group or others; it is created `0700` when missing. The client checks it before connecting or starting a server, `kill-server` before connecting, the server before binding, also under `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` (a name without a folder is checked in the current folder). The refusal suggests removing the folder only on the default path, which is cornercase's own; under `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` it asks to change the variable, since that folder may hold other files. Without it, another local user could create `/tmp/cornercase-<uid>` first (no `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` nor `TMPDIR`, as in some SSH sessions and containers) and pose as the server: read every key and send a `Restart`. Read access for others is allowed, since connecting needs write access to the socket file.
- Both ends check the peer's uid (`protocol::check_peer` on `process::peer_uid`: `SO_PEERCRED` on Linux, `getpeereid` on macOS): the client refuses a server of another user, and the server drops another user's client, which a loose umask could otherwise let in.
- **A client of its own on the socket** (#283): a program that does not run `cornercase` connects to the socket and frames each message as a 4-byte little-endian length followed by its postcard bytes (`protocol::send`, at most 64 MiB). A request is `ClientMessage::Request(json)`, variant 4 (the variant as a varint, then the string's length as a varint and its UTF-8 bytes), and each answer `ServerMessage::Response(json)`, variant 5; `events` ends with `Detached` (2), `Shutdown` (3) or `Restart` (4), and `surface` with `Detached` (2) when its tab closes, else 3 or 4. No `Hello` comes first, so no build check: requests are what crosses releases, and the TUI's same-build handshake is untouched. The server checks the peer's uid like any client's. One request per connection. `tests/fixtures/control/postcard/` holds the exact bytes; JSON over postcard rather than a second wire, since the framing is a few lines in any language and the socket already has one.

## Remote windows (`remote.rs`, `client.rs`, `client/window.rs`, `ui/remote.rs`)

- **`cornercase remote <destination>`** (#104) runs the window locally and attaches it to the server of another machine: `ssh -T … -- <destination> sh -c '<PATH>; exec cornercase proxy'`, and the protocol goes through ssh's stdin and stdout instead of the socket. The user's own `ssh`, so their config, keys, agent, `ProxyJump` and `ControlMaster` apply and cornercase never handles a credential; `--` ends ssh's options, so a destination can never be read as one. Nothing in the protocol needed the socket: frames, OSC 52 and notifications already went through the client, and the colours and the notification channel come from the local terminal in `Hello`. `ssh -t host cornercase` already worked but ends with the connection and puts one more terminal layer on the remote side.
- **`cornercase proxy`** (hidden, like `server`) prints a banner line, `cornercase-proxy <protocol> <release>`, reads the client's first message, and only then connects (starting the server like a client does, `client::connect_or_start`, which `cli` hands to `remote::proxy` as a parameter, so `remote` imports nothing from `client`; moving it into `protocol` was rejected, since it would drag the server's start, the log file and a polling interval into the framing module), so a client that refuses the version starts nothing there. Then it copies bytes both ways; the socket's end ends it, and its stdin's end shuts the socket's write side, so the server sees the client go. The banner is a text line rather than postcard so that any future version can read it; the client skips other lines first (`BANNER_LINES`, `BANNER_BYTES`), since some shell rc files print in non-interactive shells, and reads it byte by byte through the `BufReader` it then keeps for the protocol. The socket folder and peer checks stay, since the proxy is a local process of the same user there.
- **Versions**: `Hello`'s build id is the binary's modification time, so two machines never match (and are often not the same platform). The client attaches only when the banner's protocol and release equal its own, and says which side to update otherwise (`Error::RemoteOlder` / `RemoteNewer`, from `version::newer`); a cornercase from before the proxy fails with clap's `unrecognized subcommand` (`RemoteTooOld`), a missing one with sh's 127, or 126 and `No such file or directory` from bash, which is macOS' `sh` and says so for a path that is not there (`RemoteMissing`). With a terminal, an older remote is offered `cornercase update` through `ssh -t`. The proxy replaces the build in the forwarded `Hello` with its own (`remote::rebuilt`), so the server's check still means what it means locally: the server must come from the binary that answers. A server from an older build there is offered a restart like locally, through `ssh <destination> cornercase kill-server`, with `update::restart::confirmation(None)` (asking the old server for its list would take another ssh connection).
- **PATH**: a command run through ssh gets a short `PATH` without the login shell's profile, where the install script's `~/.local/bin` and Homebrew's folders are usually added. The remote command appends those, `~/.cargo/bin` and `/usr/local/bin` to the `PATH` (after it, so the user's own order wins) and `--command` replaces `cornercase`, put in the script as is so `~` and `$HOME` expand. The script is single-quoted for the login shell, which may be fish or zsh, and run by `sh`.
- **Reconnecting**: a remote window does not end when its link does (`Received::Ended`, or the server's `Restart` after an update, which it never `exec`s for). It keeps the last screen and draws a notice over it (`ui::remote`: the rounded dialog, ` quit ` as a grey pill, written cell by cell to the backend so the blank cells inside cover the old screen, which ratatui's diff would skip). Attempts run on a thread (`Incoming::Linked`, numbered by `generation` so a late answer for a dropped link is ignored): at once, then after 1, 2, 4, 8 and every 15 s (`backoff`), the countdown redrawn on the second (`until_next_second`). A link counts as healthy only once it showed a frame (`Retries`): the banner comes before the proxy reaches the socket, so a proxy that fails after it (a socket folder it refuses, a server that does not start) would otherwise reset the count and run ssh back to back; the notice then shows the remote's error. ssh errors are retried and shown (the last of the 20 stderr lines it keeps, `remote::Stderr`); a version mismatch, a missing cornercase or a rejection ends the window (`remote::fatal`). Once linked, the client sends `Hello` again and the server, which counts it as a new client, clears and redraws everything. `Esc` or a press on ` quit ` gives up, a detach and not an error. The sidebar's ` Quit ` (`Detached`) and `kill-server` (`Shutdown`) still close the window.
- **Noticing a dead link**: a laptop that sleeps or changes network leaves a TCP connection that nobody closes. ssh's `ServerAliveInterval=5` and `ServerAliveCountMax=3` end it within ~15 s; they override the user's config (the command line wins in ssh), which was preferred to a heartbeat in the protocol, since an old server would not answer one. `ConnectTimeout=10` bounds each attempt. Reconnects add `BatchMode=yes`: a password or passphrase prompt would read the terminal the window holds in raw mode. The first connection is made before the window opens, on the plain terminal, so it can still prompt (ssh asks on `/dev/tty`; its stderr is piped and kept for the error).
- **The client loop** (`Window`): one channel (`Incoming`) for terminal input, server messages, link attempts and signals, so the main thread can draw the notice and take `Esc` while no server is there. Messages to the server go through a writer thread per link (a hung ssh blocks it, not the loop). The input thread polls the tty for at most 100 ms and stops when `attach` returns (`Threads`), waiting at most 300 ms for it. Each read follows readiness and EOF or hangup detaches the window (Client / server), including while reconnecting. A thread left reading after a rejection could consume the terminal's answer to the next window's colour query; owning the bounded reader and decoder also gives each attach fresh parser state. Its signal thread is closed there too (`Handle::close`).
- **Tests**: a fake `ssh` first on the `PATH` (`fake_ssh` in `tests/e2e/remote.rs`) skips the options and the destination and `exec`s the command locally, so the proxy reaches the test's own socket and keeps the fake's pid: killing it drops the connection, while without `exec` a `sh -c` left between them would keep the pipe open.
- Left for later: `--remote` for the commands for scripts and agents (they would go through the same proxy), and several servers in one sidebar (the server renders the whole screen, so the client would have to merge them).

## Browser access (`web/`, `web/tailscale.rs`, `app/remote_access.rs`, `ui/remote_access.rs`)

- **Why** (#178): agents keep working after you leave the computer, and what they need from you is short (a `!`, a `✓`, a look at who still works). From a phone that meant a mobile SSH app, Remote Login on the Mac (often locked on a managed laptop) and reconnecting by hand after every drop; `cornercase remote` cannot help, since it needs the binary on the device showing the window. So the server can serve itself to a browser, and a QR code opens it on a phone.
- **The browser is one more client.** `web/` listens on `127.0.0.1:remote_port` (`config::DEFAULT_PORT`, 47681) inside the server process, and each WebSocket (`web/bridge.rs`) connects to the server's own Unix socket and speaks the protocol a terminal client speaks, like `cornercase proxy` does: a `Hello` (the page's size and theme, `terminal=browser`, `Channel::Bell`, no image protocol), `ClientMessage::Event`s, frames back. So `Server` needed no change and the browser follows every rule the other clients do: the shared size is the last used client's (attaching counts), overlays and the toast are shared, `Hello`'s build check passes since the bridge is the same binary. Input arrives as the bytes xterm.js emits and goes through the terminal client's own parser (`client::input::Parser`), so keys, SGR mouse and bracketed paste decode exactly as from a terminal. The bridge writes the modes the terminal client sets itself (alternate screen, mouse, bracketed paste) before the first frame, since frames never carry them.
- **Only HTTP, WebSocket and a QR code are new** (`httparse`, `tungstenite`, `qrcode` without its image features): blocking, one thread per connection, at most 32, which fits the server's threads; `tiny_http` and `axum` were not needed (the latter would bring tokio). The page (`web/assets/`) is xterm.js 5.5 and its fit addon, vendored with their license (no CDN: the phone may load it on a slow link, and nothing from elsewhere runs next to the shells), and `app.js`, about 290 lines. The listener polls `accept` every 50 ms instead of blocking, so switching off joins its thread and frees the port at once (waking a blocked `accept` with a connection of its own left the port taken for a moment on macOS, and switching back on failed). Frames wait for the browser in a queue of 64 (`sync_channel`): when a slow phone fills it, the bridge stops reading the socket and the server's 1 s write timeout drops it like any stuck client, instead of the queue growing in the server's memory. The page's size is capped at 1000×500 cells, since the browser becomes the shared size.
- **For phones**, from trying ttyd with `tailscale serve` on an iPhone before writing this: `width=device-width` in the viewport (without it iOS lays the page out at ~980 px, the client attaches at ~124 columns and the phone gets the wide layout, cropped); a vertical swipe becomes wheel events at the finger's cell (xterm.js scrolls its own scrollback on a swipe, and the alternate screen has none, so scrolling a Claude Code conversation did nothing), a tap stays a click; the terminal takes the visual viewport's height (`visualViewport`, plus `interactive-widget=resizes-content` for Android), since iOS lays the keyboard over the page without resizing it and the cursor's row, an agent's input box at the bottom, went under it, so with the keyboard up the session gets fewer rows and the bottom row sits right above it; both sides send a ping every 15 s and give up after 45 s of silence (ttyd's 5 s ping dropped the link every 8-30 s on mobile data); a dropped link keeps the screen and reconnects on its own with the backoff of `cornercase remote` (1, 2, 4, 8, then every 15 s), and at once when the page comes back to the foreground. A link refused before it opened asks `/` why: a browser no longer paired (forgotten in Settings) or another account says so and stops retrying. A detach (` Quit ` on the phone) stops reconnecting until the notice's button is tapped. The page follows the phone's light or dark scheme and sends its colours in the hello, so the muted greys and tints work as with a terminal. Left for later: a row of the keys a phone lacks, a native field for long prompts, images (xterm.js's image addon) and notifications (the Notification API).
- **Authentication.** Loopback is reachable by every local user and process, and the port is published to the tailnet, so nothing but the static assets is served without pairing. Opening ` Remote ` makes a pairing token (16 random bytes from `/dev/urandom`, single use, 10 minutes, dropped as soon as the dialog closes, compared in constant time); `GET /pair?t=` exchanges it for a cookie (`HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, a year, `Secure` when it came through Tailscale) and redirects to `/`, so the token leaves the address bar. Paired browsers live in `browsers.json` next to `config.json` (`0600`, written like `secrets.json`), with only a SHA-256 of their secret, so the file alone opens nothing. `/` and `/ws` need the cookie; `/ws` also needs an `Origin` equal to the `Host` or `X-Forwarded-Host` it came with, against cross-site WebSocket hijacking.
