A request that came through a proxy (`X-Forwarded-For`, `X-Forwarded-Host` or `Tailscale-Headers-Info`, which `tailscale serve` adds) must carry `Tailscale-User-Login` naming the node's owner, from `tailscale status --json`, so another member of a shared tailnet cannot pair, and neither can a tagged device, which `serve` sends without a login; a local process could forge those headers, so they back the cookie up rather than replacing it. The cookie is scoped to the host, not the port (cookies have no port), so another web service on the same machine name would receive it; a Mac that is itself a tagged node has no owner login and every phone gets the other-account page. Both are in the guide. Settings › Remote lists how many browsers are paired; `Enter` there forgets them all and drops their connections. The log says when a browser pairs, connects and goes, and what was refused; never a token or a cookie.
- **Tailscale** (`web/tailscale.rs`): cornercase drives the CLI the user already has (`tailscale` on the `PATH`, else the macOS app's bundled binary), so it never holds a Tailscale credential. Switching `remote` on runs `tailscale status --json` (connected, the node's MagicDNS name, HTTPS certificates on for it, its owner's login) and then `tailscale serve --bg --yes --https=8443 http://127.0.0.1:<port>`; off runs `tailscale serve --https=8443 off`. Both go through one worker thread in the order they were asked for: on threads of their own, switching on and off quickly could run `off` before `serve --bg`, which survives reboots, and leave the port published with the setting off. A command that passes the limit is killed, not left behind. 8443 rather than 443, so a user's own `serve` on 443 is left alone; `--yes` and a closed stdin, since `serve` would otherwise wait on a prompt, and a 15 s limit. `--bg` survives reboots and points at a dead port while no server runs (the phone gets a 502 until cornercase starts). What is missing is said in Settings and in the dialog (not installed, not logged in, HTTPS certificates off for the tailnet with the admin console's address), and opening ` Remote ` tries again on the listener it already has. Embedding Tailscale was rejected for now: the libtailscale wrappers load a Go runtime and need Go to build, and tailscale-rs is a preview; the phone needs the Tailscale app either way. A public tunnel (Funnel, Cloudflare) was rejected: a public address to the shells.
- **UI**: `remote` in `config.json` (Settings › Remote, section Browser, off by default; `remote_port` has no row). While it is on, or a Telegram or Discord bot is set (see Telegram and Discord), ` Remote ` sits at the right of the ` Usage ` row (`ui::remote_button`, like ` ↑ x.y.z ` on the settings row), so no layout changes for anyone who uses neither; it is left out when the row has no room for both, as in the compact layout's thirds below about 51 columns (a phone showing the compact layout is usually the remote end already). It shows a green `●` while a browser is attached or you are away on a chat app. It opens a menu (`MenuAction::Browser`, `MenuAction::Away`, like ` + New `'s): **open in your phone's browser** and, per chat app, **continue on Telegram** / **stop messaging on Telegram** (the same for Discord); one that is not set up reads **set up …** and opens its row in Settings › Remote (`App::open_settings_on`). They live in one tab and behind one button because they answer the same need, using cornercase away from the computer: the browser to work there, a chat app to be told. The browser's dialog (`Overlay::Remote`, modal) draws the QR code in half blocks with fixed black and white (palette 16 and 231, never themed, so it scans on any theme), with four modules of quiet zone, as the QR standard asks, the address as text under it, and who the phone must be logged in as; a window too short for the code shows the address alone. The dialog closes when a browser pairs with its code, with a `a browser connected` toast.

## Telegram (`telegram/`, `messenger/`, `app/messenger.rs`, `app/messenger/`)

- **Why**: once you leave the desk, what agents need from you is short (an answer, a look at what one wrote), and you want to be told rather than to check. Browser access (#178) shows the whole window, but needs Tailscale and a screen to read it on; a chat is pushed to you, works on mobile data, and a reply is one line. **WhatsApp was rejected**: its Cloud API needs a Meta business account, a number of its own, a public webhook (the public address Browser access rejected), a 24-hour window and paid replies, and the unofficial linked-device libraries break its terms and can get the user's own number banned.
- **One bot per user, long polling**: the user makes a bot with @BotFather and pastes its token; the server asks Telegram for updates (`getUpdates`, 30 s) and sends with the Bot API, all over outgoing HTTPS. No webhook (a public address to the server) and no relay run by cornercase (every message would go through it).
- **The model** (`telegram/mod.rs`): `Bot` makes the calls through `http::post_within`, which leaves the statuses to Telegram's own rules: 401 or 404 (a malformed token) is a refused token, 409 another program reading the bot, 429 its `retry_after`. The token is scrubbed from every error, since ureq can quote the URL that holds it. `Order` (in `messenger/`, like the rest of this list) is what the app sends, `Update` what it reads (messages and button presses), `Tap` the button data (`<run>:<verb>:<ids>`, at most 64 bytes, with a random nonce per server run: pane ids restart with the server, so an older button is refused as stale rather than pressing keys in another pane), `choices` the numbered options at the bottom of a screen (read upwards until `1.`, so a numbered list in the conversation above is not taken), `Saved` the bot's name and the paired chat. `html.rs` turns an agent's Markdown into the HTML Telegram takes (bold, italics, code, `pre`, http links only, lists as bullets; the agent's own HTML is escaped), in messages of at most 4000 UTF-16 units (Telegram allows 4096 after entities), at most four, the rest left in cornercase; a block too long is cut by lines, keeping code in `pre`. A title goes in front of the first one only when the two fit in 4096 (`html::fits`), else on its own, and a screen under a title gets the room the title leaves (`html::room`); places in titles are cut at 120 characters. Two threads run per bot: the poller (`telegram/link.rs`, backoff from 1 to 60 s, messages older than a minute before it started dropped, so a reply sent while the server was down does not land in a pane that now has another id) and the sender (`messenger/link.rs`), in order, waiting out `retry_after` (at most 60 s, three tries), sending again as plain text when Telegram cannot parse the entities, and answering each sent message's id (`Sent`) so replies can be routed. A link has an id and its answers are dropped once it is replaced; dropping it stops the poller after its current poll.
- **Telegram is an internal client of the commands**: `App::run_request` (split out of `request`) takes a parsed `Request`, and Telegram sends `send`, `keys`, `start` and `last-message` with client ids from `1 << 62`; `answer` hands those to `chat_answered`. So a reply gets every refusal and the confirmation `send --enter` has, and `server.log` shows Telegram's actions as the commands they are. Calling the app's methods directly was rejected: the refusals and the confirmation would have to be repeated.
- **Pairing**: the phone row opens the Remote dialog (`ui::RemoteAccess` got a title) with a QR of `https://t.me/<bot>?start=<token>`, the token from `web::auth::Pairing` (single use, ten minutes, compared in constant time, dropped when the dialog closes). A `/start <token>` from a private chat pairs that chat and user; groups cannot. `telegram.json` (0600, next to `config.json`) keeps the bot's name and the chat, `secrets.json` the token (`telegram_bot_token`). Only that chat and user are heard; anything else is ignored and logged by chat id. A new bot forgets the chat, and pairing again replaces it.
- **Away changes only by hand** (the user's choice, over always on, which is noise at the desk, and on after idle minutes, which guesses): **Remote › continue on Telegram** and **stop messaging on Telegram**, `o` in the keys menu (**away on chat apps**: back on every platform that is away, else away on every ready one; with none ready it opens the first bot row in Settings), `cornercase away [--off]` (an `away` request answering `{"away": bool}`), `/away` and `/back`. Nothing turns it off by itself: a first version ended it on any use of a window, which also ended it when you only looked at a phone's browser, and stopped the messages without telling. Forgetting to turn it off costs some messages at the desk; the `●` on ` Remote ` shows it is on. Away is refused while Telegram refuses the token (the link stopped), so it never looks on while nothing reaches the chat; the menu and `o` then lead to the bot's row. The bot says when it starts and ends. Not saved: a server restart turns it off. Toasts and desktop notifications are unchanged.
- **When to message**: while away and paired, an agent's state (`agents::activity::Pane::state`) has to hold for a second (`SETTLE`). Turning to waiting sends `! claude needs you · project › workspace`, the last 15 lines of its screen and a button per option found, plus `esc` and `screen`. A turn that ends (idle, done, failed, or a background shell) after the agent was busy (working or waiting at any read since its last settled state) sends `✓ claude finished` and its last message from its record, or its screen when there is none. An explicit OSC `done` also sends a finished notice without an observed working sample, including for a visible tab, whose UI mark is already acknowledged; Telegram reads `Pane::reported_done` rather than losing that signal to `Pane::state`. Reported `idle` cancels a turn without a finished notice. A reported failure also sends `! claude failed`, even without an observed working sample. Busy is remembered rather than compared with the settled state, so a turn shorter than the second still counts. This is not the desktop notifications' rule: the visible tab never notifies there, but away, a visible tab proves nothing. The watch runs whenever a phone is paired, so turning away on does not report an old change.
- **Replies**: a reply to the bot's message goes to the pane that message was about (message ids kept in memory, at most 256, filled from `Sent`); one that replies to nothing goes to the only agent, else the bot asks which, with a button per agent holding the text (at most 16 held). Before `send`, an agent that is waiting gets its buttons again (a text would answer its question), Claude Code's survey a button for `0`, a dialog `screen` and `esc`. A 👍 reaction says the agent took the text; a refusal comes back as a reply. A digit pressed on a question removes that message's buttons, so an answer is not pressed twice. **The next question** (#248): an agent can stay `waiting` from one question to the next (Claude Code's `AskUserQuestion` with several questions, a permission prompt right after another one, when the turn between them is shorter than `SETTLE`), so no change of state says a new one is on screen, and the bot sent only the first. Each watch keeps the question it last sent (`notices::question`: the excerpt the notice shows, with `messenger::MARKS` trimmed from each row, so moving the selection is not a new question; `None` when the screen has no numbered options). A pane that is still waiting is looked at again only when input reached it since that notice (`Term::input_at`, from a button, a reply or the window), it printed after that input and since it was last looked at (so a screen drawn before the input is never taken for its answer), and it has been quiet for `SETTLE`; a question that differs is sent like the first. So a redraw of the same question sends nothing, a screen that keeps changing sends nothing until it settles, and there is at most one new notice per input. The choices alone were rejected as the key: two permission prompts in a row offer the same options. A next question whose excerpt is identical to the one sent (the same 15 lines) is not sent again: it cannot be told from the same question left on screen after an answer the agent ignored, and the screen above a new question (the previous tool result) almost always differs. Watching the screen without the input condition was rejected too: anything the agent redraws while it waits would become a message. A question without numbered options (free text) gets no second notice, since nothing on its screen tells it apart from other output.
- **Commands**: `/status` lists the agents in sidebar order with the tabs' icons, model and context, and per agent buttons to write to it (a forced reply), show its last 40 lines, or press `esc` (at most 25 agents, models cut at 40 characters, in as many messages as the limit needs with the buttons on the last, `html::lines`). `/new` asks in turn for the project, the workspace or a new worktree (only in a repository root) and its branch, the agent when the default is `auto` (installed ones only), and the prompt, then sends `start` without focus, so the window stays where it is. `control::Start` got `project`, used with `worktree` so the worktree is made in the project chosen, not the one shown; the command line does not set it. Topics in private chats (Bot API 9.3 and later) were left out: they need a setting in @BotFather and one regression was reported for them; replies work everywhere.
- **Never logged**: texts and the token. The log says what kind of message or button came in and its size (`message kind=reply bytes=14`, `button tap=keys`). Chats with bots are not end-to-end encrypted, so Telegram keeps what agents write; the guide says so.
- **Settings** (Remote page, section Telegram, under the browser's section, then section Discord with the same two rows, `settings::Row::Bot(Via)` and `Row::Chat(Via)`): `bot` (a masked token field checked with `getMe` on a job; `Backspace` removes it) and `phone`, once a bot is set (`Enter` pairs, `Backspace` forgets). `Settings::removal` replaced `removable` and returns the action, for tokens and these two rows.
- **Simulation**: the landing's has neither Tailscale nor a chat app: its Remote tab shows the Browser, Telegram and Discord sections, and their rows, like its `o`, say they need a real cornercase.
- **Shared with other chat bots** (#265): a Discord bot is to do what this one does, and both can be paired at once, so what does not depend on Telegram moved out of it before a second platform exists. `messenger/` holds the orders, updates, buttons (`Tap`), choices, the paired chat (`Saved`, one file per platform, `telegram.json` unchanged) and `Via`, one identity per platform whose exhaustive arms call into the platform's folder, as `agents::Known` does for agents: its name and log target, token key, file, API, pairing link and code, the size of a button's data, and its `Markup`. `Markup` is the formatting the app writes with (escape, bold, a screen in `pre`, an agent's Markdown, lists), which each platform renders and splits by its own limits; `telegram::html::Html` keeps the HTML and the 4096 above. A link starts from two halves: the platform's listener (Telegram's poller) and a `Transport` that performs one order and says what a failure means (`Next`: wait, try again, or done), which the shared sender loops on with three tries; the transport keeps its state across the tries of one order (`Transport::Attempt`), so Telegram's plain-text fallback, once taken, holds across a `retry_after` wait, as before the move, and its `message is not modified` stays in its `Transport`. Each link event comes with its `Via`. The app keeps one `Messenger` per `Via` (`App::messengers`), each with its own link, routes, watches, drafts and away, and every method of `app/messenger.rs` and `app/messenger/` takes the `Via` it answers for; request client ids get a range per platform (`CLIENTS_PER_VIA`), so `chat_answered` finds the one that asked. `cornercase away` turns away on for every paired platform, succeeds when at least one went away (the others' refusals are dropped; the first is answered only when none could) and `--off` turns it off for all; its answer names the platforms now away (with `--off`, the ones it turned off) as `platforms`, which the command prints (`away: agents message you on Telegram until you turn it off`, or `back: nothing was away` when `--off` found none), and an older server's answer without them can only be Telegram's, so it is printed as `Telegram`; the `o` key, turning several platforms on at once, toasts the ones that went away and why the others did not in one line; the Remote menu has an entry per platform. That move changed nothing a user sees; Discord (below) then added its rows in Settings, and `o` and the help texts now speak of every platform.
- **Tests**: `app::tests::control_requests::telegram` drives the app with captured orders (`Link::capture`), updates made by the test and the fake Claude Code (`Agent`, moved up to `control_requests` to be shared); `telegram::link` runs the threads against `FakeHttp`; `messenger::tests` keeps the buttons, choices and the saved chat. Checked live with the real binary against a local fake Bot API (pairing, away, notices, replies, buttons, `/new` with a worktree), not against Telegram itself.

## Discord (`discord/`, `app/messenger/pairing.rs`, #265)

- **Why**: a Telegram bot needs a Telegram account, and many developers already live in Discord all day (their teams', their projects' servers). It does what the Telegram bot does (see Telegram) through the shared `messenger/` and `app/messenger/` code: one `Via::Discord`, its arms in `messenger/mod.rs`, and nothing in the app that names the platform but the pairing job. Telegram and Discord can be paired and away at the same time, each with its own `Messenger`.
- **One app per user**: the user makes an application in the Discord Developer Portal, turns on **User Install** and pastes its bot token (`discord_bot_token` in `secrets.json`; `discord.json`, 0600, keeps the bot's name and the chat like `telegram.json`). Like Telegram, no bot run by cornercase: every message would go through it, and its token would reach every user's shells. `CORNERCASE_DISCORD_API` points the REST calls at another server, for tests.
- **Pairing by the app's owner, without a code**: Telegram pairs whoever sends `/start <token>`; Discord has no link that carries a payload to a DM, and a bot can only message someone it shares a server with or who installed it. A user install adds the app to the owner's own account, after which the bot can open a DM with them without any shared server (checked live with a prototype). So the phone row opens the Remote dialog with a QR of the install link (`discord::install_link`: `oauth2/authorize?client_id=…&integration_type=1&scope=applications.commands`), and while that dialog is open a job (`app/messenger/pairing.rs`, every `PAIR_EVERY`, 3 s, one in flight) asks Discord for the app's owner (`/oauth2/applications/@me`, the team's owner for a team app), opens a DM with them and sends the welcome there. Until the app is installed Discord answers `50007` (cannot message this user), which keeps waiting quietly, and a rate limit (429) waits quietly too, for as long as its `retry_after` asks before the next try (`messenger::Owned::Later`), instead of showing in the dialog and retrying every 3 s; any other error is shown in the dialog and logged once per distinct error. Success saves the chat, closes the dialog and toasts `Discord connected with <name>`; the bot already sent the welcome, so the app does not send it again. Only the owner's DM is heard: a message from anyone else, or in a server, is ignored. A code typed into the DM was rejected: the owner is the only person who can pair anyway, since only they hold the token, so the code proved nothing and cost a step on the phone.
- **The app's id is read from the token**: its first segment is the bot's id in base64, which is also the application's id, so the install link and the webhook for ephemeral answers need no call; a token that does not decode falls back to `/users/@me` for the webhook and shows `the token does not name its app` instead of a QR.
- **A private app, said in the window** (`discord::advice`): a public bot can be added by anyone who finds its install link or id; it would then share servers with strangers and receive their DMs. The bot only listens to its owner's DM, but a private app (**Public Bot** off, which Discord accepts only once **Install Link** is **None**) leaves nobody else a way in, and **User Install** is what lets the owner pair at all. The guide lists the steps, and the window says them too: the token field's label names the three settings, and the token check, every pairing read and one read when the link starts (at server start and after a new token; `App::read_advice`, a job, never polled, Discord only) of `/oauth2/applications/@me` look at `bot_public` and `integration_types_config` (key `1`, user install). A public app or one without user install puts a short line in the `bot` row's note in place of `connected` (`turn on User Install in the Developer Portal`, else `public app: Install Link to None, then Public Bot off`), after `away · ` while away (`connected · away…` would push the advice off the row), short enough that both fit beside a ten-letter bot name at the 110-column capture width, and at the bottom of the pairing dialog; the next read after fixing it in the portal clears it, without pasting the token again. It never blocks pairing: a public app still works, and the owner may keep it public on purpose. A read that fails says nothing and changes nothing, whether it is the token check's, a pairing read's or the link's (`messenger::Advice::Unread`, apart from `Read(None)`, a read that found nothing to advise), so a 429 while pairing does not make the advice flicker, and the advice is kept in memory only: the read at the link's start brings it back after a server restart, so an already paired public app does not show `connected`.
- **The Gateway** (`discord/gateway.rs`): Discord delivers messages only over its WebSocket Gateway (no long polling), so one listener thread per bot opens it (`/gateway/bot` for the address, `v=10`, JSON, TLS through tungstenite's rustls with webpki roots, already a dependency for browser access) with the `DIRECT_MESSAGES` intent alone (`1 << 12`). Message content arrives in DMs without the privileged Message Content intent, so the user never has to turn one on in the portal. It identifies on hello, sends heartbeats at the interval Discord gives (the first after a random part of it, as Discord asks), beats at once on op 1 without waiting for the last beat's acknowledgement (only the scheduled beat checks it, so an op 1 right after a beat is not taken for a dead connection), and reconnects when a scheduled beat finds the last one unacknowledged. A dropped link, op 7 (reconnect) and op 9 with `d: true` resume the session (op 6 with the last sequence, at `resume_gateway_url`), so nothing sent meanwhile is lost; op 9 with `false`, 4007 and 4009 identify anew after 2 s; 4004 is a refused token (`Event::Rejected`, like Telegram's 401); 4010-4014 (bad intents, sharding, API version) stop the listener with one problem shown, since retrying cannot fix them. Resuming rather than identifying each time also because Discord allows 1,000 identifies a day per bot. Other failures back off from 1 to 60 s with `messenger::link::backoff`; messages older than the listener's start are dropped, as Telegram's are.
- **Buttons are acknowledged on the Gateway thread**: Discord shows `This interaction failed` unless a press is answered within 3 s, and the sender may be waiting out a rate limit, so the listener acks each press itself (callback type 6, deferred update: nothing visible changes) before handing it to the app. The ack has its own 3 s limit (`ACK_TIMEOUT`) rather than the 20 s of other calls: past 3 s Discord refuses it anyway (`10062`), and the thread neither reads nor beats meanwhile; a rate limit of at most a second is waited out and the ack tried once more. The texts the app answers a press with (`stale`, a refusal) go as ephemeral follow-ups (flag 64) through the interaction's webhook, seen only by the owner; an empty answer sends nothing.
- **Messages**: 2,000 characters, in Discord's Markdown (`discord/markdown.rs`, the `Markup` for this platform): bold as `**`, a screen in a fenced block (a ``` inside it broken with a zero-width space), split at 1,900 by blocks without breaking a fence (closed and reopened with the same opener; a `~~~` fence, which Discord does not draw as code, becomes one of backticks, with the ``` inside it broken the same way), at most four messages and `*… the rest is in cornercase*`, like Telegram's. No mentions are parsed (`allowed_mentions` empty), so an agent writing `@everyone` pings nobody.
- **Buttons**: at most 5 per row and 5 rows per message, labels cut at 80; a row of more is split, and rows past the fifth go in following messages (`…`) with the rest, so `/status` with many agents keeps every button. A send that fails part way remembers how many calls went out and the ids they got (`discord::Sent`), so a retry after a rate limit does not send the first messages twice. Every message of a send is reported (`Transport::more`, one `Sent` event each with the send's tag, which is kept until 256 newer ones push it out rather than dropped on the first), so a reply to a continuation routes like a reply to the first message. Editing and removing buttons only touch the one message the order names, with the first five rows of its keyboard, never the rest of its send: `/status` is never edited, so the buttons of its continuations stay and still act on what they name; a press on a continuation of the agents to pick for a held text edits that continuation, and the buttons left on the other messages then find the text delivered and do nothing.
- **Slash commands** (`Order::Commands`, sent when a link starts, as Telegram's `setMyCommands`): the first version had none, and `/status`, `/new`, `/away` and `/back` were typed as text. Testing it live showed why that fails: Discord's client autocompletes a typed `/` with the slash commands of every other app the user has (`/new` became `/wordle`), so the message could not be sent. So the bot registers the four from `messenger::COMMANDS` with one bulk overwrite (`PUT /applications/{app}/commands`, the app's id from the token), which also drops a command removed later, each with `integration_types: [1]` (user install, the only way the app is installed) and `contexts: [1]` (only the DM with the bot, so they never show in servers or other DMs). A failure is logged like any undelivered order and the commands keep working as typed text where the client lets them through. On the Gateway a command is an `INTERACTION_CREATE` of type 2, turned into an `Update::Message` whose text is `/<name>` from the invoking user in that channel, so the app handles it exactly like the typed one (paired check, closing the open question). Discord shows `The application did not respond` unless it is answered within 3 s, so the listener acknowledges it on its own thread like a press, with a deferred ephemeral message (type 5, `flags: 64`), and once the app has the update deletes that `thinking…` placeholder (`DELETE /webhooks/{app}/{token}/messages/@original`, the app's id from the interaction), so the chat holds only the bot's ordinary messages. Answering with the bot's reply itself (type 4) was rejected: the app makes its reply later, on the sender thread, and a message sent through the interaction's webhook is not one the rest of the code edits and replies to by its channel and id like the others. Ephemeral, so if the deletion fails only the user sees the leftover placeholder. A command that cannot be acknowledged comes in all the same.
- **Replies**: a reply to the bot's message routes to its pane, as on Telegram. Discord has no forced reply (`Via::forces_reply`), so a message that asks for one (`ask`: the prompt of `/new`, a branch's name, **write**) ends with a small `reply to this message` line, cut so the whole stays within 2,000 characters, and the bot remembers it as the open question (`Messenger::open_ask`). In a DM the natural answer is a plain message, which without this would go to the only agent running (typing the new agent's prompt into the old one) or ask which agent. So a plain message that is not a command and replies to nothing routable answers the open question; the question is closed once answered, replaced by a newer one, by any command, by a reply that answers it, by any newer bot message about an agent (a notice, a screen, a refusal: a plain message sent after `✓ codex finished` is meant for codex, not for the prompt of a `/new` asked before it, which would start an agent), and after ten minutes (`ASK_FOR`), so a message sent long after is not taken for an answer. Telegram keeps its forced reply and never has an open question.
- **Rate limits** are per route on Discord: a 429 gives its `retry_after`, which the shared sender waits out (at most 60 s, three tries) before trying that order again; the bot sends one order at a time, so it never needed to track buckets ahead.
- **Not end-to-end encrypted**: Discord keeps what agents write, like Telegram; the guide says so. Texts and the token are never logged, the token is scrubbed from errors and left out of `Debug`.
- **Checked**: live, a prototype paired by owner over a user install and talked over the Gateway, and the Gateway's hello was read from `wss://gateway.discord.gg`; the rest (sending, buttons, acks, slash commands, follow-ups, rate limits, resuming, close codes) only against fakes: `discord::tests` and `app::tests::control_requests::discord` (pairing through `50007` then success, a refused token in the dialog, a message and a button round trip, `/new` answered with a plain message while another agent runs, a reply to a continuation, `away` with one platform unpaired, `o` with both and with none, the advice read when a link starts and cleared when it finds the app private, kept when a pairing read fails, shown with away in the `bot` row); the Gateway against a local fake WebSocket (`discord::gateway::tests`: identify, heartbeats and op 1, a dead connection, resume on op 7 and op 9, identify anew on op 9 `false`, 4007 and 4009, refused tokens and intents, recovery, messages before the start, closing on stop).

## Panics (`panics.rs`, `server.rs`)

- An uncaught panic on the server's main thread ends the process, and every shell with it (`Term::drop` kills them), so `Server::serve` runs each `Step` under `catch_unwind` (`Server::contain`): the refresh, the answers to requests, the draw, the save and each event. The step that panicked is dropped, the loop goes on, and `App::report_bug` shows `cornercase hit a bug, see server.log` (`Error::Bug`'s text) with a red `✗` for `BUG_FOR` (6 s). Each panic still has to be fixed where it happens; this only keeps the shells alive.
- `App` keeps whatever the step had changed. A panic in input or drawing also resets the interaction (`App::reset_interaction`: overlay, compact menu, hover, drags, selection, the TODO field, the filter's focus), since a dialog that panics while it draws would otherwise freeze the screen for good. A background one (refresh, save, job answers, PTY output) does not, because one that repeats every tick would close every dialog as soon as it opens. A panic while drawing also starts every client's screen over (`reset_screen`), since ratatui's buffer is left half drawn. Saving the session and exiting was rejected: it kills the shells just the same.
- Background jobs start through `App::spawn_job` (`app/jobs.rs`), which begins the `log::Job`, runs the work under `panics::job`, which turns a panic into `Err(Error::Bug)`, lets the job's `answer` closure build the event (and apply its fallback and `Job::note` its result fields), ends the job with the work's outcome and sends the event if there is one. A busy dialog (creating a worktree, installing an update, checking a token, starting an issue) cannot be closed, so a job that never answered would leave `kill-server` as the only way out; issue lists, people, details, usage and changes would stay loading, and a worktree being removed would stay `removing…` (a panic in its job counts as a refusal and puts the row back). `↓n` counts a panic as 0 for every workspace it checked, like a git that fails, so an old count does not stay. The branch list and unchanged lines are caught too, but send nothing after a panic (nothing waits for them), and their end line says `failed`; a job whose answer has a fallback (the `↓n` count, the files panel's lists and searches) ends `failed` after a panic as well, where it used to end `done`; memory and Codex discovery already see the dropped sender (`Disconnected`) and start over.
- The server replaces the panic hook (`panics::log_to_stderr`; stderr is `server.log`, see Log): a timestamped `ERROR panic:` line with thread, location, message and a backtrace (`Backtrace::force_capture`; release builds keep function names, not lines). The first panic at a location is written in full, later ones there without the backtrace and only at 2, 4, 8… times, so one that repeats every tick cannot fill the disk. It writes with `writeln!` and ignores errors: `eprintln!` panics when writing fails, and a panic inside the hook aborts.
- Panics must unwind: with `panic = "abort"` in any profile every contained panic would kill the server again, so `panics.rs` does not build without `cfg(panic = "unwind")`.
- Not contained: a panic inside a libghostty-vt callback (`on_pty_write`, `on_clipboard_write`) crosses an `extern "C"` frame and aborts, so keep those callbacks trivial; a crash in Ghostty's Zig code is no Rust panic. A draw that panics for a reason the reset does not touch leaves the screen blank, while the shells keep running.

## Log (`log.rs`, `paths.rs`, `time.rs`, `app/trace.rs`)

- **Who reads it**: whoever fixes a bug, most often a coding agent told to look at the log. So it is a timeline of what the server did, detailed enough to turn into steps or a failing test; the user only sees it through `cornercase logs`. Before (#114) the server wrote only panics and about 15 failures, and bugs were found by reproducing them by hand.
- **Where**: `server.log` next to `session.json` (`$XDG_STATE_HOME/cornercase`, else `~/.local/state/cornercase`), or next to the socket when `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` is set (`paths::log`). It used to sit next to the socket in `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, which is cleared at logout and reboot, so the log of the crash that matters was gone. The client opens it as the server's stdout and stderr (early failures still land there), and `log::start` reopens it and `dup2`s it onto stderr, so panics, a stray `eprintln!` and Ghostty's own output share the file and the rotation.
- **Format**: one line per event, `2026-10-07T21:09:51.482Z INFO  worktree: add done ms=812 branch=fix-login`: UTC time (local time needs a time zone database; `time::Stamp` does the calendar by hand), level, target (`server`, `client`, `app`, `ui`, `control`, `launch`, `activity`, `worktree`, `changes`, `git`, `issues`, `files`, `images`, `usage`, `stats`, `awake`, `update`, `pr`, `star`, `search`, `log`, `panic`), a message, then `key=value` fields; a value with spaces, quotes or control characters is quoted with Rust's escapes, so a line never breaks. Panic reports are the one exception: their first line has the time and `ERROR panic:`, and the message and backtrace follow on lines of their own, since a backtrace escaped onto one line cannot be read. Text rather than JSON lines: it reads well in a pane with `tail -f`, and `grep` and agents take it as is.
- **Own writer, not `tracing`**: `log::info!` and friends (`warning!`, since `warn` clashes with the built-in attribute) check the level, format the line on the caller and `try_send` it to a writer thread through a bounded queue (4096 lines); a full queue drops the line and the writer says how many it dropped, so a stuck disk never blocks the server loop. Rotation has to move stderr with the file, which `tracing-appender` cannot do, and spans were not needed: `log::Job` gives a background job its start, end, duration and error. A job logs at its level; a failed `Info` one becomes `WARN`, and a `Debug` one that took a second or more (`log::SLOW`) becomes `INFO`, so slow git runs stand out without debug.
- **Size**: past `MAX_BYTES` (8 MiB) the writer renames the file to `server.log.1` (replacing the older one) and starts a new one, `dup2`ing it onto stderr again, so the log stays under 16 MiB however long the server runs. When the rename fails the file is emptied instead, with a line saying why, so the cap holds anyway. The panic hook's throttling stays.
- **Levels**: `info` by default, cheap enough to be always on; `CORNERCASE_LOG=debug` (read by the server when it starts) adds every event the loop handled (keys as their name only, `a character` for text; mice without moves; pastes as their length), connections, frequent jobs (the diff every second, `↓n`, the files panel) and frames slower than 16 ms. A config key was left out: it is a switch for a bug hunt, set when starting the server.
- **What is logged**: the server's start (version, build, pid, OS, socket, session path, level) and stop, clients attaching (size, the terminal's XTVERSION name, now sent in `Hello`, the notification channel, whether its background is known), rejected and gone, the shared size changing, kill-server, restart and signals; the restore (how many projects, complete or not, each tab that failed and why) and saves (debug); every command for scripts with its safe fields (`Command::fields`) and its answer or refusal with the time it took and how a wait ended; each step of a `Launch` (stage before and after, bytes written, abandoned); every agent status change per pane, and each notification and the channel it went out on; background jobs; shell exits with their status; menu actions, moved rows and panes, renames, settings changed (keys only), issues started; the errors toasts show; panics and the step they hit, and any loop step slower than 100 ms.
- **Changes are found, not announced** (`app/trace.rs`): after every step that can change the app (a refresh, input, a request, a job's answer; not PTY output, drawing or saving) `App::trace` compares the hierarchy (groups, projects, workspaces, tabs, panes, with their parent and phase) and the focus, overlay, side panel, compact menu and toast with what it saw last, and logs what opened, closed, moved or changed phase, closed ones from the inside out. One place covers the UI, the commands and background answers alike, so a new code path cannot forget to log a tab it opens. It is a merge of two sorted lists of ids, with labels read only for new items (a workspace's label reads `.git/HEAD`), so a step costs about 6 µs with 80 items (release build). The same diff feeds `cornercase events` (see Commands for scripts and agents): it yields `Change`s, of which the log takes what opened, closed, moved or changed phase, and the events what opened, closed, changed agent state or ended a program. It runs while the log is at `info` (the default) or someone follows events.
- **Never logged**: typed characters (debug names only the other keys, such as `Enter` or `Esc`, and says `a character` for the rest), pasted text (only its length), PTY output, screen contents, prompts, issue texts and titles (the branch of an issue's worktree carries a slug of its title, and is logged like any branch: it is the workspace's name, its folder and its label everywhere else), search queries, TODO texts, agents' messages, the cells a surface streams, tokens and environment variables. Commands log what they carry as sizes or yes/no (`send` its bytes, `start` whether it has a prompt, `wait --until text` without the pattern, `read --last-message` whether it found one), and a request that cannot be parsed logs only its size, since serde's error may quote it. Paths and the names of groups, projects, workspaces, tabs and branches are fine: they say where something happened, and the log stays on the user's machine, readable only by its owner (created `0600`, and set back to it when an older log is reopened). `e2e::server::the_server_log_tells_what_happened_but_never_what_was_typed` types, pastes and sends markers at debug level and checks the log has none of them.
- `cornercase logs [-n N] [--follow] [--path]` reads the file directly (no server needed): the last lines, from `server.log.1` too when the new file is short, then follows it across a rotation (`log::Follower` reopens when the inode changes or the file shrinks). The agent skill names it, so an agent debugging cornercase knows where to look. The client appends throttled `WARN` lines with target `client` for input errors and other threads' panics. For `cornercase remote`, these go to the log on the machine running the window. Left for later: a `bug-report` bundle.

## Commands for scripts and agents (`cli.rs`, `cli/`, `control.rs`, `app/control.rs`, `app/control/`, `app/events.rs`)

- `clap` (derive) parses every command, the old ones included; `cornercase` alone still opens the UI and `server` is hidden. Help texts go in `about`/`help`/`after_help` attributes, never `///`. Features: `std`, `derive`, `help`, `usage`, `error-context`, `suggestions` (no colours, no wrapping). Exit status: 2 for wrong usage (clap's), 1 for errors and timeouts (the message says which). A `requires` pointing into an exclusive `#[group]` is satisfied by any member of the group (it conflicts with the one given), so `--remove-worktree` uses `conflicts_with_all` instead.
- One request per connection: `ClientMessage::Request(json)`, answered by one `ServerMessage::Response(json)`, both after the existing variants (`protocol::tests::compatibility`). JSON, not postcard, so a command and a server from different releases still work together after `cornercase update`: unknown fields are ignored (`#[serde(default)]` everywhere) and an unknown command answers an error naming the server's version (`Command::NAMES`, checked before parsing). Requests skip `Hello`'s build check. A server from before them reads `InvalidData` and answers `Rejected`, which the command turns into "too old, restart it". The connection is an ordinary client without a size, so it gets no frames and never counts as a use (it never changes the shared size). `events`, `watch` and `surface` are the requests answered by a stream (see Following events, Watching the session and Streaming a tab's terminals).
- Ids come from the app's counter, so they last one server run. Every shell gets `CORNERCASE_PANE=<id>` and `CORNERCASE_SERVER=<token>` (`App::pane_env`, with `control::server_token`: the server's pid and start time; `term` knows nothing of them, `App::spawn` and `App::open_in_editor` pass them in `SpawnOptions::env`); the command sends both, and a server takes the pane as the `caller` only when the token is its own: every server numbers its panes from 1, so a pane of another server (reached through `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`) would be taken for one of its own, and `send --enter` would type into it. Inside a pane the defaults are the caller's pane, tab, workspace and project; outside, the active ones (`Here`); with `--view ID` they are what that view shows, before the caller's pane (an explicit flag beats the inherited `CORNERCASE_PANE`; view ids come from this same counter). `--tab` means the tab's agent pane, else its active one. `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` is never added to panes: it would also move `config.json` and `session.json`; shells inherit the server's environment, so `cornercase` in a pane finds its server anyway.
- **A workspace by its branch** (#132): every command that takes `--workspace ID` (`new-tab`, `close`, `rename`, `focus`) also takes `--worktree BRANCH`, the flag `start` already had, so a script that started `start --worktree feat/x` closes it with `close --worktree feat/x --remove-worktree` instead of parsing `status` for the id. Only `start` makes the worktree when it is missing; the others only look it up. It is looked up in the project the defaults use (the caller's, else the shown one: `Report::workspace_on`), by the branch `git::branch` reads, among every workspace there and not only those with a worktree of their own, as `start --worktree` reuses whichever workspace has the branch open; so `--worktree main` finds the project's own checkout, and a plain workspace sharing it makes the name ambiguous. No match or several exit with 2 (`Error::WrongUsage`, which `main` turns into clap's `error: ` and status), listing the project's workspaces or the matching ones like `status` does, so the script or agent can pick an id. The lookup runs in the command, through a `status` request, and sends the usual request with the id: no protocol change, so it works against a server from before it, whereas a `worktree` field in the requests would be ignored by such a server (unknown fields are) and a `new-tab --worktree` would open its tab in the caller's workspace instead. The cost is a second request and the window between them, in which the workspace may close; the id request then fails as with a stale id. `--branch` as the name was rejected: `start` already says `--worktree`, and one flag for one meaning reads better in `--help`. A `--project` to look in another project was left out: `close` and `focus` already use `--project` for the item itself.
- **Nothing changes what the UI shows except `focus` and `--focus`** (with `--view` they move that view instead, and the window stays): the selection is shared by every client, so an agent opening a tab must not move everyone's view. The creation helpers (`push_tab`, `add_project`, `split_pane(.., focus)`) never touch the active indices; `add_tab`/`open_project` are the UI's focusing wrappers.
- A server that never had a window nor a `start-server` has no size and has not restored the session, so commands that create terminals refuse instead of making 1×1 ones (`area: None`), and `NO_SIZE` names `cornercase start-server` (or a window) as the way out. `start-server` (see Client / server) is the request a script sends first: the last name in `Command::NAMES`, so an older server refuses it by name and the caller knows it needs a window. Its answer goes through `refuse_or_answer` like every request, after the server took it from `App`'s outbox (`take_start`, as `take_restart`): the restore lives in `Server`, beside its `Saver`, so it is not done twice.
- Typing goes through `Launch`: `Launch::command` (new-tab/split: the shell step only), `Launch::enter` (send --enter: the submit step only), `Launch::new` with `prompt: None` (start without a prompt). A request that types answers once it is typed, so a following `send` cannot land before the command. The submit step presses Enter after 300 ms of quiet or at most 3 s (`SUBMIT_LATEST`): a working agent's spinner never goes quiet. Launches carry a `key`; `drive_launches` reports `(key, started)` to the requests.
- **A model and a mode per call** (`start --model M --mode NAME`, #263): through `agents::start_line`. The model takes the agent's own flag (`Known::model_flag`) and first removes every spelling of one already in `agent_args` (`--model x`, `--model=x`, `-m x`, `-m=x`; the `-mVALUE` form is not handled; `agents::with_model`, which a flow's roles use too), since clap-based CLIs such as Codex refuse a flag twice and start nothing; the mode is one of `agents::modes` by its name, and an unknown one is refused with the agent's modes. With either flag the request goes as `start-with`, which an older server refuses by name: it would drop the unknown fields from a plain `start` and start, say, `--mode plan` in the settings' mode that never asks. Without them it stays `start`, which every server takes. `flow list` and `flow run` are under Flows.
- Waits are pending requests in `App`, checked by `check_requests` in `refresh` after `watch_agents`, and answered through `take_answers`; `App::tick` asks the loop to wake for their deadlines, but only for instants still ahead (a past one would spin), and the server never waits longer than its 500 ms tick (a 10 s deadline once starved `watch_agents` of reads and the agent was never seen working). A client that leaves drops its waits; what it created goes on (a worktree being made still gets its tab and agent, a launch still types), since a timeout or a killed command does not prove the work was not done.
- `--until shell` needs the shell in the foreground, output since the last input written to the pane (`Term::input_at`, set by `Term::write`: the echo of a typed command at least) **and** a quiet pane: right after a command is typed the shell is still in the foreground until it forks, and its echo may not have been read yet. The quiet is 300 ms once the wait saw another program in the foreground after that input (`Watch::left`), else 1 s (`SHELL_UNSEEN`): a builtin, a command that ended before the wait, or a shell whose preexec hooks run before it forks. After a submission (the last Enter of a launch, `send --enter`, `keys` with `enter`; `Term::submitted`), a wait that ends on idle first needs the agent to react (`agents::activity::Pane::reacted`: working, or a change between two known activities, since then; a pane that lost and found its agent did not react) within 10 s, else it fails: the status file can still say idle (or the old `waiting`) for a moment after the Enter. This covers a separate `wait` after `send --enter` too, not only `--wait`. An agent that finishes faster than one 500 ms read fails that check; the message says to `read` the pane. A background shell (`Activity::Shell`) is not working there: an agent left with one at the Enter would otherwise count as reacting at once, and `--until turn-over` would end on its old turn before the status file shows the new one; it reacts once it is busy again or its activity changes.
- **Turn over with a background shell** (#127): the tab keeps showing a Claude Code agent whose turn is over but whose `run_in_background` Bash still runs as working (see Agent status), and the default `wait` keeps waiting, since the agent wakes up when the shell ends and its scripts may count on that. Scripts see the difference: `status --json` keeps `status: "working"` for the pane and its tab, so a script reading `working` behaves as before, and adds `background_shell: true` (a separate flag rather than a `shell` status, which would have broken those scripts; no count of shells, since the session file does not give one); the text shows `working (background shell)`, and the `activity` log line names `shell`. `wait --until turn-over` ends on what the default ends on (idle, done, waiting) and also on a background shell, printing `shell` (the session file's word; `--until shell` prints the same word for the shell prompt coming back, but one wait has one condition, so the two never meet). Waiting is included because the turn is over for the script's purposes: the agent needs an answer. The default wait's timeout says when only a background shell is left, naming `--until turn-over`. `start --wait` and `send --wait` take `--until` with every state `wait --until` has (`Start::until`, `SendText::until`, default `Stops`); `--text` and `--quiet` stay on `wait` alone. A request from a newer command to an older server: `wait` fails to parse the unknown state and says to update, while `start`/`send` lose the unknown `until` field and wait the default way. Codex, Gemini CLI and opencode have no such state: what they report (Codex's title and rollout turns, Gemini's title, opencode's messages) has no background process in it, and their turn ends when they say it does, so for them `turn-over` is the default wait. Guessing one from their child processes was rejected: a dev server an agent started on purpose would look the same.
- `send` refuses an agent that is `waiting` (an Enter would answer its question or permission prompt); `keys` is how to answer. Agent conditions fail on a pane without Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI or opencode (a status, or one of them detected in the foreground; another agent has no state to wait for; opencode never reaches `waiting`), and a pane cannot wait on its own agent or shell (it would wait for itself).
- **A dialog over the input box, and confirming a prompt was taken** (#128, checked live with Claude Code 2.1.295 and Codex 0.161.0 in a dev server): `send --enter` reported success while Claude Code had its `Shell details` panel open (a background shell's panel: `x to stop`, `Esc/Enter/Space to close`): the paste went into the panel, the Enter closed it, and `--wait` passed its reaction check because the background shell kept the agent `working`. `at_prompt` in `status --json` could not have told: it is whether the pane's *shell* is in the foreground (`Term::shell_in_foreground`, what the restart texts read), false whenever any program runs, and says nothing about an agent's input box; it stays as it is.
  - **No agent says that a dialog is open.** Claude Code's session file has the status (`waiting` covers its permission prompts and questions, already refused) and no field for panels, pickers, the transcript view or the prompt history (checked on 2.1.295), and its title neither; Codex and opencode keep their UI state in memory. The screen is the only signal. Claude Code draws its input box as a line that starts with `❯` right under a rule of `─` (`agents::claude::screen::input_box`); every panel and picker checked live (`Shell details`, `/model`, Ctrl+O's transcript, Ctrl+R's history) replaces that pair, and its suggestion list leaves it. Its shell mode (`!` at the start of the input) draws `!` instead of `❯`, and is refused too: a prompt pasted there would run as a shell command. `/model` (and other pickers) also set the session file to `waiting`, so that refusal comes first; the `Shell details` panel leaves it at `shell`, which is why the incident got through. A screen match breaks when the agent changes its UI, and a check that then refused every send would be worse than none, so it calibrates itself: it counts only once cornercase has seen the box drawn by the running process (`Term::input_seen`, the agent's pid, looked for on each agent read until found), so a version that draws it differently is never refused, only confirmed. Codex's composer marker `›` also marks the choices of its menus, and opencode's input is a box of block characters with nothing that tells it from its dialogs, so neither gets a check. `send` refuses a pane whose agent shows a dialog (`Term::dialog`), with or without `--enter` (a paste alone can press `x`); `--force` sends anyway. `--force` does not lift the `waiting` refusal: that one comes from the agent's own file, and `keys` is how to answer. `status` shows the same check as `dialog` in the JSON and `(dialog open)` in the text.
  - **So the confirmation is the safety net**, from the agent's own record of what it was sent: `send --enter` to Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI or opencode answers once the agent has recorded a prompt at or after the moment the request arrived (`agents::context::Pane::prompted`, the newest one its readers saw, kept across a new conversation so that `/clear` counts), and otherwise fails `CONFIRM_WITHIN` (10 s) after the Enter with `not confirmed: the prompt may not have been submitted`. Claude Code: a `user` line with `origin.kind` `human`, a typed slash command (`<command-name>`, as a `user` or `system` line) or shell input (`<bash-input>`), or a `queue-operation` `enqueue`, which it writes at once for a prompt sent while it works (task notifications and other agents' messages are enqueued too, and are told apart by their tag). Codex: an `item_completed` event whose item is a `UserMessage`. opencode: a `user` message, by its `time.created`. Times come from the record (`time::Stamp::parse` for the UTC times Claude Code and Codex write), so a reader that lags a tick cannot take an older prompt for the new one; a record without a time it can read counts from when it was read, so a change of format confirms a little too readily instead of failing every send, which would make scripts send their prompts twice.
  - A Codex that works keeps a prompt sent meanwhile in memory (`Messages to be submitted after next tool call`) and writes it when it takes it, after its next tool call or at the end of the turn (checked live on 0.161.0), so for Codex the 10 s count from the last moment it was seen working or waiting (`holds_prompts`), and `--timeout`, now allowed with `--enter` alone, bounds the wait. Claude Code and opencode write such a prompt at once (checked live: Claude Code 2.1.295 wrote the `enqueue` 5 s into a 20 s Bash call and took the prompt when the call ended, with a `remove` (`absorbed_mid_turn`) and a `queued_command` attachment that do not count again; opencode 1.18.35 writes a `user` row it shows as `QUEUED`), so for them the 10 s are not extended.
  - Matching the text was rejected: Claude Code wraps pastes in `<pasted_content>` tags, agents expand or shorten long pastes, and a confirmation that failed on a transformed text would make scripts send the prompt again; without it, only a prompt someone else submits in the same pane in those seconds can confirm a lost one. Reading the screen after the Enter (the issue's alternative) has the screen's fragility without a fallback. Claude Code's peer messaging socket (`messagingSocketPath` in its session file) would take a prompt without going through the input box, but it is undocumented and needs the token cornercase keeps out of panes. A pane without one of the four (a shell, an agent cornercase does not read) has nothing to confirm against, and `send --enter` returns once Enter is pressed, as before. `start` has no confirmation: a fresh agent has no dialog open, and its launch already waits for the screen.
  - The `agents::claude::fake::FakeClaude::CONTROL` script used by `app::tests::control_requests` and the fake Claude Code of `tests/e2e/commands.rs` writes a prompt record for each line it reads, with a time rounded up to the end of its second (`date` has no milliseconds on macOS), and leaves out what it reads while it shows its fake panel.
  - **Claude Code's feedback survey** (#143, checked live on Claude Code 2.1.296): Claude Code sometimes draws `● How is Claude doing this session? (optional)` and `1: Bad    2: Fine   3: Good   0: Dismiss` in the band right above its input box. The box stays and the session file stays `idle`, so neither check above saw it. One character alone in the empty box answers it (a digit, or what a French keyboard's number row types for one: `&`, `é`…), 400 ms after it is typed or at once on Enter: a pasted `2` rated the session `Fine`, left the box empty, and the Enter after it submitted nothing, so `send --enter 2`, the answer to an agent's numbered question, was lost. A longer text hides the survey and goes to the box, as when a person types over it. The surveys after `/compact`, in a long conversation and about a plugin draw the same answers under their own question (the plugin's adds `4: Unsure`).
  - `send` refuses any text while the survey shows, unless `--force`, and says to dismiss it with `cornercase keys --pane N 0` (`Esc` does not close it). Refusing only a one-character text, all it takes today, was rejected: which keys it takes is Claude Code's undocumented choice, and a longer text closes the survey on the user's behalf, which `send` never does to the agent's own UI. Dismissing it before sending was rejected for the same reason: `0` is an answer, and Claude Code reports it. The survey is rare (Claude Code spaces its appearances out), so a refusal costs one `keys 0`.
  - It is found by its shape, not its words (`agents::claude::screen::survey`): the answers line exactly as Claude Code draws it (two columns in, each answer in a cell of 10 columns, so the same words with other spacing, as in the issue, never match), at most three lines above the input box's rule (the `◐ medium · /effort` hint or a blank line sits between), and above it, through at most three lines indented by two (a wrapped question, the plugin's reason), a line starting with `● ` in the first column, the survey's bullet. Lines higher up never count, so the words in the conversation do not either; nor does a quote ending the last message: Claude Code indents a message under its own bullet and a code block further, and ends each turn with a `✻ … for 3s` line. A message ending with an exact copy of the survey, right above the box, would pass for it if a version dropped that line. Not recognised, so `send` goes ahead as before: a pane under about 44 columns (the answers wrap), the memory survey (blank lines inside its question), and what follows an answer `send` never gives: the thanks line (`Press [1] to tell us more`, gone after a few seconds) and the question asking to share the transcript (`y`, `n`, `d`).
  - `status` shows it as `survey` in the JSON, `(survey open)` in the text and the last column of `--panes` (after `path`, since 0.12.16 shipped the others and new columns only go at the end), apart from `dialog`: a dialog replaces the input box and closes when whoever opened it does, the survey sits above the box and waits for its `0`, so a script that sees `survey` knows what to press without reading the screen, and `dialog` keeps the meaning it had (the two never hold at once). A script that only checks `dialog` still meets `send`'s refusal, which names the key. `keys` sends digits as before: it is how to answer. `read` and the skill say what the survey is, so an agent reading a pane does not take it for the other agent's output.
  - Tests: `agents::claude::screen::tests::survey` holds screens copied from the live Claude Code, quotes of the survey in the conversation among them, and `agents::claude::fake::FakeClaude::CONTROL`, used by `app::tests::control_requests`, draws the survey above its box after a `survey` line and drops it on `0`. The live check ran Claude Code with `CLAUDE_FORCE_DISPLAY_SURVEY=1` (its switch to show the survey at start) and a scratch `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR`, against a local fake of the API.
- **Gemini sends**: confirm ordinary `send --enter` from a real user's parseable session timestamp. Reading a restored history must not count as a fresh prompt. Gemini queues input in memory while working and joins it with blank lines when it takes it. Share Codex's `holds_prompts`: extend the 10 s confirmation clock while working or waiting, bounded by the request's `--timeout`. Trimmed text starting with `/`, `?` or `!` is local input and may write no user record. Return after Enter without confirmation, avoiding spurious failures and duplicate commands. `--wait` still needs the usual reaction; inspect commands that open dialogs or leave status unchanged with `read`. Enter without text still confirms, because cornercase cannot know what is already in the composer.
- **Cursor sends**: `send --enter` is confirmed from a prompt the store records (`Pane::prompted`, a turn whose message was not there before), timed by the user message's `started_at_ms` when present and by the moment it was read otherwise, and fails `CONFIRM_WITHIN` after the Enter like the others. Cursor holds a prompt typed while it works, so it shares `holds_prompts`. `/` and `!` lines return after Enter without a confirmation. It has no input-box check: the screen was not seen live, and a check that refused every send would be worse than none. `read --last-message` reads the store (`Record::Cursor`), skipping thinking, tools and shell turns. All of this rests on the unverified store layout (see Context use): a different layout makes the confirmation fail and the last message unavailable, visibly, never a wrong answer.
- Gemini gets no input-box check. Its `>` marker also appears in picker help, YOLO/shell/search modes change it, and narrow or hidden footers change the layout. No shape proved as distinctive and self-calibrating as Claude Code's rule and composer. A fragile check could refuse every send or accept a picker. Refuse the real waiting title, confirm ordinary prompts from the record, and have scripts read other dialogs before sending.
- Gemini's last-message reader shares the bounded backwards iterator. Keep the last version of each id and stop at `$set.messages` boundaries, so superseded replies and removed history cannot reappear. Return the newest Gemini text with its own timestamp; omit thought parts and synthetic summary/context replies without a model, which are not shown as an answer.
- **Waiting on several panes** (#129): `wait` takes `--pane` and `--tab` more than once, with `--any` or `--all`, since an agent coordinating others had to run one background wait per agent and keep track of which one ended. Each target gets its own `Watch` with the same condition: `Stage::Several` holds them as plain `Pending` parts that `advance` moves like any other, so every condition, the reaction check after a submission and `--until turn-over` work the same on several panes; the `--timeout` is the request's, and its message says what each pane still being waited for is doing. `--any` answers with one pane, the first in the order given among those that ended on the same check: one line is what a script reads without parsing (`read -r pane ended`), and a wait on the rest hears about the others at once, since their condition still holds. `--all` keeps each pane's first ending rather than asking every condition to hold at the same moment, which would never end for agents sent more work meanwhile. A line is `<id> <ending>` (the matched line for `--text`, as for one pane), the id being the one given, so a tab's line starts with the tab's id (ids come from one counter, so it never names a pane), in the order given, `--pane` ones first (clap keeps no order across two options). The same id given twice is watched once. A pane that closes ends as `closed` and counts as ended: closing a tab is ordinary while agents run side by side, and failing would throw away the wait on the others. Other failures (the agent exited, it never took the Enter) fail the whole wait as they do for one pane, since something needs looking at. One target without `--any` or `--all` keeps the old answer and output; several without either is wrong usage (exit 2, checked after parsing since clap cannot count across two options) rather than a default, so a script says which it means. The answer is `Done::panes`, so `--json` prints `{"panes": [...]}`, an object like every other answer. On the wire it is a command of its own, `wait-several`, with the `Wait` fields plus `panes`, `tabs` and `all`: a server from before it ignores unknown fields and would wait on the caller's pane alone, while an unknown command is refused with `has no wait-several command; update cornercase and restart the server`. A wait on one pane still goes as `wait`, so it works with older servers.
- `read` and `--text` use libghostty's formatter: `screen_text` formats a selection of the active area, `last_lines` the whole screen with its scrollback (10 MB, see Scrollback); both unwrap soft-wrapped lines and drop trailing empty lines. `--text` re-reads the screen after new output, at most every 100 ms (`TEXT_EVERY`). `regex` has `unicode-perl` so `\d`, `\s`, `\w` work.
- **The last message** (`read --last-message`, #130): an orchestrator that wanted to know what an agent did read its screen, and had to strip the input box, the status lines and the footer, and could not tell Claude Code's grey prompt suggestion from typed text; the workaround was asking every agent to write its report to a file. `read --last-message` prints the agent's last message from its own record instead, whole even once it scrolled off: Claude Code's transcript, Codex's rollout, Gemini's session file and opencode's database, the ones the context line already follows (kept by `watch_agents`). The record is looked up for the agent found in the pane (`agents::context::Pane::record_for`), never taken as whichever one the pane holds: a program that execs another agent keeps its pid, so the pane can still hold the first agent's record next to the new one's, which would read a Claude transcript under Codex's name or refuse an opencode that already has its own; without a record of that agent, the pane answers as one whose record was not found yet. It is a request of its own, `last-message`, rather than a field of `read`: an older server drops an unknown field and would print the screen, which a script would take for the message, while an unknown command answers that the server has none. The reading runs on a thread (`agents::context::Record::last_message`, under `panics::job`) and answers through `AppEvent::LastMessage` (`Stage::Reading`): a transcript can hold tens of MB, and opencode's database waits up to 100 ms for a lock.
- **Searching** (#272): `cornercase search` finds the panes where some text was said (see Search).
- **Reading several last messages** (#156): `read --last-message` takes `--pane` and `--tab` more than once, so an orchestrator gets what all its agents did in one command after finding their ids with `status --panes`. The command sends the existing `last-message` request once per distinct target, panes first and then tabs, each in the order given; there is no protocol change, so servers that already support one last message support several. Text puts each message under a header with its actual pane id, agent, `turn_over` and `written` (`unknown` for metadata the server did not provide). JSON wraps the unchanged individual answers in `{"panes": [...]}`. A failure gets its own block or an object with `error` and the requested `pane` or `tab` id (a failed tab request has no resolved pane id); all targets are still read, and any failure makes the command exit 1 without another error on stderr. One distinct target, including an implicit one, keeps the previous output and error behavior. Without `--last-message`, more than one target is wrong usage (exit 2), checked before contacting the server: reading several screens was not needed.
- **What the last message is**: the newest assistant text, whatever the turn. Claude Code: the newest assistant `message.id` with text, its text blocks (one line each in the transcript) joined with a blank line; sidechains and `<synthetic>` lines are skipped, except an API error (`isApiErrorMessage`), which is what the agent last showed. Codex: the newest `response_item` `message` with role `assistant`, a progress note (`phase: commentary`) as well as a final answer; `task_complete`'s `last_agent_message` was not used, since an interrupted turn has none and it only comes at the end. opencode: the newest assistant message with text in the `part` table, its parts in `id` order as opencode itself shows them (`orderBy(part.id)`), leaving out parts marked `synthetic` (written for the model, never shown) and the summary `/compact` writes as a reply (`summary: true`), which is for the model too; Claude Code writes its summary as a user line, so it never counts either. While the agent works it is the newest so far; `--json` adds `turn_over` from the live status, with the rule of `--until turn-over` (`Condition::TurnOver`), and `written`, the record's own time (the line's `timestamp` for Claude, Codex and Gemini, `time.completed` for opencode, as `time::Stamp` writes it). Only the latest turn's text was rejected: an agent interrupted before it wrote anything would have none, and `written` says how old the message is. Errors exit 1 and say which: no Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI or opencode in the pane, its record not found yet (Codex writes its rollout from the first turn; two Codex, two Gemini with the same home, or two opencode, in one folder hide each other, as for the context line), no message yet, a record that cannot be read.
- Records are read backwards (`agents::context::message::Backwards`: 256 KiB chunks, the newest complete line first, a line still being written left out), at most 64 MiB, so a message behind megabytes of tool output is still found and a long transcript is never read whole. It is a plain iterator over a JSONL file's lines, for whatever else needs the end of one.
- **No input box in `read --json`**: the issue also suggested marking the input box's contents in `read --json`, and whether they are Claude Code's grey suggestion rather than typed text. Left out: it needs each agent's screen layout and the cells' styles, which change between versions, and what it was for, knowing what the agent said, is what the record gives.
- `status` text is a tree of `kind id  name  details  (marks)` lines for humans and agents; `--json` prints the server's JSON (serde_json sorts the keys).
- **A flat list of panes** (#141): `status --panes` prints one row per pane, since every script that coordinates agents started with the same recursive walk of `status --json` (a Python one-liner, or `jq '..|objects|select(has("at_prompt"))'`) to find panes by id, and the tree's text is for reading, not for `awk`. The rows are flattened in the command from the `Report` that `status` already answers (`Report::panes`), so there is no new request: it works against any server that answers `status`, an older one giving the defaults of the fields it lacks (`dialog` false, `at_prompt` null), and plain `status` and `status --json` do not change. A `panes` request computed in the server was rejected for that: a server from before it would refuse it. Each row (`control::PaneRow`) holds what a script filters on: the ids of the pane, its tab, workspace and project; program, agent, status, `background_shell`, `dialog`, `at_prompt`, model and context as `PaneInfo` has them; `caller`, `shown` (the pane the window shows, as `(shown)` in the tree, not every pane of a split tab on screen) and `active` (its tab's active pane); the tab's and workspace's names, the branch, `worktree` and the project's name; and the pane's path, in full. Groups are left out, as in `events`: nothing runs in them. Rows come in the sidebar's order (`Report::loose`, then `Report::grouped` per group, which the tree's text uses too). Filters (`--agent`, `--status`) were left out: `awk` and `jq` already do that on a flat list, and the issue's own alternative, `jq` recipes in the docs, would still leave every script copying the walk.
  - **Text**: tab-separated, under a header line of the column names, which are the JSON's keys. Tabs because names, models and paths hold spaces, which aligned columns would break for `awk` and `cut`. The header makes the output say what it holds, for an agent or a person reading it, and a filter on a value never matches it; `NR > 1` or `tail -n +2` drops it. The output is the same whether or not it goes to a terminal: switching like `gh` does (aligned with a header on a terminal, bare tab-separated values otherwise) would hide the header from an agent, whose tool is no terminal. A missing value (null, or an empty name) is `-`, never an empty cell: `read` with `IFS=$'\t'` treats tabs as whitespace and merges two in a row, which would shift every column after. Control characters in a name become spaces, so a row stays one line with its columns. The values are the JSON's (`true`/`false`, `context` as a number, absolute paths rather than the tree's `~`), taken from the serialized row (`cli::pane_table`), so the two forms cannot drift apart; a test checks that `PaneRow::COLUMNS` are the row's keys in order. New columns only ever go at the end, so `$7` stays the status. `status` writes through `cli::print_out`, which takes a closed pipe as the end (`quietly`, as `logs` does), since `status --panes | head` is how scripts peek and `println!` panics there.
  - **JSON**: `{"panes": [...]}` rather than a bare list, like every other answer (`wait --any`'s `panes`, `todo list`'s `todos`), which also leaves room for keys next to the list later. Every row has every key, `null` when there is no value, so `jq` sees one shape; keys come in the column order (the struct is printed directly, not through a `Value`, whose map would sort them).
- **Following events** (#131): `cornercase events [--pane ID …] [--json]` prints one line per event until the server stops (with `--pane`, until those panes have all closed), so a script that coordinates agents reacts to each change instead of polling `status` or waiting again after each `wait --any`. The events: an agent's state changed (`status`, with `agent`, `from` and `to`: `working`, `shell`, `waiting`, `done`, `idle`, or `null` for no agent, so the background shell of #127 is told apart; another agent taking the pane gives two events, the old one to `null` and the new one from `null`), the program in the foreground ended (`exited`, with `program`), a project, workspace, tab or pane opened or closed (`opened`, `closed`, with `kind`). Each carries `time` (UTC with milliseconds, as in `server.log`, so the two can be read side by side) and the ids of the item and its parents, which a closed item no longer has anywhere else. Groups are left out (they arrange the sidebar, nothing runs in them), and so are moves and phases: a workspace being removed ends with `closed`, or stays. Text lines read like `status`'s (`pane 5  claude  working → waiting`, `tab 3  opened in workspace 2`); an event the command does not know (a newer server) prints as its JSON.
- **Where events come from**: the trace diff (see Log), not hooks in each code path, so a new way to open a tab or change an agent's state is told without anyone remembering to. Each pane's entry in `trace::Seen` also keeps its agent, its `agents::activity::Pane::state` and its foreground job, compared on every traced step (strings are only copied when they change). A new subscriber first makes the diff run, so it starts from now: with the log off nothing ran it, and the first subscriber would have got every item as opened. The agent's state changes on the 500 ms reads of `watch_agents` and when its tab is shown (done to idle), and both are steps the diff follows.
- **Programs ending**: `Term::job` (the foreground process's pid and its name as `status` gives it, `Term::current_job`) is read on the same 500 ms reads, only while someone follows events (`None` otherwise, so nobody pays for it, and a job left over from a past subscriber cannot end in front of a new one). The job ended when the shell is back in the foreground or another pid took its place (`cargo build && cargo test`), but not when the same pid `exec`s, which only renames it. A program that runs for less than one read may not show; `wait --until shell` stays the exact way to wait for one. There is no exit code: the program is the shell's child, not cornercase's. A pane's own shell exiting is its `closed`.
- **The stream** keeps the protocol: the `events` request is answered once like any other (`{}`, or the error for an unknown pane), then each event is one more `ServerMessage::Response`, and the server ends it with `Detached` once every `--pane` closed (ids are never reused within a run, so nothing more could come) or with `Shutdown`/`Restart` when it stops; the command exits 0 on those and 1, saying so, when the connection just ends.
