No new message variant was needed, so the protocol tests did not change; a server without `events` answers that it has no such command (`Command::NAMES`), one from before the commands is "too old", and the one-answer commands are untouched (`client::requests::ask_on`, which `client::follow` reuses for the first answer). A snapshot of the current state at the start was left out: `status --json` is that, and the help says to start `events` first and read `status` after.
- **Slow readers never block the server**: the loop only queues messages for a client's writer thread, and each client's `Outbox` counts what is queued (the writer takes one off after each write). Past `EVENTS_QUEUED` (1024) a streamed event is dropped and counted instead of queued, and once the queue has room again a `dropped` event with the count goes first, at the next answer step even with no new event, so a script knows to catch up with `status`. Disconnecting at once was rejected: a script that is told it lost events can catch up and go on, one that is cut off has to restart as well. A reader that stops reading altogether still meets the 1 s write timeout every client has, which closes the connection, so a stuck reader costs one thread and the bounded queue until then, and the command says the connection ended.
- **Watching the session** (#285, `control/watch.rs`, `app/events.rs`, `server.rs`; `cornercase status --follow`): an external UI, or a script that draws the sidebar its own way, has to keep a whole model of the session (groups, projects, workspaces, tabs and panes, their names, icons, colours and folds, the split tree, the agents' states) and keep it right. `status --json` lacked the presentation and the splits, and `events` says nothing of renames, moves, folds, dividers or group styles, so such a client could only poll. Two changes:
  - **`status --json` grew**, by additions only: each group's `icon` and `colour`, each project's and workspace's `custom_name` (`null` without one) and `collapsed`, each tab's `custom_name` and `layout`, the split tree with pane ids as leaves (`{"leaf": 4}`, `{"split": {"dir": "right", "ratio": 0.6, "first": …, "second": …}}`), the shape `session.json` gives `Node`. It is `control::Layout`, a type of the contract rather than `Node<u64>` serialized as it is: the answer goes through `serde_json::Value`, which widens the `f32` ratio (`0.6000000238418579`), so the ratio is turned into an `f64` from its short form; and a change to the session's own type cannot change the contract by accident. `Eq` went from `Report` with the float. The text of `status` and `status --panes` did not change.
  - **`watch` answers with the current state, then sends a new one each time it changes.** Each message is the whole `Report` with a `revision` (`control::Snapshot`), so a client replaces its model with each one and never applies a change to a model that may have drifted. Patches (only what changed) were rejected: the issue left them optional, a client that misses one is wrong until it notices, and they would need a format of their own to version; a whole snapshot costs more bytes, which a session of usual size can afford. The snapshot is `status --json` itself, `caller` aside (`null`), `shown` and `active` included, since a UI wants to know what the window shows, rather than another model to keep in step with the one `status` already gives.
  - **No gap between the first state and the stream**: the request is answered in the step it arrives in, by building the `Report`, offering it to the clients already watching (so a change they had not seen goes to them, and not twice to the new one) and only then adding the new client, whose answer is that state. Answers go out before streamed messages in `Server::answer`, so the first state always arrives first, and everything after it comes with a higher `revision`. Starting `events` and reading `status` after, as `events` asks, leaves a moment where a change is in neither; this was why the first state is the answer rather than a separate request.
  - **`boot` and `revision`**: `revision` goes up by one per snapshot sent and is kept by `App` for the server's life, also when every watcher left and another one comes (the next one then gets a new revision even for the same state, since the last one is forgotten with the last watcher), so a client that reconnects sees it never go back. It only compares within one `boot` (in every snapshot, see the contract below): a restart starts again from 1, with new ids. Recovering is reconnecting: a new `watch` is a full bootstrap, so there is no resynchronisation message.
  - **Only what changed is sent**: a `Report` equal to the last one sent is dropped without raising the revision, so a step that changed nothing a client sees sends nothing. Some fields move by themselves (an agent's context percentage, `at_prompt`, `program`, `path`, `shown`, `active`, `keep_awake`), so a working agent gives a snapshot every few seconds; they are what a UI draws, so they stay.
  - **What it costs**: the `Report` looks up every pane's process and reads each workspace's `.git/HEAD`, about 3.7 ms with 50 panes in a release build on macOS, so it is built only while someone watches, after the trace diff (see Log), and only when something it shows may have changed. Every traced step but the refresh (input, a request, a job's answer, a client coming or going) marks it changed (`App::trace`); the refresh, which runs on every turn of the loop including those woken by PTY output, traces through `App::trace_refresh`, which looks again only when that refresh read the processes and agents (`watch_agents`, every `WATCH_AGENTS_EVERY`, 500 ms), and then marks it changed only when what the refresh reads differs from the last look (`App::refreshed`: a hash of each workspace's branch, `↓n`, setup and pull request with its checks, each tab's status, each pane's foreground process, its name and folder, its agent's state, model and context, dialog, survey and resume, and keep awake). That fingerprint costs about a third of the `Report` (1.1 ms against 3.2 ms with 50 panes in release: the process name without its arguments, and nothing serialized), so a quiet session builds nothing, output that changes only cells never builds it, and what moves without a step of its own (a program starting, a branch checked out by hand, an agent's status) shows within half a second. A pane's arguments are left out of the fingerprint, since reading them is the costly part: a program that only changes its arguments shows with the next change. Building is also capped at once every `SNAPSHOT_EVERY` (500 ms): a change inside that window marks it due, and `App::tick` asks for a wake-up when the window ends, at once when it already has, so the last change is never left unsent. Comparing only what changed, instead of building the whole `Report`, was left out: names, agent states and processes have no change signal of their own. 200 ms was the first choice for the window and was dropped once measured: up to five builds a second of almost 4 ms each is 2 % of a core, for a feed whose agent states are read only every 500 ms anyway.
  - **Slow readers get the latest**: a snapshot replaces every earlier one, so a reader that falls behind needs only the newest. The server queues at most `SNAPSHOTS_QUEUED` (4) messages for a watcher, which it never takes back, so a reader that falls behind still reads those older snapshots first; past them it holds one snapshot aside (`Client::held`), replaced by each newer one, and sends it when the queue has room, at the next answer step. The jump in `revision` tells the client it skipped some, which it need not act on. Dropping and counting them, as `events` does, would leave the reader on an old state. A reader that stops reading meets the 1 s write timeout like any client, and a large session on a slow remote link can too; reconnecting is the answer.
  - **The CLI is `status --follow`**: one JSON line per snapshot, the first one included, until the server stops (`Shutdown`, `Restart`, `Detached`: exit 0) or the connection drops (exit 1, saying so). Always JSON, `--json` accepted: a tree of text reprinted at each revision helps nobody. It conflicts with `--panes`, a view a script computes from the snapshot. A command of its own (`cornercase watch`) was rejected: the snapshot is `status --json`, and the issue asked for `status --follow`; `watch` stays the wire name, which an older server refuses by name. Like `status`, it needs no window, so it works on a server nobody has attached to yet. The streamed lines are `{"ok": …}` in both modes, as `events`'s are.
- **Streaming a tab's terminals** (#286, `control/surface.rs`, `app/surfaces.rs`, `server/surfaces.rs`; `cornercase surface --tab ID --cols N --rows N`): `read` gives plain text and a window gets ANSI for the whole TUI, so an external UI (an editor extension, a GUI) could show neither the live terminals with their colours and cursor nor the splits, short of parsing the rendered sidebar. The daemon already owns Ghostty, its cell snapshots and the split tree, so the `surface` request streams them.
  - **The frame** is one JSON object (`control::surface::Frame`), the first answer and every streamed line (`{"ok": frame}` in both modes), always complete: `boot` (`control::server_token()`), `session` (the revision of the latest `status --follow` snapshot sent, `Snapshots::revision`, kept for the whole server run and 0 only before the first one), `revision` (counts this stream's frames from 1), `tab`, `cols` and `rows` (the request's), `layout` (the whole split tree, as `status --json`'s `Layout`, naming hidden panes too), `dividers` (`Node::dividers`: `dir`, `path` from the root with `false` = first, the region split, the line's cells), `styles` and `panes`. A pane has its `rect` in the surface, its terminal's own `size`, `active`, `cursor` (`null` when hidden or scrolled out), `modes` (mouse, mouse encoding, alternate screen, alternate scroll, bracketed paste, application cursor, focus events, kitty keyboard flags), `scroll` (Ghostty's scrollbar: `total`, `offset`, `rows`) and `lines`.
  - **Cells and styles**: a line is runs of one style (`{"style": i, "cells": [...]}`), one string per terminal cell (the grapheme Ghostty stores), a blank is `" "`, and the right half of a wide character is `""` so a client does not draw it (`Cell::spacer`, from Ghostty's `CellWide::Wide`, not `unicode-width`: Ghostty decides the width). Cells after the last one that is not a default-style blank are left out, so an empty line is `[]`; the test for "default" is the wire style, since the emulator writes `fg(Reset).bg(Reset)` for styled cells. The `styles` table is shared by all the frame's panes and deduplicated; index 0 is `{}`, the terminal's own colours. Colours stay palette indices (`{"index": n}`, or `{"rgb": [r, g, b]}`), never resolved, so the client's theme applies, as Terminals says for the window. A selection made in a window shows as `inverse`, since selection is shared state.
  - **Per-pane grids, not a composed one**: composing one grid through `ui::pane::draw_tab` (as herdr's frame data does) would bake the window's chrome into the cells (dimming of inactive panes, the `↓ n more` badge, link underlines, padding) and crop terminals whose PTY size differs from their rectangle. Per-pane grids carry everything and are what a native UI draws.
  - **Sizes**: the caller's `cols` and `rows` lay the panes out through `Tab::shown`, the window's own rule (a tab too small for all its panes shows only its active pane over the whole surface, with no dividers). The PTYs keep the size the window gives them, so a pane's `size` can differ from its `rect`; `lines` and `cursor` index `size`. A `--tab` surface resizes nothing: `App::resize` sizes every tab on every draw, so a surface that resized would make the PTY bounce between two sizes. A view's surface (`surface --view`) is laid out at the view's size, and the PTYs take that size only while the view controls the tab (see Views of your own). The minimum is 1×1 (`start-server`'s 90×10 is for the window layout), the maximum 1000×500 (as `start-server` and the browser bridge).
  - **When frames are drawn**: `Step::Surfaces` runs every loop turn after `Step::Draw`, so it sees the sizes that draw just set. A subscription is dirty after output in a pane it shows (`App::surface_printed`, looked up by pane, so output nobody shows stays the early exit of `Server::due`: the window is not drawn for a pane only a surface shows, and nothing renders when neither does), after any other server event (`touch_surfaces`, where `handle` sets `changed`), and at least every 500 ms (`REFRESH_EVERY`, which catches what refresh changes without an event). A dirty one renders at most every 16 ms (`FRAME_EVERY`, the window's `FRAME`), and sends only when its content differs from the last one sent, so an unchanged frame raises no revision. `App::tick` wakes for the next render (`surface_wake`), always in the future, so the loop never spins. Everything renders on the server loop: `Emulator` is `!Send`.
  - **Slow readers**: `render_surfaces` takes the ids of the clients with fewer than `SURFACES_QUEUED` (2) messages queued, and a subscription whose client is full is not rendered at all, so work never piles up; it stays dirty and is looked at again after one frame, and once there is room it gets one frame with the latest state. Every frame is complete, so a skipped one never corrupts a display and the revisions received have no gaps. A reader that stops reading meets the 1 s write timeout like any client, and its connection closing drops the subscription. A frame that panics while it is built ends that stream and shows the bug toast, so one broken frame cannot panic every turn.
  - **`boot`, `session` and `revision`**: `boot` changes on every server start, so a frame of another run is stale (ids are reused). `session` relates a frame to the session snapshots: a frame below the snapshot the client holds was drawn before that change, one above it after a change whose snapshot the client has not received yet; they arrive on different connections, so there is no order between them. `revision` orders one stream's frames. To recover a baseline, reconnect: the first answer is always complete, from revision 1; there is no resync message.
  - **Outcomes**: the first answer is the frame, built in the step the request arrives in and answered before any later one (`Step::Answer` runs before `Step::Surfaces`). A refusal is an ordinary error with a code: `not-found` (no such tab), `malformed` (no tab, size out of range), `failed` (a second `surface` on the connection, which streams one tab: open another connection for another tab or size), `stale-boot`, `unsupported` (an older server). A pane's shell exiting drops it from the next frame. The tab closing (last pane exited, or the tab, workspace or project closed or removed) ends the stream with `Detached`; the server stopping with `Shutdown` or `Restart`. Closing the connection unsubscribes (logged as `surface ended`, with the frame count) and leaves the shells and the window as they were. `cornercase surface` exits 0 on all of these, and 1 when the connection is lost any other way. It needs no window.
  - **Not sent yet** (documented as unsupported): patches (every frame is complete), images (kitty, sixel, iTerm2) and hyperlinks (OSC 8), underline styles and colours, overline, a program's own palette or default-colour changes (OSC 4, 10, 11), the title, bell, notifications and clipboard writes, input and explicit pane sizes (#287), a scroll position or selection per client (both are shared with windows), pixel sizes, the cells of panes hidden because the tab is too small, and the window's chrome.
  - **Rejected**: patches (the issue makes them optional, and complete frames make slow readers trivially safe), sharing snapshots between streams of one tab (left for later: a frame snapshots every shown pane, about the window's draw cost, at most ~60 times a second per stream and only while something changes), a text output mode (the stream is for programs), and going through `serde_json::Value` for streamed frames (it would allocate a node per cell; the first answer does, through `refuse_or_answer`, which sorts keys, so clients must not depend on key order).
  - **Never logged**: cells and frames. The request's `tab`, `cols` and `rows`, each frame's revision and size at debug, and the frame count at the end are.
  - **Tests**: the encoding is unit tests (`control/surface/tests.rs`: runs, trailing blanks, wide characters, the shared table, colours, attributes, sizes) and `tests/fixtures/control/surface/v1/frame.json`, written and read back, and as a streamed line; `app/tests/control_requests/surfaces.rs` drives subscriptions with explicit instants (first answer, styles, cursor, splits, a hidden tab, the early exit, pacing, a reader without room, closing, reconnecting, refusals, the session revision); `server/surfaces/tests.rs` the queue limit, that output only a surface shows draws no window, and the end with `Detached`; `e2e::commands` streams a tab the window does not show.
- **Views of your own** (#288, `project/view.rs`, `app/views.rs`, `control/view.rs`, `cli/view.rs`; `cornercase view`): #283 to #286 let an external program read the session and a tab's cells, but it shared one selection with the window and could not own a size: `focus` moved everybody, and `surface` laid out at its size while the PTYs kept the window's. A **view** is a context a program opens over the socket: its own project, workspace and tab selection, an optional size, an active flag.
  - **Lifetime**: `view` is a stream request (answered with the first snapshot, then one line per change on that connection), and the view lives while its connection does (`App::forget` → `forget_views`). No close request: a crashed program releases nothing by hand. One view per connection (a second is `failed`); views are never saved and die with the server (`boot` changes). A view's id comes from the item counter, so it never equals another id of the run. Its lines are `watch` snapshots (`Snapshot`, one revision per view stream) whose `Report` carries `view` (`ViewInfo`: `id`, `projection`, `active`, `cols`, `rows`, `shown`, `controls`), paced and held for slow readers like `watch`'s (`publish_views`; a change the view itself made skips the 500 ms pacing for the view's line only, never for `watch` readers, whose build stays due). Rejected: a view without an owner connection (nothing releases it when the program dies), `watch --view` (per-watcher projections of the global feed would break its one revision), and a `CORNERCASE_VIEW` variable (panes inherit the environment, so a shell started from a GUI's terminal would act in that view by surprise).
  - **Naming it**: the request envelope has `view` (`Request::view`, left out when absent so every old byte and fixture stays; it does not opt into structured mode, and `"view": null` is dropped like `contract` and `boot`). The CLI's `--view ID` sets it for the commands that take one (`control::view::takes_view`: `status`, `focus`, `open`, `new-workspace`, `new-tab`, `split`, `start`, `send`, `keys`, `read`, `wait`, `mark`, `rename`, `surface`, `view-set`); any other command refuses it (`malformed`: ``X takes no view``) rather than ignoring it, as #283 asks for every field that matters, and an unknown view is `not-found`. `close`, `queue` and `flow` refuse it for now (`flow run --focus` opens the TUI's Flow panel). A server without the `views` feature would ignore the field and move the window, so the CLI checks the descriptor once per invocation before sending any `--view` (`client::use_view`, which keeps the id for the process: one invocation, one view). Socket clients follow the documented order, `contract` first.
  - **Navigation and defaults**: `Asker { caller, view }` is who asks (the pane it runs in, the view it acts for); `here`, `target`, `here_workspace` and `agent_kind` take it (anything convertible from a bare caller, so the queue and flows are untouched) and a view beats the caller. `focus --view` and `--focus --view` go through `show_to` (`show` for the window, unchanged): a group goes to its first project without expanding it (folds are shared), a project to its active workspace, a pane also focuses itself (pane focus is shared). They never touch `App::active`, the compact menu, folds or a file shown full screen. The answer carries the view's new `shown` ids and `projection`. The async worktree path keeps the view in `Then`, and skips the focus (logged) if the view closed meanwhile.
  - **Settling** (`project::view::Shown::settle`, pure, after every traced step and at the top of requests and surface renders): the selection is held by id with the last known indices, so a moved row keeps it and a removal falls where the window would: a closed tab to the one before it (else the first), a workspace going away to the nearest open one (earlier first), a closing project to the previous one (else the next), nothing left to an empty selection, which adopts what the window shows when something exists again (it reads the window, never moves it). A settle raises `projection` and is **not a claim**: a close must not silently resize a tab someone else is looking at. So a view moved by a settle controls nothing until it claims again, and its surface is laid out at the view's size while the PTYs keep the size of whoever sizes the tab.
  - **Who sizes a tab**: every tab has one geometry controller, so two programs showing one tab never make a PTY bounce between two sizes. The window (every attached window, which keep mirroring each other, or `start-server`'s size) is the default, sized as before (`layout(area).pane`). A view is eligible while connected, active, sized and showing that tab. Claims share one sequence: a view claims when it opens eligible, turns eligible, navigates (re-focusing the same tab included), or with `view-set --claim` (refused before anything changes if not eligible, with the reason); the window's claim is also dropped when the last window detaches (`App::window_gone`, from `Server::remove`), so a view then takes the tab back; the window claims the tab it shows when it is used (a key, paste or click, the events that make a window the last used, `App::window_used`), and that claim lapses when the window's focused tab changes (the focus, not `visible_tab`, so the compact menu or a full-screen file does not flip sizes). The controller is the latest claim, the window winning ties (`project::view::controller`, a pure function with table tests). Retention and handoff are only that rule re-evaluated: when the controller deactivates, navigates away, closes or disconnects, the tab goes to the latest remaining claim, else the window; the processes stay and are resized once. `size_tabs` gives each tab exactly one area (its controlling view's `cols×rows`, with no sidebar, tab bar or panel, else the window's pane area) and `Term::resize` does nothing when the size is unchanged, so calling it on every draw cannot bounce a PTY. The TUI draws a view-controlled tab in its own rectangle (cropped or blank), and pane mouse events are clamped to the terminal's own size so a click in the blank part never reaches a program beyond its columns. New terminals still spawn at the window's size and are resized within the request that focuses them. Rejected: the first viewer keeps the tab (a GUI left open would hold it, and the person at the window could never get it back), the smallest viewer wins (tmux's `smallest`: a phone would shrink every GUI, and every attach would resize), and a size per pane or per subscription (explicit resize operations come with #287, on top of this ownership).
  - **Active and metadata-only**: a view without a size, or inactive, claims nothing, sizes nothing and renders nothing, but its stream keeps following the session. `view-set --inactive` releases its claim and pauses its surfaces; `--active` resumes and claims. `surface --view` takes the view's tab and size (it refuses `tab`, `cols` and `rows`, and is refused while the view is inactive, sizeless or tab-less, so the first answer is always a whole frame). The subscription resolves tab and size from the view on every render, so it follows navigation; while paused it is neither rendered nor dirty (a dirty sub with a past `next` would make the loop spin), and it ends with `Detached` when its view closes, never because its tab closed (the view settles on a neighbour). `--tab` surfaces are unchanged: passive, they never claim or resize.
  - **Projection**: `projection` starts at 1 and goes up each time what the view's surface shows changes: its tab (navigation or settle), its size, or `active`. Frames of a view surface carry `view` and `projection`, and a change of projection forces a whole frame even if the cells did not change. A client shows a frame only if its `projection` equals the latest it knows for the view; an older one was drawn before its last navigation or activation. `boot` still fences restarts, `revision` orders one stream, and `session` keeps its meaning.
  - **What stays shared** by windows and views: the processes and terminal contents, the split layout and its ratios, the focused pane of a tab (`focus --view --pane` moves it for everyone), the scroll position and the text selection (each terminal has one Ghostty viewport), the active children a view lands on when it navigates to a container, folds and group collapse, and names. Window-only, which views have none of: overlays, hover, the toast, the compact menu, side panels, column widths, list scrolls and the pointer. Independent history viewports per view are not in this version.
  - **Wire**: `view` and `view-set` are new operations (names 34 and 35), `Report.view`, `TabInfo.controller` (the view sizing the tab, absent for the window), `Done.projection`, `Frame.view` and `Frame.projection` are optional and left out when empty, `CONTRACT` stays 1 (additions only), and the feature `views` and the stream `view` are listed.
  - **Tests**: the pure model (`project/view/tests.rs`: settling, the controller table), the JSON (`control/view/tests.rs`, the `view/v1` fixture), `app::tests::control_requests::views` (two clients on two tabs, a size without chrome, two sizes on one tab with no bounce, handoff, inactive and sizeless observers, coexistence with the TUI and the click clamp, projection fencing, settling, refusals), the server's input path and `e2e::views`.
- **A versioned contract** (#283, `control/contract.rs`, `app/control/contract.rs`, `cli/contract.rs`): an external UI or a script written for one release talks to the server of another, and before it had no way to ask what that server understood: an unknown command answered with an error to parse, an unknown field was dropped in silence and the request still succeeded, and an id kept from before a restart could name another pane. The JSON requests already crossed releases (see above), so the contract builds on them rather than on a new transport, and changes nothing a command prints.
  - **Discovery**: the `contract` request (`cornercase contract [--json]`) answers a `control::contract::Descriptor`: `version` (the server's release), `contract` (`CONTRACT`, an integer from 1), `boot`, `operations` (every name of `Command::NAMES` with `available` and, when not, a `reason`; `start-server`, `watch` and `surface` are listed there, additions that do not raise `CONTRACT`), `features` (`boot`, `structured-errors`, `strict-fields`) and `streams` (`{"name": "events", "version": 1}`, `{"name": "watch", "version": 1}`, `{"name": "surface", "version": 1}`). It needs no window, like `status`. `available` is the mechanism for an optional operation that a server can turn off (a feature missing on that machine) while `status` and the rest go on working; no operation is off today. Clients read the descriptor leniently (`#[serde(default)]`), so a newer server's extra keys pass.
  - **`boot`** is `control::server_token()` (the server's pid and start time, what `CORNERCASE_SERVER` holds in every pane), and `status --json` carries it too. It changes on every start, `restart` included, since pane, tab and workspace ids start again from 1. A request that names a `boot` other than this server's is refused with `stale-boot` before any id is resolved or anything is done, so a `close` or a `send` kept from before a restart cannot hit the pane that took its id. The older `server` field stays as it was: it only drops the `caller` of another server's pane (a pane reached through `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` is legitimate), and turning it into a refusal would break `cornercase` run inside panes.
  - **Opt-in by two fields of the request**, `contract` and `boot` (either one, not null): a request that carries one is *structured*, and only then do errors take the form `{"error": {"code", "message"}}` and unknown fields fail. Without them every answer is byte for byte what it was (`{"ok": …}` / `{"error": "…"}`): an installed command parses `Response` with serde, and any extra key or an error object makes it fail (checked with serde_json 1), so changing the shape for everyone would break every older CLI talking to a newer server. The CLI itself stays in the legacy mode, so its output and exit codes do not change. A wrapper (`{"contract": 1, "request": {…}}`) was rejected: an older server cannot parse it as a request at all, where two additive fields keep one shape for both modes; the cost is that an older server drops them in silence (below). The mode is kept per client (`Requests::structured`) until it is answered or forgotten; a text that is not JSON cannot say its mode, so it is answered in the legacy shape, which a structured client also reads.
  - **Codes** (`control::contract::Code`, kebab-case, stable): `unsupported` (a command this server does not have, checked by name before parsing, or a `contract` newer than `CONTRACT`: a client written for later rules must not be read under these, so it fails rather than being taken as version 1), `malformed` (JSON that is not a request, or arguments that do not deserialize), `stale-boot`, `not-found` (an id that names no group, project, workspace, tab or pane), `unknown-field`, `unavailable` (an operation listed in `app/control/contract.rs`'s `UNAVAILABLE`, which both the descriptor and the request reader use, so a request for it is refused with its reason; the list is empty today, so no server sends it yet) and `failed` for everything else (`From<String>` on `Refusal`, so handlers keep returning their texts). The `message` is the text a legacy client gets, so the two modes never drift apart; clients branch on the code, never on the message. Internal clients (the queue, the chat bots, flows) keep reading the message.
  - **Strict fields**: in structured mode a key the server does not know, in the envelope or anywhere under `args`, answers `unknown-field` naming its path (`extra`, `args.bogus`, `args.entry.repeat`) instead of being ignored. It is found by serializing the parsed request and comparing keys (`contract::unknown_fields`), since `Request` flattens its command and `deny_unknown_fields` does not work with `flatten`; this relies on no request struct skipping a field when it serializes, so a `skip_serializing_if` added to one would make that field look unknown at its default. The envelope's `contract` and `boot` are the exception: they skip when absent, so the CLI's bytes stay as they were, and a `null` one in the request is dropped before comparing, since serializers that write `None` as `null` (Python's, Go's, serde's without `skip`) would otherwise get `unknown-field` on the first `contract` call, made before the boot is known. Legacy requests keep ignoring unknown fields, which is what `start-with` and `wait-several` were designed around.
  - **Compatibility rules**: fields are added, never renamed or removed, and an older peer ignores what it does not know (answers too: read them leniently, and do not depend on key order, since `events` lines come out sorted); a new behaviour that must not be ignored is a new operation name (as `start-with`, `wait-several`, `restart-when-idle`, `last-message` already are), which an older server refuses by name; `contract` goes up only when something that was there changes meaning or shape, never for an addition, which shows in `operations`, `features` or `streams`. A client calls `contract` first: a server that answers `has no \`contract\` command` (or `Rejected`, from before requests) knows none of this and would ignore `boot` and act, so such a client must not send ids kept from before.
  - **Streams have versions**: `events` lines are format 1 (`EVENTS_VERSION`, the JSON of `control::Event`), pinned by one fixture per event in `tests/fixtures/control/events/v1/` that each test serializes and reads back, so changing the format without a new version fails. `watch` lines are format 1 too (`WATCH_VERSION`), pinned by `tests/fixtures/control/watch/v1/snapshot.json`, a snapshot with a group's style, custom names, a fold and a split `layout` whose leaves are the tab's panes; the test writes it, reads it back and checks the ratio streams as `0.6`. `surface` frames are format 1 too (`SURFACE_VERSION`), pinned by `tests/fixtures/control/surface/v1/frame.json`; `view` lines (#288) are `watch` format 1 plus `view` and the tabs' `controller` (`VIEW_VERSION`), pinned by `tests/fixtures/control/view/v1/snapshot.json`, and the feature `views` and the operations `view` and `view-set` show in the descriptor. The streams of later issues get their own entry in `streams`. Fixtures of the legacy and structured answers (`responses/`) and of the framed postcard bytes of a request and an answer (`postcard/`, hex) pin the rest of what a direct client speaks.
- `skills/cornercase/SKILL.md` (Agent Skills format) is what teaches agents: installed with `npx skills add usecornercase/cornercase-terminal --skill cornercase -g`, printed by `cornercase skill` (`include_str!`), never installed by cornercase (like hooks). It stays short and points to `cornercase --help`.
- A panic must not leave a command waiting forever: its timeout lives in the server too. `Requests::open` lists the clients still owed an answer; after every contained panic `Server::contain` calls `App::answer_lost_requests`, which answers `Error::Bug`'s text to each of them that has no pending entry left (the request panicked while it was handled, or `check_requests` lost the entries it had taken out). Pending ones keep waiting, so whatever answers an entry takes it out of `pending` before doing the work that can panic (`worktree_ready`, `worktree_gone`); one left behind would wait for an answer that never comes.
- Whoever reaches the socket can type into every shell; the docs page says so for sandboxed agents.

## Plugins (`plugins/`, `app/plugins.rs`, `cli/plugin.rs`, `client/window.rs`)

- **What a plugin is** (PR 1 of 3 is the core; PR 2 adds installing, below; the catalogue is PR 3): a folder with `cornercase-plugin.toml` that declares argv commands, modelled on herdr's plugins (`herdr-plugin.toml`). The plugin's API is the existing commands for scripts and agents: it calls `$CORNERCASE_BIN status --json`, `events --json`, `send`, `notify`… No SDK and no runtime inside cornercase (WASM, Lua, JS were rejected): every improvement to the commands is one to plugins at no cost, and a plugin can be written in any language. TOML, like herdr's, so plugins port easily; it costs the `toml` crate (parse and serde only). Entries: `[[actions]]` (one-shot, in the menus), `[[events]]` (one-shot per event), `[[service]]` (kept running by the server), `[[window]]` (kept running by each local window), and for installing `[[build]]` and `[download.<target>]` (see Installing). Left out: `[[tab]]` (`new-tab --command` covers it), link handlers, per-entry `platforms`, plugin-defined settings, and surfaces inside the TUI (sidebar areas, panel tabs).
- **The manifest** (`plugins/manifest.rs`): `id` (lower-case letters, digits, `.`, `_`, `-`, at most 64), `name`, `version` required; `min_cornercase` (refused when newer than the binary, as herdr does), `api` (an integer, 1 today, refused when newer: it moves only when a command or event changes incompatibly, which the tolerant JSON makes rare), `description`, `platforms` (`macos`, `linux`; empty is everywhere), `permissions`. Unknown fields are ignored, so a manifest written for a newer cornercase loads; warning about them was left out (it needs another crate). Unknown contexts and event names are refused by serde with the list of known ones. Every text in it (ids, name, version, description, platforms, permissions, titles, every command and argument, the build steps and the downloads' targets, addresses and sha256) is refused when it holds a control character or an invisible one (`text::hidden`: Unicode's format characters, such as the bidi overrides and isolates `U+202A`–`U+202E` and `U+2066`–`U+2069`, the zero-width ones `U+200B`–`U+200F` and `U+FEFF`, the line and paragraph separators, variation selectors and tags, and the fillers that draw as blanks), since such a character makes a command read differently from what runs (`bin/run\u{202E}hs.txt` shows as `bin/runtxt.sh`): a plugin is chosen by reading what it runs. Escaping them for display instead was rejected for the manifest: a plugin has no reason to use them, and one refusal covers the CLI, Settings and every future preview. Errors about a manifest or a program, which can quote the text that failed, show such characters escaped (`text::escape_hidden`, `\u{202e}`), never raw. The file is opened non-blocking and read only when it is a regular file, at most 64 KiB (the read itself stops there), and a plugin declares at most 64 entries: a FIFO named like the manifest would otherwise block the server loop, which reads manifests (below). The plugin's folder and its manifest must belong to the user and be writable by no other user, close to the rule the socket's folder follows: a plugin linked from a shared folder such as `/tmp` would let another user run code as you. herdr checks neither the owner nor the mode of a plugin's folder (only that an installed plugin's manifest sits inside its managed checkout); cornercase does. Group write is refused too, unless the group is the user's own primary group: distributions with a group per user (umask `002`) make every clone group-writable, and refusing those would refuse most plugins there for no gain. A manifest that cannot be read never hides the others (herdr #4312): the plugin is listed with its reason and cannot be turned on.
- **Commands are argv, never a shell**: nothing is expanded or split. A program with a `/` resolves inside the plugin's folder: `..` is refused when the manifest is read, and at run time the path is canonicalised and must stay under the canonical folder, so a symbolic link leading out is refused too. A bare name goes through the `PATH`, like any command. The resolved program and every folder from the plugin's folder down to it get the folder's and the manifest's check (owned by the user, writable by no other user, group write only for the user's own primary group), since a program another user can rewrite runs as you all the same. A bare name, an absolute path and the files a program reads (`["python3", "main.py"]`'s script) are not checked: the plugin's own code is the user's to keep. The check is about mistakes and surprises, not containment: a plugin runs as the user and its own script can run anything.
- **The registry** (`plugins/registry.rs`): `plugins.json` next to `config.json` (`paths::plugins`), private (`persist::write`, `0600`), `{"version":1,"plugins":[{"id","source","enabled"}]}`. `source` is a tagged enum with `{"kind":"link","path"}` and `{"kind":"git",…}` (see Installing) today, so the catalogue may add variants without a new version. A missing, corrupt or unknown file reads as empty for the readers (the supervisors, the menus, Settings' rows), but the writers (`Registry::open`: the commands, Settings' switch) refuse a file they cannot read rather than write an empty registry over it, saying so. Every writer (the commands, Settings) loads it from disk, changes one entry and writes it back, so the commands work with no server running and two writers never work from a stale copy.
- **Nothing runs until the user turns it on**: `link` adds a plugin turned off and says how to turn it on; `enable` (or `Enter` on its row in Settings › Plugins) is the explicit step, and both say that it runs as the user and what it asks for. Following herdr #2109, acquiring and enabling are separate steps. PR 2 follows what herdr does on `plugin update` (`src/cli/plugin.rs` at `6521382`): an update fetches the recorded ref, says it is up to date and changes nothing when the commit is the same, and otherwise shows the whole preview again (source, commit, every command) and asks before installing, with `--yes` required when there is no terminal; a build that changes the manifest after the preview aborts the update. A linked plugin cannot be updated and is read again when it changes, as in herdr, since it is a folder the user is writing.
- **Permissions are shown, not enforced** (the user's decision): a plugin runs as the user, and anything the user can do through the socket, it can do, including typing into every pane. Enforcing would need per-plugin tokens and a server that refuses requests without one, and since any local process of the user can reach the socket, that buys little until every unauthenticated request is refused, which would change how scripts work. The socket's authentication is unchanged. The risk is accepted and said plainly in Settings' hint, `enable`'s output and the docs. Also accepted: a linked plugin's manifest and scripts are read again when they change and keep running without asking again, since linking is for a folder the user is writing (PR 2 pins installed plugins to a commit); a plugin can read `secrets.json` (it is the user's file), its log holds whatever its commands print, and an action runs in the clicked workspace's folder, whose files it can change.
- **What a command inherits** (`plugins/run.rs`): the server's environment (the window's, for window processes), so `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` reaches the right server, minus `CORNERCASE_PANE`, `CORNERCASE_SERVER`, `CORNERCASE_RESTARTED`, Claude Code's session variables (as `term` strips them from panes) and the issue trackers' token variables (`SHORTCUT_API_TOKEN`, `LINEAR_API_KEY`, `JIRA_API_TOKEN`, `PLANE_API_KEY`), which the server may hold and a plugin has no use for; plus `CORNERCASE_BIN` (`current_exe`), `CORNERCASE_PLUGIN_ID`, `_ROOT`, `_CONFIG` and `_STATE`, and `CORNERCASE=1` from the server. An action gets the ids of what was clicked (`CORNERCASE_PROJECT`, `_WORKSPACE`, `_TAB`, `CORNERCASE_PANE`), `CORNERCASE_PLUGIN_ACTION` and `CORNERCASE_CONTEXT`; only an action on a pane gets `CORNERCASE_SERVER`, since that token is what makes a command take `CORNERCASE_PANE` as its caller, and a hook or service has no pane to be. Tokens never go in the environment: `secrets.json` is never read for plugins. stdin is `/dev/null`; stdout and stderr go to the plugin's log. Each command is the leader of its own process group, so stopping it (`kill_process_group`) also stops what it started; a plugin that `setsid`s away escapes that, as in herdr, and the docs say a service must not detach.
- **Folders and the log**: `plugins/config/<id>/` next to `config.json` and `plugins/state/<id>/` next to `session.json` (`paths::plugin_config`, `paths::plugin_state`), created `0700`; the two prefixes keep them apart under `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`, where config and state share one folder. The log, `plugin.log` (`0600`), holds what the commands print and a line from cornercase per start, stop and exit of a service or window process and per failed hook or action, in `server.log`'s format. It moves to `plugin.log.1` past 1 MiB, checked each time a command starts: a service that runs for days keeps writing to the moved file until it restarts, accepted for a simple rotation without a pipe thread per process. `server.log` gets one line per start, stop, exit and hook or action (`plugins` target, through `log::Job`), never what the plugin printed, which keeps its never-logged rules.
- **Actions in the menus** (`app/plugins.rs`, herdr #1722, its most asked-for plugin request): an action's `contexts` (`project`, `workspace`, `tab`, `pane`) put it at the bottom of the ` ⋯ ` and right-click menus of those rows, the tab bar and the pane menu, below the built-in entries (`App::target_menu`, `App::plugin_menu`). `MenuAction::Plugin` holds the spot, the action's index among the enabled plugins' actions and the registry's revision, so it stays `Copy`; an index from before a reload is ignored rather than running another action. An action runs on a job (`spawn_job`) in the clicked workspace's folder (the project's for a project), is killed after 5 minutes, and a failure comes back as `AppEvent::Plugin` with a toast naming the exit status and `cornercase plugin logs <id>`. No keys: a `plugins` group in the keys menu was left for later.
- **Event hooks**: the trace diff that feeds `cornercase events` also feeds hooks (`App::run_hooks` after `Events::publish`), so a hook sees exactly what `events --json` prints, as `CORNERCASE_EVENT`. While an enabled plugin has hooks the diff runs as if someone followed events (`App::following`, also for reading foreground jobs), and when hooks first appear the diff is run once before, as a new subscriber does, so the first one does not hear every open item as opened. Each hook is a job, killed after 60 s; at most 16 run at once and the rest are dropped with a `WARN`, so a burst of events cannot start a thread per pane forever. A long reaction belongs in a service following `events --json`.
- **Services and window processes** (`plugins/supervise.rs`): a `Supervisor` thread: one in the server for services, and one in each local window's client for window processes, the third kind of thread kept by hand beside the file read and the Tailscale worker (see Code layout), since it runs for the server's or the window's life rather than once. It reloads the registry when `plugins.json` or an enabled plugin's manifest changes (inode, modification time and size, checked every 250 ms), starts what is wanted, restarts what ended after 1, 2, 4… up to 60 s (back to 1 s once it ran for a minute) and stops what is no longer wanted (`SIGTERM` to its group, `SIGKILL` after 2 s). Watching the file rather than asking the server means `plugin enable` needs no new request (it works with no server and against older ones), and editing a linked manifest reloads it. A changed command or manifest restarts the entry. The server starts the service supervisor once the session is restored (`App::start_plugins`, after `open_first_terminals`, also when that failed to open a first terminal), so a service's first `status` sees the projects, and stops it in `Server::shutdown`, before the clients are told, on `kill-server`, a signal and a restart; the new server starts them again. What a supervisor is doing is shared with Settings as one note per plugin (`Supervisor::note`). A window process is started by each local window's client (`client::window::attach`, dropped when it returns, also before a restart's `exec`), so two local windows run two; under `cornercase remote` none runs, since the window's machine has no socket (a relay over the link was left for later).
- **Running files** (`plugins/supervise.rs`): each supervisor writes `plugins/state/<id>/running/<pid>` (`0600`, in the `0700` state folder; its text is the kind and entry) when it spawns a service or window process, and deletes it once that process is stopped or reaped, so the files list what is alive in the server's services and in every local window's processes alike. Only the leader of the command's process group is recorded, the one the supervisor signals and waits for. `plugin remove` (`install::remove`) sets the plugin off in `plugins.json` (nothing else to ask: the supervisors notice within 250 ms and stop it, `SIGKILL` after 2 s), polls `running/` every 100 ms for at most `STOP_WAIT` (10 s) and counts a file only when `process::alive` says its pid lives; a file for a dead pid is stale and deleted, so a supervisor killed with `SIGKILL` never blocks a removal. **An empty `running/` is not proof by itself, and neither is elapsed time**: a supervisor may hold an enabled list from before the save, a pass can be slow (stopping each changed entry takes up to 2 s before the next one is spawned), and a process exists for a moment before its file does, so no grace period or number of empty polls can show that spawning has stopped. Removal excludes spawns with a file lock instead (`flock`, as `server.lock` does): `plugins/state/<id>/running.lock` (`0600`). A supervisor spawns an entry only while holding a shared lock on it (`try_shared`, non-blocking: if removal holds it, that pass skips the entry and tries again later, so a supervisor never waits) and, once it has the lock, re-reads `plugins.json` from disk and spawns only if the entry is still the same one and still on (`on_disk`); it writes the marker before releasing, and never holds the lock while stopping or sleeping. `remove` saves the plugin off, then takes the exclusive lock (`exclude_spawns`, retried every 20 ms until the `STOP_WAIT` deadline, else it fails with the plugin installed and off) and releases it at once: every spawn that saw the plugin on has finished and written its marker by then, and every later one re-reads it as off and skips. The markers are then polled until none names a live process, within the same deadline. A supervisor never spawns from a folder that is gone (`spawn` checks it and takes the failed-start path with its backoff). Reading `running/` returns a `Result`: a missing folder is empty, any other error (the folder, or one entry) makes `remove` fail with the plugin installed and off, since a failed read proves nothing; likewise a supervisor that cannot write a file stops that child at once and counts a failed start. After the wait `remove` reopens the registry and goes on only if the entry equals the one it just turned off (same source, still off): Settings or another command may have turned it on or replaced it meanwhile, and then nothing is deleted. On timeout it fails naming the pids, leaving the plugin installed, listed and off, and says to run `remove` again; the same wait precedes forgetting a linked plugin (no folder is deleted there), while `unlink` stays a plain edit of the list. Files rather than a request to the server: the commands work on the files and never use the socket (a plugin can be removed with no server running), and window processes belong to each window's client, which the server does not know about. A reused pid can hold a stale file for as long as that other process lives, which the timeout bounds and `remove` again clears once it ends; a descendant that left the group (`setsid`) is not tracked, as for stopping.
- **The server loop never waits on a plugin**: hooks and actions run on job threads with timeouts, supervision on its own thread, and the loop only reads `plugins.json` and the manifests (bounded, regular files) once a second (`App::watch_plugins`) and when Settings opens or switches a plugin.
- **Settings › Plugins** (`settings::Page::Plugins`, last): one row per linked plugin under **Installed** (`Row::Plugin`), its name and version, `[x] on` or `[ ] off`, and as note what it asks for while off and its description or its service's state while on; a plugin that cannot run shows its reason and refuses `Enter`. No plugin shows **no plugins yet** with the command that adds one. The page's hint says plugins run as the user and their permissions are not enforced. The app has no catalogue of its own (see Finding plugins).
- **The commands** (`cli/plugin.rs`): `cornercase plugin link <folder>`, `unlink`, `list [--json]`, `enable`, `disable`, `logs [-n] [--follow] [--path]` (sharing `cornercase logs`' printing). They work on the files, never through the socket.
- **Installing** (`plugins/source.rs`, `plugins/install.rs`, `plugins/archive.rs`; `cornercase plugin install|update|remove`): one model, in steps that never touch a terminal, so the CLI and any later dialog drive it the same way:
  1. `source::Origin::parse(spec)`: `owner/repo` (GitHub, `https://github.com/owner/repo.git`), an `https://` address without credentials, or an absolute folder, each with an optional `@ref` (a branch, tag or commit; letters, digits, `._/+-`, not starting with `-`, `/` or `.`, no `..`). Every other transport is refused (`file://`, `ssh://`, `git://`, `http://`, `ext::`, scp-like `host:path`); the CLI turns a relative or `~` folder into an absolute one first. **Plugins in a folder of a repository**, as herdr's `owner/repo[/subdir…]`: a repository can hold several plugins. On GitHub the parts after `owner/repo` are the folder (`acme/tools/plugins/notes`, also as `https://github.com/acme/tools/plugins/notes`). Any other address, and a folder on disk, cannot tell where the repository's path ends, so the folder comes after `//` (`https://host/acme/tools.git//plugins/notes`, `/srv/tools.git//notes`), the separator Terraform and go-getter use; `//` never occurs in a clean repository address or path, and `Origin::spec` writes it back the same way. Each part is letters, digits, `._-`, never empty, `.` or `..`, and never starting with `-`. `fetch` walks the folder in the checkout part by part and refuses one that is missing, a file or a symbolic link; the manifest is read there, a download unpacks and the build runs there, and only that folder is moved to `plugins/installed/<id>/<commit12>/`, so it is the plugin's root and `Manifest::program` confines its commands to it (a link to a sibling folder of the repository is refused like one out of the plugin). `Source::Git` keeps `subdir`, optional and left out when empty, so `update` fetches the same folder, and an id installed from another folder of the same address counts as another source.
  2. `install::fetch(&origin, &places) -> Fetched` (network, no plugin code runs): clones into a private staging folder (`plugins/installed/.staging/<pid>-<n>`, `0700`, cleared of folders whose process is gone), resolves the ref (a full 40-digit commit only as that commit, never as a branch or tag an author could name after a reviewed commit, and checked to be exactly it after the checkout; otherwise `origin/<ref>`, `refs/tags/<ref>`, then `<ref>` as a commit; `HEAD` without one), checks the commit out detached, drops `.git`, and reads the manifest without checking its programs (a build may make them). It refuses an id already linked or installed from another address. `Fetched` holds the origin, the full commit, the manifest and `replacing` (whether an installed version is on, so the preview says an update of a plugin that is on runs its new commands at once); dropping it deletes the staging folder, so a cancelled preview leaves nothing.
  3. `Fetched::preview() -> Vec<String>`: plain lines for any view: name, version, id, description, source (with its folder) and commit, permissions, the download (URL and sha256) or the build steps, every command it will run and in which entry, and that it is installed off and runs as you once on.
  4. `install::install(fetched, &places) -> Installed` (runs only after a confirmation, on a job thread in the app): downloads and unpacks the archive for this target when `[download]` has one, else runs the `[[build]]` steps in the checkout (argv, the plugins' environment, output to the plugin's log, 15 minutes each); then reads the manifest again and aborts if it differs from the previewed one in any field, checks its programs (`Manifest::load`), moves the checkout (or the plugin's folder of it) to `plugins/installed/<id>/<commit12>/` and records it in `plugins.json` as `{"kind":"git","repo","url","ref","subdir","commit","path"}`. A new plugin is off; an update keeps it on or off, since the user confirmed the new preview. Each version is its own folder, so a running service keeps its files until the supervisor restarts it on the new path; the folder before it is kept, older ones deleted. Installing again at the commit already installed (`owner/repo@stable` when `stable` is the installed default branch's commit) moves nothing: the existing folder is reused, the staged copy is dropped, and the entry is rewritten with the new origin (ref, address, subdir) and the same `enabled`, so later updates follow the new ref.
  5. `install::check_update(&places, id) -> Update::{UpToDate(commit), Changed(Box<Fetched>)}`: fetches the recorded address and ref again; the same commit changes nothing, another goes through `preview` and `install`. A linked plugin is refused. `install::remove(&places, id) -> Removed::{Unlinked, Deleted}` first turns the plugin off, then waits until nothing it ran is alive (below), and only then drops the entry and deletes `plugins/installed/<id>/`, so a service never loses its files while it still runs; a linked plugin is only forgotten, and the plugin's config and state folders stay.
  - Installed copies live next to the plugins' state (`paths::plugin_installs`, `plugins/installed/` beside `session.json`, so under `CORNERCASE_SOCKET` next to the socket), not in `$XDG_DATA_HOME`: one fewer base folder to find, clear and test.
  - **`[download]`**: `[download.<target>]` with `url` and `sha256`, the four targets `update::target` names. The URL must be `https://` (or `http://127.0.0.1:`/`localhost:`, for tests); the archive is a `.tar.gz` downloaded with `http::download` (256 MiB cap), checked against the sha256 before anything is written, and unpacked with the `tar` and `flate2` crates (both new; `flate2` was already built as a dependency of a dependency). A crate rather than the `tar` program, as `update.rs` uses, because each entry has to be looked at before it is written, which GNU tar and bsdtar do not let a caller do portably: only files and folders are unpacked (a symbolic link, hard link, device or FIFO refuses the archive), a path that is absolute or has `..` refuses it, a file is never written through a symbolic link already in the checkout (every folder above it is checked, and the file is created anew), modes are `0755` or `0644` (no setuid), and at most 20,000 entries and 1 GiB unpacked. A target without a download runs the build steps instead.
  - **git** runs as a process with no prompts, as elsewhere, plus `GIT_ALLOW_PROTOCOL` and `protocol.allow=never` with only `https` (or `file` for a folder) allowed, `core.hooksPath=/dev/null`, `transfer.fsckObjects`, a low-speed timeout, `--` before the address and `--end-of-options` before the ref, and without the `GIT_DIR`-style variables a shell may have set. Submodules are not fetched.
  - **Security review** (of this diff against the core): the one finding was that a manifest could carry terminal escapes (`\u001b[6A\u001b[J`, `\r`) in a command argument, the description or a permission, which, printed in the preview, moved the cursor up and erased the build step or the changed service the user was about to confirm. `Manifest::check` now refuses a control character in any text of the manifest (every string, walked through its serde value, so a field added later is covered too), and `Fetched::preview` and git's errors escape any control or invisible character that would reach them (`text::escape_hidden`, as the core's errors do). The rest was checked and found as designed: options and transports, `.git` and hooks, the archive's paths and link types, the sha256 before unpacking, nothing built before the confirmation, the manifest compared again after the build, the private staging folder, and ids that cannot climb out of `plugins/installed/`. **Accepted risks**: the plugin's build steps and, once on, its commands run as the user (the preview and the confirmation are the defence, as for any installer); a build step can change any file in its folder but the manifest, so what the preview says it runs is what is recorded, not what those programs then do; a git folder given as the source is cloned with git's local transport, so it is trusted like any folder of the user's; `git` follows the user's own configuration (`url.<x>.insteadOf`, credential helpers, `git-lfs` filters), which is theirs to set; and the preview is shown once, so whoever confirms with `--yes` without reading it accepts what it says.
  - **The symlink gap of PR 1**: a command whose program is a symbolic link out of the folder was only refused when it ran, so `link` and `enable` accepted it and `list` showed it on. `Manifest::load` now resolves every action, hook, service and window program (`Manifest::read` is the same without it, for previews), so `link` refuses it, `enable` refuses it, and `list` and Settings show the plugin as cannot run with the reason, as for any manifest error. A missing program counts too: a linked plugin whose binary is not built yet cannot be linked until it is. The manifest itself is opened with `O_NOFOLLOW`.
- **Finding plugins** (`site/src/lib/plugins/`, `site/scripts/plugins-index.mjs`, `site/plugins/`, `src/pages/plugins/`) is herdr's marketplace (herdr.dev/docs/marketplace): found automatically, not reviewed, listed by repository. A curated index reviewed per commit, and a catalogue inside the app, were built first and dropped: review by maintainers does not scale once anyone can publish, a plugin runs as the user whatever a reviewer read, and herdr has neither.
  - **The crawl** (`crawl.ts`): GitHub's repository search for `topic:cornercase-plugin fork:false archived:false`, 100 a page, at most 10 pages (the search stops at 1,000 results); for each repository the head commit of its default branch, its tree at that commit (`git/trees?recursive=1`), and every `cornercase-plugin.toml` in it, at the root or in a folder, nearest first, at most 20 per repository, read from `raw.githubusercontent.com` (64 KiB each), eight repositories at a time. A repository whose head commit is the one in the published index is not read again: its plugins and its count of invalid manifests come from the index (`skipped` keeps the repositories with no valid manifest for that), as herdr rescans only when the head changes. Each manifest is parsed with `smol-toml` (a dev dependency of the site); one that does not parse or breaks the rules `src/plugins/manifest.rs` applies counts as invalid, checked by one function (`checkManifest`): `api` a whole number from 0 to 1 when present; `actions`, `events`, `service`, `window` and `build` lists of tables with their required fields, a non-empty command of strings whose program does not climb out of the folder, action contexts from `project`, `workspace`, `tab` and `pane`, event names from `opened`, `closed`, `status` and `exited`, ids of the app's shape and unique per kind, at most 64 entries, `download` targets with an https address (or loopback) and a 64-digit sha256, and no control or bidirectional formatting character in any string, keys included, however deep. What the crawler cannot know, such as whether the plugin runs on the reader's platform, is left to the app. **A failed request is not an invalid manifest**, and what fails it depends on the scope. *The whole crawl* stops, and `plugins-index.mjs` keeps the published index, only when the search fails, is incomplete or is malformed (a non-success answer, `incomplete_results` true, no `items` or `total_count`, as `refresh-flows.mjs` treats its own search; a complete, empty search is accepted): then nothing is known about who is listed. *One repository* never stops the others, as in `refresh-flows.mjs`. A repository that is gone (404, 410 or 451 on its head or its tree) is skipped. Any other error for it (a 5xx, 403, a rate limit, a malformed answer, a head without a commit or a tree that is not a list of objects (a truncated but well-formed one is read as it comes), a failing manifest download, a network error) keeps that repository's card from the published index when it has one and skips it otherwise; each case is logged with `console.warn` naming the repository and the reason, and a kept card goes through the current blocklist and the duplicate rules like any other. Unchanged: a 409 on the head (an empty repository) leaves it out, and a 404 for a manifest the tree listed counts as an unreadable one, so only a transient failure is ever retried. Before, a failed request dropped the repository for the run, or was stored as an invalid manifest and reused for as long as the commit stayed the same, and then aborted the whole crawl and froze the catalogue for everyone. **A kept index gets the current blocklist** (`keep`: the published index is turned back into `assemble`'s input and assembled again, so the blocklist, the counts and the duplicate marks follow the same rules, and `blocked` marks survive so the repository is read again once unblocked). Cards that were hidden as all-duplicate are not in the published file, so a kept index cannot bring them back and its `total` counts only what it lists. Forks and archived repositories are left out, and so is a repository with no valid manifest. Two plugins with one `id`: the one in the older repository (by creation date) keeps it and the other is marked `duplicate` and not shown, so a newer repository cannot take an id by gathering stars.
  - **Moderation is a blocklist** (`site/plugins/blocklist.json`: `repos` and `ids`), changed by pull request. The published index holds the plugins left after the blocklist, so a repository reused from it could never get a blocked plugin back. A card or `skipped` entry that lost plugins to the blocklist therefore says how many (`blocked`), and `previousScans` does not reuse it: that repository is read again on every run until the id is unblocked, and a blocked repository, which has no entry at all, is read again too. Filtering when the page is drawn over scans that keep the blocked plugins was rejected: the published file would then list what moderation removed. The cost is one tree and its manifests per blocked repository per run.
  - **The index** (`plugins/index.json`, version 1): `generated`, `source` (`total`, `missing`, `invalid`, `duplicate`, `blocked`), `repos` (one card per repository: `repo`, `description`, `stars`, `language`, `pushed`, `created`, `commit`, `invalid`, `blocked` and `plugins`, each with its folder `path` and the manifest's `id`, `name`, `version`, `description`, `platforms`, `permissions`, `min_cornercase`) and `skipped`. It holds no address: the page builds `https://github.com/<repo>/tree/<commit>/<path>` and `cornercase plugin install <repo>[/<path>]@<commit>` itself (`owner/repo/sub/dir`, see Installing). `catalogue.ts` validates it strictly (unknown fields, types, at most 1,000 repositories and 8 MiB) at build time, so a bad crawl fails the build instead of publishing.
  - **Joined and star history** (`joined`, `stars_history` on a card, optional, `VERSION` stays 1; `site/src/lib/plugins/market.ts` for what reads them): trending and new need what a single crawl cannot see, so a card carries `joined` (when the crawl first listed the repository) and up to 8 daily samples `[YYYY-MM-DD, stars]`, oldest first, one per UTC day. `assemble` takes the published index as its last argument and carries both forward: a repository it lacks joins at the crawl time; a published card without `joined` (written before this) gets its GitHub `created` date, so the first run after deploy does not mark everything new; the first crawl of a UTC day appends that day's sample and a second one the same day changes nothing; the ninth sample drops the oldest. A card kept after a per-repository failure, and every card of a kept index (`foundFrom`), carries its `joined` and history as they were, without a new sample, since its stars are as old as the card. Validation refuses unknown fields as before, a history of more than 8, a sample that is not a real `YYYY-MM-DD` day with a whole non-negative number, and days that are not strictly ascending. A repository that was only in `skipped` (no valid plugin) has no `joined`, so it joins when it first gets a card.
  - **Trending and new, herdr's rules** (`market.ts`, pure, tested at each edge): the **weekly gain** is the stars now minus the sample dated exactly 7 days before the crawl's UTC day, else the newest sample at least 7 days old, and none when there is no such sample (it never trends). The **trending score** is `gain / max(stars − gain, 1)`, only for a gain of 10 or more, so the long tail's 1 to 3 stars cannot flood it while a big repository must grow in proportion; the shelf is the top 6 by score and is hidden when fewer than 3 qualify. **New** is joined within 14 days of the index's `generated` time and created within 60 days (a long-known project that was only found this week is not new), newest joined first, 6 shown, hidden under 3. The sorts are Popular (stars, then last push), Trending (score with none as −1, then gain, then stars), Recently updated (push), Newest (`joined`, else `created`) and Name (`localeCompare`). Search lower-cases and splits the query on whitespace, and every word must be a substring of one text made of the repository, owner, name, description, language and each visible plugin's name, id and description. Visible plugins exclude duplicates, and a card with none is neither shown nor counted (the empty card of a repository whose only plugin copies another's id); the page's counts are visible plugins, shown cards, the sum of positive weekly gains and the cards joined within 7 days. Language colours are GitHub linguist's for nine languages and nothing for the rest. Relative times floor each unit (year 365 days, month 30) and say `just now` under a minute.
  - **The dev fixture** (`site/tests/fixtures/plugins/fixture.ts`): crawl input, not a finished index: 14 fake repositories (several with more than one plugin, a duplicate id whose newer repository must disappear, invalid manifests, one blocked by a fixture blocklist, histories and dates that fill trending and new), with dates relative to a clock, assembled through the real `assemble` by `fixtureIndex(now)`. The tests use a fixed `now`; `npm run plugins:dev` writes it for the current time to `site/plugins/index.dev.json`, which is not committed, for the page to read when asked (`plugins/index.json` never holds fake data).
  - **The page** (`/plugins/`, `src/pages/plugins/index.astro`, `src/lib/plugins/page.ts`, `client.ts`): a standalone page, not Starlight (no docs search, no table of contents), replicating herdr's marketplace page in the landing's style: its `Nav` and `Footer` (taking a `standalone` prop, so their anchors go to the landing, and the star-count fetch is off), fonts, tokens and purple, dark only. Nothing on it reads like herdr's page: the header is one column at every width: a muted command line (`$ cornercase plugin list`, as the sections' ones), `Extend your terminal.`, the count as that command's output in mono (`13 plugins · 10 repositories · refreshed every hour`, with singular forms, from `page.ts`'s `headline`), one sentence of lead and the trust line (herdr's big number in a right aside and its `Publish yours` link are gone, since the publish line at the bottom says it); then **Rising this week**, **Just landed** and **Every plugin**, each with a small muted command line above its title in the landing's eyebrow style (`$ sort --stars --since 7d`, `$ ls --newest`, `$ ls --all`) instead of a subtitle, and no ids or `aria-labelledby` changed (search with a `/` hint that also focuses it, five sorts, a stats line, 24 cards then `show N more` naming the remainder, never in the URL) and, instead of a closing card, a **Publish yours** section titled like the others (the command line `$ gh repo edit --add-topic <topic>` above a visible h2), one paragraph saying to add the manifest (`MANIFEST`) to a public repository and tag it with the topic, that it shows up on the next refresh with no submission and no review, the guide link as an item of its own, and last a terminal line in the look of the landing's install box holding the full command (built from the `TOPIC` constant, with a copy button), its text directly on the purple box with no inner field: the light inline-code look is only for the `<code>` of the paragraph. The standalone footer leaves out the landing's call to action (`Footer`'s `standalone` prop), which has no reveal here and showed as an empty block with a glow. **Everything is rendered at build time** from the validated index by `view()` (market.ts's rules): the shelves, every card in Popular order, the counts and the stats line, so the page works without JavaScript (all cards shown); each card carries its rank for each sort (computed by market.ts, so the script holds no second copy of the comparators) and a lower-cased haystack, and the script only reorders, hides and pages them, matching words with market.ts's `matches`. Times are `<time datetime>` with the date as text, turned into `2h ago` by the script (the build has no clock a visitor shares). Cards are not links (a copy button cannot sit inside one): `owner/repo ↗` links to GitHub in a new tab with `noopener noreferrer`, and the card highlights as a whole. **The install command is in the card** (herdr shows none): a single-plugin card shows its commit-pinned command with a copy button, a repository with several visible plugins an `N plugins` pill that opens each plugin's name, version and command. A card with no visible plugin never renders. The language dots are set by attribute selectors in the page's stylesheet (a style attribute would break the CSP), and a test keeps their colours equal to market.ts's table. **Empty state**: while the index lists nothing (at launch it is empty), the header's count line reads `no plugins yet · be the first` (`headline`), and the Every plugin section is not rendered at all, so there are no search or sort controls and no count line to look at; the one Publish section, titled `Publish the first one` and saying `Your plugin shows up here` (the header already says nothing is listed), is then the page's only call to publish. Showing the empty controls and a second call at the bottom was rejected: two invitations and controls that do nothing read as broken. With plugins listed the page is unchanged.
  - **The CSP and the avatars**: a meta policy as the stats page has (`default-src 'none'`, `script-src 'self'`, `style-src 'self'`, `connect-src 'none'`: the script makes no request) except `img-src 'self' data: https://avatars.githubusercontent.com https://github.com`, for each owner's avatar (`https://github.com/<owner>.png?size=64`, redirected to `avatars.githubusercontent.com`). The trade-off, chosen by the user over copying the avatars into the site: the visitor's browser contacts GitHub, which sees their IP address and that they opened the list; `referrerpolicy="no-referrer"` keeps the page's address from it, and an image that fails (or is blocked) is replaced by the owner's initial. Self-hosting them would have made the crawl download and the site serve up to a thousand images. `CSP` lives in `page.ts` and a test pins it. **In dev only** (`import.meta.env.DEV`) the policy of this page and of `/stats/` is relaxed by one helper (`src/lib/csp.ts`: `'unsafe-inline'` for scripts and styles, and `ws:`/`http:` to localhost for the dev server's socket), since `astro dev` injects its CSS as inline `<style>` and an inline script and the strict policy left the preview unstyled; the production strings are pinned unchanged by a test, and the dev page was checked in a headless browser (styles apply, no violation reported). The plugin pages' copy buttons carry `data-plugin-copy`, so the listener cannot touch the shared footer's install box, which has its own handler and `data-copy`.
  - **Dev preview**: `PLUGINS_INDEX=dev` makes the page read `plugins/index.dev.json` (the fixture, see above) instead of `plugins/index.json`, read from disk only on that branch and only under Astro's dev mode (`import.meta.env.DEV`), so a build never reads it, whatever the variable says, and a production page can never show fake repositories: `npm run dev:plugins` writes the fixture for the current time and starts the dev server on it. `index.json` and its endpoint are unchanged.
  - **When**: the Pages workflow runs on its one hourly `schedule` (`23 * * * *`), shared with the community flows' refresh, besides pushes, so every scheduled build refreshes both; outside pull requests it runs `npm run plugins` (with the workflow's token, for GitHub's limits) after `npm run check` and before `npm run build`, with the flows' refresh and its stale warning before the check. The script first fetches the published index, both to skip unchanged repositories and to keep it when the crawl fails, so an outage of GitHub's API never empties the list. Pull requests build with the committed `plugins/index.json`, an empty list, so their checks never depend on the network.
  - **Pinning**: the page's command installs the commit the crawl saw, and PR 2 resolves a full commit only as that commit, never as a branch or tag of that name, so what someone copied is what installs, with the preview and the question before anything runs.
  - **No catalogue in the app**, as herdr has none: Settings › Plugins lists what is installed or linked; finding plugins is the website, installing is `cornercase plugin install`.
- **Tests**: fake plugins are `/bin/sh` scripts in temporary folders (`plugins::*::tests`, `app::tests::plugins`, `e2e::plugins`); supervision is tested on the real thread with a service that records its pid, killed, disabled and dropped.

## Updates (`update.rs`, `cli/update.rs`, `version.rs`, `http.rs`, `app/update_dialog.rs`)

- Release builds only (`debug_assertions` off), so `cargo run` and tests never call GitHub. The server asks `releases/latest` when it starts and then every hour (`update::CHECK_EVERY`, `check_updates` in config). Unauthenticated, GitHub allows 60 requests an hour per IP, so hourly leaves room for many servers behind one address; a refused check is only logged and the next hour tries again. A newer one shows ` ↑ x.y.z ` at the end of the settings row and a toast; a later release found before updating replaces it and toasts again, the same one stays quiet. The dialog shows every `CHANGELOG.md` section after the running version up to the release, newest first, so a skipped release's notes are not lost: `CHANGELOG.md` is a release asset (`extra-artifacts` in `dist-workspace.toml`), downloaded only once a newer release is found (never by `cornercase update`). Releases without that asset, or a download that fails, fall back to the `## Release Notes` part of the release body. Scrolled by wheel or ↑/↓.
- Updating downloads `cornercase-<target>.tar.gz` and its `.sha256`, unpacks with `tar` next to the binary, runs `--version` on it (a binary that cannot run here never replaces a working one), then renames it over the old one. Homebrew installs (`/Cellar/`…), a folder we cannot write and unknown platforms get a command to copy instead. Homebrew's is `brew update && brew upgrade cornercase`: `brew upgrade` refreshes taps only when the last refresh is older than `HOMEBREW_AUTO_UPDATE_SECS` (a day by default), so on its own it often answered that the release cornercase had just announced was not there.
