The check still reads GitHub's latest release, not the tap's formula, although the formula is pushed about a minute after the release: a check that falls in that minute is rare, and trying again later fixes it.
- **`cornercase update [--check] [-y|--yes]`** (`cli::update::update`) does the same from a shell: `update::install_latest` (check, then `update` or the command for `Install::Command`, which it prints and exits 1 without running), then, if a server is running, asks `RESTART` (`--yes` skips; no terminal and no `--yes` means no restart) and sends `ClientMessage::Restart`. It never attaches. Debug builds refuse unless `CORNERCASE_RELEASES_URL` is set (the e2e tests serve a fake release and update a copy of the binary). `--check` exits 0 either way, so 1 stays for errors. A same-version server from another build (after `brew upgrade`) is left to the client's `[y/N]`.
- **Before a restart, say what stops** (`update/restart.rs`): every restart (` restart now `, settings' ` restart `, `cornercase update`, `cornercase restart`, a client rejected by another build) first lists what runs in the terminals, from a `status` `Report`: agents counted by what they do, other programs by name and place, and panes at their shell prompt and the pane that asks left out (`PaneInfo::at_prompt`; a server from before it is told apart by the program's name). The app builds the Report itself, and the commands ask the running server, the old one: requests are JSON and skip the build check, and they wait at most 1 s (`client::running_now`), so a hung server or one older than requests falls back to `update::restart::generic` and never hangs the command. The text leads with what stops, not with "your session comes back", which read as if nothing was lost (#92). The installed step's dialog has ` restart now ` and ` later `, and `Enter` still restarts (the user's call); its message is one short line and the list goes in the scrollable notes area, since the message row is three lines high and the release notes were already shown before installing. `Term::shell_in_foreground` is false for a pane started with a command (`/bin/sh -c 'exec $EDITOR …'` from the changes panel, `Term::interactive`): its program runs in the shell's pid, so comparing pids alone called an editor idle, here and for `wait --until shell`. An agent always counts as running. The dialog's list is built when it opens and every `WATCH_AGENTS_EVERY` while it is open, not on every frame, since a `status` report looks up every pane's process. `cornercase update --yes` prints the list before restarting, and it and `kill-server` print what they stopped. Waiting until no agent is working is `--when-idle` (below).
- **Restarting on demand**: the settings modal has a grey ` restart ` left of ` done ` (`ui::settings_restart`, `settings::Action::Restart`), and `cornercase restart [-y|--yes]` does it from a shell. Both take the update's path: the button opens `Overlay::Restart`, the update dialog's view with its own title and message, listing what stops and refreshed like the installed step (`Overlay::lists_running`), and ` restart now ` sets `App`'s `outbox.restart`; `take_restart` hands the server the replaced binary after an update, else `current_exe`. The command shares `cli::update::restart_if_confirmed` with `update`, so it asks the same `[y/N]` with the same list and prints what it stopped; without a server it says so and succeeds, like `kill-server`. Before, the only restart without an update was `kill-server`, which closes every window instead of bringing them back. It sits in settings, not in the footer next to ` Quit `, where a slip would stop every agent.
- **Restarting once no agent is working** (#142, `cornercase restart --when-idle`, `update --when-idle`): every restart stops every program, and an agent in the middle of a turn loses the turn (its tool call, its build) even though its conversation resumes, so whoever runs several long agents could not take a release without watching for the moment they were all idle. A script could `wait --all --until turn-over` then restart, but an agent can start a turn between the two; the server instead restarts in the same step it sees every agent stopped.
  - **A request**: `restart-when-idle` (JSON, with `timeout`) is a pending `Stage::Restart` in `App`, checked by `check_requests` after `watch_agents` like the other waits. JSON rather than a new `ClientMessage`: a server from before it refuses an unknown command by name, which the command turns into `the running cornercase server is too old to wait for its agents`, whereas a postcard variant it cannot decode gets `Rejected`, which reads as another build. A command of its own rather than a field of a `restart` request, for the reason `last-message` is one: an older server would drop the field and restart at once. The plain restart stays `ClientMessage::Restart`, which older servers understand.
  - **Stopped** is the rule of `--until turn-over` (`Condition::TurnOver`) on every pane with an agent: idle, done, waiting for you, or a Claude Code turn that is over with only a background shell left (the issue's choice: waiting means the turn needs a person anyway). Also held (`App::holding`): a launch still typing (`start`, `send --enter`, `new-tab --command`), unless it is parked on a trust prompt, which waits for a person like `waiting`; a pane running an agent that got input in the last 2 s (`INPUT_HOLDS`: a prompt typed in the window shows as busy in the status file only once the agent took it, up to a read later); and a submission the agent has not reacted to within 10 s (`Term::submitted`, the reaction check of `wait`). It fires only on a refresh that has just read every agent's state (`app.agents.watched == Some(now)`), so the decision is never older than its own step, and answers with that moment's `status` Report, which the command prints as what it stopped. Programs that are not agents (a dev server, a build) never hold it, since they may run for days and the confirmation already names them; neither does an agent cornercase cannot read. opencode never says it waits for you, so one at a permission prompt holds it until the timeout. The pane the command runs in never holds it, as `wait` refuses to wait on its own pane: an agent running `update --when-idle` in its turn would wait for itself; the restart stops it like any program.
  - **The server** takes `App`'s `outbox.restart` after any step (`Server::step`) and leaves the loop at once, so no event (a key, a `send`) is handled between the check and the restart; `shutdown` sends the answers still queued before `ServerMessage::Restart`, so the command reads its answer first. A command that gets `Restart` without an answer (someone else restarted meanwhile) counts it as done; `Shutdown` (`kill-server`) is an error, since nothing comes back by itself.
  - **Giving up**: `--timeout` answers `timed out after …: the agent in pane N has not ended its turn, so the server keeps running` (status 1); a command that is interrupted closes its connection, and `App::forget` drops its pending restart like a wait.
  - **The window says so**: while a restart is pending, every window shows `↻ restart pending until 2 agents end their turn` with a ` cancel ` button (`ui::ToastIcon::Restart`, `ui::ToastButton::Cancel`, beside ` undo `), so nobody starts a long turn by surprise. Toasts last 2 s, so this one is not timed (`Toast::lasts`): `App::show_pending_restart`, after `check_requests`, puts it back whenever no other toast shows, keeps its count current and drops it once nothing is pending; a toast that comes meanwhile (an agent needs you) shows for its own time first. ` cancel ` answers every pending restart `the restart was cancelled in the window` (status 1), since whoever sits at the window may not see the command's terminal (a script, an agent, another machine). A mark in the footer or on the settings row was rejected: the toast is where the window says things, and covering a corner of the pane while it waits is the price of nobody missing it.
  - **Confirming**: the `[y/N]` is asked when the command starts, not when the restart comes: nobody sits at a prompt for an hour, and the answer covers what the list names (the agents resume, the other programs stop). The question is `Restart it once no agent is working?`; `--yes` skips it and works without a terminal, and no terminal without `--yes` does not restart, as before. `update --when-idle` installs first, then waits, so the release is on disk however the wait ends, and the restart reaches the new binary the usual way (the clients `exec` their own replaced file).
- `ClientMessage::Restart` (last variant) makes the server save and send `ServerMessage::Restart`, like ` restart now `, so attached clients come back, even when `update` ran inside one of its panes (it prints everything first). A server from before it cannot decode it and answers `Rejected` (`protocol::tests::compatibility`), so `client::restart_server` falls back to `KillServer`, whose clients just exit.
- The running server keeps the old code. ` restart now ` saves the session, sends `ServerMessage::Restart(path)`, and each client `exec`s its own executable (`current_exe`, read at start; the update replaced that file), never the path in the message, which starts a new server that restores the session. A new client rejected by an older server asks `[y/N]` on the plain terminal before running `kill-server`.
- **The window stays on screen across a restart** (#144): the client used to leave the alternate screen and raw mode before its `exec`, and the new one started the server on the plain screen, then entered a blank alternate screen, so every restart showed the shell the window was started from, its scrollback, then a blank screen: it read like a crash right after an update, and every merged pull request is a release. Now, on `Ending::Restart`, the client deletes its images, pops its keyboard flags (else the terminal's stack grows by one per restart), turns mouse and bracketed paste off and leaves raw mode, but keeps the alternate screen, which is the terminal's state and survives `exec`, so the last frame stays up while the old server stops and the new one starts; the server's first frame for the new client clears and redraws it. Raw mode goes because crossterm keeps the attributes it found before entering it in the process's memory, which `exec` loses: a new client started raw would take raw for the original and leave the shell raw when it quits (`e2e::updates::a_restart_keeps_the_window_on_the_alternate_screen` runs it under a shell that prints `stty -a` after it). Mouse and paste go with it, so a mouse moved during the restart is not echoed over the frame as text; a key typed then is, until the first frame. The new process learns it comes from a restart from `CORNERCASE_RESTARTED` (`protocol::RESTARTED_ENV`), set on the `exec`: an environment variable rather than a hidden flag, so an older binary `exec`'d after a downgrade ignores it instead of clap refusing it. It then builds its `Terminal` with `ratatui::init_with_options` (raw mode and the panic hook, no `EnterAlternateScreen`: `?1049h` while already there clears the screen on some terminals). `start_server` removes the variable, so neither the server nor its shells inherit it, and only the first `open` reads it (the `[y/N]` after a rejection opens again on the plain screen). An `exec` that fails, or a new client that cannot reach or start its server, turns mouse and paste off and leaves the alternate screen before the error is printed, so the terminal is never left raw with an invisible message. A `restarting…` notice over the frozen frame was left out: the frame stays up for about as long as the server takes to restore the session. The first update to the release with this still flashes, since the old client does the `exec`; remote windows never `exec` and already reconnect on the alternate screen.

## Saved session (`state.rs`, `persist.rs`, `app.rs`, `app/session.rs`)

- Groups (name, icon, colour, collapsed), projects (with their group's index and whether they are folded), workspaces (with their changes base and fold), tabs, panes (cwd, the agent conversation to resume and its `done` and `pending` marks), split layouts, custom names, active children, column widths (and the stacked line and the agents section's height), the changes panel (open, scope, mode), whether the TODO, files or Flow panel is open, and a workspace's flow run (see Flows), in `$XDG_STATE_HOME/cornercase/session.json` (or next to the socket). The TODO list is not in it (see TODO panel). Processes are not restored; each pane gets a new shell in its folder. `project::group::known_icon` replaces an unknown saved group icon with `GROUP_ICONS[0]`. Preset selection and saved-icon normalization belong to the group model, so `App` calls its rules instead of defining them.
- **The mapping is in `state.rs`** (#195): `ProjectState::of`, `WorkspaceState::of` and `TabState::of` build `State::projects`; `restore` on each builds the model from the saved data and clamps the active child. `App` keeps what is not mapping (canonical paths, skipping missing folders, spawning, resuming, toasts) and takes ids in the same order as before: a project's and a workspace's id before their children's, a tab's after its panes'.
- Saved only once the state is stable for 2 s, so the burst of `Exited` events at logout does not save an empty session.
- Only a fresh server restores, once: on its first window's `Hello` or its first `cornercase start-server`, whichever comes first (see Client / server). Missing folders are skipped. `VERSION` is 5; older versions are migrated: versions 3 and 4 are read as is (a version 3 file has every project ungrouped), then, like versions 1 and 2, every project but the active one is folded.
- A tab whose shell cannot start (a folder it may not enter, no PTY left, a `$SHELL` that is gone) is left out whole, also a split one, and the restore goes on: its workspace and project stay, so `+ Tab` can try again, a toast names the projects (`could not restore n tabs (…), see server.log`) and `server.log` has, per tab, the folder that failed and the cause: `Error::SpawnShell` puts both in its message, like `RunGit`, since `thiserror`'s `Display` leaves out a `#[source]`, and `cornercase new-tab` or `split` into such a folder answer the same text. `App::restore` returns whether every tab came back. When one did not, the server copies `session.json` to `session.json.bak` before its `Saver` can write the smaller session over it; an older `.bak` moves to `session.json.bak.1` first, so a second incomplete restore with other failing tabs cannot overwrite the only full copy. Keeping an existing `.bak` instead (a review suggestion) was rejected: after one incident every later one would keep a stale copy. If the copy fails the `Saver` still saves: it writes to the same folder and disk, so what breaks the copy breaks the save too, and holding it back would lose every later change of that server run. Stopping at the first failure was the bug (#24): it dropped every project after it and then saved that. The fallback shell opens in the current folder when no project came back, as when there is no session, or when the restore was incomplete and no shell came back at all (`App::has_terms`). A session saved without tabs (closing the last tab keeps the workspace) comes back as it was, without it.
- **Agents resume their conversations** (#28, `resume_agents` in `config.json`, Settings → Agents, on by default): a pane that ran Claude Code, Codex, Gemini CLI, Grok Build or Cursor CLI comes back in the same conversation instead of a bare shell, since finding it again by hand with `claude --resume` was what a restart cost most. `App::watch_agents` (every 500 ms, every pane) keeps `Term::resume`, a `agents::AgentState`: the agent's kind, its conversation (`agents::context::Pane::conversation`: the `sessionId` of `sessions/<pid>.json` once its transcript has content, or the id of the Codex rollout the context line follows, which Codex creates with the first turn, or Gemini's `sessionId` after a real prompt or a Gemini response, including a compressed history) and its mode, read from the process's arguments (`agents::mode_of`), so an agent started in plan mode comes back in plan mode whatever the settings say now. A conversation with nothing in it is not kept: `claude --resume` on it fails with `No conversation found`. The pane saves it in `PaneState::agent`; once the shell is back in the foreground it is forgotten, so an agent that was quit comes back as a shell. The field is optional, so `VERSION` stays 5: a bump would make an older cornercase drop the whole session, and an older one ignores the field. On restore, `App::resume` types `agents::resume_line` (`claude <args> --resume <id>`, `codex resume <args> <id>`, `gemini <args> --resume <id>`, `cursor-agent <args> --resume <chat id>` (the chat id is the name of the chat's folder in the store, kept once the root has a turn; it must run in the folder the chat started in), the command and extra arguments from settings, the mode from the saved state) as an agent launch without a prompt (`Launch::resume`, #324): it waits for the shell to be quiet, types the line, then waits until the agent is in the foreground and has drawn its screen, as `start` does; the resumed agent is idle, and what it was doing is not started again. A trust question parks it for the user (it is not answered, whatever `accept_trust_prompts` says, as before), except for a flow's role resumed in its role's own agent (the role's command, whose first launch answered it too); a flow pane that saved another agent resumes with that agent's ordinary line and leaves the question to the user. It was a `Launch::command`, done once the line was typed, which left nothing to say when the agent was back. The restored conversation is kept until the agent shows up with it, or for at most `RESUME_GRACE` (30 s, `Term::resuming`): until the command is typed the shell is in the foreground, and an agent that is slow to start has no session file yet, so a state saved meanwhile (2 s of quiet is enough) would lose it, and a restart then would not resume. A resume that fails (`No conversation found`) is forgotten once the grace runs out. Its JSON in `status` (`PaneInfo::resumes`) lets the restart texts count `n agent conversations resume in their tabs` instead of the `claude --continue` hint, which stays for a server from before it and with the switch off. Off by default was rejected: the agent comes back idle, so resuming costs nothing but the screen, and a resumed agent in a dangerous mode was already running in it. Reading the agent's own arguments to resume with them was rejected: they may hold a prompt, which would be sent again.
- **A resuming pane takes no input** (#324): the window shows as soon as the server restores the session, while each resume waits for its shell, types its line and the agent starts, which takes seconds; keys typed meanwhile went in front of the resume line (`lsclaude --resume …`, a resume that fails) or into the agent's start-up, where an `Enter` can answer a question nobody saw. So while a pane has a resume launch (`Launch::resume`, or a flow orchestrator's restored brief) that is not parked on a question for the user (`App::resume_holds`), what the window sends it is dropped: keys and pastes (`App::holds_input`, which toasts `claude is resuming its conversation in this pane; wait for it, or press cancel`), mouse events for its program (`forward_mouse`) and the wheel's arrow keys on the alternate screen; **ask agent** copies its reference instead, as with no agent. Dropped rather than queued: sent once the agent is back, a queued `Enter` could still answer whatever the agent shows first. cornercase's own mouse (selection, the scrollback wheel, the pane menu, dividers) still works, and so do other panes. The hold ends when the launch does (the agent drew and went quiet, or the shell came back because the agent exited), or once `RESUME_GRACE` (30 s from when the resume was queued) runs out, whichever comes first: the launch carries that deadline (`Launch::resume`'s `until`, the pane's own `Term::resuming` for an orchestrator's brief) and `Launch::holds` reads it, since the launch's own timeouts restart at each stage and could hold the pane longer. The launch is not dropped at the deadline, so a slow orchestrator still gets its brief. A trust question parks the launch, so the pane takes keys to answer it. The pane shows ` resuming claude… ` and a grey ` cancel ` pill on a surface band across its middle (`ui::resume_notice`, shared by drawing and the click). **cancel** drops the launch: before the line is typed it is never typed, and the pane forgets the conversation and its marks (`Pane::give_up`); after, the agent keeps starting and only the keys come back, since a typed command cannot be taken back. `Esc` was left out as the way to cancel: it is a key, and AGENTS.md asks before adding any. Only window input is held, not the commands: `send` refuses a resuming pane unless `--force` (its text would land in the same places), `keys` goes through since it is how to answer things, and `status` shows `resuming` (in the JSON and as the last column of `--panes`). Holding every launch that types into a fresh pane (`start`, the issues, the queue) was left out: those panes open while the user is doing something else, where a restart puts every pane on screen at once.
- **Marks come back with the conversation**: a pane saves `done` (`✓`, `activity::Pane::unseen`) and `pending` (see Agent status) only while it has a conversation to resume (`Term::resume`), as optional fields of `PaneState`, so `VERSION` stays 5. Without them a restart or an update wiped every `✓` and lost which agents were still to review. A shell pane saves neither: a mark without its agent means nothing, and would show on whatever agent is started there later. On restore, `App::resume` returning true (resuming on and a resume line for that agent) hands them to `Pane::restore`, which keeps them while the resumed agent has not shown up (`follow` from no agent keeps a restored pane's marks; `update` with no activity leaves them), so they reach the same agent whatever its start-up time, and marks it as told (`notified`), so a restored `✓` does not toast or send a desktop notification again for work finished before the restart. If the agent never shows up within `RESUME_GRACE`, `Pane::give_up` drops them with the resume. With `resume_agents` off nothing comes back, as with the conversation.
- **Role worktrees come back too** (#313): `WorkspaceState::role_worktrees` (role, folder, branch), optional like `run`, so `VERSION` stays 5, lives on the run's workspace and outlives the `Run`, which ends when its panes close while the worktrees stay until the workspace goes. Panes already save their folder, so an own role comes back in its worktree; one whose folder is gone gets a shell in the run's worktree and its agent is not resumed there (a toast names the role, and `server.log` has a warning), since the conversation would continue in the shared tree. `sync_worktrees` drops entries whose folder is gone.
- **A flow run comes back with its workspace** (#263, see Flows): `WorkspaceState::run` holds the flow, the task, each role's tab and pane position and where the plan stands, and the session's `flow` flag (beside `todo` and `files`, skipped when false) reopens the Flow panel. Both are optional, so `VERSION` stays 5 and the v5 fixture is unchanged. A role resumes with its role's command (its mode that never asks, model, args and env) rather than the settings' line, so a role on another provider stays there.

## Website (`site/`)

- **DNS** (DigitalOcean): GitHub Pages' A/AAAA records on the apex `usecornercase.dev`, and a `www` CNAME to `usecornercase.github.io`; it moved here from AGENTS.md to keep that file under Codex's 32 KiB.
- **TypeScript majors are ignored in `site/`** (Dependabot): `@astrojs/check` 0.9.10 accepts only TypeScript 5 and 6 (7 is the Go port, without the JS API its language server uses); drop the `ignore` once it supports 7.
- **The landing's demo loads as a still picture** (`scenes.ts` → `poster`, a `<Snapshot>` in `Demo.astro`) that already shows the point of the product: a busy session. Seven projects in two groups; web-shop active with three worktrees named as a team names them (`feat/dark-mode`, `feat/gift-cards`, `fix/empty-address`; its own checkout is closed, so the list is only lines of work), a Claude working (`◐`) in each; the first one's conversation in the pane, two turns deep, with the paths it names (`src/theme.rs:7`, `src/checkout.rs`) as links, one of them hovered in the picture so it is underlined: a click on it opens the files panel at that line; the TODO list on the right (eight items, three done); and on the project rows `orders-api` needing you (`!`) and `blog`'s agent done (`✓`). The simulation (`boot.ts`, `client.ts`) is mounted only on the first interaction: the `Try cornercase live` button, which mounts it as the picture shows it and hands it over, or a click on the picture itself, which mounts it and replays that click, so the window answers where it was touched (a path click opens the file, `Issues` opens the dialog). The button says what the window is, a real simulation to use, rather than offering the tour: a tour is something you watch, and the strip offers it (`watch the tour`) once the window is live. Four chapter thumbnails under the window were tried and dropped: a row of cards repeating the section below. Before (#80 and earlier) the simulation mounted at load and the tour started by itself when the hero scrolled into view: it cost CPU for everyone, moved while the headline was being read, and its first frame showed an almost empty pane. `prefers-reduced-motion` needs no special case: nothing moves until asked. Below 560 px the picture is `poster-compact` (compact mode, 34 rows), below 900 px `poster-102` (102×32, the tablet tier's columns at its widest). Above it the picture is one of four at 46 rows (`poster` at 177 columns, `poster-190`, `poster-202`, `poster-220`), picked by a media query so that its columns are never fewer than the live window's: a picture with fewer columns than the window scales taller than the rows and is clipped, one with more leaves a band under it, and the breakpoints (1281, 1367 and 1446 px) make the fit exact at 1280, 1366, 1440 and from 1560 px up, with at most ~8 % of band in between. The TODO panel opens in the picture only from 120 columns (`CHANGES_FROM`): narrower, the pane next to it would wrap every line of the agent's. The stacked sidebar's split is 14 rows (`world()`, what seven projects in two groups need), so at 46 rows both lists show whole; at 32 rows the workspaces list shows the active worktree and `↓ n more`, and chapter 3 of the tour scrolls it to `#482` before clicking (`show`).
- **The window has a real session's proportions, not a zoomed-in one**: a 6.6 px cell (11 px font), 177×46 at a 1280 px viewport (`boot.ts`, `cell` and `rows`). At the 9.6 px cell it had before, the 122×32 window looked crammed next to a real terminal: a session in a 1850 px window has about 185 columns and 52 rows, and what makes it breathe is the window's size relative to the font, so at the landing's width only a smaller font gives the same air. The tiers follow the viewport (`window.innerWidth`, what the media queries read, so the pictures and the heights agree with the live window): 7.4 px and 34 rows below 560 px, 7.8 px and 32 rows below 900 px, 6.6 px and 46 rows from there. The window keeps its height; a smaller cell means more rows. The "Why" pictures are windows or crops at that same cell size, rendered at 578 px in their column: the issues dialog from a 90-column window, where it is 86 columns wide, so its body wraps instead of being cut; `status` a whole 96×43 window in the poster's state (sidebar and pane, no panel: 96 columns is where the agent's prose wraps inside the picture and the welcome box fits, and 43 rows is what the two turns need, so nothing in it is cut); and `changes` the 87-column panel with its border, from the #482 worktree with its fix. So every picture on the page has the window's zoom. Before, each one was a close-up at its own zoom.
- **The tour's four chapters are the four steps of "Why cornercase"** (Start, In parallel, Review, Leave and come back), in that order, with the same names, so the demo and the section tell one story. Review comes before leaving (it used to come after), so chapter 3 waits until the agent has edited the files (`Task.onStep` marks the workspace as fixed after its third step) rather than until it is done. The narration goes in a strip inside the window, under the screen, not in a line below the window nor over the rows. The tour starts from the same world without `fix/empty-address` (`world(app, { scripted: true })`): chapter 1 starts #482 from the issue, and the poster's third worktree is that same bug already under way, so both at once would show one bug fixed twice. The section's pictures are `issue` (the dialog), `status` (a narrow window in the poster's state: the sidebar and the conversation) and `changes` (the #482 diff, from `fixedState`: the world plus the issue's worktree with its fix done).
- **One session in every picture** (#80): the landing, its tour, `og.png` and every docs screenshot show the same cast, so a reader who saw the landing page recognises the docs. Two groups made with the first two presets (`work` `●` blue, `personal` `◆` green: exactly what `+ New → new group` gives twice): `web-shop`, `orders-api`, `mobile-app` and `payments` in `work`, `blog`, `dotfiles` and `playground` in `personal`; the docs' session opens four of them (`web-shop`, `orders-api`, `blog`, `dotfiles`) and its folder picker lists all seven. The story is issue #482 of `acme/web-shop` (`src/returns/address.rs`), started in the tour and shown in the changes and files guides; the other lines of work are dark mode, gift cards and the pagination of `orders-api`. The fake projects' files, commits and issues live once, in `site/src/lib/demo/data.ts`, and feed both the simulation (`world()`) and the captured repositories. The landing's cut of it: all seven projects open, web-shop with its three worktrees and a Claude in each (the dark-mode one two turns into a conversation that names `src/theme.rs:7`), Codex on orders-api's `fix/pagination`, which asks about `src/orders.ts` (so the row shows `!`), and a Claude in blog that finished before the picture is taken (`✓`); the docs' session keeps web-shop's `main` next to `feat/dark-mode` and `feat/gift-cards`, since the worktree and changes screens compare against it. The capture sessions save `stack: 11` (what four projects need), the landing's `world()` 14 (seven), so both lists show whole in every picture.
- **No line over 44 characters inside a pane**, in the simulation's shells and in the capture stand-ins: terminals wrap mid-word, and 44 is what fits a phone in compact mode (about 46 columns at 360 px) and the narrow panes of the tour. Exempt as chrome rather than prose: the agents' box lines (62), nvim's status line and the pasted issue URL (45). The simulated agents' conversations are exempt too: they wrap at words (`text.ts` `wrapWords`, the continuation indented under the bullet), as Claude Code wraps its own output, so a reply can be a real paragraph; only a tool call's `Name(argument)` keeps the limit.
- **The simulated Claude looks like Claude Code** (`programs.ts` `Agent`, checked against a 2.1 session): the welcome box, the user's message after a dim `>`, each tool call as `● Read(src/theme.rs)` with its result under it (`⎿  Read 14 lines`, shown as `Running…` until the next call), the reply as prose with bold leads, inline code in blue and `-` lists, then `✻ Churned for 31s · done 9:39 AM` (the clock starts at a fixed time and advances by each turn's duration, so the pictures are the same on every build), the spinner line `✻ Churning… (esc to interrupt · 19s)` while it works, and under everything the rule, the `›` prompt and the mode line (`⏵⏵ accept edits on`, `⏸ plan mode on`). The asterisks come from the font's fallback; `⎿` is drawn as a line shape like `└`. A task (`taskFor`, keyed by words of the prompt) is a list of tool calls and a reply, with `onStep` on each call for the tour's `fixed` flag. Other agents get the same structure with `•` and `└`. An earlier turn can be replayed whole (`history`), which is how the poster's conversation is two turns deep. The `og` scene is the 46-row window cropped to its top 56×32, so the card shows the welcome box and the question rather than the tail of the conversation.
- Capture instances explicitly set their own `CORNERCASE_SOCKET`, so their stop commands cannot reach a developer's attached server; their config, session and stats live beside that private socket.
- **Docs screenshots are real captures, made by a script** (`site/capture/`): the app itself, in tmux, with a fake `HOME` built from `data.ts` (git repositories with fixed authors and dates, so hashes never change; `origin/HEAD` pinned so the base label does not depend on the git version), a `session.json` per screen, and stand-ins on the `PATH` for `claude`, `codex`, `gh`, `nvim`, `npm` and `cargo` that print the same cast's work (the #482 fix, dark mode, gift cards, pagination) and write the agents' status and context files like the test fakes; the `claude` and `codex` ones draw a plainer screen than the simulation (a box, `●` steps, a spinner line), not yet its Claude Code look (the non-agent stand-ins are signed copies of `bash` named after the program, since a tab's label is the foreground process's name), plus a fake `ssh` that runs the proxy locally and then refuses and hangs, for the reconnecting notice. Every screen ends with a wait on text that is only on screen in its final state (an exact row with its padding when a hover must be gone), never a longer sleep, so two runs give the same bytes and a pull request's diff shows only what the UI changed. Rendering the docs from the simulation was rejected: the screenshots are proof that the real app looks like this. Wide screens are 110×38 (38, not 30, so both stacked lists fit whole with the agents' two-row tabs), the panel screens 140×38 with the panel 45 columns wide (the default 40 cut code lines), the agents section 110×44, compact ones 64×35 (the smallest height at which the compact projects menu shows both groups, the four projects and `+ New`). Issue ages and usage resets are computed from now when the data is dumped, so they read the same on every run. The `gh` stand-in answers `pr view` with the pull requests of `data.ts`'s `PULLS` (by the branch it runs on) only once a screen creates `$CC_DATA/gh/pulls` before starting, and `no pull requests found` otherwise, so the other screens show no pull request mark and do not depend on when the first answer arrives.
- **Flows in the simulation and the captures** (#263): the simulation has one Full issue run, in `payments` (web-shop's three worktrees are the poster and the tour's story, and a fourth workspace of five tabs would push them out of the stacked sidebar). Its script (`flowrun.ts`) plays only while its Flow panel shows and starts over after its end: a run playing in the background would be over before a visitor found it, and its agents finishing would keep moving a landing page meant to be calm; nothing claims a real run pauses. The first time its workspace shows, the narration says to open **show flow**; there is no tour chapter, since the four chapters are the four steps of Why cornercase. The Flows and start dialogs are left out (forms that would double the work without showing anything the screenshots do not), and their entries say they need a real cornercase; the Settings Flows tab (#322) lists the five built-in flows, hard-coded, at the settings dialog's 140×40 size, and a click on one says so. For the docs, the captures' `claude` stand-in plays the part its first input gives it: a brief makes it an orchestrator that reads the pane ids from the brief and sends real `cornercase send` requests, so the server records the panel's messages; a role prompt makes it a member that works for two seconds on each message. A prompt arrives pasted as one line, so it drains the paste, turns echo off and redraws; any other first input keeps the old behaviour, so the other screens did not change.
- **`og.png`** (1200×630) is a screenshot of a scratch page that composes the headline and the `og` scene (`scenes.ts`: the poster's state at 100×32, cropped to its first 56 columns), taken with Chrome headless by `node site/capture/brand.mjs`, which also updates `readme.png` when the hero or poster changes. It uses the installed site dependencies and `chromium` on the PATH, or `CORNERCASE_CAPTURE_BROWSER` for another executable.
- **The terminal's colours are Tokyo Night** (`palette.ts` `theme`): the demo, the section pictures, `og.png` and the docs screenshots all use it, and the demo's window bar has a theme picker (`Demo.astro` `.themes`, `boot.ts` `pick`): Tokyo Night, Catppuccin Mocha, Dracula, One Dark Pro, GitHub Dark, Gruvbox Dark and Midnight, the site's own purple-black palette that was the default before. Picked after measuring who uses what (2026-10-10): VS Code's marketplace is led by GitHub Dark (20 M installs), One Dark Pro (13 M) and Dracula (11 M), but the people who live in a terminal, cornercase's readers, pick Tokyo Night and Catppuccin first (dotfyle's ranking of Neovim configurations; 8 k and 20 k GitHub stars), and Tokyo Night's blue-black with purple and cyan is the closest of them to the brand. The picker demonstrates "your colours": a click mounts the live window in that theme, the chrome follows the theme's background (`--term` on the demo's root), and the diff tints (`changes.ts` `tintsOf`) mix the theme's own background, red and green, so they are right in every theme. Each theme gets its surfaces from its background and foreground (`make`: 235, 236, 239, 254) and its brand purple (99) from its own purple. To go back to Midnight everywhere: point `theme` at `midnight`, set `--term` in `landing.css` and the frame colours in `Screen.astro` back to `#0e0d14` and `#15131f`, and regenerate `og.png`.
- **`readme.png`** (`site/public/`, the README's picture) is a banner, 1600×840 shot at 1.5×: the hero's purple-black glow and dot grid, the logo and wordmark, the headline in three lines (`in parallel.` in the brand purple), the pitch, three pills (`Linux & macOS`, `Rust`, `open source`) and, coming in from the right edge, the `readme` scene (the poster's state in a 110×43 window, no panel) in a window frame with a slight perspective (`rotateY(-7deg)` about its left bottom corner, so the right side is nearer), about 80 columns of it in view: the sidebar whole, footer included, and the first 45 columns of the conversation. 43 rows rather than the docs' 38, since at 38 the third worktree hides under `↓ 1 more`. Rounded corners over a transparent outside, so it sits on GitHub's light and dark pages. At the README's 830 px the terminal is a mood picture (3.8 px per cell): the banner says what cornercase is before the reader scrolls, and the window shows the shape of a session; the framed window alone, used before it, was readable but a screenshot without a caption, and the social card before that was cut off at that width. A generated page shot with Chrome headless by `site/capture/brand.mjs`, like `og.png`; its glow is a gradient behind the window rather than a shadow on it, because headless Chrome rasterises a blurred shadow on a 3D-transformed element as a white block. Regenerated with `og.png` when the hero or the poster changes.
- **The landing page in Spanish and Brazilian Portuguese** (#93): `/es/` and `/pt-br/` render the same `Home.astro` as `/`, and each component takes its words from `src/i18n` by the page's URL (`words(Astro.url)`), so nothing passes a locale down. Only the landing is translated: the people who use cornercase already work in English, the app's layout is measured to the cell, and the docs would triple the pages every feature change keeps in sync. Spanish is neutral, with `tú` and no regional words (`teléfono`, not `móvil` or `celular`); Portuguese is Brazil's, where most of that audience is, and still needs a native speaker's review. Each locale file exports `page` (the components) and `demo` (the tour's captions and the simulation's narration under the window, `App::narration`); the demo picks its words from the root's `data-locale`, and the bundler drops the `page` objects from the client script. What the simulation draws inside the terminal stays English, as does what the tour clicks, since it finds buttons by their text. Strings that need markup carry marks instead of HTML (`{working}`, `{done}`, backticks), rendered by `Rich.astro`, since HTML set with `set:html` would miss the component's scoped styles. The speed numbers are untouched, chart labels included; the prose around them writes its numbers the locale's way (`3472 líneas`, `32.255 arquivos`, `8,5 segundos`), since `3,472` reads as a decimal in both languages. The stretched headline lengthens the word's last letter instead (`Rápidooo.`), and the hero's first line was rewritten (`Lo más rápido para`) so it keeps to two lines, as in English. SEO: `<html lang>`, a canonical URL per page, `hreflang` alternates for the three plus `x-default` on `/`, and `og:locale` with its alternates. The switcher is three plain links in the nav (`EN ES PT`); there is no redirect by `Accept-Language`, which a static host can only do with JavaScript, which hurts indexing and surprises whoever wanted English. Links to the docs carry `hreflang="en"`, and the nav and footer say the docs are in English. The switcher made the nav wider, so the section links fold at 1040 px (was 860) and the docs link and install button below 600 px (was 480), in every locale, rather than at widths per language. A `Plugins` link before `Docs` (`Plugins (EN)` in Spanish and Portuguese, since the catalogue is in English too) moved the fold to 1160 px, the width the Spanish nav needs; between 600 and 1160 px only `Docs` stays, and below 600 px it goes too, as before.
- **The feature list leaves out what the four steps already tell** (issues to agents, the changes panel, agents that run agents) and groups the rest in three lists (agents, code, terminal), each line linking to its docs page.

## Test helpers (`test_util.rs`, `agents/{claude,codex,gemini,opencode,grok,cursor}/fake.rs`, `app/tests.rs`)

- App tests share event draining, drawing, config writing and mouse geometry at the top of `app::tests`, so those helpers can move together when the test modules move into their own files. `drain_in` takes an explicit area; each caller keeps whether it refreshes before or after draining. `render_frame` clones the completed frame's buffer, since cells marked to skip a backend diff never reach `TestBackend` and the image tests inspect those cells.
- `FakeCodex` appends the turn fixtures to its rollout and its script sets the title it finds in a `title` signal file (OSC 0). `FakeOpencode` is a script named `opencode` that holds a database built from the fixture schema, whose rows the test writes. `FakeGemini` writes a session JSONL under a temporary `GEMINI_CLI_HOME` and sets OSC 0 titles from a signal file.
- `agents::cursor::fake::FakeCursor` builds a chat store (the SQLite blobs and meta row, with a test-only protobuf encoder and `sha2`) and a transcript under temporary config and data folders, and a script named `cursor-agent` that holds the store open (`exec 3<`, so `open_files` sees it, as for opencode) and sets OSC 0 titles from a signal file; its helpers write turns, replies, thinking and tool steps, the context, the model and the turn endings. No test runs the real Cursor or reads the real `~/.cursor`.
- Each agent's `fake.rs` owns its fake: `agents::claude::fake::FakeClaude` owns the temporary config and script folders for both status and command tests. Generic helpers (`TempDir`, `Sleeper`, `Running`, executable writing, append and title signals) stay in `test_util` because several agents share them. Its four scripts keep their original bytes. Starting it stays in an app test trait, since setting `App`'s `agents.claude_dir` and typing into a pane need the app's private state. The command harness keeps `app` first so its panes stop before the fake's folders are dropped. Probe, issue-launch and integration scripts stay with their tests because they serve different protocols.
- `test_util::fixture!` resolves from `CARGO_MANIFEST_DIR`, so moving a test one folder deeper cannot change what fixture it reads. The saved-session and config fixtures pin both the loaded structs and the exact serialized text, including omitted defaults and enum names. A format change updates them together; an older session fixture stays as a migration test when the version changes. The restore round trip uses canonical folders, waits for every shell's live cwd and covers each side panel separately, since `App::state()` reads live panes and only one panel can be open. Fixtures use synthetic paths and accounts and restore tests launch no agent.
